Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 9

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 9 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 9 Sexual Dysfunctions, Paraphilic Disorders and Gender Dysphoria

In this chapter
  1. 9.1 Sex, Gender and Disorder: The Science of Sexuality and How the Manuals Classify It: distinguish sex, gender identity, gender role, sexual orientation and androgyny, trace sexology from Krafft-Ebing to Kinsey, and place every disorder of the chapter in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
  2. 9.2 The Sexual Response Cycle: Models, Biology and What Disrupts It: compare the linear and circular models of sexual response, explain the nerves, blood vessels, hormones and transmitters behind arousal and orgasm, and show how anxiety, attention, relationships and culture disrupt them.
  3. 9.3 Sexual Dysfunctions I: The Shared Rules, and the Disorders of Desire, Arousal and Erection: apply the rules common to the DSM-5-TR sexual dysfunctions, estimate how common they are, and diagnose and treat female sexual interest/arousal disorder, male hypoactive sexual desire disorder and erectile disorder, including the PDE-5 inhibitors.
  4. 9.4 Sexual Dysfunctions II: Orgasm, Ejaculation, Pain and Substance-Induced Dysfunction: diagnose female orgasmic disorder, delayed and premature ejaculation, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder and substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction, explain their causes and match each to its drug and psychological treatments.
  5. 9.5 Paraphilias and Paraphilic Disorders: From Perversion to Disorder: define a paraphilia and a paraphilic disorder, trace the idea from Krafft-Ebing to DSM-5-TR, and explain how ICD-11 rebuilt the group around consent.
  6. 9.6 The Eight Paraphilic Disorders and the Other Paraphilias: diagnose the courtship, algolagnic and target-preference disorders by their focus, criteria and specifiers, and tell fetishism, transvestism and autogynephilia apart from the other named paraphilias.
  7. 9.7 Why Paraphilic Disorders Develop, and How They Are Assessed and Treated: evaluate conditioning, developmental and biological explanations of paraphilias, describe phallometric and actuarial assessment, and explain the psychological and hormonal treatments and their evidence.
  8. 9.8 Becoming Male or Female: Sexual Differentiation and Gender Identity: explain how chromosomes, gonads and hormones build male and female bodies, what the differences of sex development teach, and what the Reimer case shows about the origins of gender identity.
  9. 9.9 Gender Dysphoria and Gender Incongruence: trace the diagnosis from transsexualism to gender dysphoria and gender incongruence, apply the DSM-5-TR criteria for children and for adolescents and adults, and describe its course, causes, gender-affirming care and the Indian legal context.
  10. 9.10 Homosexuality: Pathologised and Depathologised: explain how homosexuality entered and left the DSM and the ICD, the evidence from Kinsey and Hooker that changed minds, what science now says about sexual orientation, and the Indian story from Section 377 to Navtej Singh Johar.

Weightage. 9 of the 379 questions mapped in this book (2.4%) test this chapter, 8 of them from real past papers. 0 of its 10 sections are High yield.

In Winnipeg, Canada, on 22 August 1965, Janet and Ron Reimer became the parents of identical twin boys, Bruce and Brian. When the babies were about six months old, the doctors found that both had phimosis, a foreskin too tight to be drawn back, and both were booked for circumcision, an ordinary operation. On 27 April 1966 Bruce, a baby of about eight months, went first. The doctor used an electrocautery device, an instrument that cuts by burning tissue with an electric current, and the current destroyed the baby's penis. Brian was never operated on; his phimosis cleared up by itself. The parents were told that there was no satisfactory way of surgically replacing what their son had lost.

Early in 1967, after seeing him interviewed on Canadian television, the Reimers took Bruce to the Johns Hopkins Hospital in Baltimore to meet John Money (1921 to 2006), a psychologist and sexologist famous for his work with children born with intersex conditions. Money believed that a child's sense of being a boy or a girl is not fixed at birth. It is learned, he argued, from the way the child is reared, and it firms up between about 18 months and 3 years of age. His advice followed from that belief. Bruce should be castrated and raised as a girl; an artificial vagina could be made later; and oestrogen given at puberty would feminise the body. After a great deal of anguish, the parents agreed. At 22 months Bruce's testes were removed, and Bruce became Brenda. The case looked like a perfect natural experiment, because Brenda had a genetically identical twin brother who was being raised as a boy: two children with the same genes and the same hormones before birth, reared as different sexes. Some saw it as the ultimate test of nature and nurture in the making of gender.

Money described the case in Man and Woman, Boy and Girl, the book he published with Anke Ehrhardt in 1972, and again in a 1975 report written when the child was 9. "She", he reported, had developed as a typical girl, and he took the outcome as strong support for his social-learning theory of gender identity. For years the case was cited as evidence that gender identity is learned. Inside the Reimer home the story was very different. From an early age Brenda preferred boys' games and showed little interest in dolls or sewing. At four she refused to put on her mother's make-up and demanded to shave like her father; by seven she felt like a boy, tried to urinate standing up and sometimes went to the boys' lavatory. Teased by the other girls, she fought back and was expelled from school. When oestrogen treatment began at 12 she rebelled: she hated her developing breasts and refused to wear a bra. At 14 she told her parents that she was so miserable she was thinking of suicide, and on 14 March 1980 her father told her the truth. She decided to live as a boy and took the name David. In the words of one account, "all of a sudden everything clicked": for the first time he understood who and what he was. David later had testosterone treatment, a mastectomy and operations to construct a penis (phalloplasty). On 22 September 1990, at 25, he married Jane, and he adopted her three children. His early castration had permanently taken away his ability to father children of his own.

The world learned what had really happened in 1997. In March of that year Milton Diamond, a sexologist at the University of Hawaii, and H. Keith Sigmundson, the psychiatrist who had once supervised the case, published a long-term follow-up, "Sex reassignment at birth: long-term review and clinical implications", in the Archives of Pediatrics and Adolescent Medicine. The case had been known in medical writing by the pseudonym "John/Joan", and the follow-up reversed its lesson: the child had rejected the sex of rearing and had switched to living as a male. In December 1997 the journalist John Colapinto told the story in Rolling Stone, and in 2000 he expanded it into a book, As Nature Made Him: The Boy Who Was Raised as a Girl, which David helped to write "to save others from his experience". The scars never healed. In 2002 his twin brother died, and on 4 May 2004, amid several other losses, David Reimer died by suicide. He was 38. His life raises the questions at the heart of this chapter. What is the difference between a person's sex and a person's gender identity? Where does the sense of being a man or a woman come from, and can surgery, hormones and upbringing change it? When does a mismatch between body and identity count as a disorder, and who decides? And how do these questions differ from the problems of sexual response and of sexual interest that share this chapter with them? Sections 9.1, 9.8 and 9.9 take them up. Remember Bruce, who became Brenda and then David: you will return to him at the end of the chapter.

9.1 Sex, Gender and Disorder: The Science of Sexuality and How the Manuals Classify ItLower yield · 1 Q

No other chapter of this book has had its borders redrawn as often as this one. Within living memory, the same psychiatric manuals have listed homosexuality as a disorder and then removed it, called a woman with little interest in sex "frigid" and then dropped the word, filed a person's sense of being a man or a woman beside fetishism and exhibitionism and then moved it out of the mental disorders altogether. Each change was argued over in journals, committees and, sometimes, the streets. To follow the rest of the chapter you need three things from this first section: a precise vocabulary for sex and gender, a sense of where the science of sexuality came from, and a map of where each disorder lives in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11 and how it got there.

Three families under one roof

Picture the outpatient department of a large teaching hospital in an Indian city on an ordinary Tuesday morning. The sexual medicine clinic and the psychiatry clinic share a corridor, and four people are waiting to be seen.

Chetan is 27 and has been married for four months. He is a confident man at work, but in the bedroom he ejaculates within seconds of entering his wife, every time, long before either of them wants him to. He now dreads each attempt; he has begun to make excuses to stay late at the office, and his wife has started to wonder whether he finds her unattractive. Ambika, 24, has been married for a year, and her marriage has never been consummated. She loves her husband and wants a child, but whenever intercourse is attempted her body tightens, she feels a wave of panic and penetration becomes impossible. Her mother-in-law has begun to ask pointed questions about grandchildren. Omkar, 34, has not come by choice. A magistrate's court has referred him for assessment after his second arrest for pressing himself against women in crowded buses, and his lawyer has told him that a report from the hospital may affect his sentence. He says he does not know why he keeps doing it. Trisha is 23. She was assigned male at birth, has lived as a woman for the last three years, and has come with a letter from her general practitioner asking for a psychological assessment before she starts hormone treatment. She is calm, articulate and tired of explaining herself.

All four are, in a broad sense, "sexual" cases, and fifty years ago a psychiatrist might have filed them under a single heading. Look more closely, though, and you will see three quite different kinds of problem. Chetan and Ambika have problems of sexual function: the body's sexual response does not go the way the person wants it to. Each of them is attracted to an adult partner who wants the same thing they want; what fails is the response itself. These are the sexual dysfunctions, defined by DSM-5-TR as a group of disorders typically marked by a clinically significant disturbance in a person's ability to respond sexually or to experience sexual pleasure. Omkar's problem is of a different kind. His response works perfectly well; what is unusual is what arouses him, and the fact that acting on it involves people who have not consented. That is a problem of sexual interest and its consequences, and it belongs to the paraphilic disorders. Trisha's situation is not sexual at all. It concerns her gender identity, her sense of herself as a woman, and the distress that can arise when that identity does not match the sex recorded at birth and the body that came with it. The diagnosis for that distress, when it reaches clinical significance, is gender dysphoria. As Barlow and Durand put it, the condition "is not sexual but rather a disturbance in the person's sense of being a male or a female".

The manuals have slowly come round to this view. As late as DSM-IV and its text revision, all three families still shared one chapter, "sexual and gender identity disorders". DSM-5 gave each its own: sexual dysfunctions became chapter 13 of Section II, gender dysphoria chapter 14, and the paraphilic disorders chapter 19, placed after the personality disorders near the end of the list (the full order of the twenty chapters is in Section 1.8.2). ICD-11 went further and moved two of the three families out of the mental disorders chapter altogether, as Section 9.1.6 explains. Figure 9.1 is the map of this chapter: the three families, the disorders in each, the sections of this chapter that teach them and the ICD-11 chapter in which each family now lives.

Why should a clinical psychologist care about this chapter? There are four good reasons. The first is that sexual problems are common and mostly hidden. Population surveys find that a large minority of adults report a sexual difficulty in any given year (the figures are in Section 9.3.2), yet very few raise it with a doctor, and fewer still with a psychologist. In India, where talking about sex is often difficult even between spouses, the silence is deeper; a man may visit a roadside "sex clinic" or buy a herbal tonic long before he tells anyone in a hospital. The second reason is that sexual problems rarely travel alone. They come with depression and anxiety, with marital conflict, with diabetes, heart disease and other physical illness, and with the medicines used to treat all of these, including many of the antidepressants and antipsychotics a psychologist's clients take every day (Sections 5.7.2 and 4.10.5). A clinician who never asks will never know, and the sexual history is a standard part of the personal history for exactly this reason (Book 2, Section 2.3.6, which notes that worry about semen loss is common among young Indian men). The third reason is forensic: courts, prisons and probation services refer men like Omkar for assessment, and the psychologist's report may affect a sentence or a decision about risk. The fourth is that more and more gender-diverse people like Trisha are seeking psychological assessment and support, and the clinician who meets them needs to know what the manuals now say, and why they changed.

Clinical link

Each of the four people in the waiting room needs a different kind of help. Chetan and Ambika need a careful sexual and medical history, a check for physical causes, and a treatment aimed at the response that has gone wrong (Sections 9.4.3 and 9.4.4). Omkar needs an assessment of what arouses him, of how often and how far he has acted on it, and of the risk he poses to others (Section 9.7). Trisha does not need anyone to change her identity; she needs an assessment of her gender history, of any distress she feels, of her mental health in general and of her readiness for the treatment she is asking for (Section 9.9). Putting them all in one box, as the older manuals did, would have led to the wrong question being asked of at least three of them.

The vocabulary of sex and gender

Before anything else, you need words that mean one thing each. This is harder than it sounds. In English the word "sex" means both being male or female and sexual activity; the word "gender" was borrowed from grammar in the twentieth century and has been used in a dozen ways since; and the vocabulary of gender identity has grown quickly in recent years. DSM-5-TR itself admits that the area "is highly controversial and has led to a proliferation of terms whose meanings vary over time and within and between disciplines". The sensible course is to learn the definitions the manual uses, because those are the ones the examiners use, and to notice what each term includes and leaves out.

Start with the body. In DSM-5-TR, sex refers to the biological indicators of male and female, understood in relation to reproductive capacity: the sex chromosomes (usually XX or XY), the gonads (ovaries or testes), the sex hormones, and the internal and external genitalia. In most people these indicators line up with one another. In some they do not: a child may have XY chromosomes and testes but a body that cannot respond to testosterone, or genitals that are neither typically male nor typically female. These are the disorders of sex development (also called differences of sex development, DSDs, and once called intersex conditions), and Section 9.8.4 teaches them in full. For now notice only that "sex" is not one fact but a bundle of facts, which usually, but not always, agree.

When a baby is born, somebody looks at the genitals and says "It's a boy" or "It's a girl". DSM-5-TR calls this gender assignment, and the result is the person's assigned gender, usually the birth-assigned gender. The manual notes that this used to be called "biological sex" and, more recently, "natal gender", and it has moved away from both terms. "Biological sex" suggests one simple biological fact, where in reality there are several indicators that can disagree; "natal" suggests that gender is fixed at birth. Calling it an assignment is more accurate, because it is a decision made by other people from one visible indicator, and the American Psychiatric Association described the change, in DSM-5-TR, as a move to more culturally sensitive language (so "natal male" became "individual assigned male at birth"). Assigned gender also covers assignments and reassignments made later in infancy or early childhood, usually in children with DSDs. Brenda, in the opening story, is the textbook case of these layers coming apart: her chromosomes and early hormones were male, her assigned gender after the age of 22 months was female, and her own sense of herself, as she grew up, was male.

Gender, in DSM-5-TR, is the public, sociocultural and usually legally recognised lived role as a boy or a girl, a man or a woman, or another gender. Biology contributes to it, the manual says, but always in interaction with social and psychological factors. Gender identity is something more private: it is "a category of social identity", a person's identification of himself or herself as male, as female, as something in between (sometimes called gender fluid) or as something other than male or female (sometimes called gender neutral). Nobody can see another person's gender identity; you learn it by listening to what the person tells you about themselves. Trisha's gender identity is female, whatever is written on her birth certificate.

Two further terms describe how gender is lived in public. A gender role is the set of behaviours, tasks and attitudes that a society expects of people of each gender. In many Indian households it is still taken for granted that the wife will cook and the husband will earn, and a man who stays at home while his wife runs a business may be teased by his relatives; in other families the same arrangement raises no eyebrows at all, and expectations shift from one generation to the next. Gender expression is the way a person presents gender to the world: dress, hair, name, voice, manner. A person's expression may follow the local gender role closely or not at all, and neither tells you for certain what the person's identity or orientation is. Behaviour that departs from the usual pattern for one's assigned gender is called gender-atypical or gender-nonconforming, and DSM-5-TR is careful to define it as atypical "in a statistical sense" for people of the same assigned gender "in a given society and historical era". The qualification matters. A girl in Mysuru who plays cricket with the boys and refuses to wear a frock is gender-nonconforming by the standards of some families today; a woman in trousers was gender-nonconforming in much of the world a century ago and is unremarkable now. Gender nonconformity is not a diagnosis and not the same thing as gender dysphoria.

When a person's gender identity differs from the gender assigned at birth, the person is transgender; DSM-5-TR uses the word for "the broad spectrum of individuals" whose identity differs in this way. A person whose gender is congruent with the birth-assigned gender is cisgender (the manual also gives "nontransgender"). An older word, transsexual, is described by DSM-5-TR as "a historic term" for a person who seeks, is undergoing or has undergone a social transition from male to female or female to male, which often but not always includes gender-affirming hormones or surgery. You will still meet it in older books and in ICD-10, whose diagnosis F64.0 is called "transsexualism".

Gender identity is about who you are. Sexual orientation is about whom you are attracted to: the sex or gender of the people a person finds sexually and romantically attractive. People attracted mainly to the other sex are heterosexual, those attracted mainly to their own sex are homosexual (gay men and lesbian women), and those attracted to both are bisexual. People who feel little or no sexual attraction to anyone may describe themselves as asexual, and asexuality has an important place in DSM-5-TR: if a lifelong lack of sexual desire is better explained by the person's self-identification as asexual, the manual says, a diagnosis of female sexual interest/arousal disorder or of male hypoactive sexual desire disorder is not made (Section 9.3). Orientation and identity vary independently. As Pinel and Barnes put it, "sexual orientation, gender identity, and body type are sometimes unrelated": some transgender people are attracted to women, some to men and some to neither, and transition does not change that.

The last term in this family is the one examiners like to slip in as a distractor. In the early 1970s most psychologists, and most personality tests, treated masculinity and femininity as the two ends of a single scale: the more masculine you were, the less feminine you had to be. The masculinity-femininity scale of the MMPI, for example, was scored that way. Sandra Lipsitz Bem (1944 to 2014), then at Stanford University, argued that this was a mistake. A person can be both assertive and gentle, both independent and affectionate. Masculinity and femininity, she proposed, are two separate dimensions, and a person who scores high on both is psychologically androgynous. Androgyny (from the Greek words for "man" and "woman") is therefore a blend of personality traits that the culture labels masculine and feminine. It describes personality, not the body, not gender identity and not sexual orientation, and it is not a disorder of any kind.

Key study: Bem's measurement of psychological androgyny (1974)

The question. Can masculinity and femininity be measured as two independent dimensions, so that a person can be high on both? The method. Bem built the Bem Sex Role Inventory, published in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology in 1974 under the title "The measurement of psychological androgyny". It has 60 personality adjectives: 20 judged more desirable for a man than a woman in American society of the time (such as self-reliant, assertive and willing to take risks), 20 judged more desirable for a woman (such as affectionate, gentle and sympathetic) and 20 neutral fillers. The person rates how true each adjective is of him or her on a 7-point scale, and masculinity and femininity are scored separately. The result. The two scores were largely independent of each other, and many people scored high on both. Bem and her colleagues then reported that androgynous people of both sexes behaved more flexibly: they showed "masculine" independence when pressed to conform and "feminine" nurturance when playing with a kitten, while strongly sex-typed people were low on one or both.

The criticism. Later scoring split people into four groups: masculine, feminine, androgynous (high on both) and undifferentiated (low on both), a distinction drawn by Janet Taylor Spence and her colleagues. The claim that androgyny is the healthiest pattern did not survive well. When Bernard E. Whitley pooled 35 studies of sex role and self-esteem in a 1983 meta-analysis, the results best supported a "masculinity model": self-esteem rose with the masculine (assertive, instrumental) score, not with androgyny as such. The adjectives are also tied to one culture and one era. Bem herself moved on to gender schema theory (1981), the idea that sex-typed people process the world through a ready-made mental category of what is masculine and feminine; that theory belongs to the Developmental and Personality books, and the inventory as a test to the Assessment book.

Table 9.1 brings the vocabulary together, along with the three diagnostic terms that this chapter is about. Read the last column carefully: most exam mistakes in this area come from treating one of these terms as if it were another.

Exam trap

A person's sex, gender identity, gender expression and sexual orientation are separate dimensions that can combine in any way. A question that describes "a person whose sexual identity does not match his or her physical gender" is describing a mismatch between gender identity and assigned sex, which was called gender identity disorder in DSM-IV and ICD-10 and is gender dysphoria in DSM-5 and gender incongruence in ICD-11 (Section 9.9). The distractors are each something else: a paraphilia concerns what arouses a person, a sexual dysfunction concerns the sexual response, and androgyny is a blend of masculine and feminine personality traits that Bem measured in 1974. Androgyny is never a diagnosis.

The birth of sexology

For most of European history, sexual behaviour was the business of the church and the courts. It was judged as sin or as crime, and the experts on it were priests and judges. In the second half of the nineteenth century that began to change. Doctors, and psychiatrists in particular, claimed sexual behaviour as a medical subject, and a new science of sexuality, sexology, was born. Its founders were few, and each is remembered for one big idea. You will meet their detailed contributions later in the chapter, where they belong; here you need to know who they were.

The first and most influential was Richard von Krafft-Ebing (1840 to 1902), a German psychiatrist who held chairs of psychiatry at Strasbourg, Graz and finally Vienna. In 1886 he published Psychopathia Sexualis, a forensic reference book written for psychiatrists, physicians and judges who had to decide what to do with people brought before them for sexual offences. To keep it out of the hands of curious lay readers he gave it a scientific Latin title and wrote some passages in Latin. The book grew with every edition; the last that Krafft-Ebing prepared himself, the twelfth, held 238 case histories. Krafft-Ebing believed that the purpose of sexual desire was reproduction, so that any sexual behaviour that could not lead to reproduction was a perversion, and he explained most perversions as signs of an inherited "degeneration" of the nervous system, an idea he took from French psychiatry. He also gave medicine two of its lasting words. He coined masochism, the finding of sexual pleasure in being hurt or humiliated, from the name of the Austrian novelist Leopold von Sacher-Masoch (1836 to 1895), whose novel Venus in Furs (1870) describes a man who longs to be dominated by a woman. And he brought into medicine the word sadism, the finding of sexual pleasure in hurting others, named after the French writer the Marquis de Sade (1740 to 1814); "sadism" was already in use in French, so Krafft-Ebing turned it into a diagnosis rather than inventing it. Section 9.5.3 tells how his catalogue shaped every later list of paraphilias.

Henry Havelock Ellis (1859 to 1939) was an English physician and writer who approached sex less as a court doctor and more as a naturalist. His Studies in the Psychology of Sex began to appear in 1897 and eventually ran to seven volumes, the last in 1928. The volume called Sexual Inversion, written with the poet and critic John Addington Symonds, appeared first in German in 1896 and in English in 1897; it was the first English medical textbook on homosexuality, and it did not treat homosexuality as a disease, a vice or a crime. A London bookseller was prosecuted for selling it. Ellis also wrote about "auto-erotism", and in 1920 he coined "eonism", after a famous eighteenth-century French cross-dresser, as his name for what others were calling transvestism. Ellis's ideas on homosexuality are taken further in Section 9.10.2.

Magnus Hirschfeld (1868 to 1935) was a Berlin physician who combined research with campaigning. In 1897 he co-founded the Scientific-Humanitarian Committee to fight Paragraph 175, the German law that made sex between men a crime (Section 9.10.2). In Die Transvestiten (1910) he coined the word "transvestite" for people who wear the clothes of the other sex, and in 1923 he used the word "transsexual". On 6 July 1919 he opened the Institute for Sexual Science (Institut für Sexualwissenschaft) in Berlin, one of the first institutes of its kind, with a library, a clinic and a large practice with transgender patients; some of the earliest gender-affirming operations were carried out there (Section 9.9.1). On 6 May 1933, three months after the Nazis came to power, Nazi students stormed the institute and carried off its library, and its books were thrown on the fire in the Berlin book burning of 10 May 1933. Hirschfeld, who was Jewish and was then abroad, never returned to Germany; he died in exile in Nice in 1935.

Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) made his contribution in a single short book, Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905). Its first shock was the claim that sexuality does not begin at puberty but in infancy. Its second was that the young child's sexuality is "polymorphously perverse": not yet tied to the genitals or to any one kind of partner, it can take pleasure from many parts of the body and in many ways. The adult "perversions", in Freud's account, are what happens when development gets stuck at, or falls back to, one of these early childhood forms, so that the pervert is not a different kind of creature but an ordinary person whose development took a particular turn. Freud famously added that the neuroses are, so to say, the negative of the perversions: what the pervert acts out, the neurotic represses. The psychosexual stages that grew out of this book belong to the Personality book.

The twentieth century brought counting and measuring. Alfred Charles Kinsey (1894 to 1956), a zoologist at Indiana University, set aside case histories of patients and asked thousands of ordinary volunteers, face to face, what they actually did. His two reports, Sexual Behavior in the Human Male (1948) and Sexual Behavior in the Human Female (1953), and the criticism that his volunteers were not a representative sample, are told in Book 1, Section 10.9.6; his famous 0 to 6 scale and what it showed about homosexuality are taught in Section 9.10.3. Where Kinsey asked, the gynaecologist William Howell Masters (1915 to 2001) and his research partner Virginia Eshelman Johnson (1925 to 2013) watched: in Human Sexual Response (1966) they described the body's sexual response, recorded in the laboratory, as a cycle of four phases, and in Human Sexual Inadequacy (1970) they described how to treat its failures (Book 1, Section 10.9.6, and Book 2, Section 7.9; their model is the starting point of Section 9.2). Later surveys replaced Kinsey's volunteers with random samples of the population. The best known, the National Health and Social Life Survey, was carried out in 1992 by a team led by Edward Laumann at the University of Chicago and reported in The Social Organization of Sexuality (1994); what it found about how common sexual problems are is told in Section 9.3.2.

India has its own long history of writing about desire. The best known text is the Kamasutra attributed to Vatsyayana, whose date is unknown; historians have placed it anywhere between about 400 BCE and 300 CE, most often in the early centuries of the Common Era. It is often mistaken for a manual of sexual positions, but it is a much broader guide to love, courtship, marriage and pleasure, treated as one of the legitimate aims of a good life. The colonial period brought a very different attitude: the Indian Penal Code of 1860 made "carnal intercourse against the order of nature" a crime under Section 377, a law whose story ends in the Supreme Court in 2018 (Section 9.10.8). Today, sexual medicine in India is shaped by both inheritances. Sexual clinics see far more men than women (Section 9.3.2), many of the men come with anxieties about semen loss and potency that are rooted in traditional beliefs about the body (dhat syndrome, Section 1.10.4), and the women who need help most often never come at all.

Exam trap

Krafft-Ebing wrote Psychopathia Sexualis (1886), not Ellis or Freud. He coined "masochism" (from Sacher-Masoch), but "sadism" (from de Sade) was already a French word that he turned into a diagnosis. Hirschfeld coined "transvestite" (1910) and founded the first Institute for Sexual Science (1919); Ellis coined "eonism" for the same behaviour. Freud's Three Essays date from 1905. Keep the methods apart: Kinsey (1948, 1953) interviewed people about what they did; Masters and Johnson (1966) observed and recorded the body's response in the laboratory.

What makes sexual behaviour a disorder

Chapter 1 (Section 1.1) and Book 2 (Section 1.6, with the four Ds in Section 1.6.5) set out the criteria that clinicians use to decide what counts as abnormal: statistical rarity, the breaking of social norms, personal distress, impaired functioning and danger to oneself or others. Sexual behaviour is the hardest test of those criteria, and watching them strain here teaches you more about them than any definition.

Take statistical rarity first. It fails in both directions. When Kinsey asked ordinary Americans what they did, he found that many behaviours that the law and the churches condemned, from masturbation to sex before marriage to same-sex experience, were common (Section 9.10.3); if rarity were the test, they could not be disorders, and yet the textbooks of the day treated several of them as exactly that. And the reverse holds too. A rare sexual interest, such as being aroused by a particular kind of shoe, may be enjoyed in private, with a willing partner, and harm nobody. Rarity alone tells you nothing about whether anything is wrong.

Social norms fare no better, because they differ so much from one society to another. In the 1980s the anthropologist Gilbert Herdt described the Sambia of Papua New Guinea, among whom boys took part in ritual same-sex practices as part of their initiation into manhood and then, as young men, married and lived exclusively heterosexual lives. In about half of more than a hundred societies surveyed around the world, sex before marriage is accepted and even encouraged; in the other half it is forbidden. Within India itself, what is acceptable differs between communities, between cities and villages, and between grandparents and grandchildren. If breaking the local norm made a disorder, the same act would be healthy in one place and sick in another.

History makes the same point. Eighteenth- and nineteenth-century doctors taught that masturbation caused physical decline and even insanity, and the belief survives in the fears about semen loss that bring young men to clinics in India today (Section 1.10.4). The words "nymphomania" and "frigidity" sound like relics, yet they were still printed as inclusion terms in the World Health Organization's ICD-10, under "excessive sexual drive" (F52.7, which also lists "satyriasis") and "lack or loss of sexual desire" (F52.0); neither survives as a diagnostic term in ICD-11. Medicine's idea of normal sexuality has moved with the moral climate of its time.

Personal distress is more useful, and the manuals lean on it heavily: most sexual dysfunctions are diagnosed only when they cause clinically significant distress (Section 9.3.1). But distress has a trap of its own. A person may be distressed not because anything is wrong with them but because the people around them say there is. A gay man in a hostile family may hate his own orientation, and in 1980 DSM-III gave that distress a diagnosis, "ego-dystonic homosexuality"; the diagnosis was dropped in 1987 when critics pointed out that it medicalised the effects of stigma (Section 9.10.4, and Section 2.1.9 for the terms ego-syntonic and ego-dystonic). DSM-5-TR has learned the lesson: gender dysphoria, it says, "does not refer to distress related to stigma", which is a distinct, though possibly co-occurring, source of distress.

That leaves harm and consent, and for sexual behaviour these turn out to be the decisive criteria. Omkar's problem is not that his interest is rare, or that his community disapproves of it, or even that he feels bad about it; it is that he acts on it with women who have not agreed and are frightened and humiliated by it. Harm to others, and the absence of consent, is what separates a paraphilic disorder from a harmless paraphilia in DSM-5-TR (Section 9.5.2), and it is the organising idea of the whole group of paraphilic disorders in ICD-11 (Section 9.5.4).

The manuals now build these lessons into their definition of a mental disorder. DSM-5-TR's definition (Section 1.1.3) requires a dysfunction in the psychological, biological or developmental processes underlying mental functioning, and it adds that socially deviant behaviour, naming political, religious and sexual deviance as examples, and conflicts that are primarily between the individual and society are not mental disorders unless they result from such a dysfunction in the individual. Three of the biggest changes DSM-5 made in this chapter follow directly from that sentence. A paraphilia is not, by itself, a disorder; only a paraphilia that causes distress or impairment, or harm or risk of harm to others, is a paraphilic disorder (Section 9.5.2). A gender identity is not a disorder; only the clinically significant distress or impairment that may accompany incongruence is diagnosed (Section 9.9.2). And a sexual difficulty becomes a dysfunction only if it is persistent, happens on most occasions and causes distress, not because it falls short of somebody's idea of normal performance (Section 9.3.1).

Indian context

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 writes the same principles into Indian law. Section 3(1) says that mental illness is to be determined according to nationally or internationally accepted medical standards, including the latest edition of the WHO's International Classification of Diseases, and section 3(3) says that mental illness shall not be determined on the basis of "non-conformity with moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs prevailing in a person's community" (Book 2, Section 1.6.2). The Act also guarantees the right to mental health care without discrimination on the basis of "gender, sex, sexual orientation, religion, culture, caste" and other grounds (section 18(2)), and equal treatment with persons with physical illness, with "no discrimination on any basis including gender, sex, sexual orientation" (section 21(1)(a)). A clinician in India who treats a client's orientation or gender identity as an illness is out of step not only with the manuals but with the law.

Exam trap

Being gay, lesbian or bisexual, being transgender, or having an unusual sexual interest that harms nobody is not, by itself, a mental disorder in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11. What can be diagnosed is a persistent problem of sexual response with distress (a sexual dysfunction), an atypical interest that causes distress or impairment or harms or endangers others (a paraphilic disorder), or clinically significant distress or impairment linked to gender incongruence (gender dysphoria). Statistical rarity and social disapproval are never enough.

From "sexual deviation" to three chapters: the DSM story

The American manuals have reorganised this territory in almost every edition, and each reorganisation tells you what psychiatry thought sex and gender were at the time. Table 9.2 sets out the story edition by edition; the general history of the editions is in Sections 1.7.2 and 1.7.4, and the detailed histories of the paraphilias, of the gender diagnosis and of homosexuality are in Sections 9.5.3, 9.9.1 and 9.10.4.

The first two editions had no chapter on sex at all. In DSM-I (1952) homosexuality and the other "sexual deviations" were listed as a form of sociopathic personality disturbance, the same family as antisocial behaviour. The message was that sexual variation was a defect of character, a failure to fit into society. Problems of sexual function hardly appeared: impotence and frigidity were mentioned only as psychophysiological reactions, physical complaints in which emotional factors were thought to play a part. DSM-II (1968) kept the same plan. It listed the sexual deviations (homosexuality, fetishism, pedophilia, transvestitism, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sadism and masochism, with "other" and "unspecified") among the "personality disorders and certain other non-psychotic mental disorders", and it mentioned dyspareunia (painful intercourse) and impotence only as examples of a psychophysiologic genito-urinary disorder "in which emotional factors play a causative role". After the American Psychiatric Association's decision of December 1973, the seventh printing of DSM-II in 1974 replaced homosexuality with "sexual orientation disturbance", a diagnosis for people distressed by their orientation (Section 9.10.4). Neither edition had any diagnosis for gender identity.

DSM-III (1980) changed everything. For the first time, sexuality had a class of its own, the psychosexual disorders, divided into four groups. The first was the gender identity disorders: transsexualism, for adults, and gender identity disorder of childhood. The second was the paraphilias, the word now entering the DSM in place of "sexual deviation"; the list included fetishism, transvestism, zoophilia, pedophilia, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sexual masochism and sexual sadism. The third was the psychosexual dysfunctions, the first time problems of sexual response had a group of their own: inhibited sexual desire, inhibited sexual excitement, inhibited female orgasm, inhibited male orgasm, premature ejaculation, functional dyspareunia and functional vaginismus. Notice the logic of that list. It follows the phases of the sexual response cycle, desire, excitement and orgasm, with pain added at the end, the phases that Masters and Johnson and then Helen Singer Kaplan had described (Section 9.2.2); the dysfunctions have been sorted by the phase they disrupt ever since. The fourth group, "other psychosexual disorders", held ego-dystonic homosexuality, the successor of sexual orientation disturbance.

DSM-III-R (1987) renamed the class "sexual disorders" (paraphilias and sexual dysfunctions) and moved the gender identity disorders out of it altogether, into the disorders usually first evident in infancy, childhood or adolescence, and added gender identity disorder of adolescence or adulthood, nontranssexual type. It dropped ego-dystonic homosexuality, added frotteurism to the paraphilias and stopped naming zoophilia as a separate paraphilia. It also renamed most of the dysfunctions: inhibited sexual desire was split into hypoactive sexual desire disorder and sexual aversion disorder, and inhibited sexual excitement became female sexual arousal disorder and male erectile disorder. DSM-IV (1994) and its text revision, DSM-IV-TR (2000), reunited the three families in one class, sexual and gender identity disorders. The dysfunctions were grouped by phase (desire, arousal, orgasm, pain); the orgasm disorders became female orgasmic disorder and male orgasmic disorder; and two new kinds of dysfunction, due to a general medical condition and substance-induced, were added. There were eight named paraphilias, with transvestism renamed transvestic fetishism. Transsexualism and gender identity disorder of childhood were merged into a single gender identity disorder, diagnosed in children or in adolescents or adults.

DSM-5 (2013) broke the class into three chapters. Sexual dysfunctions (chapter 13) were rebuilt around the sexes: in women, the old desire and arousal disorders were merged into female sexual interest/arousal disorder, and vaginismus and dyspareunia into genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder, both new in DSM-5; in men there were male hypoactive sexual desire disorder, erectile disorder, delayed ejaculation (the old male orgasmic disorder) and premature (early) ejaculation; sexual aversion disorder disappeared as a named disorder (DSM-5-TR now gives it only as an example of "other specified sexual dysfunction"); and most of the dysfunctions acquired explicit rules of duration and frequency (Section 9.3.1). Gender identity disorder became gender dysphoria (chapter 14), a change Section 9.9 explains. The paraphilias became the paraphilic disorders (chapter 19), with "disorder" added to each name (transvestic fetishism became transvestic disorder) and, most important of all, a formal distinction between a paraphilia and a paraphilic disorder (Section 9.5.2). DSM-5-TR (2022) kept the same chapters and, in substance, the same criteria and revised the text; in gender dysphoria (in the text and in the wording of the posttransition specifier) it replaced "desired gender" with "experienced gender", "cross-sex" hormones and procedures with "gender-affirming" ones, and "natal male" and "natal female" with "individual assigned male (or female) at birth".

Table 9.2 Sexual and gender diagnoses in the DSM, edition by edition.
EditionWhere they satSexual interestSexual functionGender identity
DSM-I (1952)Sexual deviation, a form of sociopathic personality disturbanceHomosexuality and other "sexual deviations"No group; impotence and frigidity only as psychophysiological reactionsNo diagnosis
DSM-II (1968)Sexual deviations, among personality and other non-psychotic disordersHomosexuality, fetishism, pedophilia, transvestitism, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sadism, masochism; 1974: sexual orientation disturbanceNo group; dyspareunia and impotence as psychophysiologic genito-urinary disorderNo diagnosis

DSM-5-TR (2022)The same three chaptersText revisedText revised"Experienced gender", "gender-affirming"

Why did DSM-5 separate the families? Because they are different kinds of problem, as the waiting room in Section 9.1.1 showed. Sexual dysfunctions are problems of response; paraphilic disorders are problems of interest and of the harm that acting on it may do; gender dysphoria is a problem of identity and the distress that may come with it. Barlow and Durand put the first point bluntly: paraphilic disorders "have little to do with sexual dysfunctions except for the fact that they both involve sexual behavior". The second point is more serious. Keeping gender identity disorder in the same class as fetishism and exhibitionism implied that being transgender was a kind of sexual deviation, a message that clinicians and transgender people alike had come to find both wrong and harmful. Giving gender dysphoria a chapter of its own, and placing it far from the paraphilic disorders, was a statement that it is not one.

Exam trap

DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR have three separate chapters: sexual dysfunctions (13), gender dysphoria (14) and paraphilic disorders (19). The paraphilic disorders chapter comes after the personality disorders, near the end of Section II, not next to the sexual dysfunctions. The one combined class, "sexual and gender identity disorders", belongs to DSM-IV and DSM-IV-TR; DSM-III's class was "psychosexual disorders". The word paraphilia entered the DSM in 1980; DSM-I and DSM-II said "sexual deviation". There was no gender identity diagnosis at all before DSM-III.

The ICD: from F52, F64, F65 and F66 to Chapter 17

The World Health Organization's classification tells a parallel story, and in the end a more radical one. How ICD-10 and ICD-11 are built, and when they came into use, is explained in Sections 1.9.4 to 1.9.6: ICD-10 was endorsed in 1990, and ICD-11 was adopted by the World Health Assembly in May 2019 and came into force on 1 January 2022. This subsection follows the sexual and gender diagnoses from one to the other, and it is one of the most examined pages in the chapter.

ICD-10's Chapter V, the chapter of mental and behavioural disorders, spread them across four blocks. The first, F52, was "sexual dysfunction, not caused by organic disorder or disease", and it sat among the behavioural syndromes associated with physiological disturbances (F50 to F59, beside the eating and sleep disorders). It held F52.0 lack or loss of sexual desire (which included "frigidity"), F52.1 sexual aversion and lack of sexual enjoyment, F52.2 failure of genital response (erectile failure in men, failure of lubrication in women), F52.3 orgasmic dysfunction, F52.4 premature ejaculation, F52.5 nonorganic vaginismus, F52.6 nonorganic dyspareunia, F52.7 excessive sexual drive, and the residual F52.8 and F52.9. The other three blocks sat among the disorders of adult personality and behaviour (F60 to F69). F64, gender identity disorders, held F64.0 transsexualism, F64.1 dual-role transvestism (wearing the clothes of the other sex for part of one's life to enjoy temporary membership of it, without sexual arousal and without wanting a permanent change), F64.2 gender identity disorder of childhood, and F64.8 and F64.9. F65, the disorders of sexual preference, held F65.0 fetishism, F65.1 fetishistic transvestism, F65.2 exhibitionism, F65.3 voyeurism, F65.4 paedophilia, F65.5 sadomasochism, F65.6 multiple disorders of sexual preference, F65.8 other disorders of sexual preference (among them obscene telephone calls, frotteurism and necrophilia) and F65.9. And F66, "psychological and behavioural disorders associated with sexual development and orientation", held F66.0 sexual maturation disorder, F66.1 egodystonic sexual orientation, F66.2 sexual relationship disorder, and F66.8 and F66.9, under a note that "sexual orientation by itself is not to be regarded as a disorder" (Section 9.10.5 tells that story).

Notice the words "not caused by organic disorder or disease". ICD-10 split every sexual dysfunction in two. If the cause was psychological, the problem was coded in Chapter V; if it was physical, it went to another chapter entirely, so that impotence of organic origin was N48.4, and organic dyspareunia and vaginismus were N94.1 and N94.2, in the chapter on diseases of the genitourinary system. The irony is that ICD-10's own definition of F52 admits that the split is artificial: "Sexual response is a psychosomatic process," it says, "and both psychological and somatic processes are usually involved in the causation of sexual dysfunction." Think of a 50-year-old man with diabetes who has had a few failures and now watches anxiously for the next one. His erectile problem is both organic and psychological, and forcing the clinician to choose one code for it distorts the picture.

ICD-11 dissolved that split by creating a new chapter outside the mental disorders, Chapter 17, "Conditions related to sexual health". Into it went three groups. The sexual dysfunctions are HA00 hypoactive sexual desire dysfunction; HA01 sexual arousal dysfunctions (HA01.0 female sexual arousal dysfunction and HA01.1 male erectile dysfunction); HA02 orgasmic dysfunctions (HA02.0 anorgasmia); HA03 ejaculatory dysfunctions (HA03.0 male early ejaculation and HA03.1 male delayed ejaculation); and HA0Y and HA0Z for other specified and unspecified dysfunctions. Each can be further coded as lifelong or acquired and as generalised or situational. The sexual pain disorders are HA20 sexual pain-penetration disorder, which replaces vaginismus and dyspareunia, with HA2Y and HA2Z. And gender incongruence is HA60 gender incongruence of adolescence or adulthood, HA61 gender incongruence of childhood and HA6Z unspecified (Section 9.9.1 teaches their definitions). The chapter ends with HA8Y and HA8Z for other and unspecified conditions related to sexual health.

The cleverest part of the new chapter is a set of qualifiers, grouped under HA40, "aetiological considerations in sexual dysfunctions and sexual pain disorders". Instead of forcing a choice between organic and nonorganic, ICD-11 lets the clinician add as many of these as apply to any dysfunction: HA40.0 associated with a medical condition, injury, or the effects of surgery or radiation treatment; HA40.1 associated with psychological or behavioural factors, including mental disorders; HA40.2 associated with use of psychoactive substance or medication; HA40.3 associated with lack of knowledge or experience; HA40.4 associated with relationship factors; and HA40.5 associated with cultural factors. The man with diabetes now receives one diagnosis, male erectile dysfunction, with two qualifiers, medical and psychological, which is a far truer description of his problem. ICD-11's definition of the sexual dysfunctions is also looser than DSM-5-TR's: the difficulty must occur frequently, though it may be absent on some occasions, must have been present for at least several months, and must cause clinically significant distress. DSM-5-TR, as Section 9.3.1 explains, asks for about 6 months and for the problem on about 75 to 100 percent of occasions.

One family did not move. The paraphilic disorders stayed in Chapter 6, the mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders, as codes 6D30 to 6D3Z: 6D30 exhibitionistic disorder, 6D31 voyeuristic disorder, 6D32 pedophilic disorder, 6D33 coercive sexual sadism disorder, 6D34 frotteuristic disorder, 6D35 other paraphilic disorder involving non-consenting individuals, 6D36 paraphilic disorder involving solitary behaviour or consenting individuals, and 6D3Z unspecified. Chapter 17 lists them only as a cross-reference to their home in Chapter 6. The group was rebuilt around consent: most of its categories concern arousal focused on people who have not consented or cannot consent, and Section 9.5.4 teaches it in detail.

Several old categories disappeared altogether. The whole of F66, including egodystonic sexual orientation, was deleted. So were three disorders of sexual preference that involve only the person or consenting adults, fetishism (F65.0), fetishistic transvestism (F65.1) and sadomasochism (F65.5); such behaviour can now be coded only under 6D36, and only if it causes marked distress or a real risk of injury or death. Dual-role transvestism (F64.1) was deleted as well. And F52.7, "excessive sexual drive", has no place in Chapter 17. ICD-11 does have a category called compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72), but it is defined differently, as a persistent failure to control intense, repetitive sexual impulses or urges, and it sits in Chapter 6 among the impulse control disorders, not among the sexual dysfunctions or the paraphilic disorders (Section 9.5.7).

Why such a large reorganisation? Geoffrey Reed of the WHO and the members of its Working Group on Sexual Disorders and Sexual Health set out their reasons in World Psychiatry in 2016. The new chapter for the sexual dysfunctions was meant to be an integrated classification, "overcoming the mind/body separation that is inherent in ICD-10". Gender incongruence was moved out of the mental disorders because the double stigma of being both transgender and labelled mentally ill had contributed to legal insecurity, human rights violations and barriers to health care; but it was kept in the ICD, rather than deleted, because most health systems need a diagnosis before they will provide or pay for gender-affirming treatment (Section 1.9.6 makes the same point). The paraphilic group was redrawn to separate conditions that matter for public health and clinical psychopathology from those that "merely reflect private behaviour". And F66 was deleted because its categories had no evidence-based treatments, had attracted almost no research and in practice singled out people with same-sex orientation or gender nonconformity. Figure 9.2 shows where each ICD-10 group went.

Table 9.3 is the master code table for the whole chapter. Each disorder section repeats only its own codes, and they match the table. Notice that the codes DSM-5-TR prints are longer than the WHO's. That is because DSM-5-TR uses ICD-10-CM, the clinical modification of ICD-10 used for official coding in the United States, which adds extra digits to split the WHO's categories more finely (Section 1.8.3). The WHO's single F65.5 sadomasochism becomes F65.51 sexual masochism disorder and F65.52 sexual sadism disorder; F65.8 "other" becomes F65.81 frotteuristic disorder and F65.89 other specified paraphilic disorder; F52.3 orgasmic dysfunction becomes F52.31 for women and F52.32 for men; and F52.2 failure of genital response becomes F52.21 erectile disorder and F52.22 female sexual interest/arousal disorder. Substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction has no code of its own: it takes the code of the substance, and the code changes with whether a use disorder is also present.

Exam trap

When gender incongruence left the mental disorders chapter in ICD-11, it did not travel alone. The sexual dysfunctions (HA00 to HA03) and the sexual pain disorders (HA20) went with it to Chapter 17, Conditions related to sexual health, and so did gender incongruence of childhood (HA61), which is no more a mental disorder in ICD-11 than the adult category. Only the paraphilic disorders (6D30 to 6D3Z) stayed in Chapter 6. Compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72) is also in Chapter 6, but among the impulse control disorders, not the paraphilic disorders. And gender incongruence went to Chapter 17: not to Chapter 5 (endocrine, nutritional or metabolic diseases), and not out of the ICD altogether (Section 9.9.1).

Talking about sex: the clinician's stance

Lavanya, 31, has been taking an antidepressant for three months. At her review she says the tablets "are not working" and she wants to stop them. Her mood, when the psychologist asks, is in fact much better. Only when she is asked, plainly and in the same tone as every other question, whether the medicine has affected her sex life does she say that she has not been able to reach orgasm since she started it, and that she was too embarrassed to mention it (Sections 9.4.5 and 5.7.2). Without that one question she would have stopped a treatment that was helping her, and nobody would have known why.

Clients rarely raise sexual problems first. They are embarrassed, they do not know that the problem is relevant, or they fear being judged. The remedy is to ask routinely, as part of the personal history (Book 2, Section 2.3.6), in a neutral, matter-of-fact way that signals that any answer is acceptable. Annon's PLISSIT model (Book 2, Section 7.9.6) reminds the clinician that most people need only the first levels of help, permission to talk and limited information, and that specific suggestions and intensive therapy are needed by fewer. Use the client's own words for their body and their identity, and the name and pronouns they use. Do not assume that a client is heterosexual or that everyone is either a man or a woman: "Do you have a partner?" closes fewer doors than "Are you married?" (Book 2, Section 10.6.9). In India the interview often happens with a spouse or a parent in the room, and in a joint family privacy at home may be scarce; see the client alone for part of the session, and explain the limits of confidentiality before sensitive questions are asked. Two rules have no exceptions. Sexual contact between a therapist and a client is forbidden (Book 2, Sections 1.8 and 1.9, and the clinical link in Section 7.9.2). And if anything the client says suggests that a child may be at risk, the clinician's legal duty to report under the POCSO Act 2012 overrides confidentiality (Book 2, Section 10.6.5).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. A person's own sense of being male, female, something in between or something else is that person's .
  3. Scoring high on both the masculine and the feminine scales of Bem's 1974 inventory is called .
  4. Krafft-Ebing, author of Psychopathia Sexualis (1886), coined the word masochism from the name of the novelist Leopold von .
  5. Magnus Hirschfeld coined the word in 1910 and opened the Institute for Sexual Science in Berlin in 1919.
  6. The word paraphilia replaced "sexual deviation" in DSM- (1980).
  7. The single class called "sexual and gender identity disorders" belongs to DSM- and its text revision.
  8. ICD-10's block F52 covered sexual dysfunction "not caused by disorder or disease".
  9. In ICD-11 the sexual dysfunctions, the sexual pain disorders and gender incongruence are in Chapter , Conditions related to sexual health.
  10. When gender incongruence left the mental disorders chapter of ICD-11, the only group of this chapter that stayed in Chapter 6 was the disorders.

Answers: (1) 19, (2) gender identity, (3) androgyny, (4) Sacher-Masoch, (5) transvestite, (6) III, (7) IV, (8) organic, (9) 17, (10) paraphilic.

9.2 The Sexual Response Cycle: Models, Biology and What Disrupts ItLower yield · not asked yet

Before anyone can say that a sexual response has gone wrong, someone has to say what going right looks like. A doctor who calls a man's blood pressure high is comparing it with a known normal range; a clinician who calls a woman's desire "hypoactive" or a man's ejaculation "premature" is comparing them with an idea of normal sexual response, whether or not the idea is stated. This section makes that idea explicit. It starts with the models of the response cycle that the diagnostic manuals have borrowed, and shows how each phase became the home of a family of disorders. It then goes inside the body, to the nerves, blood vessels, hormones and chemical messengers that produce desire, erection, lubrication and orgasm, because every drug treatment in Sections 9.3 and 9.4 acts on one of these links. After that it turns to the mind: the balance of excitation and inhibition, the anxious self-watching that Masters and Johnson called spectatoring, and the experiments in which David Barlow showed that anxiety is not quite the villain it was thought to be. It ends with the relationships, beliefs and cultures in which every sexual response is embedded, and with the instruments clinicians use to measure it.

Why a model of normal response matters for diagnosis

Picture a woman of 45, married for twenty years, who tells her doctor that she almost never finds herself thinking about sex or wanting it out of the blue. When her husband reaches for her, though, she is usually willing, and once they have begun she becomes aroused, enjoys it and is glad afterwards that they made love. Does she have a disorder of sexual desire? If normal sexual response is a sequence that must begin with a spontaneous feeling of wanting sex, then she lacks the first step, and a clinician could diagnose her. If normal response can begin with willingness and let desire arrive later, she is entirely ordinary. Nothing about her has changed between the two answers; only the model has.

That is why the models matter. A sexual response cycle is a description of the sequence of bodily and mental changes that runs from the first stirring of interest, through arousal and orgasm, to the return to rest. Every sexual dysfunction is defined by comparison with such a sequence, and the manuals have taken their sequence from the researchers you will meet in a moment. From DSM-III (1980) onwards the American Psychiatric Association organised the sexual dysfunctions by the phase of the cycle each one disturbs, beginning with desire (Book 2, Section 7.9.5): disorders of desire, disorders of arousal, disorders of orgasm, and, outside the cycle, disorders of pain. The arrangement is tidy. Each phase can fail, and each way of failing gets a name.

But tidiness rests on an assumption: that everyone passes through the same phases in the same order, so that a failure at one point can be cleanly separated from a failure at another. When researchers in the 1990s listened carefully to women in long relationships, the assumption began to look shaky. Many women said that desire came after arousal rather than before it, and in the clinic, women's problems of desire and of arousal proved very hard to tell apart. DSM-5 (2013) responded with the largest change in this chapter of the manual: for women it merged the old desire disorder and the old arousal disorder into a single diagnosis, female sexual interest/arousal disorder, while for men it kept desire and arousal as separate disorders (Sections 9.3.4 and 9.3.5). The choice of model therefore decides, quite literally, who receives a diagnosis and what it is called. Keep the woman of 45 in mind as you read the next two subsections; Section 9.3.4 returns to her kind of case.

Masters and Johnson's four phases and Kaplan's desire phase

The first model is the one you already know from Book 1, Section 10.9.6 and Book 2, Section 7.9.1, so it needs only a reminder here. William Howell Masters (1915 to 2001), a gynaecologist in St Louis, and his research partner Virginia Eshelman Johnson (1925 to 2013) recorded the bodily changes of 382 women and 312 men in the laboratory and published the results as Human Sexual Response (1966). They described four phases, the same in both sexes: excitement, plateau, orgasm and resolution. Two physiological processes drive the whole sequence: vasocongestion, the swelling of tissues as blood flows into them faster than it can drain away, and myotonia, the build-up of muscle tension that is released in the rhythmic contractions of orgasm. After orgasm men pass through a refractory period, during which another erection or orgasm is not possible; women have none and can return to orgasm if stimulation continues. Thirteen years later Helen Singer Kaplan (1929 to 1995), a New York psychiatrist and psychologist who kept meeting patients whose bodies worked perfectly well but who simply did not want sex, published Disorders of Sexual Desire (1979). Her triphasic model put a desire phase first and reduced the cycle to three phases that can fail independently: desire, excitement and orgasm. The sequence the DSM adopted, desire, excitement, orgasm and resolution, joins the two models together. Book 2 draws the curves (Figure 7.14) and compares the two schools (Table 7.21); they are not redrawn here.

What matters for this chapter is what each phase contributed to the classification, and Table 9.4 sets it out. The desire phase is the home of the disorders of low desire. The excitement phase, the phase of vasocongestion, is the home of the arousal disorders: failure of erection in men and failure of lubrication and swelling in women. The orgasm phase is the home of three different ways for orgasm to go wrong: too early, too late, or not at all. Notice the two empty rows. No disorder has ever been assigned to plateau, because a problem that appears at the plateau stage is experienced by the person either as a loss of arousal (the erection fades) or as a problem of orgasm (it comes too soon or not at all), so the plateau adds nothing that the other phases do not already capture. No disorder has been assigned to resolution either: the refractory period is normal physiology, not a disorder, however inconvenient it may be. Two further rows lie outside the cycle altogether. Pain during sex is not a phase; it can strike at any point, from the first attempt at penetration to the end of intercourse, which is why DSM-III-R and DSM-IV gave dyspareunia and vaginismus a group of their own, the sexual pain disorders, and why DSM-5-TR has one pain diagnosis, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder (Section 9.4.4). And a drug, alcohol or illness can disturb any phase, so the substance-induced dysfunction belongs to every row at once (Section 9.4.5). ICD-11 follows the same logic in its own Chapter 17 (Section 9.1.6): HA00 for desire, HA01 for arousal, HA02 for orgasm, HA03 for ejaculation and HA20 for pain.

Table 9.4 The phases of sexual response and the dysfunctions that disrupt them. DSM-IV names are those of 1994; DSM-5-TR names are those of 2022. ICD-11 codes are from Chapter 17, "Conditions related to sexual health" (Table 9.3).
Phase (which model)What happens in the bodyDSM-IV disordersDSM-5-TR disordersICD-11
Desire (Kaplan, 1979; absent from Masters and Johnson)Interest in sex; sexual thoughts and fantasies; wish for sexual activityHypoactive sexual desire disorder; sexual aversion disorderMale hypoactive sexual desire disorder; female sexual interest/arousal disorderHA00 hypoactive sexual desire dysfunction
Excitement or arousal (both models)Vasocongestion: erection; swelling of clitoris and labia; vaginal lubricationFemale sexual arousal disorder; male erectile disorderErectile disorder; female sexual interest/arousal disorderHA01.1 male erectile dysfunction; HA01.0 female sexual arousal dysfunction
