Read the table across its two middle columns and you can see what DSM-5 changed. Sexual aversion disorder (an active, phobia-like avoidance of genital contact) was not kept as a disorder of its own; DSM-5-TR mentions sexual aversion only as an example of other specified sexual dysfunction (Section 9.4.6). The two female disorders of desire and arousal became one. "Male erectile disorder" lost its first word, "male orgasmic disorder" became delayed ejaculation, and the two pain disorders were fused. When you meet an old name (for example "inhibited male orgasm", the DSM-III name for what is now delayed ejaculation, Section 9.4.2), the table tells you which present-day disorder and which phase it belongs to.

Exam trap

In DSM-5-TR, female sexual interest/arousal disorder spans two phases (desire and arousal), whereas in men desire and arousal stay separate (male hypoactive sexual desire disorder and erectile disorder). DSM-5-TR says so directly: "desire and arousal disorders have been retained as separate constructs in men". No disorder belongs to plateau or resolution, and the refractory period is normal. Pain is not a phase of the response cycle: genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder sits outside the cycle. Masters and Johnson's model has no desire phase; Kaplan's has no plateau or resolution.

Rosemary Basson's circular model

Both models you have just met are linear: they draw sexual response as a road that runs in one direction, from a starting point to an end point, with desire (in Kaplan's version) as the ignition that starts the engine. Rosemary Basson, a physician in the departments of psychiatry and of obstetrics and gynaecology at the University of British Columbia in Vancouver, spent years listening to women in a sexual medicine clinic, and she noticed that many of them did not fit the road. Women in long relationships, in particular, described rarely or never feeling a spontaneous hunger for sex. Yet many of them had sex regularly, became aroused, enjoyed it and valued it. If the linear model was right, a large share of contented women had a desire disorder. Basson concluded that the model, not the women, was at fault. In "The female sexual response: a different model", published in the Journal of Sex and Marital Therapy in 2000, she proposed a cycle instead of a line. Her stated purpose was "to prevent diagnosing dysfunction when the response is simply different from the traditional human sex-response cycle", and also to define more clearly the women whose response really was disordered.

Follow the cycle round, as Figure 9.3 draws it. It begins at a point Basson called sexual neutrality: the woman is not feeling desire, but she is not averse either, and she has reasons to be sexual that have little to do with lust. She may want to feel emotionally close to her partner, to express love, to please him or her, to share physical pleasure or to feel attractive and well. Because of those reasons she makes a choice to be receptive: she is willing to look for, and to focus her attention on, sexual stimuli, such as touch, words, a kiss, a setting free of interruptions. Those stimuli are processed in her mind, and whether they produce arousal depends on biological factors (fatigue, illness, hormones, medication) and psychological ones (distraction by the day's worries, feelings about the partner, past experiences, fear of being interrupted by the children or the in-laws). If the context is right, she becomes subjectively aroused; the word "subjectively" matters, because Basson meant the felt sense of being turned on, not merely the genital response. Now comes the step that reverses the linear model: arousal, once it is under way, triggers desire. Basson called this responsive desire, desire that arises in response to arousal during the sexual encounter, and contrasted it with the spontaneous desire of the linear models, the urge that appears beforehand, unprompted, as thoughts, fantasies and a conscious wish for sex. Desire and arousal then feed each other. Finally, if the experience is emotionally and physically satisfying (with or without orgasm, which Basson did not treat as the only measure of success), it deepens intimacy and strengthens the woman's reasons to begin again next time, and the circle closes. Spontaneous desire does not disappear from the model: it can still enter the cycle and add to it, and it tends to be more common early in a relationship.

An everyday comparison helps. Many people at an Indian wedding have no wish at all to dance when the band strikes up. A cousin pulls them onto the floor, they go along to be sociable, the music and the crowd take hold, and ten minutes later they do not want to stop. The wanting came after the doing had begun. Nobody would call the reluctant dancer "hypoactive"; the desire was real, it was simply responsive. Basson's argument was that much of women's sexual desire works the same way, especially once the novelty of a new relationship has worn off, and that a model which counts only the spontaneous kind will mislabel normal women as disordered.

Why does this matter for diagnosis? Because DSM-5-TR has written Basson's insight into its rules. First, it states that a "desire discrepancy", a woman wanting sex less often than her partner does, is not enough for a diagnosis; somebody in every couple wants sex less than the other. Second, it notes that women "in relationships of longer duration are more likely to report engaging in sex despite no obvious feelings of sexual desire at the outset" than women in newer relationships: absence of spontaneous desire at the start is therefore treated as normal, not as a symptom. Third, what DSM-5-TR does treat as a sign of disorder is a lack of responsive desire: one of the six indicators of female sexual interest/arousal disorder is absent or reduced interest or arousal in response to any internal or external erotic cues, written, spoken or visual (Section 9.3.4). Fourth, the merger itself follows from the model. If desire can follow arousal and the two feed each other, then a disorder of "desire" and a disorder of "arousal" are not two separate breakdowns but two faces of one. DSM-5-TR records that its first indicator of the new disorder, reduced interest in sexual activity, "was previously the single criterion for hypoactive sexual desire disorder", and that women who have lost sexual desire "may be nine times more likely" also to have lost arousal. A woman like the one at the start of Section 9.2.1, whose desire is responsive and whose arousal and pleasure are intact, meets none of these markers and receives no diagnosis.

How good is the model? It has had a deep influence on clinical practice, and its central observation, that many women in long relationships begin sexual encounters for reasons of intimacy rather than lust, matches what clinicians hear every day. When researchers tested it directly, though, the results were more mixed. In the Nurses' Sexuality Study, Sand and Fisher (2007) mailed questionnaires to a random sample of 580 registered nurses; 133 replied, and they were asked which of three short descriptions, of the Masters and Johnson, Kaplan and Basson models, best fitted their own experience. Roughly equal numbers chose each, and the women who chose Basson's model had significantly lower scores on the Female Sexual Function Index, a questionnaire of sexual functioning, than those who chose either linear model. An online survey of 404 women by the Australian psychologists Giles and McCabe (2009) found that a linear model fitted women both with and without sexual difficulties well, whereas the original circular model fitted neither group well until it was modified, after which it described women with sexual dysfunction better than women without. The likeliest reading of both studies is that women's sexual response is varied: some women's experience runs along the line, some round the circle, and the circle may describe especially well the women who come to clinics. Critics have added two cautions. The model is hard to test as a whole, because its steps (motives, attention, appraisal) are hard to measure separately. And it risks creating a new stereotype, that women's desire is responsive and men's spontaneous, when DSM-5-TR itself notes that men too "identify a variety of triggers for their sexual desire" and "describe a wide range of reasons" for choosing to have sex. A third caution is a clinical one: "willing to be receptive" means a free choice made for the woman's own reasons. A woman who has sex only because she believes it is her duty, or because refusing is not safe, is not illustrating responsive desire, and partner violence is an exclusion in every dysfunction diagnosis (Section 9.3.1).

Exam trap

The circular model with responsive desire is Rosemary Basson's (2000). It is not Kaplan's (the triphasic model, desire, excitement and orgasm, 1979) and not Masters and Johnson's (four phases, excitement, plateau, orgasm and resolution, 1966). In Basson's model responsive desire follows arousal; the cycle starts from sexual neutrality with motives such as wanting closeness. Lack of spontaneous desire is not by itself a dysfunction, and a desire discrepancy between partners is not enough for a diagnosis.

The body in sexual response

Every drug in this chapter, the tablets for erectile disorder, the antidepressants that delay ejaculation, the hormones and the medicines that damp desire, works by pulling on one link in a chain of nerves, blood vessels and chemical messengers. You cannot understand why a drug helps one dysfunction and causes another without knowing the chain, so this subsection follows it from the first touch to orgasm. Only what the disorders and their treatments need is taught here; the anatomy of the autonomic nervous system and of the reproductive organs belongs to the Physiological book.

How an erection happens. An erection is a hydraulic event: it is produced by blood, not by muscle. Sexual stimulation reaches the spinal cord by two routes. Touch to the genitals sends signals into the lowest part of the cord, which can produce a reflex erection even when the brain is not involved (men with some spinal cord injuries keep these reflex erections). Erotic thoughts, sights and memories send signals down from the brain. Both routes converge on parasympathetic nerve fibres that leave the sacral spinal cord, at about the levels S2 to S4, and travel in the pelvic nerves to the penis. Their endings, and the cells lining the penis's blood vessels, release a small gaseous messenger, nitric oxide. Nitric oxide passes into the smooth muscle cells of the arteries and of the spongy erectile tissue, the two cylinders called the corpora cavernosa, and switches on an enzyme, guanylate cyclase, which manufactures a second messenger called cyclic guanosine monophosphate (cGMP). cGMP makes the smooth muscle relax. In the unaroused state that muscle is held contracted (by the sympathetic nerves), which keeps the arteries narrow and the spongy tissue empty; when it relaxes, the arteries widen and blood floods into the spaces of the erectile tissue. As the tissue swells inside its tough outer sheath, it squeezes shut the small veins that would normally drain it. This veno-occlusion traps the blood, the pressure inside rises, and the penis becomes rigid. The erection lasts as long as cGMP does. An enzyme called phosphodiesterase type 5 (PDE-5) steadily breaks cGMP down; when the nitric oxide signal stops, PDE-5 wins, the muscle contracts again, the veins reopen and the blood drains away. Figure 9.4 draws the chain.

Two facts follow directly from the figure, and both are examined. First, a PDE-5 inhibitor such as sildenafil does not produce an erection by itself and does not increase desire. It protects cGMP from destruction, so it makes an erection easier to get and to keep, but only once sexual stimulation has set the nitric oxide signal going. Second, these drugs must never be combined with the nitrate drugs taken for angina (such as glyceryl trinitrate), because nitrates also work by releasing nitric oxide, this time in blood vessels throughout the body. With PDE-5 blocked, cGMP builds up in vessels everywhere, they all dilate at once, and blood pressure can fall dangerously. PDE-5 is also present in other blood vessels, which is why headache, flushing and a stuffy nose are common side effects; and sildenafil weakly blocks a related enzyme in the retina, which explains the brief blue tinge to vision that some men notice (the drugs themselves are taught in Section 9.3.6). The same vascular machinery works in women: during arousal the clitoris and labia engorge with blood, and fluid seeps through the swollen vaginal walls as lubrication (vasocongestion, Book 2, Section 7.9.1). Yet blood flow is rarely the limiting factor in women's arousal difficulties, which is one reason sildenafil disappointed when it was tried in women (Section 9.3.4).

One more fact about erections turns out to be clinically priceless. Throughout life, from infancy to old age, men have erections during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, several times a night; Pinel notes that even babies have them, and that they are no more complete during dreams with sexual content than during other dreams. These nocturnal penile tumescence episodes owe nothing to desire, anxiety or a partner. A man who has firm erections in his sleep or on waking therefore has nerves and blood vessels that work, and if he cannot keep an erection with his partner, the likely cause is psychological. That logic underlies a laboratory test (Section 9.2.7) and the clinic's first question about erectile problems (Section 9.3.6). It also explains one physical cause that sleep can hide: in obstructive sleep apnoea, broken sleep and low oxygen are linked to erectile dysfunction (Book 3, Section 8.9.2).

How ejaculation and orgasm happen. Ejaculation is not one event but two. In the first stage, emission, sympathetic nerves from the lower thoracic and upper lumbar spinal cord (about T10 to L2) make the smooth muscle of the sperm ducts, seminal vesicles and prostate contract, moving semen into the back of the urethra, while the neck of the bladder closes so that semen cannot flow backwards. Once emission begins, a man feels that ejaculation can no longer be held back, the "point of no return" or ejaculatory inevitability; the stop-start technique for premature ejaculation works by learning to stop just before this point (Section 9.4.3). In the second stage, expulsion, rhythmic contractions of the bulbospongiosus and other pelvic floor muscles, driven by a somatic nerve (the pudendal nerve, from S2 to S4), propel the semen out. Orgasm is the intensely pleasurable experience that usually accompanies these contractions, and in women it consists of similar rhythmic contractions of the pelvic floor and the outer part of the vagina without emission. Orgasm and ejaculation are separable: after the prostate has been removed, for example, a man can still reach orgasm although no semen is emitted. Medical students remember the division of labour with the mnemonic "point and shoot": the parasympathetic system makes the penis point (erection), the sympathetic system makes it shoot (emission). The mnemonic simplifies, because expulsion is somatic, but it captures something important for this chapter. Erection needs calm parasympathetic activity, so a surge of sympathetic arousal, the bodily side of anxiety, works against it; emission is triggered by sympathetic activity, so the same surge can bring ejaculation on sooner. Barlow and Durand make the point for premature ejaculation: excessive sympathetic arousal may lower the threshold for ejaculation, and a man's anxiety about coming too quickly adds to that arousal and makes the problem worse. That is the physiology behind the close link between anxiety and premature ejaculation, taught fully in Section 9.4.3.

Hormones. In men, testosterone is necessary for sexual desire. The classic evidence, told in Pinel's chapter on hormones and sex, comes from castration. Bremer (1959) reviewed 215 Norwegian men whose testes had been removed, about half of them sex offenders who had agreed to the operation to shorten their prison terms. About half became completely asexual within a few weeks; others quickly lost their erections but kept some sexual interest; a few went on having intercourse, less enthusiastically, for the duration of the study. The reverse experiment came from a single case: a First World War soldier who had been castrated by a shell fragment in 1918, at the age of 19, and who could not achieve an erection. In the first case report of testosterone replacement (described by de Kruif, 1945), his erections returned after the fifth injection. Two popular beliefs, however, are wrong. A man's level of desire does not track the amount of testosterone in his blood, and extra testosterone does not raise the desire of a healthy man: "sex drive and testosterone levels are uncorrelated in healthy men, and testosterone injections do not increase their sex drive" (Pinel). The experiment that shows why was done on guinea pigs by Grunt and Young (1952). They rated male guinea pigs as low, medium or high in sexual activity, castrated them all (their sexual behaviour fell to almost nothing), and then gave every animal the same large replacement dose of testosterone. Each returned to his own previous level, no higher. Above a threshold, more testosterone adds nothing, and DSM-5-TR says much the same for men's desire, suggesting a "critical threshold" below which testosterone matters and above which it has little effect. The clinical rule that follows, testosterone only for men whose levels are proven to be low, appears in Sections 9.3.5 and 9.3.6.

In women the picture is less tidy. Removing the ovaries reduces sexual desire and fantasies, and in such women both oestradiol and testosterone supplements have restored desire and pleasure; but studies of testosterone or oestrogen after a natural menopause have been mixed, and some found an effect of testosterone only at unnaturally high doses. Pinel's summary is that both oestradiol and testosterone can influence female sexual behaviour, "at least in some cases". Women also differ from the females of most other mammals in being motivated to have sex when they are not fertile, so their sexuality is far less tightly tied to hormonal cycles. Hormones matter in one other, more mechanical way. After the menopause, falling oestrogen thins and dries the tissues of the vulva and vagina (vulvovaginal atrophy, now often called the genitourinary syndrome of menopause), which can make intercourse uncomfortable or painful and arousal and orgasm harder; DSM-5-TR lists it among the medical factors to rule out (Sections 9.3.4 and 9.4.4).

Two further hormonal facts have direct drug consequences. A raised level of prolactin, the pituitary hormone that dopamine normally holds in check, suppresses desire and arousal in both sexes, partly by lowering sex hormone levels. That is why a prolactin-secreting pituitary tumour can present as loss of desire or erectile failure, and why antipsychotic drugs that block dopamine and raise prolactin so often cause sexual side effects (Book 3, Sections 4.8.3 and 4.10.5; Section 9.4.5). And the huge doses of anabolic steroids taken by some bodybuilders do not improve sexual function: Pinel's answer is "an emphatic no". By negative feedback they shut down the pituitary hormones that drive the testes, so the testes shrink and fertility falls; in women they disrupt menstruation and masculinise the body. The distinction between the organising effects of hormones before birth and their activating effects in adulthood is taught in Book 1, Section 10.9.6 and applied to gender in Section 9.8.3; everything in this paragraph and the last concerns the activating effects.

Brain chemistry. At the level the papers ask, three transmitters are enough. Dopamine, the transmitter of the brain's reward and wanting systems, broadly promotes sexual desire and arousal; drugs that boost dopamine can raise sexual interest, sometimes excessively, as in some patients with Parkinson's disease treated with dopamine agonists. Serotonin broadly inhibits sexual response, and most of all ejaculation and orgasm. That single fact explains two things that look contradictory: selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) are a leading cause of delayed orgasm and ejaculation as a side effect (Section 9.4.5), and for exactly the same reason they are used to treat premature ejaculation (Section 9.4.3). Noradrenaline and the sympathetic system drive emission, as you saw above. Two drugs for women's low desire, flibanserin (on serotonin receptors) and bremelanotide (on melanocortin receptors), act on the brain rather than on blood flow (Section 9.3.4). DSM-5-TR also reports brain-imaging and genetic findings in lifelong premature ejaculation, which belong to Section 9.4.3. Table 9.5 gathers the biology of the phases with the drugs that act at each.

Table 9.5 The biology of sexual response, phase by phase, and the drugs that act there. The drugs are taught in Sections 9.3.4, 9.3.6, 9.4.3 and 9.4.5.
PhaseMain nervesWhat the body doesKey chemistryDrugs that act here
DesireBrain (hypothalamus and reward pathways)Sexual thoughts, fantasies, wantingDopamine promotes; serotonin and prolactin damp; testosterone needed above a thresholdFlibanserin and bremelanotide (women); testosterone only if deficient; prolactin-raising antipsychotics lower desire

Exam trap

Erection is parasympathetic (nitric oxide, then cGMP, relaxes smooth muscle so blood flows in); emission is sympathetic; expulsion is driven by a somatic nerve ("point and shoot"). Sildenafil and the other PDE-5 inhibitors block the breakdown of cGMP; they do not create an erection without sexual stimulation and do not raise desire, and they are dangerous with nitrates. Erections in REM sleep point to a psychological cause of waking erectile failure. Testosterone is necessary for male desire, but extra testosterone does not raise desire in men with normal levels (Grunt and Young's guinea pigs). Serotonin inhibits ejaculation, which is why SSRIs both cause delayed ejaculation and treat premature ejaculation.

Excitation, inhibition and anxiety: why arousal fails

Here is the puzzle that the last subsection leaves. A man whose nerves and blood vessels are healthy has firm erections in his sleep, on waking and when he is alone, and yet he loses his erection every time he is with his partner. A woman who becomes aroused easily when she reads a novel feels nothing when her husband touches her. Nothing in the body has changed between the two situations. What has changed is the mind, and three ideas explain how: the clinical description of performance anxiety and spectatoring, Barlow's experiments showing that anxiety itself is not the culprit, and the dual control model, which explains why some people are far more vulnerable than others.

Performance anxiety and spectatoring. In Human Sexual Inadequacy (1970) Masters and Johnson identified the force that keeps most sexual dysfunctions going, and Book 2, Section 7.9.2 tells the story of their treatment programme. Performance anxiety is the fear of failing sexually: of losing an erection, of not reaching orgasm, of coming too soon, of disappointing a partner. Its companion is spectatoring, their word for the way the anxious person mentally steps out of the experience and watches it, like a spectator in the stands, checking and judging instead of feeling. Picture a young husband who lost his erection once, on a night when he was exhausted and had drunk too much at a cousin's reception. The next time he and his wife are together, part of his mind is keeping watch: "Is it happening? Is it firm enough? It is going to fail again." Every ounce of attention spent on watching is attention taken away from the touch and the closeness that produce arousal, so the erection does fade, the fear is confirmed, and the next attempt begins with even more watching. Viktor Frankl had described the same trap as hyper-reflection, excessive attention to oneself that disrupts the very act attended to (Book 2, Section 6.5). Helen Singer Kaplan built the idea into her distinction between the immediate causes of a dysfunction (performance anxiety, spectatoring, a partner's demands, poor technique) and its remote causes in deeper personal and relationship conflict (Book 2, Section 7.9.5).

For decades the lesson seemed simple: anxiety kills arousal. Masters and Johnson thought so, and so did Kaplan. Then a psychologist set out to test it.

Barlow's experiments: anxiety is not the problem, attention is. David H. Barlow (born 1942), the American clinical psychologist better known for his work on panic and the anxiety disorders, ran a sexuality research laboratory at the State University of New York at Albany in the 1980s. Because he studied anxiety, he was well placed to ask whether anxiety really does what sex therapists assumed. His laboratory measured arousal in men directly, with a penile strain gauge (Section 9.2.7), and the answer it produced was a surprise.

Key study: does anxiety reduce sexual arousal? (Barlow, Sakheim and Beck, 1983)

Question. If performance anxiety causes sexual dysfunction, then making men anxious about their performance should reduce their arousal. Does it? Method. Young men without sexual problems watched erotic films while a strain gauge recorded their erections. Before the films each man received a harmless but somewhat painful electric shock to the forearm, so that later threats would be believed. Each man then watched films under three conditions: a control condition (relax, no chance of shock); a non-contingent threat (a 60 percent chance of a shock at some point during the film, whatever happened); and a contingent threat, designed to mimic performance anxiety (a 60 percent chance of a shock if his erection did not reach the average level reached by earlier participants). No shock was ever given during the films. Result. Both threats increased arousal compared with the control condition, and the contingent threat, the one that most resembled performance demand, increased it most of all. The paper's title says it plainly: "Anxiety increases sexual arousal". What happened next. In men with erectile dysfunction whose physical causes had been ruled out, the same kind of threat reduced arousal. A distraction experiment (Abrahamson and colleagues, 1985) had the opposite pattern: sexually functional men who had to listen to a story through earphones while watching an erotic film, knowing they would be questioned on it, became less aroused; men with dysfunction were not affected, as if they were already distracted by their own worries. Men with dysfunction also underreported their arousal: at the same measured erection, they said they were far less aroused than functional men did (Sakheim and colleagues, 1987). Criticism. These are laboratory analogues with young male volunteers, a shock is not the same as a partner's disappointment, and the strain gauge measures the genital response rather than the whole experience; but later studies with women, using vaginal photoplethysmography, found a broadly similar pattern.

How can anxiety raise arousal in one man and lower it in another? Barlow's answer, published in 1986 as "Causes of sexual dysfunction: the role of anxiety and cognitive interference" in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology, was to break "performance anxiety" into three parts: physiological arousal, negative affect and, crucially, the direction of attention. Figure 9.5 shows the two loops he proposed. Both begin in the same place, a situation that demands sexual performance, such as a willing partner in bed. A sexually functional person meets that demand with positive feelings and expectations, a sense of being in control and an accurate sense of how aroused he or she is, and so attends to the erotic cues: the partner, the touch, the sensations. The autonomic arousal that follows (and anxiety adds to it) narrows and sharpens attention still further on those same cues, arousal rises, performance succeeds, and next time the person approaches sex more readily. A person with a dysfunction meets the same demand with negative feelings, an expectation of failure and a sense of having no control, and so attends to what Barlow called the "public consequences of not performing" ("I'll make a fool of myself", "she will think I'm useless") and to any sign that the response is failing. Here the rise in autonomic arousal sharpens attention on the threat instead of the erotic cues, arousal of the genitals falls, performance fails, and the failure confirms the fear, which feeds the next round and leads to avoidance. The same bodily arousal drives both loops; the difference is where it sends attention. That is why Barlow's name for the core problem is cognitive interference: the trouble is not anxiety as such but attention captured by non-erotic, threatening thoughts. Barlow and Durand note that this process applies to most sexual dysfunctions, which tend to occur together, but particularly to the arousal disorders.

The model explains several clinical facts at once. It explains why simple reassurance ("relax, it will be fine") rarely works: the problem is not a lack of calm but a habit of attention. It explains why sensate focus, with its ban on intercourse (Book 2, Section 7.9.3), helps so many dysfunctions: when no performance is demanded there are no "public consequences" to attend to, and attention can return to sensation. And it explains why a drug that restores erections may not be enough for a very anxious man, whose attention to failure remains, a point taken up with the evidence in Section 9.3.6.

The dual control model. Barlow's loops describe what happens in the moment. They do not explain why one man falls into the negative loop after a single bad night while another shrugs off ten. In 2000 John Bancroft and Erick Janssen, at the Kinsey Institute of Indiana University, offered an answer in a paper in Neuroscience and Biobehavioral Reviews, "The dual control model of male sexual response". The dual control model holds that whether sexual arousal occurs in a given situation depends on the balance between two separate systems in the brain: a sexual excitation system, which responds to sexually relevant stimuli and switches arousal on, and a sexual inhibition system, which responds to threats and switches it off. A car with an accelerator and a brake is the usual comparison: the car's speed depends on how hard each pedal is pressed, and a car can stand still either because nobody is pressing the accelerator or because somebody is standing on the brake. Inhibition is not a fault. From an evolutionary point of view it is useful to lose arousal when there is danger nearby. The key claim is that people differ, in a fairly stable, trait-like way, in how sensitive each system is. A person with a sensitive brake and a light accelerator is vulnerable to sexual dysfunction, especially under stress; a person with a sensitive accelerator and a weak brake may be more prone to sexual risk-taking.

Bancroft and Janssen measured these propensities with a questionnaire, the Sexual Inhibition and Sexual Excitation Scales (SIS/SES; the instrument itself belongs to the Assessment book), which yields one excitation score and two inhibition scores. SIS1 measures inhibition due to the threat of performance failure (the brake pressed by worries such as "I may not be able to keep my erection"), and SIS2 measures inhibition due to the threat of performance consequences, external dangers such as being discovered, catching an infection, causing a pregnancy or hurting the relationship. In their paper they even predicted that men high on SIS2 would respond to psychological treatment, while men high on SIS1 would respond better to drugs. The idea has spread well beyond its origins in men: DSM-5-TR lists "differences in propensity for sexual excitation and sexual inhibition" among the risk factors for female sexual interest/arousal disorder, and suggests that one type of that disorder may rest on "overactivation of sexual inhibition" (Section 9.3.4). The three ideas fit together neatly. The dual control model says who is vulnerable, Barlow's model says what happens in the moment when the brake is pressed by worry, and spectatoring is what the experience feels like from the inside.

Exam trap

Anxiety does not always reduce sexual arousal: in sexually functional men it increased arousal (Barlow, Sakheim and Beck, 1983, "Anxiety increases sexual arousal"). In dysfunction the harm comes from cognitive interference, attention to failure and its consequences instead of to erotic cues (Barlow, 1986). Spectatoring and performance anxiety are Masters and Johnson's terms (1970); "hyper-reflection" is Frankl's. The dual control model (excitation versus inhibition, measured by the SIS/SES) is Bancroft and Janssen's (2000), not Barlow's or Basson's.

Relationships, culture and beliefs

Barlow's model explains how a dysfunction keeps itself going, but not where the negative feelings and the habit of watching for failure came from in the first place. For that we have to widen the lens from the person to the couple, the family and the culture. DSM-5-TR tells clinicians to weigh five groups of factors in every case (the list is given in Section 9.3.1); this subsection explains why the non-medical ones matter so much.

The relationship. Sex is one of the few things people do that needs two willing participants, and it is exquisitely sensitive to how those two feel about each other. Barlow and Durand name a marked deterioration in the close relationship as the most common of the contributing factors: it is hard to feel desire for someone you have come to resent. Anger and contempt are incompatible with the positive feelings that start the functional loop in Figure 9.5. In a power struggle, sex easily becomes the arena where one partner withholds and the other demands, and each demand adds to the performance pressure on both. Poor communication matters in a more practical way: many people never tell their partner what kind of touch they like, so the stimulation they receive is not the stimulation that arouses them; one study found that women who did not reach orgasm reported discomfort in telling their partners what would help, such as direct clitoral stimulation (Kelly, Strassberg and Kircher, 1990). And a partner's own difficulty changes the other's experience: a wife whose husband always ejaculates within moments may gradually lose interest in sex that leaves her frustrated, and then it is she who is brought to the clinic with "low desire". That is why DSM-5-TR lists partner factors first, and why Masters and Johnson insisted that the couple, not the individual, is the patient.

Scripts. The sociologist John Gagnon (1931 to 2016), who with William Simon introduced the idea in Sexual Conduct (1973), argued that human sexual behaviour is not simply driven by instinct but follows sexual scripts: culturally learned guides to what counts as sex, with whom, when, where, how it should begin and end, and what it means. Gagnon later helped to apply the idea in the large American survey published as The Social Organization of Sexuality (Laumann, Gagnon, Michael and Michaels, 1994). Scripts matter for dysfunction because a narrow script turns ordinary variation into failure. If the script says that sex means intercourse, that the man must lead and the woman must be passive, and that the act is over when he ejaculates, then a man with a softer erection has "failed", a woman who needs a different kind of stimulation is "frigid", and neither may ever discover the many other ways in which they could enjoy each other. A person who learns from the script that sex is dangerous, dirty or forbidden is more vulnerable still. Barlow and Durand note that such patterns are most evident in cultures with restrictive attitudes to sex, and that vaginismus, for example, is relatively rare in North America but considerably more common in Ireland and Turkey.

Erotophobia and myths. The social psychologist Donn Byrne gave a name to one product of a restrictive upbringing: erotophobia, a negative cognitive and emotional set towards sexuality (its opposite is erotophilia), learned early from parents, religious authorities and others, which predicts sexual difficulties later in life (Byrne and Schulte, 1990). For people with this set, sexual cues come to be associated with negative feelings long before any sexual relationship begins, which is exactly the starting point of the dysfunctional loop. A related danger is sexual myths, confident but false beliefs about what normal sex is like. Bernie Zilbergeld, an American psychologist and one of the best-known writers on male sexuality, collected the myths that many men hold, among them that all touching should lead to sex, that sex is centred on a hard penis and what is done with it, that good sex is spontaneous and needs no talking or planning, and that real men do not have sexual problems (Zilbergeld, 1999; Book 2, Section 7.9.7 gives the myths most often met in Indian clinics). Do such beliefs actually matter? Baker and de Silva (1988) turned an earlier version of Zilbergeld's list into a questionnaire and gave it to sexually functional and dysfunctional men: the men with dysfunctions believed the myths significantly more strongly. A man who believes that a real man never has a problem cannot treat one soft erection as the normal event it is.

Trauma, guilt and religion. Early sexual abuse leaves long shadows on sexual function. In the American national survey that Section 9.3.2 describes, Laumann and colleagues (1999) found that women who had been sexually touched by an adult before puberty, or forced into sexual contact, were about twice as likely to have orgasm problems as women who had not, and that men who had had sexual contact with an adult as children were more than three times as likely to have erectile dysfunction (trauma and its effects are taught in Book 3, Sections 7.2 and 7.3). DSM-5-TR's fourth group of factors, "cultural or religious factors", covers the quieter but more common influence of prohibitions against sexual activity or pleasure and the guilt they leave. A woman taught that sexual pleasure is shameful may find that arousal brings anxiety rather than delight; a man raised to see masturbation as sin may approach marriage already convinced that he has damaged himself.

Indian context

Two cultural patterns shape how sexual problems present in India, and both are taught elsewhere. Dhat syndrome, named by N. N. Wig in 1960, is distress attributed to the loss of semen, felt as weakness, fatigue and anxiety; DSM-5-TR treats it as a cultural explanation of distress and notes its links with erectile disorder and early ejaculation (Book 3, Section 1.10.4). Sexual myths, wall advertisements by self-styled sexologists, arranged marriages entered with little sex education, and unconsummated marriages are described in Book 2, Section 7.9.7. One study adds numbers. Verma, Khaitan and Singh (1998) reviewed 1,000 consecutive patients at the psychosexual clinic of the All India Institute of Medical Sciences in New Delhi: 71.3 percent complained of nocturnal emission ("night discharge"), 33.4 percent felt guilty about masturbation, and 30 percent worried that their penis was too small. The authors, as Barlow and Durand report, attribute this focus on semen to the culturally held belief that loss of semen causes depletion of physical and mental energy. Its findings on premature ejaculation and on how few women attended are given in Section 9.3.2.

Age and illness. DSM-5-TR reminds clinicians that "aging and relationship duration may be associated with a normative decrease in sexual response": erections take longer and need more direct stimulation, lubrication lessens, and desire usually declines gently. None of this is a disorder. Nor does sex stop. In a national survey of older Americans, Stacy Tessler Lindau and colleagues (2007) found that 38.5 percent of men and 16.7 percent of women aged 75 to 85 had been sexually active with a partner in the previous year; the gap between the sexes probably reflects, among other things, that many older women no longer have a living partner. The declines that do occur are tied mostly to poorer health, reduced mobility, medication and the loss of a partner. Chronic illness affects sexual function directly (diabetes damages nerves and blood vessels, pelvic surgery and spinal injury cut nerve pathways, kidney disease and hormonal disorders disturb the endocrine system) and indirectly: a man who has had a heart attack may become so preoccupied with the fear that sex will strain his heart that he cannot become aroused even after his doctor has reassured him. The particular medical causes are taught with each disorder.

Putting it together. Having reviewed the causes one by one, Barlow and Durand conclude that a sexual dysfunction is seldom caused only by psychological or only by physical factors; more often there is a subtle combination. Their own example is worth retelling in outline. A young man who is prone to anxiety and holds a few sexual myths (psychological and social factors) loses his erection one night after drinking, as many men do (a biological factor). He approaches the next encounter wondering whether it will happen again, and that worry sets the dysfunctional loop of Figure 9.5 in motion, whether or not he has had a drink. In the language of the biopsychosocial model and the four Ps (Book 3, Sections 1.3.3 and 1.3.5), anxiety-proneness and myths were predisposing, the alcohol was precipitating, and the anxious attention to failure, the avoidance and perhaps his partner's growing frustration are perpetuating. The treatment plan has to address all three, which is why so many of the treatments in Sections 9.3 and 9.4 combine a physical and a psychological method.

Measuring sexual response in the laboratory and the clinic

How does a clinician find out what is happening in a sexual response that takes place in private, at night, between two people? Barlow's group describe three parts to the assessment of sexual problems (Wiegel, Wincze and Barlow, 2002): an interview, supported by questionnaires; a medical evaluation; and, where available, psychophysiological measurement of arousal itself. The questionnaires belong to the Assessment book (two widely used ones are the International Index of Erectile Function for men and the Female Sexual Function Index for women), and taking a sexual history is taught in Book 2, Section 2.3.6. In Barlow's clinic the interview lasts about two hours, covers the relationship, physical health and other psychological problems as well as sex, uses the client's own everyday words rather than clinical terms, and includes the partner whenever possible. Questionnaires are given as well because many people will write down what they are not yet ready to say aloud.

Measuring arousal directly. In men, erection can be recorded with a penile strain gauge, a thin, flexible loop placed around the shaft of the penis that registers changes in its circumference; the version used in so much sexuality research was developed in Barlow's own clinic (Barlow, Becker, Leitenberg and Agras, 1970). Measuring the penis's response to sexual stimuli is called penile plethysmography or phallometry, and it comes in two forms: circumferential, using the strain gauge, and volumetric, in which the penis sits inside a sealed cylinder that records changes in its volume, a more sensitive but more cumbersome method devised by Kurt Freund (whose work on paraphilias is taught in Section 9.7). Circumference is not the whole story: rigidity matters too, because a large erection without enough firmness is still inadequate for intercourse. The comparable device for women is the vaginal photoplethysmograph developed by James Geer and his colleagues (Geer, Morokoff and Greenwood, 1974). Smaller than a tampon, it is inserted by the woman herself; a light at its tip and two light-sensitive cells on its sides measure the light reflected back from the vaginal walls, which changes as blood flows into them during arousal. In a typical laboratory session the person watches an erotic film or listens to an erotic recording for a few minutes while the device records, and reports from moment to moment how aroused he or she feels. The comparison is often revealing: many people with psychologically caused dysfunctions become strongly aroused in the laboratory yet cannot become aroused with a partner. In women, however, the genital response and the feeling of being aroused often diverge, and DSM-5-TR notes that research with vaginal photoplethysmography has not found differences between women who do and do not report a lack of genital arousal. That is why female sexual interest/arousal disorder is diagnosed from the woman's own report, which DSM-5-TR says is sufficient (Section 9.3.4).

Tests for physical causes. The other tests are aimed mainly at erectile problems, and DSM-5-TR lists them as its diagnostic markers. Nocturnal penile tumescence testing records erections during sleep, in a sleep laboratory or at home with a portable recorder; adequate erections in REM sleep suggest that the waking problem is psychological (Section 9.2.4). Doppler ultrasonography measures blood flow in the penile arteries, often after an injection of a vasoactive drug into the erectile tissue (the intracavernosal injection test: a good erection after the injection shows that the vessels can respond), and more invasive imaging can look for venous leakage. Nerve conduction studies of the pudendal nerve, and somatosensory evoked potentials, are used when nerve damage is suspected, as in diabetic neuropathy. Blood tests complete the picture: free or bioavailable testosterone, especially in men with diabetes, with low desire as well, or with no response to PDE-5 inhibitors; thyroid function; fasting glucose to detect undiagnosed diabetes; and serum lipids, because erectile disorder in men aged 40 and over predicts later coronary artery disease (Section 9.3.6). The same tools reappear in a different role when penile plethysmography is used to assess sexual interests in men with paraphilic disorders (Section 9.7.4).

Exam trap

The penile strain gauge (circumferential plethysmography) came from Barlow's clinic (1970); the volumetric method is Freund's; the vaginal photoplethysmograph is Geer's (1974) and measures light reflected from the vaginal walls. Good erections during REM sleep (nocturnal penile tumescence) point to a psychological cause of waking erectile failure. In women, genital measures do not separate those with and without arousal complaints, so self-report is enough for the diagnosis.

9.3 Sexual Dysfunctions I: The Shared Rules, and the Disorders of Desire, Arousal and ErectionLower yield · not asked yet

DSM-5-TR's chapter on sexual dysfunctions contains eight specific disorders: delayed ejaculation, erectile disorder, female orgasmic disorder, female sexual interest/arousal disorder, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder, male hypoactive sexual desire disorder, premature (early) ejaculation and substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction, together with other specified and unspecified sexual dysfunction for problems that fit none of them. All eight are built on one template, and examiners like nothing better than the places where a disorder breaks the template, so this section begins with the shared rules and the reason for each. It then asks how common sexual problems are and how a clinician assesses them, and teaches the three disorders of the first half of the response cycle: the two disorders of desire and arousal, one for women and one for men, and erectile disorder. Section 9.4 teaches the rest.

What counts as a sexual dysfunction: the shared template

DSM-5-TR describes the sexual dysfunctions as "a heterogeneous group of disorders that are typically characterized by a clinically significant disturbance in a person's ability to respond sexually or to experience sexual pleasure". Notice what the definition is about: the response cycle of Section 9.2, and the pleasure it brings. It says nothing about what or whom a person finds arousing (that is the territory of the paraphilias, Section 9.5) or about how a person experiences his or her gender (gender dysphoria, Section 9.9). That is why a person whose sense of gender does not match the body does not have a sexual dysfunction: that mismatch is gender dysphoria (Section 9.9). The word "heterogeneous" matters too: the disorders differ in cause, course and treatment, and one person can have several at once, a man with both erectile disorder and premature ejaculation, for example. When that happens, DSM-5-TR says that all of them should be diagnosed.

Almost every disorder in the chapter then repeats four requirements, and each exists to rule something out.

  • A duration of approximately 6 months. Sexual response is easily disturbed by fatigue, illness, bereavement, a new baby, a move, a quarrel or a new partner, and most such disturbances pass on their own. The 6-month rule keeps these passing reactions out of the diagnosis. The word "approximately" is deliberate: DSM-5-TR allows the clinician to judge persistence when the exact duration cannot be established.
  • The problem on almost all or all occasions, approximately 75 to 100 percent. Occasional failure is normal. In a classic study, Ellen Frank and her colleagues (1978) gave questionnaires to 100 well-educated, happily married couples who were not seeking treatment; more than 80 percent described their marital and sexual relations as happy and satisfying, yet 40 percent of the men reported erectile or ejaculatory difficulties and 63 percent of the women reported difficulties with arousal or orgasm. Those difficulties did not spoil their overall satisfaction. A diagnostic rule that counted every occasional failure would turn most contented couples into patients.
  • Clinically significant distress. Many people with a measurable sexual difficulty are not troubled by it. Bancroft, Loftus and Long (2003) surveyed a national sample of 987 American women who had been in a heterosexual relationship for at least 6 months. About 44 percent had at least one difficulty that would meet objective criteria for a problem, yet only 24.4 percent of the women reported marked distress about their sexual relationship or their own sexuality, and the best predictors of that distress were the woman's general emotional well-being and her emotional relationship with her partner during sex, not her lubrication or orgasm. A woman who rarely reaches orgasm and does not mind is not ill; the distress criterion protects her from being labelled.
  • The exclusions (always Criterion D). The difficulty must not be better explained by a nonsexual mental disorder, or be a consequence of "severe relationship distress, partner violence, or other stressors", and it must not be attributable to a substance or medication or to another medical condition. Each exclusion sends the case to a different diagnosis, as the hierarchy below explains.

That is the template. Now the exceptions, which are where the marks are. Two disorders have no percentage of occasions at all and say only "persistent or recurrent": genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder and male hypoactive sexual desire disorder (desire is a state of mind, not something that can fail on a particular occasion). Female sexual interest/arousal disorder applies the 75 to 100 percent rule to only two of its six indicators, the two about pleasure and about genital or non-genital sensations during sexual activity. Premature ejaculation alone demands "at least 6 months", not "approximately". Substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction has no duration rule (it begins with the drug, and can begin within days) and grades severity by how often the problem occurs. Table 9.6 sets the eight side by side.

Table 9.6 The shared template of the DSM-5-TR sexual dysfunctions and the exceptions to it. Every disorder also requires clinically significant distress and the Criterion D exclusions (the substance-induced disorder has its own exclusions).
DisorderDurationOccasions ruleSpecifiersSeverity graded by
Delayed ejaculationAbout 6 months75 to 100 percent of partnered occasionsLifelong or acquired; generalised or situationalDistress
Erectile disorderAbout 6 months75 to 100 percentBoth pairsDistress
Female orgasmic disorderAbout 6 months75 to 100 percentBoth pairs; also "never experienced an orgasm under any situation"Distress
Female sexual interest/arousal disorderAbout 6 monthsOnly for 2 of the 6 indicators (pleasure; sensations)Both pairsDistress
Genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorderAbout 6 monthsNone ("persistent or recurrent")Lifelong or acquired onlyDistress
Male hypoactive sexual desire disorderAbout 6 monthsNone ("persistently or recurrently")Both pairsDistress
Premature (early) ejaculationAt least 6 months75 to 100 percentBoth pairsTime to ejaculation
Substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunctionNo duration ruleNone in the criteriaOnset during intoxication, during withdrawal, or after medication useOccasions: mild 25 to 50, moderate 50 to 75, severe 75 percent or more

The rule about inadequate stimulation. Sometimes a person does not become aroused or reach orgasm simply because the stimulation is not the kind that works for him or her, often because neither partner knows what effective stimulation is. DSM-5-TR says that in such cases "there may still be a need for care", in the form of education, but a sexual dysfunction is not diagnosed. A woman whose husband believes that a minute of kissing is enough preparation for intercourse does not have an arousal disorder.

The subtypes. Every disorder except genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder (which has only the first pair) and the substance-induced dysfunction (which records onset with the drug instead) uses two pairs of specifiers. Lifelong means the problem has been present since the person's first sexual experiences; acquired means it began after a period of relatively normal function. Generalised means it is not limited to certain kinds of stimulation, situations or partners; situational means it occurs only with certain kinds of stimulation, situations or partners. The first pair describes onset, the second describes context, and DSM-5-TR explains why they are worth recording: "the time of onset may indicate different etiologies and interventions". An erectile problem that is acquired and generalised in a 58-year-old smoker with diabetes suggests disease of the blood vessels; one that is lifelong and situational in a 25-year-old, who has good erections alone, suggests anxiety. Severity is rated mild, moderate or severe by the degree of distress, with the two exceptions already met: premature ejaculation is graded by time and the substance-induced disorder by the percentage of occasions.

The five factors. Alongside the subtypes, DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to consider five groups of factors that may contribute to cause or treatment: (1) partner factors, such as the partner's own sexual problems or health; (2) relationship factors, such as poor communication or a difference in desire; (3) individual vulnerability factors (poor body image, a history of sexual or emotional abuse), psychiatric comorbidity (depression, anxiety) or stressors (job loss, bereavement); (4) cultural or religious factors, such as prohibitions against sexual activity or pleasure; and (5) medical factors relevant to prognosis, course or treatment. Section 9.2.6 explained why the first four matter; the medical factors are taught with each disorder. DSM-5-TR adds that ageing and the length of a relationship may bring a normal decrease in sexual response, which must not be mistaken for disorder.

The hierarchy. What happens when something else explains the difficulty? DSM-5-TR gives four rules. If a nonsexual mental disorder such as depression, an anxiety disorder, posttraumatic stress disorder or a psychotic disorder mostly explains it, only that disorder is diagnosed. If a drug or substance explains it, the diagnosis is substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction. If another medical condition, such as peripheral neuropathy, fully explains it, the person does not receive a psychiatric diagnosis at all. And if severe relationship distress, partner violence or other major stressors explain it, no sexual dysfunction is diagnosed; instead a code for the relationship problem may be recorded, such as Z63.0, relationship distress with spouse or intimate partner. The last rule deserves a moment's thought. A woman who has no desire for a husband who hits her is not suffering from a sexual disorder; her lack of desire is an understandable response to violence, and calling it a dysfunction would locate the problem in the wrong person. In practice the causes are often tangled, and DSM-5-TR accepts that a sexual dysfunction can be diagnosed alongside a medical condition, another mental disorder or substance use when each contributes.

Gender-diverse clients. DSM-5-TR adds a note for transgender, nonbinary and agender people. Despite their names, male hypoactive sexual desire disorder and female sexual interest/arousal disorder describe experiences that do not depend on a person's sex or gender, so either can be applied by clinical judgement. Diagnoses tied to reproductive anatomy (erectile disorder, premature and delayed ejaculation, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder) follow the person's current anatomy, not the sex assigned at birth.

ICD-11 handles the same ground more loosely. Its Chapter 17 asks only that the difficulty occur frequently, though it may be absent on some occasions, that it has lasted at least several months and that it causes clinically significant distress, and instead of excluding medical and psychological causes it records them with the HA40 qualifiers (Section 9.1.6). The contrast reflects a difference of philosophy: DSM-5-TR asks whether the problem is a mental disorder, whereas ICD-11 treats the sexual dysfunctions as conditions of sexual health that usually have mixed causes.

Exam trap

The 6-month and 75 to 100 percent rules are not universal. No occasions percentage: genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder and male hypoactive sexual desire disorder. Only 2 of 6 items: female sexual interest/arousal disorder. "At least 6 months": premature ejaculation. No duration rule: substance/medication-induced. Lifelong/acquired describes onset; generalised/situational describes context; genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder has only the first pair. A difficulty caused entirely by severe relationship distress or partner violence is not diagnosed as a sexual dysfunction (a Z code may be used instead), and one caused entirely by a medical condition receives no psychiatric diagnosis. A sexual dysfunction is a disturbance of sexual response or pleasure: when a question describes a person whose gender identity does not match the body, "sexual dysfunction" is the wrong option; the answer is gender identity disorder, now gender dysphoria (Section 9.9).

How common: the surveys and the clinic

Sexual problems are hidden problems, and for most of the twentieth century nobody knew how common they were. The landmark answer came from the sociologist Edward Laumann and his colleagues at the University of Chicago, who in 1992 interviewed a national probability sample of American adults, the National Health and Social Life Survey (the same survey reported more broadly in The Social Organization of Sexuality, Section 9.2.6). In a paper in the Journal of the American Medical Association on 10 February 1999, Laumann, Anthony Paik and Raymond Rosen analysed the answers of 1,749 women and 1,410 men aged 18 to 59 and reported that 43 percent of the women and 31 percent of the men had experienced a sexual dysfunction. The problems were more common in people with poor physical and emotional health and were strongly associated with unhappy sexual relationships and lower overall well-being. Table 9.7 gives the figures most often quoted.

Table 9.7 Sexual problems among American adults aged 18 to 59 in the National Health and Social Life Survey (1992), as reported by Laumann, Paik and Rosen (1999) and Barlow and Durand. These are problems, not distressing disorders (see text).
ProblemWomenMen
Any sexual dysfunction43 percent31 percent
Low sexual interest22 percent5 percent
Arousal problems (women)14 percentnot applicable
Premature ejaculation, the most common male problemnot applicable21 percent
Erectile problems (rising steeply after 60)not applicable5 percent

Two lessons follow. First, the commonest problems were low desire in women and early ejaculation in men; erectile problems were uncommon in this sample only because it stopped at age 59, and they become far more common after 60 (Section 9.3.6). Second, these figures are far higher than the prevalence of the DSM disorders, because the survey counted problems lasting several months in the previous year without asking whether they caused distress. Section 9.3.1 explained why that matters: when distress is required, the numbers fall sharply. DSM-5-TR's own estimates show the gap. For example, fewer than 2 percent of men report clinically significant distress about low desire, against the 5 percent of the survey (Section 9.3.5), and only 1 to 3 percent of men meet the 1-minute definition of premature ejaculation, against the 21 percent who reported the problem (Section 9.4.3). People also rarely seek help: DSM-5-TR reports that only 10.5 percent of men with sexual problems in the previous year sought help.

What about India? No national survey of the kind Laumann ran has been carried out, and no figure for the Indian population is given here. What exists are clinic studies, and the best known shows how skewed a clinic sample can be. Verma, Khaitan and Singh (1998) analysed 1,000 consecutive patients attending the psychosexual clinic of the All India Institute of Medical Sciences in New Delhi, published in the Archives of Sexual Behavior. Most were educated, middle-class men aged 21 to 30. The commonest complaints were premature ejaculation (77.6 percent) and nocturnal emission (71.3 percent); erectile dysfunction was a complaint of 23.6 percent. Only 36 women attended, and they came with their husbands. The figures say as much about who comes to a sexual clinic as about who has sexual problems. Young men attend with worries shaped by beliefs about semen (Section 9.2.6); many of their complaints, such as nocturnal emission, are not dysfunctions at all. Women with sexual difficulties more often go to a gynaecologist, presenting with pain or with "white discharge" and weakness, or never present at all (Book 2, Section 7.9.7), and Barlow and Durand suggest that the tiny number of women also reflects how little value is placed on women's sexual experience for religious and social reasons. A clinic sample is a window onto help-seeking, not onto the population.

The diagnostic assessment

The measures were described in Section 9.2.7; here is the order of questions a clinician works through, because each one maps onto a rule of the template. First, what exactly is the problem, in the client's own words, and which phase of the response does it affect: desire, arousal, orgasm, or pain? Second, is it lifelong or acquired? If acquired, what was happening in the person's life, health and medication when it began? Third, is it generalised or situational? The key questions are concrete: does it happen during masturbation, on waking, with another partner, on holiday, when the house is empty? Fourth, how often and for how long, and how much does it distress the person and the partner? Fifth, the five factors: the partner's own function and health, the state of the relationship (including, asked privately and safely, whether there is violence), past abuse, depression and anxiety, cultural and religious beliefs, and stressors. Sixth, the medical history: diabetes, blood pressure, heart disease, surgery, hormonal symptoms, the menopause, and every prescribed and unprescribed drug, alcohol and tobacco. Seventh, the partner's view, ideally from a joint and an individual interview, because the two accounts often differ. And finally, the client's goals: a couple who want a child, a man who wants to stop avoiding his wife and a woman who wants to feel desire again need different plans. The clues that separate physical from psychological causes of erectile problems are given in Section 9.3.6.

Female sexual interest/arousal disorder

Kamini, 38, teaches mathematics at a school in Pune. She has been married for fourteen years and has two children, aged 12 and 9. She comes to the clinic alone, a few weeks after her husband began sleeping in the other room "so as not to disturb her". "I never feel like it any more," she says. "I don't think about it, I never start anything, and when he reaches for me I pretend to be asleep. It isn't that I don't love him. Even in the early years I didn't always feel like it beforehand, but once we started I enjoyed it. Now I can't remember the last time." It has been like this for about two years, and she is distressed, for herself and because she fears what the separate rooms mean. The assessment fills in the picture. She teaches all day, coaches students in the evening, cooks, supervises homework and is rarely in bed before midnight. Her mother-in-law lives with them and the children's room shares a wall with theirs. Her husband initiates sex only very late at night, when she is exhausted, and with little touching beforehand. She sleeps adequately, still enjoys her work and her children and has no persistent low mood, so she does not have a depressive disorder; she takes no regular medicine, and her thyroid function and blood sugar are normal.

The criteria. Female sexual interest/arousal disorder (DSM-5-TR code F52.22) is a lack of, or significant reduction in, sexual interest or arousal, shown by at least three of six indicators. The six fall naturally into two groups. Three concern interest and desire: (1) absent or reduced interest in sexual activity; (2) absent or reduced sexual or erotic thoughts or fantasies; and (3) no or reduced initiation of sexual activity, together with being typically unreceptive to a partner's attempts to initiate. Three concern arousal and response: (4) absent or reduced sexual excitement or pleasure during sexual activity on almost all or all (approximately 75 to 100 percent) occasions; (5) absent or reduced interest or arousal in response to any internal or external erotic cues, whether written, spoken or visual; and (6) absent or reduced genital or non-genital sensations during sexual activity on almost all or all occasions. The symptoms must have lasted approximately 6 months (Criterion B), must cause clinically significant distress (Criterion C), and must not be better explained by a nonsexual mental disorder, by severe relationship distress (DSM-5-TR gives partner violence as its example) or other significant stressors, or by a substance, medication or another medical condition (Criterion D). The usual specifiers apply: lifelong or acquired, generalised or situational, and mild, moderate or severe distress.

Why three of six, rather than a single essential symptom? Because, as DSM-5-TR puts it, there may be "different symptom profiles across women". In one woman the disorder shows itself as no interest in sex, no sexual thoughts and reluctance to start or respond; in another, as an inability to become excited, to respond to sexual stimuli with desire, or to feel the bodily signs of arousal. A polythetic rule (any three of six) captures both without forcing them into one mould. Kamini meets the first three indicators, which is enough; her report that she once enjoyed sex "once we started" also tells the clinician that her desire was always largely responsive, in Basson's sense, and that what has been lost is the context in which responsive desire could arise.

Where the disorder came from. Until DSM-5, a woman like Kamini would have been diagnosed with hypoactive sexual desire disorder, and a woman who wanted sex but could not become aroused with female sexual arousal disorder (Table 9.4). DSM-5 merged the two, for the reasons given in Section 9.2.3: desire often follows arousal in women, the two problems usually occur together (women who have lost desire "may be nine times more likely" also to have lost arousal), and clinicians could not reliably tell them apart. The old desire diagnosis survives inside the new one as its first indicator, which DSM-5-TR says "was previously the single criterion for hypoactive sexual desire disorder".

What the criteria do and do not count. Several details of the text prevent over-diagnosis. A "desire discrepancy", in which a woman wants sex less than her partner does, is not enough; the difficulty must be hers, not merely a difference between two people. Short-term changes in interest are common and often adaptive responses to life events, and they are not a disorder. Because low desire is often a lack of responsive desire (Section 9.2.3), the clinician must judge whether the stimulation and the context are adequate: if they are not, there may be a need for care but no diagnosis (Section 9.3.1). DSM-5-TR suggests that there may be at least two types of the disorder, one based on low sensitivity to sexual cues and one based on overactivation of sexual inhibition, the two pedals of the dual control model (Section 9.2.5). Because physiological measures of genital arousal do not distinguish women with arousal complaints from women without them (Section 9.2.7), the woman's own report of reduced genital or non-genital sensation is sufficient. And if a lifelong lack of desire is better explained by the woman's identification of herself as asexual, a person who experiences little or no sexual attraction, the diagnosis is not made (Section 9.1.2): asexuality is an orientation, not a dysfunction.

How does Kamini fare against these rules? Her case is a good test. She meets three indicators for two years and is distressed, and she has no depressive disorder, no drug or medical cause, no partner violence and no relationship distress severe enough to explain her symptoms by itself. A clinician could reasonably diagnose female sexual interest/arousal disorder, acquired and generalised, with moderate distress. But the formulation matters more than the label. Her exhaustion, the lack of privacy, the timing and the brevity of her husband's approaches mean that the cycle in Figure 9.3 never gets started: there is no point at which she can choose to attend to sexual stimuli, and so no arousal from which desire could follow. A good plan works on the context, with the couple, before it reaches for anything else.

How common. DSM-5-TR estimates that about 30 percent of women experience chronic low desire; about half of these report significant distress related to the partner, and about a quarter personal distress. Prevalence varies with age, culture, the duration of symptoms and above all whether distress is required: when it is, the estimates are markedly lower. Low desire becomes more common with age, but distress about it becomes less common. Reported rates of low desire range from 26 to 43 percent across world regions, and DSM-5-TR warns that such differences may partly reflect the measures used and how acceptable it is, in a given culture, for an unmarried, menopausal or widowed woman to report sexual activity at all; a woman's level of desire has to be judged against the norms of her own cultural group. DSM-5-TR finds no evidence that rates or expressions of the disorder differ between heterosexual and lesbian women.

Risk and course. The risk factors DSM-5-TR names fall into the usual three groups. Temperamental: negative thoughts and attitudes about sexuality, a past history of mental disorder, and differences in the propensity for sexual excitation and inhibition. Environmental: relationship difficulties, the partner's own sexual functioning, and the woman's developmental history, including early relationships with caregivers and childhood stress. Genetic and physiological: medical conditions such as diabetes and thyroid dysfunction, and what DSM-5-TR calls "a strong influence of genetic factors on vulnerability to sexual problems in women". Women in longer relationships more often begin sex without feeling desire at the outset, and vaginal dryness in older women is related to age and the menopause; both are normal changes, not symptoms. The disorder commonly travels with other problems: difficulty with orgasm, pain during sex, depression, anxiety, thyroid disease, urinary incontinence, alcohol use and a history of sexual or physical abuse in adulthood.

Differential diagnosis. The main alternatives follow the hierarchy of Section 9.3.1. Major depressive disorder, with its "markedly diminished interest or pleasure in all, or almost all, activities", may explain the lack of sexual interest, in which case only depression is diagnosed; this is why the clinician checked Kamini's mood, sleep and enjoyment of other things. A drug that coincides with the onset (antidepressants, antipsychotics and hormonal contraceptives are among those DSM-5-TR names) points to the substance-induced disorder (Section 9.4.5). Diabetes, thyroid disease, endothelial disease and disease of the central nervous system can explain it medically. Severe relationship distress or violence takes the case out of the sexual dysfunctions. Other sexual dysfunctions, by contrast, do not rule it out: chronic genital pain can lead to a lack of desire for painful sex, and lack of arousal can impair orgasm, so two or three diagnoses are common. Inadequate stimulation and transient, adaptive changes after a major life event complete the list.

The ICD codes. ICD-10 recorded low desire as F52.0, lack or loss of sexual desire, and failure of lubrication as F52.2, failure of genital response. ICD-11 did not follow DSM-5's merger: in its Chapter 17, HA00 hypoactive sexual desire dysfunction (which can be given to either sex) and HA01.0 female sexual arousal dysfunction remain separate. A woman with Kamini's symptoms could therefore receive one DSM-5-TR diagnosis but, if both desire and arousal were affected, two ICD-11 codes.

Treatment. Psychological treatment comes first, and its methods belong to Book 2: sex therapy built on education, sensate focus and communication (Book 2, Section 7.9), cognitive-behavioural and mindfulness-based approaches that train attention back to sensation, and couple therapy where the relationship is part of the problem (Book 2, Section 7.8). Barlow and Durand report that roughly 50 to 70 percent of people with low sexual desire benefit from sex therapy, at least initially. Physical causes are treated directly: depression is treated, any drug that may be responsible is reviewed (an SSRI might be switched to an antidepressant with fewer sexual side effects, such as bupropion, Section 9.4.5), and vaginal dryness after the menopause is treated with lubricants or local (vaginal) oestrogen.

Two drugs have been approved in the United States specifically for low desire in women, and both act on the brain rather than on blood flow. Flibanserin (Addyi) was approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) on 18 August 2015 for premenopausal women with acquired, generalised hypoactive sexual desire disorder (the label kept the old DSM-IV name) not caused by a medical or psychiatric condition, relationship problems or a drug. Originally developed as an antidepressant, it stimulates serotonin 5-HT1A receptors and blocks 5-HT2A receptors, which is thought to reduce serotonin's braking effect on sexual response and free dopamine and noradrenaline in the frontal cortex. It is taken every day, at bedtime, and women who notice no improvement after eight weeks are advised to stop. Its benefit is modest: in the trials, women taking it had about half to one more satisfying sexual event a month than women taking placebo. Its main danger is severe low blood pressure and fainting, especially with alcohol, which earned it a boxed warning; in 2019 the FDA revised that warning so that, instead of avoiding alcohol altogether, women should wait at least 2 hours after one or two standard drinks before the bedtime dose, and skip the dose after three or more. Its approval came only after two earlier rejections, in 2010 and 2013, and after a vigorous advocacy campaign. Bremelanotide (Vyleesi), approved by the FDA on 21 June 2019 for the same group of women, works differently: it stimulates several melanocortin receptors in the brain (its label names the MC1 and MC4 subtypes as the most relevant at the doses used), although exactly how this raises desire is not known. It is injected under the skin of the abdomen or thigh at least 45 minutes before expected sexual activity, no more than once in 24 hours and no more than eight times a month, and its commonest side effect is nausea, reported by about 40 percent of women in the trials, with flushing and headache. Neither drug is approved for postmenopausal women or for men.

Two other physical treatments are often asked about. Testosterone has been tried for low desire in postmenopausal women, and some studies find a benefit, but the evidence is mixed (Section 9.2.4) and no testosterone product is approved by the FDA for women. Sildenafil, which works so well for erections, was disappointing in women: in Barlow and Durand's account, one study found some improvement in postmenopausal women with arousal problems, but only in those whose desire was not reduced. The reason follows from Sections 9.2.4 and 9.2.7: in most women with these complaints, blood flow to the genitals is not what is missing.

Clinical link: a pill for desire?

Flibanserin was quickly nicknamed "the female Viagra", a label that is wrong twice over: sildenafil acts on blood vessels in the penis and works only once sexual stimulation is under way, whereas flibanserin is taken every day and acts on brain chemistry in the hope of creating desire. The debate around its approval revived an older critique. In 2000 the psychologist Leonore Tiefer and colleagues launched the "New View" campaign with a manifesto arguing that the DSM's phase model, based on Masters and Johnson's laboratory, was built around genital performance and fitted men better than women, and that women's sexual problems often arise from relationships, culture, money and power rather than from a faulty body; the collection A New View of Women's Sexual Problems followed in 2001. The lesson for a clinician is not that drugs are never useful, but that for a woman like Kamini the first questions are about exhaustion, privacy, the relationship and the kind of touch she receives.

Exam trap

Female sexual interest/arousal disorder needs 3 of 6 indicators, lasting about 6 months, with distress; the 75 to 100 percent rule applies only to the two indicators about pleasure and sensations during sex. It replaced DSM-IV's hypoactive sexual desire disorder (for women) and female sexual arousal disorder.
