A desire discrepancy is not enough, an asexual identity excludes the diagnosis, and self-report of reduced sensation is sufficient. ICD-11 keeps desire (HA00) and female arousal (HA01.0) separate. Flibanserin (2015) and bremelanotide (2019) act on the brain; sildenafil acts on blood vessels and was disappointing in women.

Male hypoactive sexual desire disorder

Girish, 44, manages a bank branch in Lucknow. It is his wife who brings him, and she does most of the talking at first. For about a year, she says, he has shown no interest in sex at all; he never approaches her, turns away when she approaches him, and seems not to miss it. When the clinician sees Girish alone, he agrees. He rarely has a sexual thought or fantasy now, has no wish for sex, and feels guilty that his wife is unhappy. It began, he thinks, after he was passed over for promotion and moved to a smaller branch, which he still finds humiliating. He wakes most mornings with an erection. His blood tests, arranged by his physician, show normal testosterone, prolactin, thyroid function and blood sugar, and he takes no medicines. Is this depression, a relationship problem, a reaction to stress, or a sexual dysfunction? The criteria are designed to answer exactly that question.

The criteria. Male hypoactive sexual desire disorder (DSM-5-TR code F52.0) is persistently or recurrently deficient or absent sexual or erotic thoughts or fantasies and desire for sexual activity (Criterion A). Both halves are required, a point DSM-5-TR makes explicitly, so a man who still has a rich fantasy life but no wish for sex with his partner does not meet it. The judgement of "deficient" is the clinician's, made in the light of factors that affect sexual functioning, such as the man's age and the general and sociocultural context of his life: a man of 75 is not expected to have the desire he had at 25. The symptoms must have lasted a minimum of approximately 6 months (Criterion B), must cause clinically significant distress (Criterion C), and must not be better explained by a nonsexual mental disorder, by severe relationship distress or other significant stressors, or by a substance, medication or medical condition (Criterion D). The specifiers are the usual ones; there is no percentage of occasions (Table 9.6). DSM-5-TR gives an unusually clear reason for the duration rule: it is "meant to safeguard against making a diagnosis in cases in which a man's low sexual desire may represent a reactive but temporary response to adverse life conditions", and its examples are being fired from a job or a business failure. If the stressor and the low desire both persist beyond 6 months, the clinician must judge whether the diagnosis is appropriate.

Several features of the text shape how the criteria are applied. As with women, a desire discrepancy, in which a man wants sex less than his partner does, is not sufficient. Many men with the disorder no longer initiate sex and are minimally receptive when their partner initiates, but because some men prefer their partner to take the lead, a lack of receptivity matters as much as a lack of initiation. Sexual activity, partnered sex or masturbation, may still occur despite low desire. And if the man's lifelong low desire is better explained by his identification as asexual, no diagnosis is made.

How common. Estimates in representative samples range from 3 to 17 percent, depending on the country and the method. Desire problems are less common in younger men (3 to 14 percent at ages 16 to 24) than in older men (16 to 28 percent at ages 60 to 74). A persistent lack of interest lasting 6 months or more affects a smaller proportion, about 6 percent, and fewer than 2 percent of men report clinically significant distress about low desire, a clear illustration of how much the distress criterion narrows the field. Desire declines normally with age: in one set of figures DSM-5-TR cites, low desire rises from about 5.2 percent at age 27 to 18.5 percent at age 50. Across world regions, rates in men aged 40 to 80 range from 12.5 percent in Northern Europe to 28 percent in South-East Asia, which is the only figure for Asia that DSM-5-TR gives in this chapter. Low desire is also reported more often by gay men (19 percent) than by heterosexual men (9 percent).

Risk. Mood and anxiety symptoms are strong predictors: up to half of men with a past history of psychiatric symptoms may have moderate or severe loss of desire, against only 15 percent of men without such a history. A man's feelings about himself, his sense of whether his partner desires him and his feeling of emotional connection all affect his desire, in either direction. Restrictive sexual attitudes and conservative beliefs are commonly associated with low desire, and so are a lack of erotic thoughts, worries about erection during sex and low confidence in erectile function. Environmental factors include alcohol use, a troubled relationship, reduced attraction to the partner, living in a long-term relationship, sexual boredom and work stress. Among physical factors, the endocrine disorders hyperprolactinaemia and hypogonadism (underactive testes with low testosterone) significantly reduce desire, and age is itself a risk factor. Whether men with low desire in general have abnormally low testosterone is unclear; DSM-5-TR suggests that there may be "a critical threshold" below which testosterone affects desire and above which it has little effect, exactly the pattern of Grunt and Young's guinea pigs (Section 9.2.4).

Men are not women. DSM-5-TR states plainly that, in contrast to women, "desire and arousal disorders have been retained as separate constructs in men". Men on average report a higher intensity and frequency of desire than women, although desire and erectile function also overlap a great deal in men who seek help: persistent erectile failure can lead a man to lose interest in sex, and DSM-5-TR reports evidence that up to half of men with low desire also have erectile difficulties. The disorder is rarely the only sexual diagnosis: erectile disorder, delayed ejaculation and premature ejaculation are often present too, along with depression and endocrine problems. The presence of another dysfunction does not rule the diagnosis out.

Differential diagnosis. The alternatives are the hierarchy of Section 9.3.1 applied to desire. Major depressive disorder, with its markedly diminished interest or pleasure in almost all activities, may explain the low desire, in which case only depression is diagnosed. A drug whose start coincides with the loss of desire points to the substance-induced disorder: SSRIs, antipsychotics that raise prolactin, opioids, finasteride and others (Section 9.4.5). Hypogonadism, diabetes, thyroid dysfunction and disease of the central nervous system can explain it medically. Severe relationship distress or other major stressors can explain it too. ICD-10 recorded the disorder as F52.0, lack or loss of sexual desire; ICD-11 uses HA00, hypoactive sexual desire dysfunction, the same code it gives to women.

Back to Girish. His morning erections show that his erectile machinery works, and his normal blood tests make hypogonadism, hyperprolactinaemia, thyroid disease and diabetes unlikely. The clinician's next task is to look for depression, carefully, because a humiliating setback at work is a classic trigger: does he still enjoy anything, how is his sleep and appetite, does he feel worthless, has he thought of death? If he meets the criteria for a major depressive episode, the diagnosis is depression and the low desire is one of its symptoms. If not, the clinician asks about the marriage: is there an old conflict that the demotion has exposed, and would the loss of desire be better explained by severe relationship distress? If neither depression nor a severe relationship problem explains the picture, and the low desire has lasted more than 6 months, distresses him and has persisted although the acute stress has passed, he meets the criteria for male hypoactive sexual desire disorder, acquired and probably generalised.

Treatment. No drug is approved for low desire in men; flibanserin and bremelanotide are licensed only for premenopausal women. Treatment therefore means treating the cause. Testosterone replacement is given only when hypogonadism has been confirmed by blood tests, because, as Pinel stresses, extra testosterone does not raise desire in men whose levels are normal (Section 9.2.4). A raised prolactin is investigated for a pituitary tumour (a prolactinoma) and treated, usually with a dopamine agonist such as cabergoline, which mimics dopamine's natural brake on prolactin release. Drugs that may be responsible are reviewed with the prescriber, depression is treated, and the psychological work is couple-based: sex therapy, communication and the cognitive work on beliefs and performance worries described in Book 2, Section 7.9.

Exam trap

Male hypoactive sexual desire disorder needs both deficient sexual thoughts or fantasies and deficient desire for sexual activity, judged against the man's age and context, for about 6 months, with distress; there is no occasions percentage. The 6-month rule exists to exclude a temporary reaction to adversity, such as losing a job. In men, desire and arousal remain separate disorders. Fewer than 2 percent of men report distressing low desire. Testosterone helps only men with proven hypogonadism. An asexual identity excludes the diagnosis.

Erectile disorder

Two men sit in the same outpatient waiting room on the same morning with what looks like the same complaint. Hemant, 56, runs a hardware shop in Indore. He has had diabetes for twelve years and high blood pressure for eight, and he admits that he rarely checks his sugar and often forgets his tablets; he also smokes. Over the past three years his erections have weakened steadily, and now he cannot obtain a firm erection with his wife, on his own, or on waking; he cannot remember when he last woke with one. Aniruddh, 29, is a software engineer in Bengaluru. Eight months ago, on the first night he spent with his new partner, Pallavi, after a party at which he had drunk a good deal, he lost his erection. Since then it has happened every time they have tried, drunk or sober; he lies awake beforehand rehearsing the failure, and Pallavi has begun to wonder aloud whether he still finds her attractive. Yet he wakes most mornings with a firm erection and has normal erections when he masturbates. Both men are distressed, and both meet the criteria for the same disorder. By the end of this subsection you should be able to say why their causes, their tests and their treatment are entirely different.

The criteria. Erectile disorder (DSM-5-TR code F52.21) requires at least one of three symptoms, experienced on almost all or all (approximately 75 to 100 percent) occasions of sexual activity: (1) marked difficulty in obtaining an erection during sexual activity; (2) marked difficulty in maintaining an erection until the completion of sexual activity; or (3) a marked decrease in erectile rigidity (Criterion A). The three options exist because men fail in different ways: some never become firm, some become firm and lose it, and some, like many older men, have erections that are present but too soft for penetration. The symptoms must have lasted approximately 6 months (Criterion B), must cause clinically significant distress (Criterion C), and must not be better explained by a nonsexual mental disorder, severe relationship distress or other significant stressors, or be attributable to a substance, medication or another medical condition (Criterion D). The specifiers are lifelong or acquired, generalised or situational, and mild, moderate or severe distress. In the language of Section 9.3.1, Hemant's disorder is acquired and generalised and Aniruddh's is acquired and situational.

Words. DSM-5-TR is careful about terms, and the care is examinable. Erectile dysfunction is the widely used descriptive term, used in ICD-10 and in medicine generally, for difficulty getting and keeping an erection. Erectile disorder is the narrower DSM diagnostic category in which erectile dysfunction has persisted for at least about 6 months and causes distress. The older word impotence (and its female counterpart, "frigidity") has been abandoned: Barlow and Durand call both old and somewhat derogatory, and imprecise because they do not identify which phase of the response has failed. DSM-IV's name, "male erectile disorder", lost its first word in DSM-5.

What it is like. DSM-5-TR describes men with erectile disorder as often having low self-esteem and self-confidence, a decreased sense of masculinity and depressed mood, with feelings of guilt, self-blame, failure and anger and a fear of disappointing their partner; fear and avoidance of sexual encounters may follow, and partners commonly report less sexual satisfaction and less desire of their own. Aniruddh's rehearsing of failure and Pallavi's question about attraction are both typical. DSM-5-TR lists the same five factors as for every dysfunction, with the medical ones spelt out (surgery such as transurethral resection of the prostate, hypogonadism, and neurological conditions such as multiple sclerosis and diabetic neuropathy), and adds a sixth: the use of substances or medications.

How common. Erectile problems rise steeply with age, particularly after 50. Internationally, DSM-5-TR estimates that erectile disorder affects about 13 to 21 percent of men aged 40 to 80: under 10 percent of men younger than 40, about 20 to 40 percent of men in their 60s and 50 to 75 percent of men over 70; in one Australian longitudinal study, 80 percent of men aged 70 and over had the disorder. The best-known study of ageing men, the Massachusetts Male Aging Study of 1,290 men aged 40 to 70 (Feldman and colleagues, 1994), found at least some degree of erectile impairment in 52 percent of the men overall, in about 40 percent of men at age 40 and in nearly 70 percent at age 70, and complete erectile failure tripled from 5 to 15 percent between those ages. The contrast with Laumann's 5 percent (Table 9.7) is mostly a matter of age and definition: Laumann's sample stopped at 59 and counted only clear problems, whereas the Massachusetts figures include mild impairment. At the other end of life, about 20 percent of young men in mostly Western studies feared erectile problems on their first sexual experience, and about 8 percent had problems that prevented penetration. Erectile problems are the commonest reason men seek sexual help: Barlow and Durand report that they account for half or more of the men referred to specialists for sexual problems.

Course. Failure on the first attempt at intercourse is common and is linked to having sex with an unfamiliar partner, drugs or alcohol, not really wanting sex, and peer pressure; most such problems are thought to pass without treatment. Acquired erectile disorder, by contrast, is often associated with biological factors such as diabetes and cardiovascular disease and is likely to persist in most men. Lifelong erectile disorder is linked in clinical experience to psychological factors that are self-limiting or respond to psychological treatment. Distress about erectile problems is lower in older men than in younger ones.

The medical links. Erectile disorder has the strongest physical ties of all the sexual dysfunctions, because an erection depends on healthy nerves, healthy blood vessels and adequate hormones all at once (Section 9.2.4). DSM-5-TR names age, smoking, lack of physical exercise, diabetes and decreased desire as risk factors for the acquired form. Diabetes is the classic physical cause, because it damages both the small nerves (neuropathy) and the blood vessels (vascular disease) on which erection depends; in the Massachusetts study, 28 percent of men with diabetes had complete erectile failure. Vascular disease works in two ways: arterial insufficiency, in which narrowed arteries cannot deliver enough blood, and venous leakage, in which blood drains away too quickly for an erection to be maintained, a failure of the veno-occlusion described in Section 9.2.4. Smoking is a risk in its own right: in a study of more than 4,000 male army veterans, cigarette smoking alone was associated with increased erectile dysfunction even after alcohol and vascular disease were allowed for (Mannino, Klevens and Flanders, 1994). Other medical causes include prostate surgery and radiotherapy, lower urinary tract symptoms from an enlarged prostate, multiple sclerosis, spinal cord injury, hypogonadism, kidney disease and obstructive sleep apnoea, and many drugs: antihypertensives (Barlow and Durand single out the beta-blockers such as propranolol), antidepressants, antipsychotics, alcohol, nicotine and cocaine (Section 9.4.5).

One medical link turns erectile disorder into a warning light. One widely cited explanation, the "artery size hypothesis" of the Italian cardiologist Piero Montorsi and his colleagues, is that the arteries of the penis are narrower than those of the heart, so the same amount of atherosclerotic plaque obstructs them sooner and the problem shows itself there first. Whatever the mechanism, DSM-5-TR states that erectile disorder in men aged 40 and over is predictive of future risk for coronary artery disease, which is why serum lipids are checked, and Barlow and Durand note the recommendation that men presenting with erectile dysfunction be screened for cardiovascular disease. A man like Hemant who comes for a sexual problem may be telling his doctor, without knowing it, that his heart is at risk.

Clinical link: erectile disorder and depression

DSM-5-TR reports that the risk of depression is significantly higher in men with erectile disorder, and markedly higher in the first year after onset. Among men treated for erectile disorder who were also depressed, raised rates of suicidal thoughts and behaviour have been observed; the men themselves blamed the erectile problem, though depression was probably also contributing. Elevated suicide rates in men with prostate cancer may be related in part to the erectile dysfunction caused by its treatment and the depression that follows. A clinician who sees a man for erectile problems should therefore ask about mood and suicidal thoughts, and a clinician treating a depressed man should ask about sexual function (suicide risk assessment is taught in Book 3, Chapter 5).

Organic or psychogenic? The most difficult part of the diagnosis, DSM-5-TR says, is ruling out erectile problems that are fully explained by medical factors, because such cases receive no mental disorder diagnosis at all; and the distinction is usually unclear, because many cases have mixed biological and psychological causes. A problem is called organic when a physical cause (vascular, neurological, hormonal or drug-related) is responsible and psychogenic when psychological factors are. DSM-5-TR gives the clues, and Table 9.8 arranges them. Erectile dysfunction due to organic factors is generally generalised and gradual in onset, with one exception: a sudden onset after injury to the nerves of the genitals, as in spinal cord injury. Problems that are situational and inconsistent and that begin suddenly after a stressful life event are most often psychological, and an age under 40 also points to a psychological cause. Normal erections in sleep or on waking, or during masturbation, show that the physical machinery works. If the man is older than 40 to 50 or has other medical problems, medical causes, especially vascular disease, must be considered. And one warning cuts across the whole table: "the presence of an organic disease known to cause erectile problems does not confirm a causal relationship". A man with diabetes can develop erectile disorder in response to psychological stress, so a diagnosis of diabetes does not end the assessment.

Table 9.8 Clues that point to an organic or a psychogenic cause of erectile problems (after DSM-5-TR). Most cases are mixed; the clues weigh probabilities, they do not decide.
CluePoints to an organic causePoints to a psychogenic cause
OnsetGradual, over months or yearsSudden, often after a stressful event or a first failure
PatternGeneralised: with every partner, alone and in every situationSituational and inconsistent: fails with a partner, works alone or with another partner
Erections in sleep and on wakingAbsent or weakPresent and firm (normal nocturnal penile tumescence)
AgeUsually over 40 to 50Usually under 40
HistoryDiabetes, high blood pressure, heart disease, smoking, high lipids, pelvic surgery or radiotherapy, nerve disease, a new drugAnxiety, depression, relationship conflict, a new partner, performance worries, myths
CautionNerve injury (spinal cord, surgery) can cause a sudden onsetA known disease does not prove the cause: a man with diabetes can fail from anxiety

Push the two men through the table. Hemant's problem began gradually, is generalised (it occurs with his wife, alone and on waking), and comes with uncontrolled diabetes, high blood pressure, smoking and an age over 50: every clue in the middle column. The likeliest cause is the combined vascular and nerve damage of diabetes and hypertension; his blood pressure tablets, if they are of a type that impairs erection, may be contributing. If the problem is fully explained by his medical condition, DSM-5-TR would give him no psychiatric diagnosis; in ICD-11 he would receive HA01.1 male erectile dysfunction with the qualifier HA40.0, associated with a medical condition. His workup includes fasting glucose, lipids, testosterone and a cardiovascular assessment. Aniruddh's problem began suddenly after a failure with a new partner and alcohol, is situational (it occurs only with Pallavi), spares his morning erections and masturbation, and he is under 40: every clue in the right-hand column. He is the young man of Section 9.2.6 made real, and Figure 9.5 shows exactly what keeps his problem going. He meets the criteria for erectile disorder, acquired and situational.

Culture and sex. Rates of erectile disorder vary across countries, and DSM-5-TR is unsure how much of the variation reflects genuine differences and how much reflects how willing men are to report it. Cultural concern about appearing weak or less masculine, differing norms about whether erections should be expected to change with healthy ageing, and expectations about marriage, sexual performance, fertility and gender roles can all shape the anxieties that contribute to the disorder. DSM-5-TR's only regional details come from an online survey: concern about genital size in the United States and the Middle East, and fear of male infertility more often in the Middle East. It reports no Indian figure, and none is given here, although the pressures it describes (to perform on the wedding night, to produce a child soon after marriage) will be familiar from Book 2, Section 7.9.7. By definition the diagnosis is given only to men; distressing arousal difficulties in women belong to female sexual interest/arousal disorder.

Differential diagnosis and comorbidity. Major depressive disorder and erectile disorder are closely associated; if the erectile problem is better explained by depression, erectile disorder is not diagnosed. A man with excessive expectations may in fact have normal erectile function, so the differential includes normal function judged against an unrealistic standard (an older man who expects the instant erections of his twenties, for instance). An onset that coincides with starting a drug and clears when it is stopped points to substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction. And a medical cause must be ruled out, as above. Erectile disorder often occurs with premature ejaculation and male hypoactive sexual desire disorder, with anxiety and depressive disorders, with posttraumatic stress disorder, with lower urinary tract symptoms of prostatic enlargement, and with dyslipidaemia, cardiovascular disease, hypogonadism, multiple sclerosis and diabetes. ICD-10 coded the nonorganic form as F52.2, failure of genital response; ICD-11 codes it as HA01.1, male erectile dysfunction, whatever the cause, with an HA40 qualifier to record the cause.

Treatment: the drugs. Erectile disorder is the sexual dysfunction that drugs transformed. Sildenafil (Viagra) was approved by the FDA on 27 March 1998, and three more phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors followed: vardenafil (Levitra) on 19 August 2003, tadalafil (Cialis) on 21 November 2003 and avanafil (Stendra) on 27 April 2012. Section 9.2.4 explained how they work: they block PDE-5, the enzyme that destroys cGMP, so that the nitric oxide signal produced by sexual stimulation lasts longer and the erection is easier to obtain and keep. They do not create an erection without stimulation and they do not increase desire. They differ mainly in how long they last; tadalafil acts for up to about 36 hours, which frees couples from having to time sex around a tablet. In the clinical trials Barlow and Durand summarise, 50 to 80 percent of men had erections sufficient for intercourse on the drug, against about 30 percent on placebo. Common side effects follow from the presence of PDE-5 in other blood vessels: headache (reported by up to 30 percent of men in some studies, particularly at higher doses), flushing, indigestion and a stuffy nose, and, with sildenafil, a transient blue tinge to vision. The one absolute rule is never to combine them with nitrates: a nitrate should not be given within 24 hours of sildenafil, or within 48 hours of tadalafil, because of the risk of a dangerous fall in blood pressure. Generic sildenafil and tadalafil are cheap and widely sold in India, so many men try them before they see a doctor; the clinician's task is then to make sure the warning sign for heart disease has not been missed and that no nitrate is being taken.

Restoring an erection is not the same as restoring a sex life. When Virag (1999) followed a large group of men treated with sildenafil, 61 percent had erections firm enough for intercourse, but only 32 percent rated the result good (an adequate erection and satisfaction of at least 7 on a 0 to 10 scale); 29 percent rated it fair and 39 percent unsatisfactory. Results are poorer in men who are particularly anxious about sex, and the great majority of men stop using the drug after a few months or a year. Bach, Barlow and Wincze (2004) therefore added cognitive-behavioural treatment to sildenafil, and couples reported greater satisfaction and more sexual activity with the combination than with the drug alone. The lesson echoes Barlow's model: a tablet can repair the hydraulics, but if attention stays fixed on the threat of failure, or the relationship is strained, the problem returns.

Treatment: when the tablets fail or cannot be used. For men who do not respond to PDE-5 inhibitors or cannot take them, there are second-line physical treatments, summarised in Table 9.9. A man can learn to inject a drug that relaxes smooth muscle, such as papaverine or alprostadil (a prostaglandin, approved by the FDA for erectile dysfunction as Caverject on 6 July 1995), directly into the erectile tissue shortly before sex; because the drug acts on the muscle itself, it works even when nerves are damaged, producing an erection within about 15 minutes that lasts from 1 to 4 hours. Injections are somewhat painful and artificial, and half or more of men give them up after a short time; a prolonged, painful erection (priapism) is a rare emergency. Alprostadil can also be inserted into the urethra as a small pellet (MUSE, approved by the FDA on 19 November 1996), which avoids the needle but is less effective and is uncomfortable for some men. A vacuum erection device is a cylinder placed over the penis; a pump creates a vacuum that draws blood in, and a ring slipped onto the base of the penis traps it; 70 to 100 percent of users report satisfactory erections, although many find the device awkward. Finally, a penile prosthesis can be implanted surgically: either semi-rigid rods that are bent into position, or inflatable cylinders filled from a small pump implanted in the scrotum. Implants fall short of restoring earlier sexual function or guaranteeing satisfaction in most men, so they are now generally used only when other treatments have failed; they have been particularly useful for men whose prostate has been removed for cancer. Testosterone has only a negligible effect on erectile dysfunction in men with normal levels (Mann and colleagues, 1996) and is given only for proven hypogonadism, where it can also restore desire. For every man with an organic cause, the treatment also includes the cause itself: better control of diabetes and blood pressure, a change of antihypertensive drug if the present one impairs erection, stopping smoking, exercise and attention to lipids.

Table 9.9 Treatments for erectile disorder. Approval dates are those of the US Food and Drug Administration. The psychological methods are taught in Book 2, Section 7.9.

Treatment: the psychological methods. These are taught in Book 2 and only their evidence is given here. Sensate focus and couple sex therapy (Book 2, Sections 7.9.2 and 7.9.3) remove the demand to perform that drives the negative loop of Figure 9.5. Masters and Johnson reported success close to 60 percent for the hardest cases, lifelong generalised erectile dysfunction, and later studies of sex therapy for erectile dysfunction report positive outcomes in as many as 60 to 70 percent of cases for at least several years, with some slipping afterwards. For Aniruddh, the plan is psychological: education about the effect of alcohol and anxiety on erection, a temporary ban on attempts at intercourse with graded sensate focus exercises with Pallavi, and cognitive work on his rehearsals of failure; a short course of a PDE-5 inhibitor can be added to give him a few successes that break the loop, provided the psychological work continues. For Hemant, the plan is medical first, with a PDE-5 inhibitor once a cardiovascular assessment has shown that sexual activity is safe and that he takes no nitrates, better control of his diabetes and blood pressure, and an invitation to his wife to take part, because their sexual life together, not only his erection, is what needs restoring.

Exam trap

Diabetes and vascular disease are the classic physical links of erectile disorder, not of premature ejaculation (which is most commonly associated with an anxiety state, Section 9.4.3). Erectile disorder needs 1 of 3 symptoms (obtaining, maintaining, rigidity) on 75 to 100 percent of occasions for about 6 months. Normal morning or nocturnal erections point to a psychological cause; a gradual, generalised problem after 50 points to an organic one, but a known disease does not prove the cause. Erectile disorder in men over 40 predicts coronary artery disease. Sildenafil needs sexual stimulation, does not raise desire and must never be combined with nitrates. The stop-start and squeeze techniques are for premature ejaculation, not for erectile disorder; sensate focus is the core psychological method for erectile disorder. "Impotence" is the rejected old term; "erectile dysfunction" is the descriptive term; "erectile disorder" is the DSM diagnosis.

Scan Your Brain

Answers: (1) resolution; desire, (2) neutrality; responsive, (3) parasympathetic; phosphodiesterase type 5 (PDE-5), (4) increased; interference, (5) inhibition, (6) 75 to 100; 6, (7) male hypoactive sexual desire disorder, (8) 3; 6, (9) 43; 31, (10) nocturnal penile tumescence; psychological (psychogenic); diabetes (and vascular disease).

9.4 Sexual Dysfunctions II: Orgasm, Ejaculation, Pain and Substance-Induced DysfunctionMedium yield · 2 Qs

Section 9.3 dealt with the first half of the sexual response: whether a person wants sex and whether the body becomes aroused. This section takes the second half and the problems that sit outside the cycle altogether. Three of the disorders concern the climax of the response, and they divide neatly by timing. In female orgasmic disorder and delayed ejaculation the climax comes too late or not at all; in premature (early) ejaculation it comes too soon. A fourth disorder, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder, is not a failure of any phase but a problem of pain, fear and muscular tension that makes penetration difficult or impossible. The fifth, substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction, can strike at any phase, and it matters to every clinician who sees a client on an antidepressant or an antipsychotic, because it is one of the commonest reasons people stop taking their tablets. The section ends by gathering every treatment for every sexual dysfunction into one table, so that you can see which psychological methods from Book 2 and which physical treatments from this book belong to which problem.

Keep two things from Section 9.3.1 in mind as you read. First, every disorder here is diagnosed against the shared template: a problem that is persistent (usually about 6 months), frequent (usually on about 75 to 100 percent of occasions), distressing, and not better explained by another mental disorder, by severe relationship distress or partner violence, by a drug, or by a medical illness. Second, the template has exceptions, and several of them live in this section: premature ejaculation is graded by the clock rather than by distress, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder has no percentage of occasions and no generalised or situational specifier, and the substance-induced disorder has no 6-month rule at all. Examiners like exceptions, so they are flagged as we go.

Female orgasmic disorder

Namrata is 31, a pharmacist in Nagpur, married for six years to a man she describes as kind and patient. She comes to a women's health clinic with a question she has rehearsed for months and still cannot say directly: she has never had an orgasm, not with her husband and not alone. She enjoys closeness and sometimes feels aroused, but the feeling builds a little and then fades, "like a song that stops before the chorus". She grew up in a strict household where sex was never mentioned except as a danger, and she remembers an aunt telling her that pleasure "was for men, not for good girls". She has never touched herself deliberately; she has never told her husband what she likes because she does not know. For a year she has felt that something is wrong with her, and lately she has started avoiding intimacy so that the disappointment will not show.

Namrata's problem is the one DSM-5-TR calls female orgasmic disorder. Its first criterion offers two routes. Either there is a marked delay in, marked infrequency of, or absence of orgasm, or there is markedly reduced intensity of orgasmic sensations: some women reach orgasm but feel it only faintly, and DSM-5-TR counts that as a dysfunction too. Either symptom must occur on almost all or all occasions of sexual activity (approximately 75 to 100 percent), in the situations where it occurs or, if it is generalised, in all of them. It must have lasted approximately 6 months, it must cause clinically significant distress, and it must not be better explained by a nonsexual mental disorder, by severe relationship distress (DSM-5-TR names partner violence as its example) or other significant stressors, by a substance or medication, or by another medical condition. The specifiers are the usual pairs, lifelong or acquired and generalised or situational, and severity is rated by the degree of distress, mild, moderate or severe. There is also one specifier found nowhere else in the chapter: never experienced an orgasm under any situation. Namrata would carry it. It matters for treatment, because a woman who has never had an orgasm needs to learn how her body responds, whereas a woman who has lost a response she once had needs the clinician to find out what changed.

Why does each rule exist? The 6-month and 75 to 100 percent rules separate a disorder from the ordinary variability of women's orgasm, which is far greater than men's. DSM-5-TR says that women show wide variability in the type and intensity of stimulation that brings orgasm, and that their descriptions of orgasm vary so much that it seems to be experienced in very different ways by different women and by the same woman on different occasions. The word "approximately" in the duration rule is deliberate: DSM-5-TR says it allows clinical judgement when a problem has not quite lasted 6 months. The distress rule exists because many women with infrequent orgasm are not troubled by it. DSM-5-TR is blunt about this: overall sexual satisfaction is not strongly correlated with orgasm, and many women report high satisfaction while rarely or never reaching orgasm. A difficulty that does not distress the woman who has it is a variation, not a disorder.

Two further rules prevent false diagnoses, and both are favourite exam material. The first concerns intercourse. Many women need direct clitoral stimulation to reach orgasm, and only a relatively small proportion always reach orgasm during penile-vaginal intercourse. Barlow and Durand put a figure on it: only about 20 percent of women reliably have orgasms during intercourse (Graham, 2010; Lloyd, 2005). So DSM-5-TR states plainly that a woman who reaches orgasm through clitoral stimulation but not during intercourse does not meet the criteria. Her pattern is the commonest pattern there is. The second rule concerns stimulation. If the problem is the result of inadequate sexual stimulation (a partner who hurries, a couple who do not know what works), no diagnosis of female orgasmic disorder is made, although DSM-5-TR adds that care may still be needed. In Namrata's case the clinician must ask whether she has ever had the kind of stimulation that would bring most women to orgasm. If the answer is no, the first task is education, and the diagnosis waits.

How common is the problem? The honest answer is that it depends on how you ask. DSM-5-TR reports that orgasm problems in premenopausal women range from 8 to 72 percent across studies, depending on age, culture, duration and severity, and warns that these figures ignore distress, so they count problems rather than disorders. About 10 percent of women internationally never experience orgasm in their lifetime. Barlow and Durand estimate that about 25 percent of women report significant difficulty reaching orgasm, that the problem is spread evenly across age groups, and that unmarried women were 1.5 times more likely to report it than married women. An inability to reach orgasm is, they add, the commonest complaint among women who seek therapy for sexual problems. DSM-5-TR also notes that reports of inability to reach orgasm vary over a twofold range across world regions, a reminder of how much culture shapes this experience.

The course of orgasm in women differs from men's. A woman's first orgasm can occur at any time from before puberty to well into adulthood; women show a more variable age at first orgasm than men, and the proportion of women who have experienced orgasm rises with age, as they meet a wider variety of stimulation and learn more about their own bodies. Women are also more consistent in reaching orgasm through masturbation than with a partner. All of this is why lifelong orgasmic difficulty in a young woman with little sexual knowledge is often a problem of learning, and why the treatment that works best for it is, at heart, a teaching programme.

The causes span every level of the biopsychosocial model (Book 3 Section 1.3.5). Among psychological factors DSM-5-TR names anxiety and worries about pregnancy, both of which pull attention away from sensation (the mechanism of Section 9.2.5). Environmental factors include relationship problems, physical and mental health, and sociocultural influences such as gender-role expectations and religious norms; DSM-5-TR adds that cultures which undervalue women's sexual satisfaction, or treat marital sex as a woman's duty rather than a pleasure, have lower rates of help-seeking. Namrata's upbringing, with its message that pleasure belongs to men, is a textbook example. Physiological factors include multiple sclerosis, damage to the pelvic nerves from radical hysterectomy, spinal cord injury, vulvovaginal atrophy (thinning and dryness of the vaginal tissues, which makes orgasm difficulties significantly more likely) and, above all in psychiatric practice, the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), which are known to delay or inhibit orgasm in women (Section 9.4.5). Menopausal status itself is not consistently linked to orgasm difficulty, and DSM-5-TR suggests there may be a significant genetic contribution to variation in women's orgasmic function. What has not been supported, it says, is any link between particular personality traits or forms of psychopathology and orgasmic dysfunction: the old psychoanalytic picture of the "frigid" woman with a characteristic personality has not survived the evidence.

The differential diagnosis follows the template. If the difficulty is better explained by another mental disorder, such as major depression, female orgasmic disorder is not diagnosed. If it began with a drug and lifts when the drug is stopped or reduced, the diagnosis is substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction. If it is due to a medical condition such as multiple sclerosis or spinal cord injury, or if severe relationship distress or partner violence explains it, female orgasmic disorder is not diagnosed either. By contrast, the presence of another sexual dysfunction does not rule it out: orgasm difficulties often co-occur with problems of interest and arousal (Section 9.3.4), and both can be diagnosed. In the manuals, DSM-5-TR prints the code F52.31; the WHO's ICD-10 placed orgasm problems of both sexes together under F52.3, orgasmic dysfunction ("orgasm either does not occur or is markedly delayed", including "inhibited orgasm (male) (female)"); and ICD-11 calls the condition anorgasmia, HA02.0, in Chapter 17. DSM-5-TR also notes that female orgasmic disorder is diagnosed only in women; distressing orgasm problems in men are considered under delayed ejaculation.

Treatment is mainly psychological, and it is among the most successful in sex therapy. The method of choice for lifelong orgasmic disorder is directed masturbation, the graded programme of self-exploration and self-stimulation developed by Joseph LoPiccolo and W. Charles Lobitz in 1972 and set out for women themselves in Becoming Orgasmic (1976) by Julia Heiman, Leslie LoPiccolo and Joseph LoPiccolo; once a woman can reach orgasm alone, the learning is carried into the relationship, and the bridge manoeuvre helps carry it into intercourse. Kegel exercises are sometimes added to increase awareness of genital sensation. Book 2 Section 7.9.4 describes each technique step by step. The evidence is strong: summaries reviewed by Barlow and Durand suggest that 70 to 90 percent of women benefit, and that the gains are stable and even improve over time. On the physical side, the clinician looks for anything that blocks the response: an SSRI that might be changed (Section 9.4.5), vaginal atrophy that can be treated, a medical illness. There is no drug approved for female orgasmic disorder. For Namrata, the likely plan is education about anatomy and response, permission to explore her own body without guilt, directed masturbation, and then joint sessions in which she shows her husband what she has learned.

Exam trap

A woman who reaches orgasm with clitoral stimulation but not during intercourse does not have female orgasmic disorder; nor does a woman whose problem is due to inadequate stimulation. Criterion A has two routes: delay, infrequency or absence of orgasm, or markedly reduced intensity. The special specifier is "never experienced an orgasm under any situation". Severity is rated by distress, not by frequency. Personality traits are not an established cause. Codes: DSM-5-TR F52.31; ICD-10 F52.3 orgasmic dysfunction (for both sexes); ICD-11 HA02.0 anorgasmia. Treatment of lifelong cases: directed masturbation (LoPiccolo and Lobitz, 1972), not stop-start or squeeze.

Delayed ejaculation

Waseem is 35, a civil engineer in Hyderabad, and he did not come to see a psychologist. He and his wife came to a fertility clinic after four years of trying for a child, and it was only when the gynaecologist asked a direct question that the problem emerged: in their entire marriage Waseem has never ejaculated during intercourse. He can maintain an erection for a long time, sometimes until both of them are exhausted and sore, but the climax never comes. Alone, using his own hand and a firm, fast rhythm he has used since he was a teenager, he ejaculates without difficulty, and he does so almost every day. He has always been ashamed to say any of this, and his wife has begun to wonder whether he finds her unattractive. The fertility specialist, finding a normal semen sample, refers them on.

Waseem has delayed ejaculation. DSM-5-TR's criteria require either a marked delay in ejaculation or a marked infrequency or absence of ejaculation, experienced on almost all or all (approximately 75 to 100 percent) occasions of partnered sexual activity, and, crucially, without the individual desiring delay. That last phrase separates a problem from a choice: a man who deliberately holds back has nothing to diagnose. The usual rules then follow: approximately 6 months, clinically significant distress, and no better explanation by a nonsexual mental disorder, severe relationship distress or other stressors, a substance or medication, or a medical condition. The specifiers are lifelong or acquired and generalised or situational, and severity is rated by distress. The partnered activity can be manual, oral, coital or anal, so the diagnosis is not limited to intercourse with a woman, although most of the research has studied vaginal intercourse. DSM-5-TR notes that it is common for men with delayed ejaculation to ejaculate easily with self-stimulation but not with a partner, exactly Waseem's pattern, and that in heterosexual couples it is often the female partner's distress that brings the couple to treatment.

The disorder has had several names, and the past papers offer one of them as a distractor, so learn them as a set. DSM-III (1980) and DSM-III-R (1987) called it inhibited male orgasm, the male partner of "inhibited female orgasm". DSM-IV (1994) renamed both, as male orgasmic disorder and female orgasmic disorder. DSM-5 (2013) replaced male orgasmic disorder with delayed ejaculation, which describes what actually happens; older clinical writing also called it retarded ejaculation. Whatever the name, the problem is the opposite of premature ejaculation: a climax that comes too late or never, rather than too soon.

How long is too long? Here DSM-5-TR is candid. There is no consensus about what constitutes a reasonable time to reach orgasm, so "delay" has no precise boundary. Research on heterosexual couples has measured the intravaginal ejaculatory latency time (IELT), the time from vaginal penetration to ejaculation, and found that for most men it lies between about 4 and 10 minutes. Nor is there a clear line between delayed ejaculation as a dysfunction and the slowing that comes with normal ageing. The diagnosis therefore rests on clinical judgement, taking account of the man's age, his medical and sexual history, the relationship and his patterns of stimulation, and DSM-5-TR warns that it should not be given if the clinician judges that the man's dissatisfaction is entirely due to unrealistic expectations.

The clinical picture has some characteristic features. The man and his partner may describe prolonged thrusting to the point of exhaustion, genital discomfort or even injury before they give up, and some men begin to avoid sex altogether. Men with delayed ejaculation report less intercourse, more relationship distress, lower subjective arousal, anxiety about performance and more general health problems than other men. Three habits are associated with the disorder, and Waseem shows all of them: highly frequent masturbation, a masturbation technique that a partner cannot easily reproduce (unusual speed, pressure or method), and a marked gap between the fantasies used in masturbation and the reality of sex with a partner. The logic is simple: a man whose arousal has been trained for years to a very specific kind of stimulation may find that a partner's touch, however loving, simply does not reach the threshold.

DSM-5-TR estimates the prevalence of delayed ejaculation at 1 to 5 percent in the United States and as high as 11 percent in some international studies, though different definitions make the figures hard to compare; one textbook estimate, from the American national survey, is about 8 percent of men (Laumann and colleagues, 1999, reported by Barlow and Durand). Men seldom seek help for it; Barlow and Durand suggest that many couples simply accommodate it, with the man reaching climax through other forms of stimulation. Prevalence rises with age. As men grow older they tend to show reduced ejaculatory volume, force and sensation and a longer refractory time, and age-related loss of fast-conducting sensory nerves and falling sex hormones may contribute.

The causes run from the bedroom to the operating theatre. DSM-5-TR names depression and relationship dissatisfaction as the predominant psychosocial contributors, and notes that delayed ejaculation may be more common in severe major depressive disorder. Medically, anything that disrupts the sympathetic or somatic nerve supply to the genitals can delay or abolish ejaculation (Section 9.2.4 explains that emission depends on sympathetic nerves): radical prostatectomy for cancer, spinal cord injury, stroke, multiple sclerosis, pelvic surgery, severe diabetes (through diabetic neuropathy), epilepsy, hormonal abnormalities such as hypogonadism, sleep apnoea and bowel dysfunction. Drugs are a major cause: alpha-blockers that inhibit the adrenergic nerve supply to the ejaculatory system (tamsulosin, prescribed for an enlarged prostate, is DSM-5-TR's example), antihypertensives, antidepressants (especially the SSRIs), antipsychotics, alcohol, opioids and cannabis. When a drug is responsible, the diagnosis becomes substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction (Section 9.4.5).

The differential diagnosis includes several urological conditions that a psychologist must know by name. In retrograde ejaculation the ejaculatory fluid travels backwards into the bladder instead of forwards out of the penis; the man feels orgasm but little or no semen appears, and Barlow and Durand note that this is almost always caused by drugs or a medical condition. In anejaculation there is no ejaculation at all, usually from hormonal, neurological or anatomical causes, and an ejaculatory duct obstruction blocks the outflow mechanically. The clinician must also separate ejaculation from orgasm. Ejaculation happens in the genitals; orgasm is primarily a subjective experience; the two usually occur together, but not always. A man who ejaculates normally but feels little or no pleasure has anhedonic ejaculation, which DSM-5-TR says is not coded as delayed ejaculation but as other specified (or unspecified) sexual dysfunction (Section 9.4.6). Delayed ejaculation matters beyond the bedroom: DSM-5-TR points out that it can contribute to difficulty conceiving and lead to extensive fertility investigations, because men rarely mention the problem unless a doctor asks directly, which is exactly what happened to Waseem.

In the manuals, DSM-5-TR prints the code F52.32; ICD-10 placed the problem under F52.3, orgasmic dysfunction, shared with women; and ICD-11 calls it male delayed ejaculation, HA03.1, among the ejaculatory dysfunctions in Chapter 17. Treatment starts with the cause: review and if possible change any drug that inhibits ejaculation, treat depression, and investigate medical causes. Then comes retraining. The man is usually asked to reduce the frequency of masturbation, to change his technique so that it resembles what a partner can provide, and to bring his fantasies closer to his real sexual life; the couple work through sensate focus and sex therapy (Book 2 Sections 7.9.2 to 7.9.4) to reduce pressure and increase stimulation that works for both of them. Couple therapy addresses the hurt that has built up, such as Waseem's wife's fear that he does not desire her. No drug is approved for delayed ejaculation. Where conception is the urgent goal, the fertility team can help the couple conceive while the sexual problem is being treated.

Exam trap

Inhibited male orgasm (DSM-III and DSM-III-R) and male orgasmic disorder (DSM-IV) are old names for delayed ejaculation, the opposite of premature ejaculation; an option reading "inhibited male orgasm" in a question about stop-start and squeeze techniques is a distractor. The delay must occur without the man desiring it and in partnered activity; most men ejaculate after about 4 to 10 minutes of intercourse. Retrograde ejaculation (semen into the bladder) is a urological differential, not the same disorder; normal ejaculation without pleasure (anhedonic ejaculation) is coded as other specified sexual dysfunction. ICD-11: HA03.1 male delayed ejaculation.

Premature (early) ejaculation

Bhavesh is 26 and works in his family's diamond-polishing business in Surat. Four months ago he married in an arranged marriage, and he and his wife live with his parents and younger brother in a flat where every wall is thin. On every attempt at intercourse he ejaculates within about half a minute of penetration, sometimes before. He has started to dread bedtime, stays late at the workshop, and pretends to be asleep when he comes home. He has told no one in the family, but he has quietly bought two "power" capsules from an advertisement and has privately decided what the cause is: as a teenager he masturbated "too much", and he believes this weakened him. His wife has said nothing directly, but she seems hurt and withdrawn, and his mother has started dropping hints about grandchildren.

DSM-5-TR calls Bhavesh's problem premature (early) ejaculation. Its first criterion is unique in the chapter because it contains a clock: a persistent or recurrent pattern of ejaculation during partnered sexual activity within approximately 1 minute following vaginal penetration and before the individual wishes it. Both halves count. A man who ejaculates at 50 seconds and is content does not have the disorder; neither does a man who ejaculates after 6 minutes and wishes he could last longer. A note adds that the diagnosis can be given for nonvaginal sexual activity, but no time criterion has been established for it; DSM-5-TR suggests that similar latencies are reasonable to assume for men who have sex with men. Criterion B requires the pattern to have been present for at least 6 months (note that the word "approximately" is missing here, unlike most of the chapter) and on almost all or all occasions (approximately 75 to 100 percent). Criterion C requires clinically significant distress and Criterion D the usual exclusions. The specifiers are lifelong or acquired and generalised or situational. Severity, unusually, is graded by time rather than distress: mild when ejaculation occurs within approximately 30 seconds to 1 minute of penetration, moderate within approximately 15 to 30 seconds, and severe before sexual activity, at its start or within approximately 15 seconds. At about half a minute, Bhavesh sits on the border of mild and moderate.

Why does a psychiatric manual use a stopwatch? Because without one the diagnosis would have no edge. Before DSM-5 the criteria spoke only of ejaculation "with minimal sexual stimulation" and "before the person wishes it", which made almost any man with a worry eligible. The research measure is the intravaginal ejaculatory latency time you met in Section 9.4.2. In research, DSM-5-TR explains, it is usually measured by the partner with a stopwatch, which is hardly ideal in real life; in the clinic, the man's own estimate is accepted instead, because estimated and measured latencies are highly correlated as long as the latency is short. How short is short? For most men the latency is about 4 to 10 minutes, and the International Society for Sexual Medicine (ISSM) reports a median of about 5.4 minutes in multinational studies. DSM-5-TR adds a comment that is easy to misread: a 60-second latency, it says, was previously considered an appropriate cutoff for lifelong premature ejaculation, but "expert consensus now considers this latency time to be too brief and instead recommends a 120-second threshold". This is DSM-5-TR reporting the views of experts; its own criterion still says approximately 1 minute. The ISSM, whose second guideline panel met in Bangalore in April 2013, offered a unified definition of its own: lifelong premature ejaculation when ejaculation always or nearly always occurs before or within about 1 minute of vaginal penetration from the first sexual experience, and acquired premature ejaculation when there is a clinically significant reduction in latency, often to about 3 minutes or less, together with an inability to delay ejaculation and negative personal consequences such as distress or avoidance of intimacy. Figure 9.6 puts these numbers on one ruler. The WHO's ICD-10 had taken a different route altogether, defining premature ejaculation (F52.4) without any clock as "the inability to control ejaculation sufficiently for both partners to enjoy sexual interaction".

Premature ejaculation comes in two forms, and they behave differently. Lifelong premature ejaculation begins with a man's first sexual experiences and persists; DSM-5-TR calls it relatively stable throughout life. Some young men ejaculate quickly in their first encounters and then gain control with experience, so it is the persistence of the problem beyond 6 months that makes the diagnosis. Acquired premature ejaculation develops after a period of normal latency, usually appears in or after the fourth decade of life, and is far less studied; DSM-5-TR links it to thyroid disease, prostatitis (inflammation of the prostate) and drug withdrawal, especially withdrawal from opioids. Two situations look like the disorder but are not. A man with a normal latency who simply wants to last longer has an ejaculatory concern that does not meet the criteria, and so does a man with episodic quick ejaculation, for example in the first encounter with a new partner, when a short latency is common or even normal. Both may be distressed, and both may benefit from reassurance and information; neither has a disorder.

How common is it? It depends entirely on the definition. Internationally, DSM-5-TR reports that 8 to 30 percent of men across all ages report the problem, with prevalence rising with age: about 9 to 11 percent among men aged 18 to 30 in Switzerland and Turkey, and concern about ejaculating quickly in as many as 55 percent of American men in their fifties. Yet when premature ejaculation is defined strictly as ejaculation within about 1 minute of penetration, only 1 to 3 percent of men would be diagnosed, and the ISSM concludes that lifelong premature ejaculation, defined that way, is unlikely to exceed 4 percent of the general population. The gap between those numbers is the gap between worry and disorder. Even so, premature ejaculation is usually described as the most common male sexual dysfunction: in Laumann's American survey 21 percent of men reported it, and in an Indian sexuality clinic described by Barlow and Durand 77 percent of male patients complained of it (both surveys are discussed in Section 9.3.2). Clinic figures are inflated by who comes, but they show what Indian clinicians see every week.

Now to the question the past papers ask directly: what is premature ejaculation most commonly associated with? The answer is anxiety. DSM-5-TR names the temperamental risk factor explicitly: premature ejaculation may be more common in men with anxiety disorders, especially social anxiety disorder, and it lists anxiety disorders among the conditions associated with the lifelong form. The physiology explains why (Section 9.2.4). Ejaculation, unlike erection, is driven partly by the sympathetic nervous system, the same system that anxiety activates. Barlow and Durand suggest that some men have a naturally low threshold for ejaculation, needing less stimulation and arousal to reach it, and that anxiety, by raising sympathetic arousal, lowers the threshold further. The result is a vicious circle you can see in Bhavesh: he worries that he will ejaculate quickly, the worry raises his arousal, he ejaculates even more quickly, and the failure feeds the next worry. Performance anxiety and spectatoring (Section 9.2.5) add to the loop by pulling his attention away from his own sensations, so that he never learns to recognise the moment before the point of no return. DSM-5-TR even notes a cultural version of this mechanism: reports of premature ejaculation were more common in arranged marriages, because of anxiety over family pressures and lack of premarital sexual experience. Bhavesh's thin walls, listening relatives and lack of experience are the anxieties DSM-5-TR has in mind.

Anxiety is the answer for the exam; it is not the whole story for an interview. DSM-5-TR reports a moderate genetic contribution to lifelong premature ejaculation, associations with polymorphisms of the dopamine transporter and serotonin transporter genes, and positron emission tomography studies showing activation in the midbrain, including the ventral tegmental area, during ejaculation. The Dutch psychiatrist Marcel Waldinger proposed that men with lifelong premature ejaculation may have reduced serotonin signalling (specifically, hyposensitive 5-HT2C receptors or hypersensitive 5-HT1A receptors); since serotonin tends to delay ejaculation, low serotonergic tone would mean a lower ejaculatory threshold. That hypothesis explains why serotonin-enhancing drugs delay ejaculation, but the ISSM guideline cautions that serotonin dysregulation has been estimated to explain only a small share (2 to 5 percent) of premature ejaculation complaints in the general population. The balanced conclusion is that lifelong premature ejaculation sits on a biological threshold that anxiety can push lower, while most complaints are driven by anxiety, inexperience and expectation.

Just as important are the causes that are not supported, because examiners offer them as options. Masturbation does not cause premature ejaculation. The belief that it does, and that semen loss drains a man's strength, is part of the cluster of beliefs behind dhat syndrome, which Book 3 Section 1.10.4 explains; for Bhavesh it is not a cause but an extra source of anxiety and guilt, and it has already sent him to buy useless capsules. Diabetes is the classic physical cause of erectile disorder, through damage to nerves and blood vessels (Section 9.3.6), not of premature ejaculation. And personality disorder has no special association with it: DSM-5-TR names no personality disorder among its risk factors or its comorbid conditions, which are anxiety disorders, erectile problems and, for the acquired form, prostatitis, thyroid disease and drug withdrawal (the personality disorders themselves are taught in Chapter 11). The phrase anxiety state in the question is simply the older name for a condition of pathological anxiety (ICD-9 grouped what are now the anxiety disorders under "anxiety states"), so it points to the same answer as DSM-5-TR's anxiety disorders. Premature ejaculation does have two important companions. It is often associated with erectile problems, and DSM-5-TR says it may be hard to tell which came first; the ISSM notes that a man worried about losing his erection may hurry to ejaculate before it fades, while anxiety can drive both problems. And it travels with anxiety disorders. Its consequences are real: lower self-esteem and confidence, a sense of lack of control, strain on the relationship and reduced satisfaction for the partner, and difficulty conceiving if ejaculation occurs before penetration. DSM-5-TR adds that single men are more bothered than partnered men, because the problem interferes with starting new relationships, and that elevated suicidal thinking has been seen in men treated for premature ejaculation with comorbid depression, though depression is likely to be a major contributor. In the manuals, DSM-5-TR and ICD-10 both use F52.4; ICD-11 calls it male early ejaculation, HA03.0.

Treatment combines a behavioural programme, taught in Book 2, with drugs, taught here. The behavioural methods are old and well known. The stop-start technique was published by the American urologist James Semans in 1956 ("Premature ejaculation: a new approach", in the Southern Medical Journal): stimulation stops just before the point of no return and resumes when the urge has passed, so that the man learns to recognise and control the sensations that precede ejaculation. Masters and Johnson modified it into the squeeze technique, in which the partner firmly squeezes the penis just below the head to make the urge subside, and a later variant, the basal squeeze, applies pressure at the base so it can be used during intercourse; Kegel exercises for the pelvic floor are sometimes added. Book 2 Section 7.9.4 describes them, and its Exam trap explains why Barlow and Durand credit Semans with "the squeeze technique": in an exam, pair stop-start with Semans (1956) and squeeze with Masters and Johnson. These methods work well at first, with 60 to 90 percent of men benefiting, but the gains fade, to about 25 percent after 3 years or more of follow-up (Barlow and Durand). That fading is the reason drugs and combined treatment have taken a larger place.

The drugs exploit a side effect you will meet again in Section 9.4.5: serotonin-enhancing antidepressants delay ejaculation. The first drug to be licensed specifically for premature ejaculation was dapoxetine, a short-acting SSRI designed to be taken on demand, about 1 to 3 hours before expected sexual activity, rather than every day. In the two large American trials pooled by Pryor and colleagues in The Lancet in 2006, involving more than 2,600 men with moderate to severe premature ejaculation, dapoxetine at 30 or 60 milligrams raised the average stopwatch latency from under 1 minute at baseline to about 2.8 and 3.3 minutes after 12 weeks, against about 1.75 minutes on placebo, and it worked from the first dose; the commonest side effects were nausea, diarrhoea, headache and dizziness. The drug's regulatory history is a lesson in itself. The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) issued a "not approvable" letter in October 2005, and dapoxetine has never been approved in the United States; it was first approved in Sweden and Finland in early 2009, has since been approved in more than 50 countries, and is marketed in India. Before dapoxetine, and still today, doctors have used daily SSRIs such as paroxetine, sertraline, fluoxetine and citalopram, and the tricyclic clomipramine, all off-label (that is, prescribed for a use the drug is not licensed for). In a meta-analysis of drug trials, Waldinger and colleagues (2004) found paroxetine, clomipramine, sertraline and fluoxetine comparable overall when taken daily, with paroxetine producing the strongest delay. These drugs carry the usual antidepressant side effects, including reduced desire, and their effect lasts only while they are taken.

A second approach works on the body's surface instead of the brain. Topical anaesthetics, creams, gels or sprays of lidocaine and prilocaine applied to the glans (the head of the penis) before intercourse, reduce its sensitivity and so delay the reflex; the ISSM calls this the oldest known drug treatment for premature ejaculation. A metered lidocaine-prilocaine spray was authorised in the European Union on 15 November 2013 and renamed Fortacin in 2014. The drawbacks are numbness of the penis and, unless a condom is used, numbness of the partner's genitals, with occasional loss of erection. Finally, the phosphodiesterase type 5 (PDE-5) inhibitors such as sildenafil (Section 9.3.6) are not a treatment for premature ejaculation itself: the ISSM review found no robust evidence for them except in men who have premature ejaculation together with erectile dysfunction, where restoring a reliable erection removes the pressure to hurry. The off-label opioid painkiller tramadol has also been tried, but its risk of dependence limits it. Table 9.10 brings the options together.

Table 9.10 Treatments for premature (early) ejaculation. The behavioural methods are taught in Book 2 Section 7.9.4; the drugs are taught here.
