Which should a man like Bhavesh receive? The answer most sexual medicine clinics give is "both, in sequence". A drug can give quick relief and break the cycle of failure and dread; the behavioural programme, couple sessions and education give him lasting control and a way of talking to his wife. For Bhavesh the clinician would first correct the myth that masturbation has damaged him, explain the anxiety loop, involve his wife if he agrees, address the problem of privacy in a joint family, and then offer either an on-demand drug or the stop-start programme, or both. That combination of information, permission and specific suggestion is Annon's PLISSIT model at work (Book 2 Section 7.9.6).

Exam trap

The stop-start (Semans, 1956) and squeeze (Masters and Johnson) techniques treat premature ejaculation. They do not treat male erectile disorder (sensate focus and PDE-5 inhibitors, Section 9.3.6), inhibited male orgasm (the old name for delayed ejaculation, the opposite problem, Section 9.4.2) or vaginismus (graded dilators, Section 9.4.4). If an item credits Semans with the squeeze technique, it is following Barlow and Durand; if both names appear separately, pair stop-start with Semans. Premature ejaculation is most commonly associated with an anxiety state (DSM-5-TR: anxiety disorders, especially social anxiety disorder), not with masturbation (a myth that feeds dhat syndrome), not with diabetes mellitus (the classic link of erectile disorder) and not with personality disorder. In an interview, add that lifelong premature ejaculation also has a genetic and serotonergic side. DSM-5-TR: within about 1 minute, for at least 6 months, severity graded by time; ICD-11 HA03.0 male early ejaculation.

Genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder

Rupali is 25, a primary school teacher from a town near Kolhapur, married ten months to a man she chose with her family's blessing. She comes first to a gynaecologist, not a psychologist, and she comes because her mother-in-law has asked why there is no news of a baby. The marriage has never been consummated. Each time her husband tries, her body seems to close: her thighs press together, the muscles tighten, she feels a sharp burning pain at the entrance to the vagina and pushes him away, crying. She has never been able to use a tampon, and at 22 she fled from a doctor's room when an internal examination was suggested. She loves her husband, enjoys kissing and holding him, and becomes aroused; it is only penetration that she cannot bear. Now the gynaecologist wants to examine her, and she is terrified.

Until 2013 Rupali would have been diagnosed with one of two disorders, or with both. The first, vaginismus, was the involuntary contraction of the muscles around the vagina that makes penetration painful or impossible (Barlow and Durand place the spasm in the outer third of the vagina, and note that it can occur during any attempted penetration, including a gynaecological examination or the insertion of a tampon). The second, dyspareunia, was pain during sexual intercourse. DSM-IV listed the two as separate "sexual pain disorders", and ICD-10 still lists them separately as F52.5, nonorganic vaginismus ("spasm of the pelvic floor muscles that surround the vagina, causing occlusion of the vaginal opening"), and F52.6, nonorganic dyspareunia, which, unlike DSM-5-TR's diagnosis, ICD-10 says can occur in both women and men. DSM-5 merged them into a single diagnosis, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder. The case for the merger was made by the McGill University psychologist Yitzchak Binik in two reviews in the Archives of Sexual Behavior in 2010. Vaginal spasm, he pointed out, had been treated as the defining feature of vaginismus for about 150 years, on the strength of expert clinical opinion, but the empirical research supported neither that definition nor the DSM-IV-TR distinction between vaginismus and dyspareunia. As Barlow and Durand put it, vaginismus and pain during intercourse overlap a great deal. Binik therefore proposed collapsing the two into a single diagnosis, genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder, defined by five dimensions: how often penetration succeeds, pain with penetration, fear of penetration or pain, pelvic floor muscle dysfunction, and medical comorbidity. DSM-5 adopted the name and the first four dimensions as its criteria.

The criteria reflect those dimensions. Criterion A requires persistent or recurrent difficulty with one or more of four things: (1) vaginal penetration during intercourse; (2) marked vulvovaginal or pelvic pain during vaginal intercourse or attempts at penetration; (3) marked fear or anxiety about vulvovaginal or pelvic pain in anticipation of, during or as a result of vaginal penetration; and (4) marked tensing or tightening of the pelvic floor muscles during attempted vaginal penetration. The problem must have lasted approximately 6 months, must cause clinically significant distress, and must not be better explained by a nonsexual mental disorder, by severe relationship distress (again with partner violence as the example) or other stressors, by a substance or medication, or by another medical condition. Notice three differences from the template, because each is a trap. There is no percentage of occasions; the wording is simply "persistent or recurrent". There is no generalised or situational specifier; only lifelong or acquired is specified. And severity, as for most of the chapter, is rated by distress. Why can one dimension be enough? DSM-5-TR's answer is practical: marked difficulty in any one of them is often enough to cause clinically significant distress. But it adds that all four should be assessed even when one is enough for the diagnosis, because they usually come together and each needs its own treatment.

Each dimension has a range, and DSM-5-TR describes them carefully. Difficulty with penetration runs from total inability in every situation (intercourse, gynaecological examination, tampons) to easy penetration in one situation but not another; the commonest presentation is the woman who cannot have intercourse with her partner. Pain is described by place and kind: superficial pain at the entrance during penetration, or deep pelvic pain felt only with deeper penetration; pain that occurs only when provoked by intercourse or touch, or pain that is also spontaneous; pain described as burning, cutting, shooting or throbbing. It may last after intercourse and occur during urination, and it can usually be reproduced in a gynaecological examination. Its intensity, interestingly, is not closely related to how distressing or disabling it is. Fear is often a reasonable reaction to repeated pain and leads to avoidance; but in some women the fear does not seem related to pain at all, and DSM-5-TR compares it to a phobia whose object is penetration or the pain itself. Tensing of the pelvic floor ranges from a reflex-like spasm in response to attempted entry to a "normal" voluntary guarding against expected pain or fear; with guarding, penetration may be possible when the woman is relaxed. DSM-5-TR says that pelvic floor function is best assessed by a specialist gynaecologist or a pelvic floor physiotherapist. Rupali shows all four dimensions: penetration has never happened, it hurts, she fears it, and her muscles tighten.

Two medical conditions are named in the text because they may be the primary cause. In vulvodynia there is chronic vulvar pain without an identifiable cause lasting at least 3 months; provoked vestibulodynia is a form of vulvodynia in which contact provokes pain at the vulvar vestibule, the area around the vaginal opening. Women with these conditions are usually distressed enough to meet the criteria for genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder as well. The disorder is frequently associated with low sexual interest (female sexual interest/arousal disorder), although desire is sometimes preserved in situations that do not involve penetration; with behavioural avoidance of sexual situations and of gynaecological examinations, a pattern DSM-5-TR compares to phobic avoidance; and with relationship problems. DSM-5-TR adds two observations that every clinician in India will recognise: it is common for women who have never achieved penetration to come for treatment only when they wish to conceive, and many women say that the symptoms diminish their feelings of femininity.

How common is it? DSM-5-TR says the prevalence of the disorder itself is unknown, but about 10 to 28 percent of women of reproductive age in the United States report recurrent pain during intercourse, and the international range is 8 to 28 percent, varying by country. Difficulties with intercourse are a frequent reason for referral to sexual dysfunction clinics. Barlow and Durand give a best estimate of 6 percent of women for vaginismus (Bradford and Meston, 2011) and note that it is relatively rare in North America but considerably more common in Ireland and Turkey, countries with more restrictive attitudes to sex, a contrast that points to the role of culture. The course is hard to chart, because women usually seek help only after sexual problems have begun, so it can be difficult to say whether the problem is lifelong or acquired. The clinician's question is whether there has ever been a consistent period of successful intercourse without pain, fear or tension; if there has, the disorder is acquired. Earlier clues often exist: difficulty with or avoidance of tampons is an important predictor of later problems. Once symptoms have lasted about 6 months, spontaneous remission becomes less likely. Pain complaints peak in early adulthood and again around and after the menopause, and may increase after childbirth.

The risk factors are many. Women with earlier mood or anxiety disorders are four times more likely to develop the disorder. Psychological processes keep it going: pain catastrophising (thinking about pain in an exaggerated, helpless, ruminating way), low confidence in managing pain, avoidance of pain and negative mood; and interpersonal factors such as insecure attachment, a partner's negative responses to the pain, and sexual motives that focus on avoiding conflict rather than seeking pleasure. Women with the disorder more often report a history of sexual or physical abuse, or fear of abuse, though many have no such history. Biological risks include previous vaginal infections (superficial pain often begins after infections and persists after they have cleared), early puberty, inflammation, early use of oral contraceptives, an increase and sensitisation of pain receptors in the vulva so that touch is felt as pain, low touch and pain thresholds, and abnormalities of the resting pelvic floor such as overactive muscles. Pain during tampon insertion, or inability to insert one, before any sexual contact is again singled out as an important risk factor.

Culture shapes how the pain is lived and reported. DSM-5-TR describes the pressure of social narratives that put men's sexual desire and penetrative sex first and portray sex as easy and natural, and it notes that some women do not report pain at all but say instead that they are "unhappy in their marriages". The disorder is diagnosed only in women. DSM-5-TR acknowledges that men can have chronic pelvic pain (an estimated 2.2 to 9.7 percent worldwide), but says the research is not yet developed enough to extend the diagnosis to them; such men may receive other specified or unspecified sexual dysfunction.

The differential diagnosis is where psychology and gynaecology must work together. Many women with the disorder also have a medical condition such as lichen sclerosus (a chronic inflammatory skin disease of the vulva), endometriosis, pelvic inflammatory disease or the genitourinary syndrome of menopause (dryness, irritation and thinning of the tissues as oestrogen falls). Treating the medical condition sometimes relieves the pain, but often it does not, and DSM-5-TR admits there is no reliable way to tell which came first. Some women may also meet criteria for somatic symptom disorder or for a specific phobia. The clinician must also check the adequacy of stimulation, since inadequate foreplay and arousal can themselves cause difficulty, pain and avoidance, and the partner's function, since a partner's erectile dysfunction or premature ejaculation may make penetration difficult; in such cases the diagnosis may not be appropriate. Comorbidity with other sexual problems, with relationship distress (usually because of the loss of intimacy rather than the pain alone), with other pelvic and reproductive disorders such as interstitial cystitis, constipation and irritable bowel syndrome, and with chronic pain conditions such as fibromyalgia is common. In the manuals, DSM-5-TR prints the code F52.6; ICD-10 separates nonorganic vaginismus (F52.5) and nonorganic dyspareunia (F52.6) from their organic forms, which it codes in the genitourinary chapter (N94.2 and N94.1); and ICD-11 merges them, as DSM-5 did, into sexual pain-penetration disorder, HA20, in Chapter 17.

Treatment begins with a medical examination, done gently, with consent and with the woman in control, to look for infection, skin disease, endometriosis or atrophy; infections and skin conditions are treated, and the dryness and thinning of the genitourinary syndrome of menopause are usually treated with vaginal oestrogen. Then comes graded exposure. Within a sex therapy programme the woman, at her own pace and only when relaxed, inserts a series of graded vaginal dilators (or her own fingers), and later her partner does so under her guidance; Kegel exercises teach her that she can deliberately tighten and relax the pelvic floor. Book 2 Section 7.9.4 describes the method, and Barlow and Durand report that 80 to 100 percent of women overcome vaginismus with it, often in a relatively short time. Pelvic floor physiotherapy treats overactive muscles directly. Cognitive-behavioural therapy addresses the fear, the catastrophic thinking and the avoidance, and couple work addresses the partner's responses and the loss of intimacy. For Rupali, the first step is to postpone the feared examination until she has been given control over it, to explain what is happening in her body, and to bring her husband into the treatment.

Indian context

In Indian clinics this disorder often presents as an unconsummated marriage, sometimes months or years after the wedding, and often through a gynaecologist or an infertility clinic rather than a psychologist, because the family's question is about a baby, not about pain. Fear of the wedding night, frightening stories from relatives, no sex education, no privacy in a joint family, and the expectation that the bride will bleed all feed the fear and the tension; the husband's own anxiety, or an erectile problem that follows repeated failure, can join the loop. Book 2 Section 7.9.7 describes the presentation and the sex therapy approach; the point to add here is that the diagnosis belongs to the woman only when the difficulty is hers, so the clinician must assess both partners before deciding whose problem it is.

Exam trap

DSM-5 merged vaginismus and dyspareunia into genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder because the two could not be reliably distinguished (Binik, 2010). One of four dimensions is enough: penetration difficulty, pain, fear of pain, pelvic floor tensing. It has no 75 to 100 percent rule and no generalised/situational specifier (lifelong or acquired only). It is diagnosed only in women; ICD-10's dyspareunia (F52.6) could be given to men. Tampon difficulty before sexual contact predicts it. Codes: DSM-5-TR F52.6; ICD-10 F52.5 vaginismus and F52.6 dyspareunia; ICD-11 HA20 sexual pain-penetration disorder (Chapter 17, not Chapter 6). Treatment: graded dilators (80 to 100 percent success), not the squeeze technique.

Substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction

Dhruv is 33, a bank officer in Pune, and three months ago his psychiatrist started him on sertraline for panic disorder. It has worked. The attacks that used to wake him at night have stopped, and he is travelling by train again. But within the first two weeks he noticed something he has not mentioned to anyone: he can no longer ejaculate. He becomes aroused and keeps his erection, but the climax does not come, and his wife has begun to ask what is wrong. He has read online that "these tablets ruin your sex life forever", and he is quietly planning to stop them before his next appointment. He tells all this to the clinical psychologist who is seeing him for cognitive-behavioural therapy, because she is the one person who asked.

Dhruv has a problem that every psychiatric clinic sees daily. The diagnosis of substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction is made when a clinically significant disturbance in sexual function is predominant in the clinical picture (Criterion A); when there is evidence from the history, examination or laboratory findings both that it developed during or soon after intoxication with or withdrawal from a substance, or after starting, changing or stopping a medication, and that the substance or medication is capable of producing it (Criterion B); when it is not better explained by an independent sexual dysfunction (Criterion C), for example one that began before the substance was used or that persists for a substantial period, about a month, after acute withdrawal or severe intoxication has ended, or where there is a history of similar episodes unrelated to substances; when it does not occur only during a delirium (Criterion D); and when it causes clinically significant distress (Criterion E). A note adds that the diagnosis is made instead of a diagnosis of intoxication or withdrawal only when the sexual symptoms predominate and are severe enough to need clinical attention.

The rules differ from the rest of the chapter in ways examiners exploit. There is no 6-month rule, because a drug effect can be recognised from its timing, and waiting 6 months would be pointless and harmful. Severity is graded by the percentage of occasions: mild when it occurs on 25 to 50 percent of occasions, moderate on 50 to 75 percent, and severe on 75 percent or more. Three onset specifiers record the timing: with onset during intoxication, with onset during withdrawal, and with onset after medication use (when the symptoms began at the start of a medication, with a change in its use, or during its withdrawal). The coding follows the substance. For alcohol the code is F10.181 with a mild alcohol use disorder, F10.281 with a moderate or severe one, and F10.981 with no use disorder (the fourth character, 1, 2 or 9, records the comorbid use disorder, which is not diagnosed separately); parallel codes exist for opioids (F11), sedatives, hypnotics or anxiolytics (F13), cocaine (F14), amphetamine-type and other stimulants (F15) and other or unknown substances (F19), and DSM-5-TR prints no row for cannabis. A prescribed medicine that fits none of these classes is coded under "other (or unknown) substance": DSM-5-TR's own example is F19.981 fluoxetine-induced sexual dysfunction. Dhruv's diagnosis would be recorded as F19.981 sertraline-induced sexual dysfunction, with onset after medication use, severe (he has the problem on every occasion). ICD-11 has no separate category for drug-induced sexual dysfunction in Chapter 17; instead the relevant dysfunction (for Dhruv, HA03.1 male delayed ejaculation) is given and the qualifier HA40.2, "associated with use of psychoactive substance or medication", is added.

Which substances do it? DSM-5-TR names intoxication with alcohol, opioids, sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics, stimulants including cocaine, and other substances; withdrawal from alcohol, opioids, and sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics; and, among medications, antidepressants, antipsychotics and hormonal contraceptives. Table 9.11 sets out the main offenders. Two groups deserve a closer look because psychologists meet them daily.

Antidepressants. About 25 to 80 percent of people taking monoamine oxidase inhibitors, tricyclics, serotonergic antidepressants (the SSRIs) and combined serotonergic-adrenergic antidepressants (the SNRIs) report sexual side effects, and the highest rates are with citalopram, fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, paroxetine, sertraline and venlafaxine. Barlow and Durand note that estimates of up to 80 percent exist but that figures closer to 50 percent seem more reliable. The commonest effect is difficulty with orgasm or ejaculation in men and with arousal in women; problems of desire and erection are less frequent. The effect occurs regardless of the level of depression, so it is a drug effect, not a symptom, and DSM-5-TR estimates that about 30 percent of the sexual complaints are clinically significant. Onset can be as early as 8 days after the first dose, as Dhruv found. About 30 percent of people with mild to moderate orgasm delay remit spontaneously within 6 months. In some cases SSRI-induced dysfunction persists after the drug is stopped, a condition the research literature calls post-SSRI sexual dysfunction. Bupropion, mirtazapine, nefazodone and vilazodone have lower rates. Book 3 Section 5.7.2 teaches the antidepressant classes and why bupropion, which acts on dopamine and noradrenaline rather than serotonin, spares sexual function; Section 6.9.4 reports Lecrubier's finding that sexual dysfunction occurred in 75 percent or more of patients treated for panic with antidepressants and was the commonest reason for stopping.

Antipsychotics. About 50 percent of people taking antipsychotics have sexual side effects affecting desire, erection, lubrication, ejaculation or orgasm, with both typical and atypical drugs. Problems are less common with prolactin-sparing drugs and those that do not block dopamine receptors. The mechanism is the one Book 3 Section 4.8.3 described: blocking dopamine in the tuberoinfundibular pathway releases the brake on prolactin, and a raised prolactin lowers sex hormones and desire. Section 4.10.5 ranks the antipsychotics by their effect on prolactin, high with risperidone, paliperidone and amisulpride and low with aripiprazole, quetiapine and clozapine.

Table 9.11 Drugs and substances that cause sexual dysfunction (DSM-5-TR unless another source is named).
GroupExamplesMain sexual effectsNotes
AntidepressantsMAOIs, tricyclics, SSRIs, SNRIs; highest with citalopram, fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, paroxetine, sertraline, venlafaxineMen: delayed orgasm or ejaculation; women: arousal; less often desire and erection25 to 80 percent report effects; onset from 8 days; may persist after stopping; lower with bupropion, mirtazapine, nefazodone, vilazodone
AntipsychoticsTypical and atypicalDesire, erection, lubrication, ejaculation, orgasmAbout 50 percent; less with prolactin-sparing drugs (Section 4.10.5)
Mood stabilisersLithium, anticonvulsants; gabapentinPossibly lower desire; orgasm problems with gabapentinEffects unclear; lamotrigine a possible exception
AnxiolyticsBenzodiazepinesErection and orgasm problemsNone reported with buspirone
Prostate and hair-loss drugsFinasteride, dutasteride (5-alpha-reductase inhibitors); tamsulosin (alpha-blocker)Erection, ejaculation, desire; delayed ejaculation with tamsulosinWidely prescribed to older men
Cardiovascular drugsAntihypertensives; beta-blockers such as propranolol (Barlow and Durand)Erection; delayed ejaculationRates unknown
Hormonal agentsHormonal contraceptivesDesire and arousalNamed by DSM-5-TR among causative medications
OpioidsHeroin, methadone, high-dose painkillersLow desire, erectile problems, difficulty with orgasmAbout 60 to 70 percent of heroin users; seldom with buprenorphine; early ejaculation in withdrawal
Alcohol and nicotineChronic heavy useErectile problems; low desire; ejaculatory problems75 percent of male alcoholics had dysfunction (Fahrner, 1987)
Stimulants and cannabisCocaine, amphetamines; cannabisLow desire, erectile problems, difficulty with orgasmCocaine: over 60 percent of users (Barlow and Durand); cannabis effects uncertain

The other drugs are worth a sentence each. DSM-5-TR says the effects of mood stabilisers are unclear, but lithium and the anticonvulsants, with the possible exception of lamotrigine, may reduce desire, and gabapentin may cause orgasm problems. Benzodiazepines may be linked to more erectile and orgasm problems, while none have been reported with buspirone. Among non-psychiatric medicines, cardiovascular, cytotoxic (anticancer), gastrointestinal and hormonal drugs are all associated with sexual problems; the 5-alpha-reductase inhibitors finasteride and dutasteride, used for an enlarged prostate and for hair loss, may reduce erection, ejaculation and libido; and Barlow and Durand single out the beta-blocking antihypertensives such as propranolol. Illicit drugs are associated with low desire, erectile dysfunction and difficulty reaching orgasm, and the rates appear related to chronic use: about 60 to 70 percent among people who misuse heroin, higher than among users of amphetamine-type substances or MDMA (ecstasy). People on methadone maintenance often have sexual problems, but those on buprenorphine seldom report them. Premature ejaculation can appear when opioids are stopped (Section 9.4.3). Chronic nicotine use and chronic heavy drinking are associated with erectile problems, and their effects may take years to appear.

Alcohol deserves its own paragraph, because almost everyone believes the opposite of the truth about it. At low and moderate doses alcohol reduces social inhibition, so people feel more like having sex and are more willing to ask for it. Physically, however, it is a central nervous system depressant, and erection and lubrication are harder to achieve when the nervous system is suppressed. Chronic heavy drinking can damage nerves, the liver and the testes, lowering testosterone and with it desire and arousal. Barlow and Durand point out that Shakespeare captured this dual effect four centuries ago, when the Porter in Macbeth says that drink "provokes the desire, but it takes away the performance". Fahrner (1987) found that 75 percent of male alcoholics had erectile dysfunction, low desire, or premature or delayed ejaculation. Cannabis is less clear: DSM-5-TR notes that, like alcohol, it is a central nervous system depressant and may be a risk factor for sexual dysfunction, but that it has also been suggested to improve satisfaction with orgasm, and Barlow and Durand suggest that any effect may be largely on attention. Cocaine produced sexual dysfunction in more than 60 percent of a large sample of users (Cocores and colleagues, 1988, reported by Barlow and Durand).

Why should a psychologist care? DSM-5-TR names the main consequence: medication-induced sexual dysfunction leads to noncompliance, stopping medication or taking it irregularly, which in turn can make antidepressants fail. Dhruv is about to do exactly that, and if he stops sertraline abruptly he risks discontinuation symptoms and a return of panic. The first skill is simply to ask, because few patients volunteer the problem. The second is to know the differential diagnosis. Depressive, bipolar, anxiety and psychotic disorders themselves disturb sexual function, so it can be hard to tell whether a problem comes from the illness or from its treatment. DSM-5-TR gives the logic of proof: the diagnosis is usually made when there is a close link in time between starting or stopping the drug and the problem, and a clear diagnosis can be made when the problem appears after the drug is started, dissipates when it is stopped, and recurs when the same drug is reintroduced, the last step being called rechallenge. Problems that appear only after long use are much harder to attribute with certainty. In Dhruv's case the timing is clear: no ejaculatory problem before sertraline, onset within two weeks of starting it, and nothing else in his history to explain it.

What can be done? The decisions are the prescriber's, but the psychologist should know the options well enough to encourage the client to raise them rather than act alone. The 2013 Cochrane review by Taylor and colleagues, which pooled 23 randomised trials with 1,886 participants, found that in men the PDE-5 inhibitors sildenafil and tadalafil improved antidepressant-induced erectile problems more than placebo, that adding bupropion at a higher dose showed benefit in some trials, that most other add-on drugs did not help, and, strikingly, that there were no randomised trials of switching to the currently available antidepressants with lower rates of sexual side effects, of psychological treatments or of "drug holidays" (stopping the drug briefly before sex). In practice, clinicians use a set of strategies whose logic follows from this section: waiting, since some early effects fade (about 30 percent of mild to moderate orgasm delays remit within 6 months); lowering the dose, if the illness allows; switching to a drug with a lower rate, such as bupropion or mirtazapine; adding a PDE-5 inhibitor for erectile problems; and, for antipsychotics, choosing a prolactin-sparing drug such as aripiprazole. Psychological support helps too: reassurance that the effect is usually reversible, sex therapy for the couple, and attention to the depression or anxiety that made the drug necessary. Dhruv's psychologist does not tell him to change his tablets; she tells him that his problem is common and treatable, that stopping on his own could bring the panic back, and that he should raise it with his psychiatrist, who has several options.

Clinical link: ask before they stop

Most clients will not mention a sexual side effect unless someone asks, and many will simply stop the tablet. A routine, matter-of-fact question at every review ("Some people on this medicine notice changes in their sex life, such as taking longer to climax or less interest. Has anything like that happened?") gives permission, the first level of Annon's PLISSIT model (Book 2 Section 7.9.6). It also protects the treatment: the client who reports the problem can be helped by the prescriber, while the client who hides it often relapses. Ask before starting the drug, too, so that a problem that was already there is not later blamed on the medicine.

Exam trap

Substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction has no 6-month rule; severity is graded by occasions (mild 25 to 50, moderate 50 to 75, severe 75 percent or more); the onset specifiers are during intoxication, during withdrawal and after medication use. A medication is coded under "other (or unknown) substance", for example F19.981 fluoxetine-induced sexual dysfunction. Symptoms persisting for more than about 1 month after withdrawal or intoxication suggest an independent dysfunction. SSRIs mainly cause delayed orgasm or ejaculation in men and arousal problems in women; bupropion and mirtazapine have lower rates; antipsychotics act partly through prolactin. Alcohol "provokes the desire, but it takes away the performance" (Macbeth).

Other specified and unspecified dysfunctions, and the treatment toolkit

Not every sexual problem fits one of the seven named dysfunctions and the substance-induced category. When symptoms characteristic of a sexual dysfunction cause clinically significant distress but do not meet the full criteria for any specific disorder, DSM-5-TR offers two residual categories. The category of other specified sexual dysfunction (F52.8) is used when the clinician chooses to record the specific reason, and DSM-5-TR's own example is sexual aversion. Other examples from this chapter are anhedonic ejaculation, in which ejaculation is normal but brings no pleasure (Section 9.4.2), and genito-pelvic pain in men (Section 9.4.4). Unspecified sexual dysfunction (F52.9) is used when the clinician chooses not to give the reason or does not have enough information to be more specific.

The example of sexual aversion has a history. DSM-III (1980) had a single disorder of desire, inhibited sexual desire. When DSM-III-R (1987) revised it, the committee divided it into two: hypoactive sexual desire disorder, a lack of interest in sex, and sexual aversion disorder, a phobic aversion to and avoidance of genital contact with a partner. DSM-IV kept both as "sexual desire disorders". DSM-5 dropped sexual aversion disorder as a separate diagnosis. Its patients now receive other specified sexual dysfunction, or, if the fear centres on penetration, they may meet the criteria for genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder. ICD-10 kept a related category, F52.1, sexual aversion and lack of sexual enjoyment, covering both the fearful avoidance of sex and the experience of normal response and orgasm without pleasure.

That completes the sexual dysfunctions, and it is a good moment to stand back. Treatment of these disorders is divided between two books: the psychological methods are taught in Book 2, Clinical Psychology, Section 7.9, and the physical and drug treatments are taught in this chapter, in Section 9.3 for the disorders of desire, arousal and erection and in this section for the rest. Table 9.12 puts them side by side, disorder by disorder, so that you can match any treatment to its target.

Table 9.12 Treating the sexual dysfunctions: the psychological methods (taught in Book 2) and the physical ones (taught here).
DysfunctionPsychological methods (originator, year)Where in Book 2Physical and drug treatmentsEvidence (approximate)
Female sexual interest/arousal disorderSensate focus (Masters and Johnson, 1970); Kaplan's brief sex therapy (1974); CBT, mindfulness, couple work7.8, 7.9.3, 7.9.5Treat depression, review SSRIs; local oestrogen after menopause; flibanserin (2015) and bremelanotide (2019) for premenopausal low desire (Section 9.3.4)Psychological treatment of low desire: 50 to 70 percent benefit

Erectile disorderSensate focus with a ban on intercourse; reducing performance anxiety and spectatoring7.9.2, 7.9.3PDE-5 inhibitors; penile injections; urethral alprostadil; vacuum devices; prostheses (Section 9.3.6)Sex therapy 60 to 70 percent; sildenafil 50 to 80 percent against about 30 percent on placebo

Premature (early) ejaculationStop-start (Semans, 1956); squeeze and basal squeeze (Masters and Johnson); Kegel exercises7.9.4Dapoxetine on demand; daily SSRIs or clomipramine; topical anaesthetics; PDE-5 inhibitors if erectile problems co-exist60 to 90 percent at first, about 25 percent after 3 years
Genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorderGraded vaginal dilators; Kegel exercises; CBT for pain and fear; sensate focus7.9.4Treat infection or skin disease; vaginal oestrogen for menopausal atrophy; pelvic floor physiotherapyDilators: 80 to 100 percent overcome vaginismus
Substance/medication-inducedEducation and permission; couple support7.9.6Wait, reduce dose, switch drug, add a PDE-5 inhibitor or bupropion (prescriber's decision)Cochrane 2013: PDE-5 inhibitors help men
Any dysfunction, first level of carePLISSIT (Annon, 1976): permission, limited information, specific suggestions, intensive therapy7.9.6Medical check for physical causesMost needs met at the first two levels

Read across the table and a pattern emerges. The psychological methods target the mechanisms you met in Section 9.2: sensate focus removes the demand to perform and so breaks the anxiety and spectatoring loop; stop-start, squeeze and dilators are forms of graded learning and exposure; directed masturbation is education by doing. The drugs target the body: they restore blood flow, delay a reflex, numb a surface or remove a chemical obstacle. Neither alone is usually enough. A tablet can restore an erection or delay an ejaculation, but it cannot remove the fear of failure, teach a couple to talk or repair a relationship that has been damaged by years of disappointment, which is why Bach, Barlow and Wincze (2004) found that adding cognitive-behavioural therapy to sildenafil increased couples' satisfaction and sexual activity (Section 9.3.6), and why so many men stop their tablets within months. Equally, sex therapy cannot reverse diabetic nerve damage or the effect of a drug. The best treatment for most clients is combined: a medical assessment, a drug where the body needs it, and psychological treatment for the anxiety, communication and relationship that surround the symptom. In India, where many people first take their worries to advertised "sexologists" and quacks, the first two levels of Annon's model, permission and limited information, meet most needs and undo much harm (Book 2 Section 7.9.7). Bhavesh needed to hear that masturbation had not damaged him; Namrata needed permission to explore her own body; Dhruv needed to know that his problem was a common, reversible drug effect. Only after that did specific techniques and drugs have their full effect.

Exam trap

Match each technique to its target: sensate focus for most dysfunctions, especially erectile disorder and low desire; stop-start and squeeze for premature ejaculation; directed masturbation and the bridge manoeuvre for female orgasmic disorder; graded dilators for vaginismus (genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder); PDE-5 inhibitors for erectile disorder; dapoxetine for premature ejaculation. "Sexual aversion" is DSM-5-TR's example of other specified sexual dysfunction (F52.8); sexual aversion disorder itself existed in DSM-III-R and DSM-IV only. Anhedonic ejaculation is other specified, not delayed ejaculation.

9.5 Paraphilias and Paraphilic Disorders: From Perversion to DisorderLower yield · not asked yet

The sexual dysfunctions are problems of response: the body does not do what the person wants it to do. This section turns to a different kind of question, about what a person wants in the first place. Some people are aroused by things most people are not: an object, a part of the body, an activity such as being bound, or a person who has not agreed to be involved. When is such an interest a disorder? The question is older than psychiatry, and the answers have swung widely. Nineteenth-century doctors called these interests "perversions" and treated almost any departure from intercourse aimed at reproduction as disease. Twentieth-century manuals listed them as "sexual deviations", and the second DSM put homosexuality at the head of the list. DSM-5 (2013) drew a new line: an unusual sexual interest is not, by itself, a mental disorder at all; it becomes one only when it causes distress or impairment, or when acting on it harms, or risks harming, others. ICD-11 went further and rebuilt the whole group around a single idea, consent. This section explains these ideas, traces how they developed, and asks who has paraphilias, how they relate to sexual crime, and where "hypersexuality" fits. Section 9.6 then describes the eight named disorders one by one, and Section 9.7 their causes, assessment and treatment.

What a paraphilia is

The word is Greek: para, beside, beyond or other than, and philia, love or attraction. Barlow and Durand gloss it as a strong attraction (philia) that is abnormal (para); the literal sense is a love that lies off to one side of the usual path. DSM-5-TR defines a paraphilia as "any intense and persistent sexual interest other than sexual interest in genital stimulation or preparatory fondling with phenotypically normal, physically mature, consenting human partners". The definition is worth reading slowly, because every phrase in it excludes something.

"Intense and persistent" rules out the passing fantasy, the curiosity, the one-off experiment: a paraphilia is a lasting and powerful pattern, not a thought that crosses the mind. "Genital stimulation or preparatory fondling" describes, in clinical language, the ordinary repertoire of foreplay and intercourse; an interest outside it may be paraphilic. Then come four words that describe the usual partner, and each excludes a class of paraphilias. "Phenotypically normal" means having a body of typical appearance, so an intense interest in amputees as a class would count. "Physically mature" excludes children and young adolescents. "Consenting" excludes the unsuspecting stranger who is watched, the stranger to whom the genitals are exposed and the person who is rubbed against in a crowd. And "human" excludes animals, objects and corpses. Notice that the definition is built by exclusion: it does not list paraphilias, it defines what is not one and calls everything else paraphilic. Ray Blanchard, who chaired the group that wrote it, argued that defining paraphilia by exclusion, as anything that is not normophilic, was preferable to trying to define each unusual interest in turn. DSM-5 (2013) called interests inside the circle "normophilic"; DSM-5-TR (2022) uses the plainer word "nonparaphilic".

Two refinements follow. First, "intense" is hard to apply to people who are very old or medically ill, who may have no intense sexual interests of any kind. For them DSM-5-TR offers an alternative test: a paraphilia is any sexual interest greater than or equal to the person's nonparaphilic sexual interests. Second, some paraphilias are better described as preferential than as intense: what marks them is not their strength but the fact that the person prefers them to ordinary sexual activity.

DSM-5-TR then sorts paraphilias by what they are about. Some concern the person's erotic activities: intense and persistent interest in spanking, whipping, cutting, binding or strangulating another person, or an interest in such activities that equals or exceeds the interest in intercourse or its equivalent. Others concern the person's erotic targets: intense or preferential interest in children, corpses or amputees, in animals such as horses or dogs, or in inanimate objects such as shoes or articles made of rubber. A practical observation completes the picture: a person's pattern of paraphilic interests is often reflected in his or her choice of pornography, which is one reason clinicians and courts sometimes treat it as evidence. Section 9.6 shows how DSM-5-TR builds its two families of named disorders on this distinction between activities and targets.

How many paraphilias are there? DSM-5-TR says that "many dozens" have been identified and named, and that almost any of them could rise to the level of a disorder through its consequences. Only eight are given their own criteria: voyeuristic, exhibitionistic, frotteuristic, sexual masochism, sexual sadism, pedophilic, fetishistic and transvestic disorder. DSM-5-TR gives two reasons for the choice. These eight are relatively common compared with the others, and some of them involve acts that, because they are noxious or potentially harmful to others, are crimes. Everything else, from necrophilia and zoophilia to obscene telephone calls, goes under other specified paraphilic disorder (Section 9.6.7). The eight, in other words, are not a natural kind; they are the paraphilias that most often come to the attention of clinics and courts.

It helps to be clear about what a paraphilia is not. It is not a sexual orientation: sexual orientation concerns the sex or gender of the adults to whom a person is attracted (Section 9.1.2), and homosexuality, although once listed among the sexual deviations, is not a paraphilia (Section 9.10). It is not a gender identity: a person whose experienced gender differs from the gender assigned at birth has a question of identity, not of erotic interest, and gender dysphoria is a separate diagnosis (Section 9.9). It is not a sexual dysfunction, which concerns the response cycle. And it is not an obsession: the unwanted, horrifying sexual thoughts of obsessive-compulsive disorder are experienced as alien and are not arousing, whereas a paraphilic urge is arousing (Book 3 Sections 6.5.2 and 6.5.8).

Exam trap

A paraphilia is a pattern of sexual interest (what arouses a person), defined by exclusion from interest in genital stimulation or fondling with phenotypically normal, physically mature, consenting human partners. It is not a mismatch between gender identity and assigned sex (that is gender dysphoria or, in DSM-IV, gender identity disorder), not a sexual dysfunction and not androgyny. DSM-5-TR names eight paraphilic disorders because they are relatively common and some involve criminal acts, not because they are the only paraphilias; "many dozens" exist. "Normophilic" (DSM-5) and "nonparaphilic" (DSM-5-TR) mean the same thing.

Interest is not disorder

Consider three men. The first, aged 42, is being assessed for depression after losing his job. Asked routinely about his sexual life, he mentions, without embarrassment, that since adolescence he has been strongly aroused by women's high-heeled shoes; his wife knows, they include shoes in their sexual life by mutual agreement, and he has never been troubled by it. The second, aged 35, is referred because he has been arrested three times in a year for exposing his genitals to women at a bus stop; he insists that each occasion was an accident, that he was "only relieving himself", and that he has no sexual interest in it at all. The third, aged 28, comes to a clinic of his own accord because he is ashamed and frightened of recurrent sexual fantasies about being humiliated, avoids relationships because of them, and has begun to drink to push them away. Which of them has a mental disorder?

Before 2013 the manuals blurred the answer. DSM-5 settled it with a distinction stated in one sentence: "A paraphilic disorder is a paraphilia that is currently causing distress or impairment to the individual or a paraphilia whose satisfaction has entailed personal harm, or risk of harm, to others." It then added the principle that governs the whole chapter: "A paraphilia is a necessary but not a sufficient condition for having a paraphilic disorder, and a paraphilia by itself does not necessarily justify or require clinical intervention." The first man has a paraphilia (a fetishistic interest) and no disorder. The second, if the clinician concludes that the exposures were sexually motivated, has a disorder despite his denials, because he has acted on the interest with nonconsenting strangers. The third has a disorder because his interest causes him distress and impairment, even though no one else has been involved.

Every named paraphilic disorder in DSM-5-TR is built in two parts that mirror this sentence. Criterion A specifies the nature of the paraphilia: recurrent and intense sexual arousal from a particular focus, shown by fantasies, urges or behaviours, over a period of at least 6 months. The 6 months rule out transient interests, such as those that may appear during a stressful period or under the influence of a substance. Criterion B specifies the consequences. For the disorders that involve other people who have not consented (voyeuristic, exhibitionistic, frotteuristic, sexual sadism and pedophilic disorder) it can be met in either of two ways: the person has acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person, or the urges or fantasies cause clinically significant distress or impairment (pedophilic disorder words it slightly differently: the person has acted on the urges, or they cause marked distress or interpersonal difficulty). For the disorders that need involve no victim (sexual masochism, fetishistic and transvestic disorder) only the second route exists: distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. DSM-5-TR reserves the word "diagnosis" for people who meet both criteria. A person who meets Criterion A but not Criterion B, which might happen when a harmless paraphilia comes to light during treatment for something else, "may be said to have that paraphilia but not a paraphilic disorder".

How can a clinician judge Criterion A when the person denies the interest? Each disorder's text in DSM-5-TR addresses this with the same procedure, which this book calls the ascertainment rule, after DSM-5-TR's phrase "ascertained as having". Disclosing individuals freely admit the interest. If they also report distress or psychosocial problems because of it, they can be diagnosed. If they report no distress (no anxiety, obsessions, guilt or shame about the impulses), are not impaired by the interest, and have psychiatric and legal histories showing that they do not act on it, they are said to be "ascertained as having" the sexual interest, but they should not be diagnosed. Nondisclosing individuals deny the interest, sometimes in the face of substantial objective evidence. A man who has repeatedly exposed himself to unsuspecting strangers on separate occasions but says each episode was accidental can still be diagnosed, because recurrent behaviour of this kind is itself evidence of the interest (meeting Criterion A) and simultaneously shows harm to others (meeting Criterion B). DSM-5-TR explains why it speaks of multiple victims on separate occasions: they increase confidence that the behaviour is driven by a paraphilia rather than by chance or opportunity. But multiple victims are sufficient, not necessary: fewer victims can satisfy the criterion if the person repeatedly targeted the same victim or if there is other evidence of a distinct interest, and the criteria can also be met if the person acknowledges intense interest. Figure 9.7 sets out the decision.

Two specifiers can be added to every named paraphilic disorder except pedophilic disorder. The specifier in a controlled environment applies mainly to people living in institutions or other settings, such as prisons or secure hospitals, where the opportunity to act on the urges is restricted, so that the absence of behaviour says little about the risk. The specifier in full remission applies when, for at least 5 years while living in an uncontrolled environment, the person has not acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person and has had no distress or impairment; for the disorders without victims (sexual masochism, fetishistic and transvestic disorder) the requirement is simply 5 years without distress or impairment. DSM-5-TR is careful to add that full remission says nothing about whether the paraphilia itself has gone: the interest "may still be present after behaviors and distress have remitted". Remission is of the disorder, not necessarily of the desire. Pedophilic disorder has neither specifier; instead it has its own (exclusive or nonexclusive type, the sex of the children to whom the person is attracted, and whether the behaviour is limited to incest: Section 9.6.4).

Paraphilias rarely come alone. DSM-5-TR says it is not rare for a person to have two or more. Sometimes the connection is obvious (foot fetishism and shoe fetishism), sometimes it is not, and the combination may be coincidental or may reflect some general vulnerability in psychosexual development. Either way, separate diagnoses are given if more than one paraphilia is causing suffering to the person or harm to others. And because the diagnosis has two prongs, severity can be judged in two ways: by the strength of the paraphilic interest itself, or by the seriousness of its consequences, the latter measurable with ordinary scales of functioning and quality of life.

Why did DSM-5 draw the line here? The DSM-5 Paraphilias Sub-Work Group was chaired by the sexologist Ray Blanchard (born 1945), within the Sexual and Gender Identity Disorders Work Group chaired by the psychologist Kenneth Zucker (born 1950). The change had two aims. The first was to stop labelling as mentally ill people whose unusual but harmless interests trouble no one, such as a man like the first in our three cases, or consenting adults who practise bondage and sadomasochistic sex together. Barlow and Durand explain that the change was made to accommodate the high prevalence of paraphilic-type fantasies among people without any mental disorder, citing the study by Ahlers and colleagues (2011) at the Charité hospital in Berlin: of 367 men aged 40 to 79 drawn from a community sample, 62.4 percent reported at least one paraphilia-associated pattern of sexual arousal, but only 1.7 percent said that it had caused them distress. If paraphilic fantasy alone were a disorder, most men would have one. The second aim was to keep a diagnosis for those who are harmed by their interests or who harm others. DSM-5 marked the change by retitling the chapter, from "Paraphilias" to "Paraphilic Disorders", and by adding the word "disorder" to every diagnosis: fetishism became fetishistic disorder, voyeurism voyeuristic disorder, and so on. The names without "disorder" now refer to the interest.

The change was controversial, and Barlow and Durand set out both sides. Its advocates argued that a fantasy never acted on harms no one, and that pathologising a private mental life does more harm than good; on this view a person with a persistent interest in children who never acts on it and is not distressed has something abnormal, in the way that being very tall or very short is abnormal, but not a disorder. Its critics replied that it is hard to believe that someone with a strong and persistent arousal pattern of that kind would never act on it in any way, for example by looking at illegal material on the internet, and that doing so is itself harm. The debate goes back to the question Book 3 Section 1.1 asked about every mental disorder: whether a condition is a disorder because of what it is or because of what it does.

Exam trap

A paraphilia is not a disorder: it is necessary but not sufficient for one. A shoe fetish that troubles no one is a paraphilia; exposing oneself to unsuspecting strangers meets Criterion B even without distress, because the person has acted on the urges with nonconsenting people. Criterion A requires at least 6 months. A person who denies the interest can still be diagnosed on the evidence of repeated acts against several victims on separate occasions; multiple victims are sufficient but not necessary. "In full remission" needs 5 years in an uncontrolled environment and is not available for pedophilic disorder; remission does not mean the interest has gone. DSM-5's work group on paraphilias was chaired by Ray Blanchard.

From "perversion" to "paraphilia": the history

For most of European history, unusual sexual behaviour was the business of priests and judges, not doctors: it was a sin or a crime, and "sodomy" covered almost any act not aimed at conception. In the second half of the nineteenth century physicians began to claim the territory, and the book that did most to make sexual behaviour a medical subject was Psychopathia Sexualis (1886) by the German-Austrian psychiatrist Richard von Krafft-Ebing (1840 to 1902), whose life is told in Section 9.1.3. Its subtitle, a clinical-forensic study, shows who it was written for: doctors and courts. Krafft-Ebing assumed that sexuality aimed at procreation was normal and that every other kind was a sign of disease, and he explained most of the conditions he described as the products of hereditary degeneration, a weakened nervous constitution passed down through families. His method was the case history, and the book grew through twelve German editions revised by Krafft-Ebing himself into a vast catalogue of cases of what he called perversion of the sexual instinct. Two of its words are still in use. He coined masochism from the name of the novelist Leopold von Sacher-Masoch, and he brought sadism, named after the Marquis de Sade, into medicine. The language of "perversion", with its moral overtones, dominated sexual medicine for the next half-century.

A year later, in 1887, the French psychologist Alfred Binet (1857 to 1911), whom Book 1 Section 9.2 introduces as the father of intelligence testing, published an essay in the Revue philosophique, "Le fétichisme dans l'amour" ("Fetishism in love"), which gave the word fetishism its sexual sense. A fetish, in the anthropology of his day, was an object believed to have magical power (the word comes through French from the Portuguese feitiço, a spell); Binet used it for an erotic attachment to an object or a part of the body. His explanation was strikingly modern. He suggested that in certain vulnerable individuals an emotionally intense early experience in which the object happened to be present could fix the attachment by association, an idea that anticipates the conditioning theories of Section 9.7. Krafft-Ebing and the English physician Havelock Ellis also thought fetishism arose from associations, though they disagreed about the predisposition needed.

Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) gave the perversions a central place in his theory in Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905). His first essay sorted the "sexual aberrations" into deviations in respect of the sexual object (the person or thing desired) and deviations in respect of the sexual aim (the act desired), a division that anticipates DSM-5-TR's targets and activities. His central claim was that adult perversions are not alien to normal sexuality but are fixations at, or regressions to, components of the sexuality that every child passes through, and he famously described the neuroses as "the negative" of the perversions: what the pervert acts out, the neurotic represses. In a later essay, "Fetishism" (1927), he explained the fetish as a substitute that protects a man from castration anxiety. The psychosexual stages themselves belong to the Personality book; for this chapter the point is that Freud made the perversions part of the story of normal development rather than signs of degeneration.

The word that replaced "perversion" came quietly. Coinage of paraphilia (German Paraphilie) is credited to the Croatian-Austrian ethnographer and sexologist Friedrich Salomon Krauss (1859 to 1938) in 1903; the Viennese psychoanalyst Wilhelm Stekel (1868 to 1940) used it regularly in the 1920s; and John Money (1921 to 2006), the Johns Hopkins sexologist you met in the opening story, popularised it in the 1980s as a non-pejorative term for unusual sexual interests. It entered the DSM in 1980.

The manuals then redrew the group edition by edition, and Table 9.13 summarises the changes. DSM-I (1952) had a single category, sexual deviation, placed under sociopathic personality disturbance alongside antisocial and dyssocial reaction, alcoholism and drug addiction. It had no coded subtypes: the clinician was simply to name the type of behaviour, and the manual's examples were homosexuality, transvestism, pedophilia, fetishism and sexual sadism (including rape, sexual assault and mutilation). DSM-II (1968) moved "sexual deviations" (code 302) to its group of personality disorders and certain other non-psychotic mental disorders and subdivided it: 302.0 homosexuality, 302.1 fetishism, 302.2 pedophilia, 302.3 transvestitism, 302.4 exhibitionism, 302.5 voyeurism, 302.6 sadism, 302.7 masochism, 302.8 other sexual deviation and 302.9 unspecified. Its definition is a window on the thinking of the time: the category was for individuals "whose sexual interests are directed primarily toward objects other than people of the opposite sex, toward sexual acts not usually associated with coitus, or toward coitus performed under bizarre circumstances as in necrophilia, pedophilia, sexual sadism, and fetishism", who "remain unable to substitute normal sexual behavior for them" even though many find their practices distasteful; and it was not to be used for people who performed such acts "because normal sexual objects are not available to them". The first clause made homosexuality a deviation by definition; Section 9.10 tells how it left the manual in 1973 and 1974.

DSM-III (1980) introduced the word "paraphilias" for a group within its class of psychosexual disorders, with nine categories: fetishism, transvestism, zoophilia, pedophilia, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sexual masochism, sexual sadism and atypical paraphilia. DSM-III's fetishism also excluded arousal by body parts. DSM-III-R (1987) placed the paraphilias among the "sexual disorders", added frotteurism, renamed transvestism transvestic fetishism, moved zoophilia out of the named list into paraphilia not otherwise specified, and renamed atypical paraphilia paraphilia not otherwise specified; a proposed diagnosis of "paraphilic rapism" was rejected. DSM-IV (1994) and DSM-IV-TR (2000) kept the same eight named paraphilias (exhibitionism, fetishism, frotteurism, pedophilia, sexual masochism, sexual sadism, transvestic fetishism and voyeurism) within a chapter of "sexual and gender identity disorders", with paraphilia not otherwise specified for the rest; its examples included telephone scatologia (obscene telephone calls), necrophilia (corpses), partialism (an exclusive focus on a part of the body), zoophilia (animals), coprophilia (faeces), klismaphilia (enemas) and urophilia (urine). Transvestic fetishism could still be diagnosed only in heterosexual men, a restriction that went back to DSM-III's transvestism ("cross-dressing by a heterosexual male"). DSM-5 (2013) gave the group its own chapter, "Paraphilic Disorders", placed after the personality disorders (Book 3 Section 1.8.2), renamed all eight as disorders, introduced the paraphilia and paraphilic disorder distinction, folded partialism back into fetishistic disorder (DSM-5-TR notes that it had been subsumed in fetishism before DSM-III), and dropped DSM-IV's restriction of transvestic fetishism to heterosexual men. Two proposals were considered and not adopted: Blanchard's proposal to extend pedophilia to hebephilia, sexual attraction to children in early puberty, as a "pedohebephilic disorder", and a paraphilic coercive disorder for men aroused specifically by sexual coercion. DSM-5-TR (2022) kept the structure and changed the word "normophilic" to "nonparaphilic".

Table 9.13 Paraphilias in the DSM, edition by edition.
EditionName and place of the groupNamed conditionsWhat changed
DSM-I (1952)Sexual deviation, a type of sociopathic personality disturbanceOne category, not subdividedSexual behaviour placed among the personality disturbances
DSM-II (1968)Sexual deviations (302), among personality disorders and certain other non-psychotic mental disordersHomosexuality, fetishism, pedophilia, transvestitism, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sadism, masochism, other, unspecifiedSubtypes listed; homosexuality first (replaced in the 1974 printing)
DSM-III (1980)Paraphilias, within psychosexual disordersFetishism, transvestism, zoophilia, pedophilia, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sexual masochism, sexual sadism, atypical paraphiliaThe word "paraphilia" enters the DSM; body parts excluded from fetishism
DSM-III-R (1987)Paraphilias, within sexual disordersExhibitionism, fetishism, frotteurism, pedophilia, sexual masochism, sexual sadism, transvestic fetishism, voyeurism, paraphilia NOSFrotteurism added; zoophilia moved to NOS; "paraphilic rapism" rejected
DSM-IV (1994) and DSM-IV-TR (2000)Paraphilias, within sexual and gender identity disordersThe same eight, plus paraphilia NOS (telephone scatologia, necrophilia, partialism, zoophilia, coprophilia, klismaphilia, urophilia)Transvestic fetishism still limited to heterosexual men (as since DSM-III)
DSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR (2022)Paraphilic disorders, a chapter of their ownVoyeuristic, exhibitionistic, frotteuristic, sexual masochism, sexual sadism, pedophilic, fetishistic and transvestic disorder; other specified and unspecifiedParaphilia versus paraphilic disorder; remission and controlled-environment specifiers; partialism back in fetishism; hebephilia and paraphilic coercive disorder rejected

Read down the table and two trends stand out. The first is a steady narrowing of what counts as disorder: from a category so broad that it included every non-procreative interest and homosexuality, to a list of named interests, to a distinction between interests and disorders that leaves most people with unusual interests outside psychiatry altogether. The second is a shift in the criterion of disorder, away from the content of a desire (is it "normal"?) and towards its consequences (does it cause suffering, or harm someone who has not agreed?). ICD-11, as the next subsection shows, took the second trend to its conclusion.

Exam trap

The term paraphilia entered the DSM in DSM-III (1980); DSM-I and DSM-II said "sexual deviation(s)". DSM-I placed sexual deviation under sociopathic personality disturbance; DSM-II listed homosexuality first among the sexual deviations. Frotteurism was added in DSM-III-R (1987), when zoophilia lost its own code. Krafft-Ebing (Psychopathia Sexualis, 1886) coined masochism and brought sadism into medicine; Binet (1887) gave fetishism its sexual meaning; Krauss (1903) coined "paraphilia"; Money popularised it. Freud's Three Essays (1905) treated perversions as fixations of infantile sexuality. Hebephilia was proposed for DSM-5 and not adopted.

ICD-10 to ICD-11: a group rebuilt around consent

The WHO's ICD-10 (1992) called the group disorders of sexual preference, F65, and noted that it included the paraphilias. Section 9.1.6 and Table 9.3 list its codes; what matters here is how it described them, because the descriptions show the older thinking. Fetishism (F65.0) was "reliance on some non-living object as a stimulus for sexual arousal and sexual gratification". Fetishistic transvestism (F65.1) was the wearing of clothes of the opposite sex principally to obtain sexual excitement, distinguished from the cross-dressing of transsexualism by its clear association with arousal and the strong wish to remove the clothes once arousal subsides. Exhibitionism (F65.2) and voyeurism (F65.3) were recurrent tendencies to expose the genitals to strangers or to watch people undressing or having sex without their knowledge. Paedophilia (F65.4) was "a sexual preference for children, boys or girls or both, usually of prepubertal or early pubertal age", a wider age range than DSM's. Sadomasochism (F65.5) was a single category, a preference for sexual activity involving the infliction of pain or humiliation, or bondage, called masochism in the recipient and sadism in the provider, with the remark that many people obtain excitement from both. F65.6 covered multiple disorders of sexual preference, noting that the commonest combination is fetishism, transvestism and sadomasochism, and F65.8 gathered the rest: obscene telephone calls, rubbing against people in crowded public places (frotteurism), sexual activity with animals, use of strangulation or anoxia to intensify sexual excitement, and necrophilia. What ICD-10 did not do was distinguish a harmless preference from a harmful one: the label described the content of the interest.

ICD-11 (adopted by the World Health Assembly in May 2019 and in force from 1 January 2022) kept the group in Chapter 6, Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders, renamed it paraphilic disorders, and redefined it around consent. Its description reads: "Paraphilic disorders are characterised by persistent and intense patterns of atypical sexual arousal, manifested by sexual thoughts, fantasies, urges, or behaviours, the focus of which involves others whose age or status renders them unwilling or unable to consent and on which the person has acted or by which he or she is markedly distressed." The examples ICD-11 gives of people who cannot consent are prepubertal children, an unsuspecting person watched through a window and an animal. Six codes cover such disorders: 6D30 exhibitionistic disorder, 6D31 voyeuristic disorder, 6D32 pedophilic disorder, 6D33 coercive sexual sadism disorder, 6D34 frotteuristic disorder and 6D35 other paraphilic disorder involving non-consenting individuals, whose examples are arousal patterns involving corpses or animals. For every one of them the person must have acted on the arousal pattern or be markedly distressed by it.

Then comes a seventh code for everything else. 6D36, paraphilic disorder involving solitary behaviour or consenting individuals, can be given for a persistent and intense pattern of atypical arousal involving consenting adults or solitary behaviour only if one of two conditions is met: either the person is markedly distressed by the nature of the arousal pattern, and the distress "is not simply a consequence of rejection or feared rejection of the arousal pattern by others"; or the behaviour involves a significant risk of injury or death to the person or to a partner, ICD-11's example being asphyxophilia, sexual arousal from restriction of breathing, which can kill by accident. The group ends with 6D3Z, paraphilic disorders, unspecified; there is no 6D3Y. ICD-11 then states the principle plainly: atypical patterns of sexual arousal that do not involve others whose age or status renders them unwilling or unable to consent, and that are not associated with marked distress or significant risk of injury or death, "are not considered to be Paraphilic Disorders".

Notice the subtle difference from DSM-5-TR in that clause about distress. Under DSM-5-TR, the third man in Section 9.5.2, ashamed and frightened of his masochistic fantasies, meets Criterion B because his interest distresses him; DSM-5-TR counts guilt and shame as distress. ICD-11 asks a further question: is he distressed by the nature of the arousal pattern itself, or only because others reject it or because he fears they would? A man whose only suffering comes from his community's disapproval would not receive a diagnosis in ICD-11. The rule is the same one ICD-11 applies to compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (Section 9.5.7), and it carries the lesson of the history of homosexuality (Section 9.10): distress that comes from stigma is not evidence of a disorder in the person.

The descriptions add several cautions. Paraphilic disorders should not be diagnosed in children and should be diagnosed only with utmost caution in adolescents, because sexual experimentation is typical in adolescence and sexual acts may occur impulsively or opportunistically rather than as part of a recurrent pattern. Many sexual crimes, ICD-11 says, involve behaviour that is not associated with a sustained underlying paraphilic arousal pattern; it may be transient, impulsive, opportunistic or related to intoxication, and in such cases a paraphilic disorder should not be diagnosed. Arrest, incarceration and impairment may follow, but they are not diagnostic requirements. And paraphilic disorders frequently co-occur with mood, anxiety and substance use disorders, and with one another.

What did ICD-11 remove? The recommendations came from the WHO's Working Group on the Classification of Sexual Disorders and Sexual Health, and were set out by Richard Krueger, Geoffrey Reed, Michael First and their colleagues in the Archives of Sexual Behavior in 2017. The group recommended that the paraphilic disorders be "limited to disorders that involve sexual arousal patterns that focus on non-consenting others or are associated with substantial distress or direct risk of injury or death". Consistent with that framework, the ICD-10 categories of fetishism, fetishistic transvestism and sadomasochism were removed, and new categories were added: coercive sexual sadism disorder, frotteuristic disorder (until then hidden in F65.8), and the two "other" categories for non-consenting individuals and for solitary or consenting behaviour. The working group based its proposals on the WHO's role as a global public health agency and the ICD's function as a public health reporting tool. The practical meaning is that a consenting adult who enjoys a fetish, cross-dressing or sadomasochistic sex with a willing partner has no ICD-11 diagnosis at all, unless the pattern causes marked distress of the right kind or carries a serious risk of injury or death. Sadism survives only in its coercive form: ICD-11 says that coercive sexual sadism disorder "specifically excludes consensual sexual sadism and masochism".

Why did the paraphilic disorders stay in Chapter 6 when the sexual dysfunctions and gender incongruence moved to the new Chapter 17, Conditions related to sexual health (Section 9.1.6)? The working group gave its reasons in the 2017 paper by Krueger and colleagues. It did consider moving the paraphilic disorders into the new chapter, because they are sexual in nature, but recommended keeping them in Chapter 6 for two reasons: they meet the general requirements for a mental disorder, and their status as mental disorders matters forensically, since legal processes such as civil commitment depend on it (Section 9.5.6). The other groups moved for reasons of their own: the sexual dysfunctions because ICD-10's split between "organic" and "non-organic" dysfunction did not fit the evidence, and gender incongruence because it is not regarded as a mental disorder, while keeping a code lets people obtain health care. The paraphilic disorders, as ICD-11 now defines them, are restricted to patterns of arousal and behaviour that harm or endanger others or cause the person marked distress, and their assessment and treatment are the business of mental health and forensic services. Table 9.14 sets the three classifications side by side, and Figure 9.8 shows the new structure.
