Exam trap

ICD-11 kept the paraphilic disorders in Chapter 6, while the sexual dysfunctions, the sexual pain disorders and gender incongruence, including gender incongruence of childhood (HA61, as well as HA60 for adolescence or adulthood), moved to Chapter 17. The working group's reasons: paraphilic disorders meet the general requirements for a mental disorder, and their legal (forensic) use depends on that status. Every named ICD-11 paraphilic disorder involves people who have not consented or cannot consent; solitary or consensual interests are coded 6D36 only with marked distress (not merely from others' rejection) or significant risk of injury or death. "Coercive sexual sadism disorder" (6D33) is ICD-11's term; DSM-5-TR says "sexual sadism disorder". ICD-11 has no fetishistic or transvestic disorder and no sadomasochism category: these ICD-10 codes (F65.0, F65.1, F65.5) were deleted. Frotteuristic disorder gained its own code (6D34). There is no 6D3Y. Compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72) is an impulse control disorder, not a paraphilic disorder.

Who has paraphilias, and how common they are

The first and most striking fact is sex. In clinics and courts, paraphilic disorders are overwhelmingly a male matter. DSM-5-TR says that voyeuristic disorder is very uncommon among women in clinical settings, that fetishistic disorder is "nearly exclusively reported in men" in clinical samples, and that people with sexual sadism disorder in forensic samples are almost exclusively men. Barlow and Durand note that paraphilic disorders were long thought to be absent in women, with the possible exception of sadomasochistic practices, and that current estimates suggest about 5 to 10 percent of sexual offenders are women. The gap narrows outside the clinic. For single voyeuristic acts the male to female ratio in surveys is about 2 to 1 or 3 to 1, and DSM-5-TR says the gender difference in fetishism is smaller for fantasy than for behaviour. So part of the male excess lies in who acts on interests and who comes to the attention of clinics and police; part may lie in development, and Section 9.7 examines the explanations.

The second fact is that paraphilic interests are far commoner than paraphilic disorders. You have already met the Berlin study by Ahlers and colleagues (Section 9.5.2), in which most men reported at least one paraphilia-associated arousal pattern but very few were distressed by it. A national survey from Sweden makes the same point for behaviour, and because it sampled the general population rather than clinics or prisons it is the study most often cited on the question.

Key study: exhibitionistic and voyeuristic behaviour in a Swedish national survey (Långström and Seto, 2006)

The question. Almost everything known about exhibitionism and voyeurism came from men who had been arrested or referred for treatment. How common are these behaviours in ordinary people, and what goes with them? The method. Niklas Långström and Michael C. Seto analysed a national survey of sexuality and health in which 2,450 randomly selected Swedes aged 18 to 60 had been interviewed in 1996. The results. Seventy-six people (3.1 percent) reported at least one incident of being sexually aroused by exposing their genitals to a stranger, and 191 (7.7 percent) at least one incident of being sexually aroused by spying on others having sex. Both behaviours were associated with being male, with more psychological problems and lower life satisfaction, with greater alcohol and drug use, and with greater sexual interest and activity in general (more partners, more frequent masturbation, more use of pornography), and people who reported either were much more likely to report other atypical sexual behaviour, such as sadomasochism or cross-dressing.

What it means, and its limits. Paraphilia-like behaviour is far from rare in the community and clusters with other sexual and psychological features, as clinical studies had suggested. But "at least one incident" of arousal is not a recurrent, intense pattern over 6 months, still less a disorder; the data are self-reported; and one country in one year may not generalise. Prevalence figures for each named disorder, including the large Quebec survey, are given in Section 9.6.

The third fact concerns age. Paraphilic interests usually become apparent around puberty or in adolescence; DSM-5-TR says, for example, that men with voyeuristic disorder often first become aware of the interest during adolescence. The diagnosis is nevertheless held back until adulthood for most disorders (voyeuristic disorder requires the person to be at least 18), because adolescent sexual curiosity is normal and DSM-5-TR wants to avoid pathologising it, as ICD-11 does with its caution about adolescents. Once established, the interests are thought to be long-lasting, and Barlow and Durand write that paraphilic disorders run a chronic course and that recurrence is common; but the behaviour, the distress and the impairment can change over time, with life circumstances, psychiatric illness, hypersexuality and impulsivity, and DSM-5-TR notes that advancing age may be associated with decreasing paraphilic preferences and behaviour, as with other sexual preferences. When paraphilic behaviour appears for the first time late in life, the clinician should think first of a brain disorder (Book 3 Chapter 10, on the neurocognitive disorders).

The fourth fact is that paraphilias cluster. Barlow and Durand write that their patients often present with two, three or more patterns, of which one is usually dominant. The classic study is by Gene G. Abel and his colleagues (1988), who collected self-reports, under an assurance of confidentiality, from 561 men with paraphilias who were not in prison. Contrary to the psychiatric literature of the time, which expected each man to have one kind of deviant behaviour, they found that most had experience with several different types, as many as ten, often crossing the lines of the victims' sex, age and family relationship. Confidentiality was the key to the finding, because men facing prosecution rarely admit more than they must. The clustering is why DSM-5-TR gives separate diagnoses for each paraphilia that causes suffering or harm, why ICD-10 had a category for multiple disorders of sexual preference, and why clinicians assessing one paraphilia ask about others (Section 9.7.4).

The fifth fact is comorbidity. In a study of 120 consecutively assessed outpatient men with paraphilias or with nonparaphilic hypersexual behaviour, the American psychiatrist Martin P. Kafka (born 1947) and his colleague John Hennen (2002) found mood disorders in 71.6 percent (especially early-onset dysthymia), substance abuse in 40.8 percent (especially alcohol), anxiety disorders in 38.3 percent (especially social phobia) and, retrospectively diagnosed, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) in 35.8 percent; ADHD was linked to conduct disorder, to having several paraphilias and to a higher chance of imprisonment. DSM-5-TR's comorbidity sections tell the same story: for exhibitionistic disorder, for instance, it lists depressive, bipolar, anxiety and substance use disorders, hypersexuality, ADHD, other paraphilic disorders and antisocial personality disorder. Finally, the differential diagnosis: people in a manic episode, with a major neurocognitive disorder, an intellectual developmental disorder, a personality change due to a medical condition, substance intoxication or schizophrenia may become sexually disinhibited and behave in ways that resemble a paraphilic disorder; DSM-5-TR says the diagnosis should not be made unless the behaviour also occurs outside such states. And in conduct disorder and antisocial personality disorder, sexual offences occur among many other kinds of rule-breaking, usually without the specific paraphilic interest.

Exam trap

In the Swedish survey of Långström and Seto (2,450 adults; interviews in 1996, published 2006), 3.1 percent reported at least one incident of arousal from exposing themselves to a stranger and 7.7 percent from watching others have sex. Barlow and Durand print the first figure as "31%", a slip for 3.1 percent (the original reports 76 of 2,450 people); if an option offers 31 percent, it comes from that misprint. Women make up about 5 to 10 percent of sexual offenders. Abel and colleagues (1988) showed that many men with paraphilias have several, under assured confidentiality. Paraphilic behaviour appearing only during mania, intoxication or dementia is not diagnosed as a paraphilic disorder.

Paraphilia, sexual offending and the law

It is easy to assume that sexual offenders have paraphilias and that people with paraphilias are sexual offenders. Both assumptions are wrong, and a clinician who works with courts must keep the two categories apart. A sexual offence is a legal category, defined by a country's criminal law: what is an offence in one country, or one decade, may not be in another. A paraphilic disorder is a clinical category, defined by a pattern of arousal and its consequences. The two overlap, but less than people think.

Consider rape. Barlow and Durand point out that it is not classified as a paraphilic disorder because most instances are better characterised as an assault by a man whose patterns of sexual arousal are not paraphilic; many rapists instead meet the criteria for antisocial personality disorder (Book 3 Chapter 11). Only a minority show a sadistic arousal pattern (Section 9.6.3). The same is true of offences against children: as many as 40 to 50 percent of men who sexually abuse children do not show pedophilic arousal patterns and do not meet criteria for pedophilic disorder; their offences are associated instead with antisocial, aggressive and opportunistic behaviour. ICD-11 makes the same point in its own words: many sexual crimes are not associated with a sustained paraphilic arousal pattern, and may be transient, impulsive, opportunistic or related to intoxication, in which case no paraphilic disorder should be diagnosed.

The reverse is also true: many people with a paraphilic interest never offend. The best-known demonstration is the Prevention Project Dunkelfeld, begun in Berlin in June 2005. Dunkelfeld is German for the "dark field", the hidden figure of crimes that never come to light. The project ran a media campaign inviting people who are sexually attracted to children, who want help to control their urges and who are unknown to the legal authorities, to come forward for confidential treatment free of charge. More than a thousand people responded within its first few years. Its existence makes a point that the DSM-5 distinction depends on: there are people who have a paraphilic interest, who are distressed by it or afraid of acting on it, and who seek help before anyone is harmed. Section 9.6.4 describes pedophilic disorder and Section 9.7 the treatments.

Why does DSM-5-TR insist that a paraphilia by itself does not justify or require clinical intervention? Partly to protect people with harmless interests, as Section 9.5.2 explained. But also because the diagnosis has legal force. In some countries, notably the United States, a diagnosed mental disorder is a precondition for the civil commitment of sexual offenders after they finish their prison sentences, under laws aimed at "sexually violent predators", and paraphilic diagnoses, including the contested "other specified paraphilic disorder, non-consent", have been used in such proceedings. DSM-5-TR's Cautionary Statement for Forensic Use of DSM-5 addresses this directly. It warns that the definition of mental disorder was written for clinicians and researchers rather than for courts, that a clinical diagnosis such as pedophilic disorder does not imply that a person meets a legal standard for mental illness, responsibility or disability, and that a particular diagnosis "does not imply a specific level of risk". A diagnosis describes a pattern; it does not, on its own, predict what a person will do. Risk is assessed with separate tools such as the Static-99 (Book 2 Section 10.9.5), and offender treatment is organised by the risk-need-responsivity model and the Good Lives Model (Book 2 Section 10.9.8).

In India, the law that a clinician meets most often in this area is the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act, 2012, which defines a child as anyone under 18 years and creates a duty to report sexual offences against children; Book 2 Section 10.6.5 explains it and the clinician's reporting obligations. The general criminal law changed recently: the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023, in force from 1 July 2024, replaced the Indian Penal Code of 1860, and it retains the earlier offences of rape, voyeurism, stalking and insulting the modesty of a woman, among others. Behaviours that a clinician might see as voyeuristic, exhibitionistic or frotteuristic can therefore also be crimes, whatever their diagnosis, and the person's legal situation will shape both the referral and the limits of confidentiality.

Clinical link: the client who has not offended

Occasionally a client discloses, often after weeks of building trust, an attraction he finds frightening and has never acted on. The clinical psychologist's tasks are to stay calm and non-judgemental, to assess what the interest is and whether there is any current risk to a particular person, especially a child, to explain clearly at the outset the limits of confidentiality (including the duty to report where a child is at risk, Book 2 Section 10.6.5), and to offer or refer for treatment. Treating such a client as an offender drives the problem underground; the Dunkelfeld project exists because help offered early, before anyone is harmed, is the most direct form of prevention.

Exam trap

A sexual offence is a legal category; a paraphilic disorder is a clinical one. Rape is not a paraphilic disorder: most rapists are not paraphilic, and many meet criteria for antisocial personality disorder. As many as 40 to 50 percent of men who sexually abuse children show no pedophilic arousal pattern, and many people with pedophilic interest never offend (the Dunkelfeld project, Berlin, 2005). DSM-5-TR's forensic caution: a diagnosis does not imply a legal standard or a specific level of risk. India: POCSO Act 2012 (a child is anyone under 18); the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita replaced the Indian Penal Code from 1 July 2024.

Hypersexuality and compulsive sexual behaviour

One more problem of sexual interest needs a place, although it is not about what arouses a person but about how much and how uncontrollably. Clinicians have long seen people whose sexual behaviour, conventional in content, has become excessive, driven and damaging: hours each day of pornography or casual encounters, money spent, a marriage or a job lost, repeated failed attempts to stop. Older medicine had names for it, nymphomania in women and satyriasis in men, and ICD-10 kept them as inclusion terms under excessive sexual drive, F52.7, which, oddly, placed the problem among the sexual dysfunctions.

The modern debate began with hypersexuality. Martin Kafka, whose comorbidity research you met in Section 9.5.5, studied men whose sexual behaviour was conventional in content but excessive in amount, which he called paraphilia-related disorders, and compared them with men with paraphilias. In a 2003 study with Hennen he found the two groups strikingly similar: both reported long periods, lasting on average about six years, of very high sexual activity, with a peak "total sexual outlet" (Kinsey's term for orgasms from all sources) of about 12 a week on average. In 2010, in the Archives of Sexual Behavior, Kafka proposed a new diagnosis for DSM-5, hypersexual disorder, a recurrent and intense pattern of sexual fantasies, urges and behaviour pursued to excess, despite consequences and often as a way of coping with low mood or stress. The DSM-5 Sexual and Gender Identity Disorders Work Group recommended it, but it was not adopted, and DSM-5-TR contains no such diagnosis. Proposals to recognise "sex addiction" have fared no better: DSM-5-TR says that "behavioral addictions" such as "sex addiction" are not included because there is insufficient peer-reviewed evidence to establish the diagnostic criteria and course descriptions needed to identify them as mental disorders, and its only behavioural addiction is gambling disorder (Book 3 Chapter 10). In DSM-5-TR, hypersexuality appears only as a feature of other conditions, notably in the comorbidity sections of several paraphilic disorders.

ICD-11 took a different route. It created compulsive sexual behaviour disorder, 6C72, and placed it among the impulse control disorders of Chapter 6, next to pyromania and kleptomania, not among the paraphilic disorders and not among the addictions. ICD-11 describes it as "a persistent pattern of failure to control intense, repetitive sexual impulses or urges resulting in repetitive sexual behaviour". The pattern shows itself in one or more ways: repetitive sexual activity has become a central focus of the person's life, to the point of neglecting health, personal care or other interests, activities and responsibilities; the person has made numerous unsuccessful efforts to control or significantly reduce it; the behaviour continues despite adverse consequences, such as marital conflict, financial or legal problems or harm to health; and it continues even when it brings little or no satisfaction. The pattern must be present over an extended period, for example 6 months or more; it must not be better explained by another mental disorder, such as a manic episode, by a medical condition or by a substance; and it must cause marked distress or significant impairment in personal, family, social, educational, occupational or other important areas of functioning. Then comes the clause that echoes the paraphilic disorders: "Distress that is entirely related to moral judgments and disapproval about sexual impulses, urges, or behaviours is not sufficient to meet this requirement."

That last clause matters in practice. ICD-11 notes that the disorder may be expressed through sex with others, masturbation, pornography, cybersex, telephone sex and other repetitive sexual behaviour, and that people with it often turn to sexual behaviour in response to depression, anxiety, boredom, loneliness or other negative feelings. It also observes that people who judge their own sexual behaviour on religious or moral grounds, or who worry about others' disapproval, may describe themselves as "sex addicts" or call their behaviour "compulsive", and it asks the clinician to examine carefully whether that perception comes only from such judgements or whether impaired control and the other requirements are really present. People with a high sex drive who keep control of their behaviour and are not distressed or impaired should not be diagnosed, and nor should the high levels of sexual interest and masturbation that are common in adolescence. A young man from a conservative family who masturbates a few times a week and feels intense guilt about it does not have compulsive sexual behaviour disorder; he may need information and reassurance, and perhaps help with guilt, but not a diagnosis. In India, where the guilt that surrounds masturbation and semen loss is common (Book 3 Section 1.10.4), this distinction saves many young men from a label they do not need. The differential diagnosis includes mania, in which increased and reckless sexual activity is a classic symptom (Book 3 Chapter 5), and the effects of stimulant drugs (Book 3 Chapter 10); the impulse control disorders as a group are taught in Book 3 Chapter 11.

Exam trap

Compulsive sexual behaviour disorder is 6C72, an impulse control disorder in ICD-11 Chapter 6 (with pyromania, kleptomania and intermittent explosive disorder). It is not a paraphilic disorder (the content of the behaviour is usually conventional), not classed as an addiction, and not in DSM-5-TR at all. It needs a persistent failure to control intense, repetitive sexual urges, over about 6 months or more, with marked distress or impairment; distress entirely due to moral judgements or disapproval does not count. Kafka's hypersexual disorder (2010) was proposed for DSM-5 and rejected. "Sex addiction" is a diagnosis in neither manual. ICD-10's F52.7 excessive sexual drive included nymphomania and satyriasis.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

9.6 The Eight Paraphilic Disorders and the Other ParaphiliasMedium yield · 2 Qs

Section 9.5 built the machinery. A paraphilia is an intense and persistent sexual interest outside the ordinary range; a paraphilic disorder is a paraphilia that is now causing the person distress or impairment, or whose satisfaction has harmed someone else or put someone at risk of harm. Criterion A of every criteria set describes the interest, and Criterion B describes its consequences. This section runs eight particular interests through that machinery. Out of the "many dozens" of paraphilias that have been described, DSM-5-TR gives named criteria to only eight, and it explains the choice in one sentence: these eight are relatively common compared with the rest, and several of them involve acts that, because they are noxious or harmful to others, are crimes. Everything else goes into a residual category, other specified paraphilic disorder, which Section 9.6.7 explains. Before you meet the disorders one by one, it is worth seeing how DSM-5-TR arranges them, because the arrangement itself carries information that examiners like to test.

The manual sorts the eight into two families, shown in Figure 9.9. The first family is defined by anomalous activity preferences: what the person wants to do. Within it, the courtship disorders are voyeuristic, exhibitionistic and frotteuristic disorder, so called because each resembles a distorted fragment of ordinary human courtship, which begins with looking, moves on to showing oneself and talking, and then to touching. Section 9.7.2 explains the theory behind that name. The algolagnic disorders (from the Greek algos, pain, and lagneia, lust) are sexual masochism disorder and sexual sadism disorder, in which the arousal is bound up with pain and suffering, received or inflicted. The second family is defined by anomalous target preferences: whom or what the interest is directed at. One is directed at other human beings, pedophilic disorder, and two are directed elsewhere, at objects or body parts (fetishistic disorder) and at the act of dressing in the clothes of the other sex (transvestic disorder).

Read the eight criteria sets side by side and a second pattern appears, which the stars in Figure 9.9 mark. Five of the disorders can involve another person who has not agreed: voyeuristic, exhibitionistic, frotteuristic, sexual sadism and pedophilic disorder. Their Criterion B has two routes. The person either has acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person, or the urges and fantasies cause clinically significant distress or impairment (for pedophilic disorder the wording is "marked distress or interpersonal difficulty"). The other three, sexual masochism, fetishistic and transvestic disorder, involve no one who has not consented, so their Criterion B has only one route: distress or impairment. The logic is the logic of DSM-5 itself. A man who exposes himself to strangers on a platform has the disorder even if he says he feels perfectly happy, because someone else has been harmed; a man with a boot fetish who is content, and whose partner happily shares it, has a paraphilia and no disorder at all. Every set also requires that the interest has lasted at least 6 months. The specifiers "in a controlled environment" and "in full remission" (no acting on the urges and no distress or impairment for at least 5 years outside an institution), explained in Section 9.5.2, are available for seven of the eight; pedophilic disorder is the exception, for a reason Section 9.6.4 explains. Table 9.15 gathers the eight in one place, with their codes.

Table 9.15 The eight DSM-5-TR paraphilic disorders. Each needs at least 6 months of recurrent, intense arousal (Criterion A). Codes: DSM-5-TR's ICD-10-CM code, then ICD-11 (Chapter 6).

Fetishistic (F65.0; none)Non-living objects or specific non-genital body partsTarget: elsewhereDistress or impairment only; not cross-dressing clothes or genital devicesNo figure given
