Two more things are shared, and learning them once saves effort later. First, the three courtship disorders have the same differential diagnosis. People in a manic episode, people intoxicated with alcohol or another substance, and people with a major neurocognitive disorder, an intellectual developmental disorder, schizophrenia or a personality change due to a medical condition such as a frontal brain injury may become sexually disinhibited and peep, expose themselves or grope. The paraphilic disorder is diagnosed only if the behaviour also happens outside those states. Conduct disorder in adolescents and antisocial personality disorder in adults are the other shared alternative: a person who breaks many rules may commit a sexual act of this kind, but the specific, recurrent sexual interest is usually missing. Second, DSM-5-TR rewrote the prevalence statements of several of these disorders. Many older textbooks and question banks still print the 2013 figures, so for each disorder this section gives the DSM-5-TR figure and, where it matters, warns you about the older one.

Exam trap

The courtship disorders are voyeuristic, exhibitionistic and frotteuristic; the algolagnic disorders are masochism and sadism; pedophilic, fetishistic and transvestic disorders are the "target" group. Only sexual masochism, fetishistic and transvestic disorder are diagnosed on distress or impairment alone. For the other five, acting on the urges with a nonconsenting person satisfies Criterion B even when the person reports no distress at all.

Voyeuristic disorder

The word comes from the French voir, to see: a voyeur is one who watches. Voyeuristic disorder is the courtship disorder of looking. Its Criterion A is recurrent and intense sexual arousal, lasting at least 6 months and shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour, from observing an unsuspecting person who is naked, in the process of undressing, or engaged in sexual activity. The single word that carries the diagnosis is unsuspecting. Watching a willing partner undress, watching pornography made by consenting adults, or watching a performer who knows she is being watched is not voyeurism, however much the watcher enjoys it. What arouses the voyeur is the secrecy itself: that the person does not know, and has not agreed. That is also why the act harms the person watched, even if she never finds out, and why Criterion B can be met in either of two ways: the person has acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person, or the urges or fantasies cause clinically significant distress or impairment.

Criterion C is unique to this disorder among the courtship group: the person must be at least 18 years old. DSM-5-TR gives its reason plainly. Adolescence and puberty bring a surge of sexual curiosity, and a 14-year-old boy who peeps at a neighbour is doing something wrong, but he is very hard to tell apart from an ordinary curious adolescent. The age limit exists "to reduce the risk of pathologizing normative sexual interest and behavior during pubertal adolescence". The adolescent's behaviour can still be addressed, as a matter of discipline, of conduct or of the law; what the rule prevents is giving a teenager a lifelong-sounding psychiatric label for a stage that most outgrow.

How does a clinician decide that the interest is recurrent when the person denies it? Section 9.5.2 explained the general rule of ascertainment, and voyeuristic disorder is the example DSM-5-TR uses to teach it. A man who has been caught spying on unsuspecting people on several separate occasions, with several different victims, may say that each episode was an accident or had nothing to do with sex. Repeated behaviour of this kind is itself enough to establish both criteria: the behaviour shows the interest (Criterion A), and the behaviour has harmed others (Criterion B). Multiple victims on separate occasions raise the clinician's confidence, but they are sufficient rather than necessary. Repeated watching of the same person, or other evidence of a preferential interest in secret watching, can do the same job, and so can a person's frank acknowledgement of an intense interest.

How common is it? Here DSM-5-TR is careful to separate acts from disorder. Voyeuristic acts, it says, are "the most common of potentially law-breaking sexual behaviors". In a survey of 1,040 adults in the Canadian province of Quebec, interviewed by telephone and online and chosen to match the province's population (Christian C. Joyal and Carpentier, published in the Journal of Sex Research in 2017), 34.5 percent reported having done something voyeuristic at least once in their lives: 50.3 percent of men and 21.2 percent of women. Only 9.6 percent, however, reported an "intense desire" to do it, and only 2.1 percent reported doing it persistently, so the prevalence of the disorder must be far lower than the prevalence of the act. The ratio of men to women for single voyeuristic acts was about 2:1 in the Quebec sample and about 3:1 in a Swedish general-population sample, but in clinics the disorder is very uncommon among women. Among offenders the figures are different again: in a study of 1,346 men imprisoned in Austria for sexual offences, 3.7 percent had voyeuristic disorder. The true population prevalence of the disorder is simply unknown. How the question is asked matters a great deal. In the Quebec survey, people answering online admitted more paraphilic interest than those interviewed by telephone, and a door-to-door survey of 1,529 sexually active adults in a rural area of south India (Suttur village, about 25 kilometres from Mysuru), in which people were interviewed at home about a range of sexual problems, recorded no paraphilias at all (Sathyanarayana Rao, Darshan and Tandon, 2015). Interests of this kind are kept hidden, and the more private and anonymous the method, the more of them it finds.

The course follows a pattern you will see repeatedly in this section. Men with the disorder usually become aware of the interest in adolescence. Whether it persists is unclear, and the distress and impairment that make it a disorder can wax and wane with other things in a person's life: depression, the general intensity of his sex drive, his impulsivity. As with sexual preferences generally, voyeuristic interest and behaviour tend to decline with advancing age. Childhood sexual abuse, substance misuse and a preoccupation with sex (hypersexuality) have been suggested as risk factors, but DSM-5-TR is cautious: the causal link is uncertain and the factors are not specific to voyeurism. The conditions that most often accompany the disorder are hypersexuality and other paraphilic disorders, especially exhibitionistic disorder, along with depressive, bipolar, anxiety and substance use disorders, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, conduct disorder and antisocial personality disorder. Remember that these comorbidity figures come largely from men who have been caught or convicted, and may not apply to a man who seeks help on his own.

Two clinical observations help to make sense of what voyeurs say. David Barlow and V. Mark Durand point out, from their own clinic, that the risk of being caught is often part of the excitement: one of their patients, who had begun peeping into windows at 14, found that the threat of arrest actually increased his arousal, and many voyeurs, they note, "just don't get the same satisfaction" from a strip show. The second observation is about technology. The oldest image of the voyeur is a man at a window or a keyhole. Today the same interest is pursued with a phone held over a cubicle wall, a camera hidden in a changing room, a hostel bathroom or a rented flat, or a lens pointed up a staircase, and the images can be copied and shared in seconds. Non-consensual recording and sharing of intimate images multiplies the harm, because the victim loses control of her image for good. These are offences in Indian law as in most countries (Section 9.5.6 gives the legal pointers); for the clinician, the point is that the diagnosis is the same whatever the device.

Clinical link: Jatin

Jatin, 24, is in the second year of a master's course and lives in a university hostel. One night the warden found him crouched outside the shared bathrooms, holding his phone to a gap in a ventilator. The phone held short clips of other residents in the shower, recorded over several months. The college suspended him and made a clinical assessment a condition of any return. In the clinic he speaks so quietly that the psychologist has to lean forward. He first looked through a gap in a neighbour's window at 15, he says, and the urge comes in waves, worst when he is tense before examinations. While he is filming he feels a rush of excitement and relief; within minutes it turns into a sick wave of shame, and he has deleted the clips and sworn never to do it again more times than he can count. He has had one brief relationship, and says that ordinary closeness "felt like nothing".

Jatin meets Criterion A (years of recurrent, intense arousal from watching people who did not know they were being watched), Criterion B twice over (he has acted on the urges with nonconsenting people, and he is distressed by them) and Criterion C (he is over 18). The psychologist also asks what DSM-5-TR asks: was any of it during intoxication or a manic episode (no), is there a wider pattern of rule-breaking that would suggest antisocial personality disorder rather than a specific sexual interest (no), and are there other paraphilic interests, especially exposing himself (he denies them). His shame is clinically important. It makes him more likely to engage in treatment, and it tells the clinician that he experiences the urges as unwanted, which shapes the plan described in Section 9.7.

Exam trap

Voyeuristic disorder requires an unsuspecting person and a minimum age of 18; it is the only paraphilic disorder with that age rule (pedophilic disorder has its own rule, at least 16 and at least 5 years older than the child, and exhibitionistic disorder has no minimum age). Watching consenting people or pornography is not voyeurism. Older books print the DSM-5 (2013) estimate that the "highest possible" lifetime prevalence of voyeuristic disorder is about 12 percent in men and 4 percent in women; DSM-5-TR dropped it and says the prevalence of the disorder is unknown, while voyeuristic acts are the most common potentially law-breaking sexual behaviour.

Exhibitionistic and frotteuristic disorders

The second courtship disorder is the disorder of showing. Exhibitionistic disorder (from the Latin exhibere, to hold out or display) is recurrent and intense sexual arousal, for at least 6 months, from exposing one's genitals to an unsuspecting person, shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour. Criterion B has the same two routes as voyeuristic disorder: the person has acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person, or the urges cause clinically significant distress or impairment. Again the word "unsuspecting" does the work. The arousal comes not from nakedness as such but from the moment of exposure to a stranger who did not expect it, and often from the stranger's shock. Barlow and Durand describe a successful lawyer who could become aroused only by riding a city bus until a young woman of the right age got on, exposing himself just before the next stop and running off the bus. When he asked a girlfriend to role-play the scene in his flat, nothing happened: a willing audience was not what his arousal required. As with voyeurism, the risk of being caught was part of the thrill, and the urge was costing him his career.

Notice two differences from voyeuristic disorder, both of them favourite exam points. First, exhibitionistic disorder has no minimum age. DSM-5-TR concedes that exposure by an adolescent can be hard to distinguish from age-appropriate sexual curiosity, but it leaves the judgement to the clinician instead of writing an age into the criteria. Second, exhibitionistic disorder has a specifier based on the people to whom the person exposes himself: sexually aroused by exposing his genitals to prepubertal children, to physically mature individuals, or to both. DSM-5-TR calls these subtypes and explains why they exist: to draw attention to the characteristics of the victims, so that co-occurring pedophilic disorder is not overlooked. The two diagnoses can sit side by side. A man who exposes himself only to young children may have both exhibitionistic and pedophilic disorder, while a man who exposes himself to whoever happens to be there, regardless of age, may have exhibitionistic disorder alone (Section 9.6.4).

How common is it? The disorder's population prevalence is unknown, and as a disorder it is highly unusual in women, but single exhibitionistic acts are not uncommon, and DSM-5-TR says they occur up to half as often among women as among men. In the Quebec survey, 30.9 percent of adults reported exhibitionistic behaviour at some point in their lives (32.6 percent of men and 29.4 percent of women), but only 4.8 percent reported an intense desire for it and 0.8 percent persistent behaviour. DSM-5-TR adds a Swedish estimate of 4.1 percent in men and 2.1 percent in women, and calls it the lifetime prevalence of exhibitionistic disorder. Read that figure with care. It comes from a national survey of 2,450 Swedish adults by Niklas Långström and Michael C. Seto (2006), in which 76 people, 3.1 percent of the sample (about 4 percent of the men and 2 percent of the women, which is where the 4.1 and 2.1 come from), reported at least one incident of being sexually aroused by exposing their genitals to a stranger. It counts people who had done it at least once, not people who meet the criteria for a disorder.

Men with exhibitionistic disorder usually say that they became aware of the interest in adolescence, somewhat later than people usually become aware of ordinary sexual interest. Very little is known about how long it lasts, though, as with the other paraphilias, it tends to fade with age. The risk factors DSM-5-TR lists are revealing. An antisocial history, antisocial personality disorder, alcohol misuse and a pedophilic sexual preference all seem to raise the risk that a man convicted of exposure will commit another sexual offence, so DSM-5-TR treats them as risk factors for the disorder in men with exhibitionistic interests. Childhood sexual and emotional abuse and hypersexuality have also been suggested, with the usual warning that cause and effect are unclear. The comorbid conditions, studied mostly in convicted men, are depressive, bipolar, anxiety and substance use disorders, hypersexuality, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, other paraphilic disorders and antisocial personality disorder.

Clinical link: Santosh

Santosh, 41, a clerk in a government office, married with two children, is referred by the court after his third arrest for exposing himself to women on a railway footbridge near his office. He describes a tension that builds over days, a feeling that he "has to go", the minute or two of waiting for a woman to come up the steps alone, and the moment of exposure, when her startled reaction is, he admits, the point of it all. Then he runs, and by the time he reaches his desk he is sick with fear and remorse. He insists that he has never wanted to touch anyone and that sex with his wife is "normal". The psychologist works through the questions DSM-5-TR raises. Over what period has this happened (four years), so Criterion A is met; he has acted on the urges with nonconsenting women, so Criterion B is met whatever he says about his distress. To whom has he exposed himself (only adult women, as far as the police record and his account show), so the specifier is "sexually aroused by exposing genitals to physically mature individuals". Is there alcohol misuse or a wider antisocial history, which DSM-5-TR links with reoffending (he drinks two or three evenings a week, often before an episode, which becomes a target of treatment). Section 9.7 takes up what can be done for him.

The third courtship disorder is the disorder of touching. Frotteuristic disorder takes its name from the French frotteur, one who rubs. It is recurrent and intense sexual arousal, for at least 6 months, from touching or rubbing against a nonconsenting person, shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour, with the same two-route Criterion B. Older writers distinguished frotteurism (rubbing the body, usually the genitals, against a stranger) from toucheurism (touching or fondling a stranger with the hands), and Kurt Freund still used both words in the 1980s; DSM-5-TR's "touching or rubbing" covers both. The setting is almost always a crowd. DSM-5-TR's own example is a man rubbing his genitals against a passenger on a crowded subway car, and Barlow and Durand observe that because the victims cannot easily escape, the act is "usually successful" (Lussier and Piché, 2008). The crowd does two things for the man: it gives him access, and it gives him deniability, because each contact can be passed off as the lurch of the train. That is exactly why the ascertainment rule matters. A man caught on several separate occasions who insists that every contact was accidental can still be diagnosed, because the recurrent behaviour itself shows both the interest and the harm.

DSM-5-TR's figures follow the now familiar pattern of common acts and rare disorder. Frotteuristic acts, including uninvited sexual touching of another person, may occur in up to 30 percent of adult men in the general populations of the United States and Canada, but an "intense desire" and "persistent behaviour" were reported by only 3.8 percent and 0.7 percent. Among men attending outpatient clinics for paraphilic disorders and hypersexuality, about 10 to 14 percent meet the criteria. The disorder is probably less common in women. Men with frotteuristic disorder usually become aware of the interest in late adolescence or early adulthood. There is no minimum age, but in younger people the behaviour can be hard to tell apart from conduct-disordered behaviour that has no sexual motive, and children and adolescents may touch unwilling others without having the disorder at all. Nonsexual antisocial behaviour and hypersexuality are possible, non-specific risk factors. The comorbid conditions are telling: hypersexuality and other paraphilic disorders, particularly exhibitionistic and voyeuristic disorder, along with conduct disorder, antisocial personality disorder and depressive, bipolar, anxiety and substance use disorders. The three courtship disorders keep turning up together in the same men, a fact that Freund's theory (Section 9.7.2) was built to explain. Omkar, whom you met in the clinic in Section 9.1.1, referred by a magistrate's court after a second arrest for pressing himself against women in a crowded bus, is the textbook presentation.

Indian context

Crowded city buses, suburban trains and metro coaches are the typical setting for frotteuristic acts in India, as on the subway trains of DSM-5-TR's example. Two points matter for the clinician. First, from the victim's side this is sexual assault, not a "harmless" brush in a crowd; women who have experienced it describe fear, humiliation and a lasting wariness of public transport, and they often blame themselves or are not believed. Euphemisms such as "eve-teasing" make such acts sound like mischief and should not be used in clinical notes or reports. Second, the person who does it is committing an offence (Section 9.5.6 gives the legal pointers), so the clinician who assesses him is often working at the request of a court, as with Omkar.

Exam trap

Match the verb to the disorder. Watching an unsuspecting person is voyeuristic disorder; showing one's genitals to an unsuspecting person is exhibitionistic disorder; touching or rubbing against a nonconsenting person is frotteuristic disorder. Exhibitionistic disorder has no minimum age (voyeuristic disorder requires 18) and is specified by the age of the people exposed to, so that pedophilic disorder is not missed. Older sources give DSM-5's (2013) estimate of exhibitionistic disorder as 2 to 4 percent of men; DSM-5-TR replaced it. One widely used textbook prints the Swedish finding as "31%" of adults aroused by exposing themselves to a stranger; the original paper (Långström and Seto, 2006) says 3.1 percent. ICD-11 codes: 6D30 exhibitionistic, 6D31 voyeuristic, 6D34 frotteuristic disorder.

The algolagnic disorders: sexual masochism and sexual sadism

The two algolagnic disorders are mirror images. In one the arousal comes from suffering received, in the other from suffering inflicted. Their names come from two nineteenth-century writers: masochism from the Austrian novelist Leopold von Sacher-Masoch, sadism from the Marquis de Sade. Section 9.1.3 tells how Richard von Krafft-Ebing brought both words into medicine. What matters here is that the mirror is not symmetrical in the criteria, because only one of the two can involve a victim.

Sexual masochism disorder is recurrent and intense sexual arousal, for at least 6 months, from the act of being humiliated, beaten, bound or otherwise made to suffer, shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour, which causes clinically significant distress or impairment. There is no "acted on with a nonconsenting person" route here, for an obvious reason: the person who suffers is the person with the interest. DSM-5-TR says the criteria are meant for people who freely admit the interest. If they are distressed by it (by anxiety, obsessions, guilt or shame) or it hampers them in pursuing other goals, the disorder can be diagnosed; if not, they are "ascertained as having masochistic sexual interest" and nothing more. One specifier belongs to this disorder alone: with asphyxiophilia, used when the person seeks sexual arousal through restriction of breathing (from asphyxia, the Greek for "stopping of the pulse"). The older name for this practice, still common in textbooks, is hypoxyphilia (from hypoxia, low oxygen); Barlow and Durand, who spell it "hypoxiphilia", describe it as self-strangulation to reduce the flow of oxygen to the brain and so intensify orgasm.

Why does this specifier exist? Because it marks the one masochistic practice that commonly kills. DSM-5-TR warns that people engaging in masochistic behaviour are at risk of accidental death while practising asphyxiophilia or other solitary sexual procedures, and that people who report an interest in asphyxiophilia seem to have more sexual distress and psychological maladjustment than the general population. It does not say how many such deaths occur, or what proportion of the dead would have met the criteria, because nobody knows; this book therefore gives no figure either. Barlow and Durand add a forensic distinction: a man who binds himself elaborately and accidentally hangs when something slips is not the same as a man who deliberately restricts his breathing for arousal, although both deaths may be found in similar circumstances. One more link is worth remembering for Section 9.6.6: DSM-5-TR notes that autoerotic asphyxia is associated with transvestic interests in a substantial proportion of fatal cases.

Much of what people with masochistic and sadistic interests actually do goes by the name BDSM, which DSM-5-TR expands as bondage-domination-sadism-masochism (other writers read the D as discipline as well as domination). DSM-5-TR lists the range plainly: restraint or restriction, discipline, spanking, slapping, sensory deprivation such as blindfolds, and dominance and submission role-play. The essential clinical point is that most people who take part in consensual BDSM do not have a disorder. DSM-5-TR says that the majority of people active in community networks that practise sadistic and masochistic behaviour "do not express any dissatisfaction with their sexual interests", and their behaviour would not meet the criteria. The Quebec survey of Joyal and Carpentier went further: interest in masochism was about as common in women as in men, and it was associated with greater satisfaction with one's own sex life, not less. Two other beliefs also fail the test of evidence. It is often assumed that people with masochistic interests were sexually abused as children, but DSM-5-TR says the evidence is insufficient to support that association. And although people who engage in sadomasochistic sex may be less sensitive to pain, it is not known whether this applies to those with the disorder.

The prevalence of sexual masochism disorder is unknown. In a representative Australian sample, 2.2 percent of men and 1.3 percent of women said they had been involved in BDSM in the previous 12 months. People with paraphilias living in the community report a mean age of onset of masochism of 19.3 years, although masochistic fantasies have been reported from puberty and even childhood. As with every paraphilia in this section, the interest and the behaviour may decline with age. DSM-5-TR adds two observations that a clinician needs. First, a study of 321 adults involved in BDSM found that stigma-related shame and guilt were associated with suicidal ideation: the danger, in other words, may come from how the person and others regard the interest rather than from the interest itself. Second, culture must be read carefully. Collective religious and spiritual rituals in many societies include painful ordeals such as suspension from hooks, self-flagellation and self-mortification, and the role of sexual arousal in them, DSM-5-TR says, "remains unknown". In India, the self-flagellation of some Muharram processions and the hook-swinging of some temple festivals are acts of devotion, not sexual behaviour, and a clinician who confuses the two has misunderstood both. Sexual masochism disorder most often co-occurs with other paraphilic disorders, such as transvestic fetishism, and one small clinical study of women suggests an association with borderline personality disorder.

Sexual sadism disorder is recurrent and intense sexual arousal, for at least 6 months, from the physical or psychological suffering of another person, shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour. Because this interest can involve a victim, Criterion B has both routes: the person has acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person, or the urges cause clinically significant distress or impairment. DSM-5-TR describes two kinds of people who come to assessment. "Admitting individuals" openly acknowledge an intense sexual interest in the suffering of others; if they are distressed or impaired by it they have the disorder, and if they are not and have never acted on it with a nonconsenting person, they have sadistic sexual interest and no diagnosis. Others deny any such interest but are known to have inflicted pain or suffering on several victims on separate occasions. As with the courtship disorders, their recurrent behaviour establishes both criteria despite the denial.

Almost everything known about sexual sadism disorder comes from forensic samples, which are nearly all men. Among people civilly committed as sexual offenders in the United States, fewer than 10 percent have the disorder; among those who have committed sexually motivated homicides, about one-third show sexually sadistic behaviour. The community picture is quite different. In a population-based Finnish sample, 2.7 percent of men and 2.3 percent of women reported sexually sadistic behaviour at some time in their lives, and of those, 68.8 percent had also engaged in masochistic behaviour, 33.3 percent in voyeuristic behaviour, 9.2 percent in transvestic fetishism and 6.4 percent in exhibitionism. Masochism and sadism, in other words, often live in the same person, which is one reason the older literature spoke of "sadomasochism" and why ICD-10 had a single code, F65.5, for both. Sadistic preferences themselves are probably lifelong, but the disorder may fluctuate with the person's distress or his propensity to harm others, and many people who engage in BDSM became aware of the interest in their teens. The legal status of sadistic behaviour varies between countries, and so, DSM-5-TR notes, may the distress and impairment that depend on it.

The most important differential diagnosis concerns sexual violence. A man who commits rape or another sexual assault may hurt his victim in the act itself, or in subduing or restraining her. That instrumental infliction of pain, DSM-5-TR insists, is not sexual sadism unless there is evidence that he derives pleasure from the pain and suffering itself: an admission that the pain arouses him, a preference for pornography with sadistic themes, or violence well beyond anything needed to commit the assault. In the same way, people with conduct disorder or antisocial personality disorder may be physically cruel and may force others into sex without any underlying pattern of arousal to suffering; their coercive acts are not grounds for this diagnosis, although if both sets of criteria are met, both disorders are diagnosed. Most rapists, as Section 9.5.6 explains, do not have a paraphilia at all. The comorbidities of sexual sadism disorder are mainly other paraphilic disorders.

Key study: Abel, Barlow, Blanchard and Guild (1977), what arouses a rapist?

Question. Do men who rape differ from other men in what sexually arouses them, or is rape simply violence by men with ordinary sexual interests? Method. Gene G. Abel, David Barlow, Edward B. Blanchard and D. Guild recorded the erections of rapists and of non-rapist comparison men while they listened to audiotaped descriptions of two kinds of scene: mutually enjoyable intercourse, and intercourse forced on an unwilling woman. Penile responses were measured by the kind of psychophysiological method described in Section 9.2.7. Result. The non-rapists were aroused by the consenting scenes but not by the forced ones. The rapists were aroused by both. The measure also picked out the rapists who had raped most often and those who had injured their victims. In later work with a third tape describing aggression with no sexual content, Barlow's group found a subgroup of rapists who were strongly aroused by violence alone and little by consenting sex. Conclusion. Some rapists have a paraphilic pattern of arousal that is best described as sadistic; many do not. Criticism. The samples were small and clinical, and penile measures are not a test for guilt; their legitimate use is described in Section 9.7.4. (The Blanchard of this study is Edward B. Blanchard, not Ray Blanchard of Section 9.6.6.)

ICD-11 draws the line more sharply still. Its coercive sexual sadism disorder (6D33) is defined around non-consent: a sustained and intense pattern of arousal involving the infliction of physical or psychological suffering on a person who has not consented, acted on or causing marked distress. Consensual sadism and masochism have no named ICD-11 disorder; they could fall under 6D36, paraphilic disorder involving solitary behaviour or consenting individuals, only if they cause marked distress or carry a significant risk of injury or death, as asphyxiation does (Section 9.5.4).

Exam trap

Masochism is being made to suffer; sadism is making another person suffer. "With asphyxiophilia" (older names hypoxyphilia, autoerotic asphyxia) is a specifier of sexual masochism disorder, not of sadism. Sexual masochism disorder is diagnosed on distress or impairment only; sexual sadism disorder can also be diagnosed because the person has acted on the urges with a nonconsenting person. Consensual BDSM without distress is not a disorder in either manual. ICD-11's name is coercive sexual sadism disorder (6D33), and it covers non-consenting victims only.

Pedophilic disorder

No diagnosis in this chapter carries more weight, in the clinic, in the courts or in public feeling, than this one, and none is more often misunderstood. So begin with three distinctions that the rest of the subsection depends on. Pedophilia (from the Greek pais, paidos, child, and philia, love or attraction) is a sexual interest: a recurrent, intense sexual attraction to prepubescent children. Pedophilic disorder is a diagnosis: the interest together with Criterion B, which means either that the person has acted on it or that it causes him marked distress or interpersonal difficulty. Child sexual abuse is a crime, defined by law and not by psychiatry. The three overlap but are not the same. Some people with pedophilia never act on it and never come to anyone's attention; some, frightened by what they find in themselves, come to clinics asking for help; and many people who sexually abuse children do not have pedophilia at all (Section 9.5.6 gives the evidence). Everything below is written as clinical description, because that is what the clinician needs: what the person presents with, what the criteria require, and what must be assessed.

The criteria have three parts. Criterion A requires, over at least 6 months, recurrent and intense sexually arousing fantasies, urges or behaviours involving sexual activity with a prepubescent child or children, "generally age 13 years or younger". Criterion B requires that the person has acted on these urges, or that the urges or fantasies cause marked distress or interpersonal difficulty. Criterion C requires that the person is at least 16 years old and at least 5 years older than the child or children in Criterion A, and a note adds that a person in late adolescence in an ongoing sexual relationship with a 12- or 13-year-old is not to be included. Each rule has a reason. The age of 13 is a guide to prepuberty, not a legal line: DSM-5-TR calls it "approximate only", because puberty begins at different ages in different people, its average age has been falling over time, and it differs across ethnic and cultural groups. The minimum age of 16 and the 5-year gap exist because, during adolescence, it is very hard to tell an emerging pedophilic interest from ordinary interest in peers or simple sexual curiosity; the rules stop the diagnosis being given to adolescents whose relationships, whatever one thinks of them, are with near-age partners. Notice too that Criterion B here says "marked" distress or "interpersonal difficulty", slightly different words from the other disorders, and that the action route does not mention consent, because a child cannot consent.

The specifiers describe the interest more precisely. Exclusive type means the person is attracted only to children; nonexclusive type means he is also attracted to adults. The clinician also records whether the person is sexually attracted to males, to females or to both, and whether the behaviour is limited to incest. What pedophilic disorder lacks is as important as what it has. It is the only one of the eight with no "in full remission" and no "in a controlled environment" specifier. The text gives the background: pedophilia itself "appears to be a lifelong condition", even though the disorder, which includes distress, impairment and the propensity to act, can fluctuate over time, with or without treatment, and advancing age tends to reduce the frequency of sexual behaviour of every kind.

How is the interest recognised? Some people disclose it, saying candidly that they have an intense sexual interest in children, and sometimes that this interest is equal to or greater than their interest in physically mature people. If they also complain of marked distress or psychosocial difficulty, they may be diagnosed; if they report no guilt, shame or anxiety, are not functionally limited, and have never acted on the interest according to their own account and the legal record, they have pedophilic sexual interest but not pedophilic disorder. Others deny any attraction despite substantial evidence, and DSM-5-TR lists the features that suggest pedophilic disorder in a person who has offended against a child: self-reported interest in children, use of sexual images of children, a history of multiple child victims, boy victims, and victims outside the family. The second of these has strong research support. Michael Seto, James Cantor and Ray Blanchard (2006) studied 685 men referred to a Toronto clinic for sexological assessment and found that men charged with child pornography offences showed greater sexual arousal to children than to adults, and that such a charge was a stronger indicator of pedophilia than having offended against a child. DSM-5-TR uses the legal phrase "child pornography"; many clinicians and agencies now prefer the term child sexual abuse material, because every such image records a real child being abused.

Two qualifications follow. First, the behaviour in Criterion B includes sexual interactions with children that involve no physical contact; some people with pedophilic disorder expose themselves to children (which is why exhibitionistic disorder has its specifier, Section 9.6.2). Second, viewing such images, although typical of people with pedophilic interest and useful evidence for Criterion A, is not by itself enough to conclude that Criterion B is met; DSM-5-TR requires acting on the urges in person, or marked distress or interpersonal difficulty. Many people with pedophilic disorder also show what researchers call emotional congruence with children: a feeling of having more in common with children than with adults, a preference for children's company, and sometimes a choice of job or voluntary work that puts them among children. It matters because studies link it both to pedophilic interest and to the likelihood that a person who has offended will offend again.

How common is pedophilic disorder? DSM-5-TR says the population prevalence is unknown but is "likely less than 3%" among men in international studies, and in women "a small fraction" of that. Men with the disorder often say they became aware of a strong or preferential interest in children around puberty, the same time at which other men become aware of their interest in women or men. Three risk factors are named. Temperamentally, there appears to be an interaction between pedophilia and antisocial traits (callousness, impulsivity and a readiness to take risks without regard for the consequences): men with pedophilic interest who also have these traits are more likely to act on it, and so to qualify for the disorder. Environmentally, adult men with pedophilia sometimes report having been sexually abused as children, but whether this is a cause is unclear. Biologically, there is "some evidence" that a disturbance of neurodevelopment before birth increases the probability of pedophilic interest, which Section 9.7.3 examines. Laboratory measures of sexual interest, useful in men, are of little use in diagnosing women because so little research has been done on them. The comorbid conditions reported (substance use, depressive, bipolar and anxiety disorders, antisocial personality disorder and other paraphilic disorders) come almost entirely from men convicted of offences against children, and may not describe men who have never approached a child.

When the history suggests pedophilic disorder but the person denies any strong attraction to children, psychophysiological measures may help. DSM-5-TR names penile plethysmography as the most thoroughly researched and longest used of them, together with viewing time, and stresses that the diagnostic marker is the person's relative response to stimuli depicting children compared with stimuli depicting adults, not his absolute response. It also warns clinicians that possessing visual stimuli of children, even for diagnosis, may itself be a crime, which is why audio descriptions are used instead in some centres. Section 9.7.4 explains these methods.

The differential diagnosis is short but each item teaches something. Pedophilia without Criterion B is an interest, not a disorder. Another paraphilic disorder may be the better account, as with the man who exposes himself to whoever is present, child or adult, and DSM-5-TR allows both diagnoses when both fit. Antisocial personality disorder raises the chance that a man primarily attracted to adults will sexually approach a child simply because the child is accessible; pedophilic disorder is added only if there is evidence of at least 6 months of recurrent, intense pedophilic fantasies, urges or behaviour. Substance intoxication can disinhibit in the same way. Finally, and most important for a clinical psychologist, obsessive-compulsive disorder. Some people come to clinics tormented by intrusive, unwanted thoughts that they might be attracted to children or might harm a child. Careful interviewing usually reveals that these thoughts bring no positive feelings and no arousal, that they are not connected with any sexual behaviour, and that the person often has other ego-dystonic intrusive sexual worries as well. This is a disorder of doubt and dread, not of desire, and it is treated as OCD (Book 3 Sections 6.5.2 and 6.5.8). Mistaking it for pedophilic disorder can do great harm to a person who is, if anything, unusually scrupulous.

Where does incest fit? The specifier "limited to incest" exists because sexual abuse within the family is not always driven by pedophilia. Barlow and Durand note that victims of incest tend to be girls who are beginning to mature physically, whereas the victims of men with pedophilic disorder tend to be younger children, and that studies measuring arousal found incestuous men to be, in general, more aroused by adult women than were men with pedophilic disorder (Marshall, Barbaree and Christophe, 1986). Incest, they conclude, may have more to do with availability and with problems in the family than with pedophilia. Book 2 Section 4.4.3 describes the treatment of one such father by covert sensitisation. A related proposal, to add a diagnosis for attraction to pubescent children of about 11 to 14 ("hebephilia"), was considered for DSM-5 and not adopted (Section 9.5.3). In ICD-10 the disorder is F65.4, paedophilia; in ICD-11 it is 6D32, pedophilic disorder, one of the named disorders involving people who cannot consent. In India the law that protects children from sexual offences, and that obliges clinicians to report, is the POCSO Act 2012 (Book 2 Section 10.6.5).

Clinical link: Yogesh

Yogesh, 29, a commerce graduate who works in a bank, comes on his own to the psychiatry outpatient clinic of a teaching hospital. He has rehearsed his first sentence for weeks. Since his late teens, he says, he has been aware that he is sexually attracted to young children and much less to adults; he has never acted on it, he has never looked for sexual images of children, and he is frightened of himself. He avoids family functions where there will be small children, has refused to stay at his sister's house, and has turned down marriage proposals because he does not want to deceive anyone. He sleeps badly and has thought, at times, that it would be better if he were dead.

The psychologist's first task, before any history, is to explain the limits of confidentiality honestly: what Yogesh says will be kept private, except that the law requires anyone who knows of, or apprehends, an offence against a child to report it (Book 2 Section 10.6.5). Then the assessment proceeds as for any serious disorder. Criterion A: the interest is recurrent, intense, longstanding and, by his account, greater than his interest in adults (exclusive or nonexclusive type is clarified, along with the sex of the children he is attracted to). Criterion B: he has never acted on it, but it causes him marked distress and interpersonal difficulty, so the disorder is diagnosed without any offence. Criterion C: he is far older than 16. The differential matters: are these thoughts arousing and wanted at some level, or intrusive and horrifying with no arousal, as in OCD? (They are arousing, and he has felt so since adolescence.) Then risk and safeguarding: his access to children at home and at work, alcohol or drug use that might disinhibit him, any use of illegal material, his suicidal thoughts, and the supports he has. Yogesh shows why DSM-5 separated paraphilia from disorder and disorder from crime. He meets the criteria, he has harmed no one, and the right response to him is treatment, which Section 9.7 describes, not punishment. Section 9.5.6 describes the programmes, such as Germany's Dunkelfeld project, that were built for people like him.

Exam trap

Pedophilic disorder: prepubescent child, generally 13 or younger (approximate, because puberty varies); the person at least 16 and at least 5 years older; a late adolescent in an ongoing relationship with a 12- or 13-year-old is excluded. Specifiers: exclusive or nonexclusive, sex of the children, limited to incest; there is no remission or controlled-environment specifier. Criterion B can be met by marked distress alone, so a person who has never offended can have the disorder; viewing images alone does not meet Criterion B. Intrusive, unwanted, non-arousing thoughts about harming or being attracted to children suggest OCD, not pedophilic disorder. DSM-5-TR says "likely less than 3%" of men; the older DSM-5 (2013) figure of 3 to 5 percent as an upper limit was replaced.

Fetishistic disorder

The word fetish began its life far from sexology. It comes through the French fétiche from the Portuguese feitiço, a charm or object of magical power, itself from the Latin facticius, made by art. A fetish was an object believed to hold a power beyond its material nature. Alfred Binet, better known to you as the father of intelligence testing (Book 1 Section 9.2), gave the word its sexual meaning in an essay of 1887 on "fetishism in love" (Section 9.5.3), and the image fits: for the person with a sexual fetish, an object is charged with an erotic power that it does not hold for others.

Fetishistic disorder has three criteria. Criterion A is recurrent and intense sexual arousal, for at least 6 months, from either the use of non-living objects or a highly specific focus on one or more non-genital body parts, shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour. Criterion B is clinically significant distress or impairment; as with masochism there is no "acted on a nonconsenting person" route, because an object is not a victim. Criterion C draws two boundaries: the fetish objects are not limited to articles of clothing used in cross-dressing (that is transvestic disorder, Section 9.6.6), and not limited to devices designed for genital stimulation, such as a vibrator (using a sex toy is not a fetish, because the device does exactly what it was made to do). The clinician then specifies the focus: body part or parts, non-living object or objects, or other. The categories are not exclusive. DSM-5-TR's own example of a combination is socks, shoes and feet, and it notes that closely related foci in one person, such as foot fetishism and shoe fetishism, are intuitively understandable.

The body-part clause has a history. An exclusive sexual focus on a non-genital body part, such as the feet, toes or hair, is called partialism. In DSM-IV-TR it was classed under "paraphilia not otherwise specified"; before DSM-III it had been treated as a kind of fetishism; and DSM-5 put it back inside fetishistic disorder, because in practice objects and body parts so often go together. The objects themselves are a familiar list: women's undergarments, men's or women's footwear, rubber articles, leather clothing, nappies and other clothing. Barlow and Durand describe two classes of fetish object: an inanimate object as such, and a source of a particular kind of touch, such as rubber or shiny black plastic, with body parts forming a third class. The use can involve every sense (holding, rubbing, smelling, tasting or wearing the object during masturbation), or the person may want a partner to wear or use it. Some people build up large collections. The pursuit can also bring trouble with the law, as in Barlow and Durand's account of a neighbourhood whose clotheslines were being stripped of bras, block after block, until a police watch caught a man with an intense fetish for them.

Here is the point that turns an interest into a disorder. Many people who call themselves fetishists report no impairment at all, and DSM-5-TR says plainly that they have a fetishistic sexual interest, not fetishistic disorder. Many people with a fetish also enjoy sex with their partners without the object, although they often find sex with it more satisfying; only for a minority is the object obligatory for arousal or orgasm. That minority is where the typical impairments arise: sexual dysfunction in a relationship when the fetish object is not available, and a preference for solitary sex with the fetish even within a loving relationship. DSM-5-TR gives two examples of fetishism that is not a disorder: a man whose partner shares, or can comfortably include, his interest in feet as part of foreplay, and a man who prefers solitary sex wearing rubber garments or leather boots and is neither distressed nor impaired by it.

Fetishistic interests usually begin at puberty, though they can start earlier, and once established the disorder tends to run a continuous course that fluctuates in intensity. Fetishistic behaviour is reported more often in men, but it also occurs in women, and the difference between the sexes is smaller for fantasy than for behaviour; in the Quebec survey, interest in fetishism was about as common in women as in men. In clinical samples, however, fetishistic disorder is reported almost exclusively in men. DSM-5-TR gives no prevalence figure for the disorder. It also asks the clinician to know what counts as ordinary sexual behaviour in the person's culture, so as to distinguish a disorder from a socially acceptable preference: enjoying the sight of a partner in particular clothes is not a fetish. Fetishistic disorder may co-occur with other paraphilic disorders and with hypersexuality, and rarely it is associated with neurological conditions.

The differential diagnosis has three items. The nearest neighbour is transvestic disorder: if the only objects are women's clothes worn by the man himself in cross-dressing, Criterion C sends the case to Section 9.6.6. Sexual masochism disorder is the second: a man who fantasises about "forced cross-dressing" and is aroused mainly by the domination or humiliation, not by the garments, has masochism. The third is fetishism without disorder, which is the commonest of the three. A careful assessment must also find out what the real focus is. Barlow and Durand describe a man who complained of uncontrollable arousal to women's open-toed white sandals, and would follow a woman wearing them for miles. Assessment showed that the sandals themselves had no erotic value for him; what aroused him was women's feet, moving in a particular way. Book 2 Section 4.4.1 describes the classic case of a man aroused by handbags and prams who was treated with aversion therapy in the 1950s. In ICD-10 fetishism was F65.0. ICD-11 has no fetishistic disorder at all: a fetish that harms no one is not a disorder, and only marked distress or a significant risk of injury could bring it under 6D36 (Section 9.5.4).

Clinical link: Pankaj

Pankaj, 37, a sales manager, and his wife are referred by a sexual medicine clinic after nine years of marriage. Since he was about 14, Pankaj has been intensely aroused by one particular kind of knee-length black leather boot: the look, the smell and the feel of the leather. Early in the marriage he could make love to his wife while picturing the boots, and she, puzzled but willing, sometimes wore a pair he bought her. Over the last few years he has needed the boots to be present to become aroused at all, and he keeps a collection of them in a locked cupboard at his office. His wife now refuses: "It is the boots he wants, not me." They have not had sex for eight months, and she is talking of going back to her parents' home.

The psychologist checks each criterion. Criterion A: years of recurrent, intense arousal from a non-living object, so the specifier is "nonliving object(s)"; when asked whether it is really the feet, Pankaj says it is the boots, even empty ones. Criterion B: the marriage is in crisis and he is ashamed, so distress and impairment are clear. Criterion C: he has never worn women's clothing, so this is not cross-dressing, and the boots are not a device for genital stimulation. The humiliation that would point to masochism is absent: he has no wish to be trodden on or dominated. His erections are reliable when the boots are present, which points to the fetish rather than to a physical cause of erectile failure (Section 9.3.6). The clients here are the couple: what his wife feels about being "replaced by a boot" is as much a target of treatment as his arousal (Section 9.7.5).

Exam trap

Arousal from an inanimate (non-living) object is fetishism (Amity 2023). The tempting distractors each need something else. Necrophilia needs a corpse (a dead human body is not an "object" in this sense; it is an other specified paraphilia, Section 9.6.7). Frotteurism needs a nonconsenting person who is touched or rubbed against. Voyeurism needs an unsuspecting person who is watched. A highly specific focus on a non-genital body part (partialism) is now also fetishistic disorder. Women's clothes worn in cross-dressing, or a vibrator, do not count as fetish objects (Criterion C).

Transvestic disorder and autogynephilia

The word transvestite (from the Latin trans, across, and vestis, clothing) was coined by Magnus Hirschfeld in his book Die Transvestiten of 1910 (Section 9.1.3). Cross-dressing, wearing the clothes associated with the other sex, is something people do for many reasons: for a play, a festival, a ritual, comfort, a sense of self, a joke. Only one of those reasons concerns this subsection. Transvestic disorder is recurrent and intense sexual arousal from cross-dressing, for at least 6 months, shown in fantasies, urges or behaviour, that causes clinically significant distress or impairment. DSM-5-TR is emphatic that the diagnosis "does not apply to all individuals who dress as the opposite sex, even those who do so habitually". It applies only when the cross-dressing, or the thought of it, is always or often accompanied by sexual excitement (Criterion A) and the person is distressed by the pattern or impaired by it (Criterion B).

What does it look like? The cross-dressing may involve only one or two items, for a man perhaps only women's undergarments, or it may mean dressing completely, inner and outer garments, with a wig and make-up. In younger men, cross-dressing often leads to masturbation, after which the clothes are taken off; older men often learn to avoid ejaculation so as to prolong the session. Some men end a session by having intercourse with a partner, and some have difficulty staying aroused during sex without cross-dressing or private fantasies of it. Assessment depends mostly on the man's own account, but one pattern of behaviour is a reliable sign of distress, and DSM-5-TR names it: purging and acquisition. A man who has spent a great deal of money on women's clothes, shoes and wigs throws them all away in an effort to conquer the urge, and then, weeks or months later, begins buying a wardrobe all over again.

The disorder has two specifiers, and the past papers' question on autogynephilia turns on the second. With fetishism is used if the man is sexually aroused by the fabrics, materials or garments themselves: the silk, the lace, the feel of the cloth. Barlow and Durand cite research showing that transvestism of this type is indistinguishable in most respects from other fetishes (Freund, Seto and Kuban, 1996). With autogynephilia is used if he is aroused by thoughts or images of himself as a woman. DSM-5-TR defines autogynephilia as "a man's paraphilic tendency to be sexually aroused by the thought or image of himself as a woman". The word is built from three Greek roots, auto (self), gyne (woman) and philia (love), so it means love of oneself as a woman. The fantasies need not involve clothes at all. DSM-5-TR says they may centre on the idea of having female physiological functions (such as menstruation or lactation), of doing things stereotypically associated with women (its example is knitting), or of having a female body, such as breasts. Barlow and Durand describe a 47-year-old man in their clinic whose most arousing scenario was imagining himself as a woman doing domestic chores, cooking for a male partner in an apron; he was clear that it was not the clothes that aroused him but the image in his own mind of himself as a woman. The two specifiers point in opposite directions: DSM-5-TR says that the presence of fetishism decreases, and the presence of autogynephilia increases, the likelihood of gender dysphoria in men with transvestic disorder.

Key study: Blanchard (1989), the concept of autogynephilia

Question. Ray Blanchard (born 1945), a psychologist at the gender identity clinic of the Clarke Institute of Psychiatry in Toronto, asked whether male-to-female transsexuals who were not sexually attracted to men had something in common. Method. In "The concept of autogynephilia and the typology of male gender dysphoria", published in the Journal of Nervous and Mental Disease in 1989, he studied 212 adult male-to-female transsexuals, divided by sexual orientation into one homosexual group (attracted to men) and three nonhomosexual groups (attracted to women, to both, or to neither, which he called analloerotic). He built a Core Autogynephilia Scale to measure a person's tendency to be sexually aroused by the fantasy of being a woman. Result. As he had predicted, all three nonhomosexual groups were more likely than the homosexual group to report sexual arousal from cross-gender fantasy. Conclusion. Blanchard proposed that male-to-female gender dysphoria comes in two main types, one in men attracted to men and one, which he called autogynephilic, in the rest, and that the nonhomosexual forms were variants of a single underlying condition. Criticism. The typology has been fiercely disputed. Many transgender people and many researchers reject it as reducing a gender identity to a sexual interest and question its data and its generality; others defend it. It remains contested, and Section 9.9.4 discusses it as a theory of gender dysphoria. What DSM-5-TR took from it is narrower and much less controversial: the term autogynephilia, as a specifier of transvestic disorder.

The development of transvestic disorder has a characteristic shape. In men, the first sign may be a strong fascination in childhood with one particular item of women's clothing. Before puberty, cross-dressing produces a general, pleasurable excitement; at puberty it begins to produce erections and in some cases leads directly to a first ejaculation. Then, in many men, something unexpected happens. Over the years cross-dressing produces less and less sexual excitement, sometimes none at all, yet the desire to cross-dress stays the same or grows stronger, and men describe the excitement as having been replaced by feelings of comfort or well-being. The course may be continuous or episodic. It is not rare for a man to lose interest in cross-dressing when he first falls in love with a woman, but the respite is usually temporary, and when the desire returns, so does the distress. DSM-5-TR says the disorder is most severe in adulthood, when the urge is most likely to conflict with sex with a partner, with marriage and with starting a family. Some cases progress to gender dysphoria: the man gradually wants to stay in the woman's role for longer and to feminise his body, and he usually reports that the sexual arousal of cross-dressing has faded or gone. Middle-aged and older men with a history of transvestism are more likely to present with gender dysphoria than with transvestic disorder.

How common is it, and who are these men? The prevalence of the disorder is unknown, though it appears to be much more common in men than in women. DSM-5-TR says that fewer than 3 percent of Swedish men report ever having been sexually aroused by dressing in women's clothes. The figure comes from the same national survey of 2,450 Swedish adults aged 18 to 60 that you met in Section 9.6.2, analysed by Långström and Kenneth Zucker (2005): 2.8 percent of men and 0.4 percent of women reported at least one episode. The men themselves are often not what people expect. In 1997 the psychologist Richard Docter and Virginia Prince (1912 to 2009), a cross-dresser who had founded the magazine Transvestia, published an anonymous survey of 1,032 men who cross-dressed periodically. Eighty-seven percent described themselves as heterosexual; all but 17 percent had married, and 60 percent were married at the time of the survey; 45 percent had sought counselling. Many wives, Barlow and Durand add, come to accept their husbands' cross-dressing, particularly if it stays a private matter between them. The functional consequences DSM-5-TR lists are about exactly this: the behaviour can interfere with heterosexual relationships, a source of distress to men who wish to keep a conventional marriage.

The differential diagnosis turns on what is going on in the man's mind. Fetishistic disorder can look identical, for example in a man who puts on women's undergarments while masturbating with them. The distinction depends on his thoughts during the activity (are there ideas of being a woman, being like a woman, or being dressed as a woman?) and on whether he has other fetishes, such as soft, silky fabrics used for things other than clothes. Gender dysphoria is the other neighbour, and the distinction is fundamental. Men with transvestic disorder do not report an incongruence between their experienced and assigned gender or a wish to be of the other gender, and they usually lack the childhood history of cross-gender behaviour seen in gender dysphoria. If a man meets both sets of criteria, both diagnoses are given. Finally, much cross-dressing has nothing to do with sex at all. In traditional Indian theatre forms such as Kathakali and Yakshagana, female roles have long been played by men; a man in costume on stage is acting, not cross-dressing for arousal. Nor is the identity of a transgender woman, or of a member of a hijra community, a paraphilia: those are matters of gender identity, taught in Sections 9.9.2 and 9.9.7. The commonest comorbid conditions are other paraphilias, especially fetishistic and masochistic interests, and as Section 9.6.3 noted, autoerotic asphyxia is associated with transvestic interests in a substantial proportion of fatal cases. ICD-10 had two codes for cross-dressing: F65.1, fetishistic transvestism (cross-dressing mainly for sexual excitement), and F64.1, dual-role transvestism (cross-dressing for the temporary experience of belonging to the other sex, without sexual motivation or a wish for permanent change). ICD-11 has neither (Section 9.1.6). Table 9.16 sets out the reasons a man may wear women's clothes and where each leads.

Table 9.16 Why a man may wear women's clothes: telling the diagnoses apart.
PresentationWhat drives itGender identityDiagnosis
Aroused by the garments, fabrics or materialsThe clothing itselfMan; no wish to changeTransvestic disorder, with fetishism (if distressed or impaired)
Aroused by the image of himself as a womanAutogynephiliaUsually man; some later develop gender dysphoriaTransvestic disorder, with autogynephilia (if distressed or impaired)
Uses women's underwear as an object, not as dressing upThe object; no thought of being a womanManFetishistic disorder
