Clinical link: Gitanjali

Gitanjali, 45, a schoolteacher, comes to the clinic with her husband three weeks after finding a suitcase of women's clothes, a wig and make-up hidden behind the water tank on their terrace. Her first fear was that he was having an affair; her second, after he told her the truth, that he must be gay, or that he wanted to become a woman. He is a 48-year-old engineer. In the session he says, haltingly, that dressing in women's clothes has aroused him since he was 13, that he dresses only when the house is empty, and that afterwards he feels so guilty that he has thrown everything away "five or six times", only to start buying again within months. He identifies as a man, has never wished to live as a woman or to change his body, and recalls no cross-gender play in childhood. When he dresses, what excites him is the feel of the fabrics; he has no particular fantasy of being a woman.

The psychologist explains what this is, and what it is not. He meets the criteria for transvestic disorder, with fetishism: recurrent arousal from cross-dressing for decades, and clear distress, shown by the cycle of purging and acquisition. It is not a sign of homosexuality (most men who cross-dress are heterosexual and married), nor of gender dysphoria, since he has no incongruence of identity. The couple, not the man alone, are the clients. Gitanjali's sense of betrayal and her worry about what relatives would say need as much attention as his guilt, and the decision about what place, if any, cross-dressing will have in their marriage is one they will make together (Section 9.7.5 and Book 2 Section 7.8).

Exam trap

Autogynephilia (Ray Blanchard, 1989) is recurrent, intense sexual arousal by the thought or image of oneself as a woman (RML 2022). Do not be pulled by the other options. Arousal by prepubescent children is pedophilia (Section 9.6.4); arousal by being humiliated is masochism (Section 9.6.3); and being sexually attracted "to oneself" in general, treating one's own body as a sexual object (the attitude Paul Näcke named narcissism in 1899, after Havelock Ellis had described a "Narcissus-like" tendency in 1898), is not a DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 diagnosis and is not autogynephilia, which is specifically about the image of oneself as a woman. In DSM-5-TR autogynephilia is a specifier of transvestic disorder, not a disorder of its own, and it raises the likelihood of gender dysphoria; "with fetishism" lowers it. Blanchard's wider typology of gender dysphoria remains disputed.

The other specified paraphilias

The eight named disorders do not exhaust the list, and DSM-5-TR says so: almost any of the many dozens of named paraphilias could, through its consequences, rise to the level of a disorder. For all of them it provides two residual categories. Other specified paraphilic disorder (F65.89) is used when symptoms characteristic of a paraphilic disorder, causing clinically significant distress or impairment, predominate but do not meet the full criteria for any of the eight, and the clinician chooses to say why; the diagnosis is recorded with the reason in brackets, as in "other specified paraphilic disorder (zoophilia)". Unspecified paraphilic disorder (F65.9) is used when the clinician chooses not to give the reason, or does not yet have enough information. DSM-5-TR names six examples for the "other specified" category, introducing them with the words "include, but are not limited to": recurrent and intense sexual arousal involving telephone scatologia (obscene telephone calls), necrophilia (corpses), zoophilia (animals), coprophilia (faeces), klismaphilia (enemas) or urophilia (urine), present for at least 6 months and causing marked distress or impairment. The category can carry the "in remission" and "in a controlled environment" specifiers.

Most of these names are built the same way, from a Greek word for the target plus philia, and once you know the roots the list becomes easy to learn. Table 9.17 gives them, together with three paraphilias that you have already met inside the named disorders. Three of the six examples, notice, involve a being who has not consented or cannot consent: the person who receives the call, the dead, and the animal. ICD-11 places exactly such interests in 6D35, other paraphilic disorder involving non-consenting individuals, and its definition gives "arousal patterns involving corpses or animals" as its examples, with the requirement that the person has acted on the interest or is markedly distressed by it. The others, which involve only the person himself or consenting partners, belong in 6D36 and only if they cause marked distress or a significant risk of injury or death (Section 9.5.4).

Table 9.17 The other named paraphilias: roots, focus and place in the manuals.
ParaphiliaRoot and meaningFocus of arousalDSM-5-TRICD-11
Telephone scatologiaGreek skatos, dung, and logos, word: "filthy talk"Obscene calls to an unsuspecting personOther specified6D35 (non-consenting)
NecrophiliaGreek nekros, corpseDead bodiesOther specified6D35 (named example)
ZoophiliaGreek zoon, animalAnimalsOther specified6D35 (named example)
CoprophiliaGreek kopros, dungFaecesOther specified6D36 if marked distress or risk
UrophiliaGreek ouron, urineUrineOther specified6D36 if marked distress or risk
KlismaphiliaGreek klysma, enemaEnemasOther specified6D36 if marked distress or risk
AcrotomophiliaGreek akrotomos, cut off at the endAmputees as partnersOther specified6D36 if marked distress
Asphyxiophilia (hypoxyphilia)Greek asphyxia, stopping of the pulseRestricted breathingSpecifier of sexual masochism disorder6D36 (risk of death)
PartialismLatin pars, partA non-genital body partInside fetishistic disorderNo disorder unless 6D36

A few of these deserve a sentence more. Telephone scatologia is the old, specific name for making obscene telephone calls, usually to strangers, for sexual arousal. Kurt Freund included obscene calling among the courtship disorders, as a distortion of the stage of courtship in which people talk before they touch (Section 9.7.2). Its modern relatives are sexually explicit messages and unsolicited explicit images sent to people who have not asked for them; the clinical literature has not settled whether these should be treated as the same paraphilia, so treat them, for now, as an extension of the old concept to new technology, and remember that sending them can be an offence. Necrophilia is rare and known mainly from forensic case reports. Zoophilia has had the most changeable history: DSM-III (1980) listed it as one of the named paraphilias, and DSM-III-R (1987) moved it into the residual category, where it remains (Section 9.5.3). Acrotomophilia, sexual interest in amputees, is not among DSM-5-TR's six examples, but DSM-5-TR mentions amputees as a class among the possible targets of a paraphilia, so it illustrates the "not limited to" clause well.

One forensic controversy belongs here. In some American courts, the diagnosis "other specified paraphilic disorder (nonconsent)" has been applied to men who have committed rape, usually in proceedings that decide whether a sexual offender can be detained after his sentence. A 2023 article in the Journal of the American Academy of Psychiatry and the Law argued that this construct is neither valid nor reliable, and Section 9.5.3 explains why DSM-5 declined to add a "coercive" paraphilia to the named list. The general lesson is the one this chapter keeps returning to: a residual category is a tool for describing a clinical problem, not a device for turning a crime into a disorder.

Exam trap

Necrophilia is arousal involving corpses and is not fetishism, even though a corpse is not alive; zoophilia (animals) is DSM-5-TR "other specified", not one of the eight named disorders. Learn the six DSM-5-TR examples by their targets: telephone scatologia (obscene calls), necrophilia (corpses), zoophilia (animals), coprophilia (faeces), klismaphilia (enemas), urophilia (urine). Hypoxyphilia or asphyxiophilia is the "with asphyxiophilia" specifier of sexual masochism disorder, and partialism is now part of fetishistic disorder; neither is a separate DSM-5-TR diagnosis.

9.7 Why Paraphilic Disorders Develop, and How They Are Assessed and TreatedLower yield · not asked yet

Why would anyone come to be aroused by a leather boot, by the shock on a stranger's face, by the thought of being bound? Every client in Section 9.6 raises the question, and most of them ask it of themselves. The honest answer is that nobody knows for certain. There are three families of explanation, each with some evidence and none sufficient on its own: learning (the interest was conditioned), development (something went wrong in the way sexual preferences are laid down in childhood and adolescence) and biology (hormones and the developing brain set the stage). Barlow and Durand, after reviewing all of them, conclude that the speculations "have little scientific support at this time", and you should read what follows in that spirit: as a set of partly supported hypotheses, each of which explains some cases and leaves others untouched. The second half of the section turns to what clinicians actually do: how they assess a person's sexual interests and risk, and how they treat paraphilic disorders with psychological methods, with drugs and, historically, with surgery.

Learning: conditioning and the role of masturbation

The simplest explanation is the one Book 1 Section 6.2 would suggest: if a neutral stimulus is repeatedly paired with sexual arousal, it should come to elicit arousal itself, exactly as Pavlov's bell came to elicit salivation. In the 1960s, at the height of the behaviour therapy movement, a young South African psychologist at the Institute of Psychiatry in London decided to test the idea directly.

Key study: Rachman (1966), a fetish made in the laboratory

Question. Can a sexual response to a non-sexual object be created by classical conditioning? Method. Stanley Rachman (1934 to 2021), later famous for his work on obsessive-compulsive disorder, worked with three volunteers, young unmarried psychologists. He first checked that the boots produced no sexual response. In each trial he projected a coloured slide of a pair of women's boots (the conditioned stimulus), followed immediately by slides of nude women (the unconditioned stimulus, which produced sexual arousal). Arousal was measured objectively, as changes in penile volume recorded with a modified version of Kurt Freund's plethysmograph (Section 9.7.4). Result. All three men came to respond to the boots alone, reaching the criterion of five successive responses after 30, 65 and 24 trials. The response could be extinguished by showing the boots without the nude slides, and it showed spontaneous recovery, as conditioned responses do; in at least one man it generalised to other black shoes. The paper, "Sexual fetishism: an experimental analogue", appeared in the Psychological Record in 1966, and a replication with five men by Rachman and Ray Hodgson followed in 1968. Conclusion. A rudimentary fetish-like response can be learned. Criticism. The responses were weak and easily extinguished, nothing like the intense, lifelong interests of the clients in Section 9.6; the volunteers were ordinary men in a laboratory, not people with paraphilias; and although the boots evoked no measurable response at the start, black knee-length boots were a familiar erotic image in the 1960s, so the experiment may have strengthened an association that was already there. It is an analogue, as its title says, not a model of a disorder.

Rachman's study showed that conditioning was possible, but it left a puzzle. Real fetishes are not created by hundreds of carefully timed slide pairings. How could a chance association in ordinary life grow into something so strong? In 1964 three clinicians, R. J. McGuire, J. M. Carlisle and B. G. Young, offered an answer in a paper in Behaviour Research and Therapy titled "Sexual deviations as conditioned behaviour: a hypothesis" (the volume is often cited as 1965). Their idea, the masturbatory conditioning hypothesis, was that the crucial learning happens not during the original experience but afterwards, in fantasy. A boy has some early, perhaps accidental, sexual experience: he glimpses a woman undressing, or is aroused while being punished. Later, when he masturbates, he recalls that scene, and the orgasm that follows reinforces it. Each repetition makes the fantasy more arousing and more likely to be used next time, while other fantasies, used less often, lose ground. Over years the scene that began by chance becomes the centre of his sexual life.

The hypothesis combines both kinds of learning that Book 1 Sections 6.2 to 6.5 teach: the scene is paired with arousal (classical conditioning), and the act of fantasising is rewarded by orgasm (operant reinforcement). It explains several things that the slide experiment cannot. It explains why the interest can grow for years before the person ever acts on it; Barlow and Durand observe that a man with pedophilic or sadistic disorder may fantasise about the act thousands of times while masturbating before he ever acts. It explains why some people report that their interest began with a particular early event, as Jatin's began with a glimpse through a neighbour's window. And, Barlow and Durand suggest, it may help to explain why paraphilic disorders are overwhelmingly male: across cultures, men masturbate more often than women, so they have more conditioning trials.

The learning account has clear limits. Most people who have early experiences of these kinds never develop a paraphilia, so learning cannot be the whole story; something must make some people more susceptible. Barlow and Durand cite the Sambia of Papua New Guinea as a counterpoint: boys took part in ritualised same-sex practices in childhood and early adolescence, yet as adults lived heterosexually, which suggests that strong social scripts can override early experience. Nor is the content of fetishes random. People fetishise shoes, underwear, rubber, leather and feet, things close to the body and to sex, not light switches or triangles, a pattern that fits the idea of biological preparedness, the readiness of some associations to be learned more easily than others (Book 1 Section 6.4.6). Finally, Barlow and Durand point out a paradox that every clinician meets: trying hard to suppress unwanted sexual thoughts seems to make them more frequent and more intense, much as trying to suppress obsessional thoughts does (Book 3 Section 6.5). Jatin's repeated vows never to do it again may have fed the very urges he was fighting.

Exam trap

Rachman (1966) conditioned mild sexual arousal to a picture of women's boots by pairing it with erotic slides: classical conditioning, an "experimental analogue" of fetishism, with weak and extinguishable responses. McGuire, Carlisle and Young (1964) proposed masturbatory conditioning: an early experience is rehearsed in masturbation fantasy and reinforced by orgasm. Do not credit the boots study to Pavlov, Watson or Wolpe, and do not say it produced a lasting fetish.

Development: lovemaps and courtship

Learning theories describe a mechanism; developmental theories ask when and why the mechanism goes wrong. The best known came from John Money (1921 to 2006), the psychologist at Johns Hopkins whose part in the Reimer case you read in the chapter's opening (Section 9.8.5 analyses it). In his book Lovemaps (1986), Money proposed that each person carries a lovemap, which he defined as "a developmental representation or template in the mind and in the brain depicting the idealized lover and the idealized program of sexual and erotic activity projected in imagery or actually engaged in with that lover". A lovemap, he argued, is formed in childhood rather as a native language is, and it is as individual as an accent. Most lovemaps develop into conventional forms. But if a child's development is disturbed, for example by sexual abuse or incest, or by being exposed to or drawn into sexual activity far too early, the lovemap may be "vandalised": the erotic part of it is displaced onto some other object or activity, and the result is a paraphilic lovemap. Once formed, Money held, a lovemap is very hard to change, which is why paraphilias tend to last.

The lovemap is a vivid and memorable idea, and it captures something clinicians recognise: that sexual preferences are laid down early, are highly specific, and resist change. As science, however, it is weak. It describes the outcome (a person's particular pattern of attraction) and gives it a name, but it does not specify the mechanism precisely enough to be tested, and almost any outcome can be explained after the fact by supposing a suitable disturbance. Treat it as a descriptive framework, and as the source of the word "paraphilia" in its modern popularity, rather than as a theory with strong evidence behind it.

A more testable developmental idea came from Kurt Freund (1914 to 1996), the Czech physician who developed penile plethysmography (Section 9.7.4) and later worked in Toronto. Freund started from an observation you met in Section 9.6: voyeurism, exhibitionism and frotteurism keep turning up in the same men. Why should three apparently different interests travel together? His answer was the courtship disorder hypothesis, developed and tested in a series of papers in the 1980s, notably "The courtship disorders" (Freund, Scher and Hucker, 1983). Ordinary human courtship, he proposed, runs through four phases in order (Figure 9.10): first, looking for and appraising a potential partner; second, pretactile interaction, smiling, looking and talking; third, tactile interaction, touching, embracing and petting; and fourth, intercourse. Each courtship disorder is a distortion of one phase. In voyeurism, the searching and looking phase is exaggerated and the rest are dropped. In exhibitionism, and in obscene telephone calling, the pretactile phase of showing and talking is exaggerated. In frotteurism and toucheurism, the touching phase is exaggerated. And in what Freund called the preferential rape pattern, the earlier phases are skipped altogether and the man moves straight to intercourse without consent. In each case, as Freund put it, one phase is intensified until it becomes a caricature of the normal, while the others are omitted or shrink to a vestige.

Freund tested the hypothesis in the way his own invention allowed. In the 1983 study, he first counted how often men referred for one of these behaviours later reported others of the same class, and found a high degree of overlap. He then measured penile responses to taped descriptions of different scenes. Men referred for one of the other courtship disorders who denied any voyeuristic activity nonetheless responded more to voyeuristic scenes than did control men, and exhibitionists who denied any touching responded more to descriptions of touching than did controls. The clustering that clinicians had noticed was there in the laboratory too. DSM-5-TR's comorbidity data fit the same picture: voyeuristic disorder co-occurs especially with exhibitionistic disorder, and frotteuristic disorder especially with exhibitionistic and voyeuristic disorder. The hypothesis explains the clustering elegantly and gives DSM-5-TR its group name. What it does not explain is why a man's courtship should become distorted in the first place, and the evidence comes almost entirely from men referred to clinics, often by courts.

Other developmental research has looked at what is missing as well as what is present. Barlow and Durand stress that paraphilic arousal often occurs together with a deficit in "desired" arousal: weak sexual interest in consenting adults, and poor social skills for forming adult relationships. The integrated theories of sexual offending, of William Marshall and Howard Barbaree (1990) and later of Tony Ward and Anthony Beech, all point to disturbed relationships in childhood and adolescence and the resulting gaps in healthy sexual and social development. Jatin's remark that ordinary closeness "felt like nothing" is the kind of observation these theories try to explain. The same theories note, however, that many people with poor social and sexual skills never develop a paraphilia. Childhood sexual abuse is the other developmental factor most often studied. DSM-5-TR lists it as a suggested risk factor for several of the disorders, while warning that the causal link is uncertain, and Barlow and Durand note that men with pedophilic disorder often report having been abused, which predicts later abusing. That is a statistical association, not a destiny: most people who were abused as children do not go on to abuse anyone.

Barlow and Durand pull these strands into a single model of how a paraphilia might develop. Early inappropriate sexual associations or experiences, some accidental and some vicarious, combine with an inadequate development of arousal to consenting adults and of the social skills needed to relate to adults. The inappropriate fantasies are then repeatedly associated with masturbation and strongly reinforced, and the person's repeated attempts to suppress the unwanted arousal and behaviour produce, paradoxically, an increase in paraphilic thoughts, fantasies and behaviour. They are candid about the model's status: like every hypothesis in this area it has "little scientific support", and it leaves out biology altogether. To that missing dimension we now turn.

Exam trap

The lovemap (and the "vandalised lovemap") is John Money's concept (1986). The courtship disorder hypothesis is Kurt Freund's: voyeurism (searching), exhibitionism and obscene calls (pretactile), frotteurism and toucheurism (tactile) and preferential rape (intercourse without the earlier phases). Freund also developed penile plethysmography. Neither theory explains why the distortion occurs in the first place.

Biology: hormones, brain development and transmitters

Start with the hormone everyone thinks of first. Testosterone is necessary for male sexual behaviour, and the most dramatic evidence of this comes from men who have lost their testes. Pinel describes a study by Bremer (1959) of 215 Norwegian men who had been surgically castrated (orchidectomised). About half had committed sexual offences and had agreed to the operation in order to shorten their prison terms. Two conclusions followed. Castration reduced sexual interest and behaviour, but the rate and degree of the loss varied: about half the men became completely asexual within a few weeks, others quickly lost the ability to have an erection while keeping some sexual interest, and a few went on having intercourse, less enthusiastically, for as long as they were followed. And of the 102 sex offenders in the study, only three were reconvicted of a sexual offence. Bremer recommended castration as a treatment of last resort for male sex offenders. His findings are the historical basis for every hormonal treatment in Section 9.7.6.

But notice what the study does and does not show. It shows that removing testosterone lowers the amount of sexual drive. It says nothing about the direction of that drive, what a man finds arousing. Pinel makes a related point about healthy men: sex drive and testosterone levels are uncorrelated in them, and giving them extra testosterone does not raise their drive, because each has far more testosterone than the minimum needed. In a classic experiment, Grunt and Young (1952) castrated male guinea pigs that they had rated as low, medium or high in sexual activity; all fell to negligible levels, and identical large injections of testosterone simply returned each animal to its own previous level. A useful way to remember the two findings together is that testosterone sets the volume, not the station. That is exactly why hormonal treatment can reduce the urgency of a paraphilic interest without changing what the interest is about, a point Section 9.7.6 returns to.

The most active biological research concerns pedophilia, and much of it comes from one group in Toronto led by James M. Cantor, working with Ray Blanchard. In 2004 they gave brief neuropsychological tests to 473 men assessed at their clinic for pedophilia or other problematic sexual interests or behaviour, with pedophilia measured by the men's sexual histories and their penile responses to standard stimuli. Pedophilia was correlated with lower IQ, with poorer immediate and delayed recall, and with non-right-handedness, even after age and IQ were taken into account. In 2005, adding a second sample of 404 men to the first, they found that the odds of non-right-handedness among men who had offended mainly against prepubescent children were about twice those of men who had offended mainly against adults, and about three times higher once men whose offences were within the family were excluded. Then in 2008, using magnetic resonance imaging, they compared 65 men referred for illegal or clinically significant sexual behaviour or interests, many of them pedophilic, with 62 men who had committed non-sexual offences only, and found that the stronger a man's pedophilic interest, the less white matter he had in the temporal and parietal lobes, concentrated in two fibre bundles, the superior fronto-occipital fasciculus and the right arcuate fasciculus. They suggested that these bundles connect cortical regions that respond to sexual cues, and that pedophilia might result from a partial disconnection within that network. Because handedness is settled very early in development, and the other differences point the same way, the findings fit DSM-5-TR's cautious statement that there is "some evidence" of a disturbance of neurodevelopment in utero.

Three cautions keep these findings in proportion. They are differences between group averages, with large overlap, so no brain scan, IQ score or handedness test can diagnose an individual. They come mostly from men referred to a forensic clinic, so they may describe men who offend rather than everyone with pedophilic interest. And they are correlations: they suggest that something in early brain development raises the probability of pedophilic interest, but they do not show what, and they neither excuse behaviour nor predict it. The famous fraternal birth order effect, in which each older brother raises a man's chance of being gay, belongs to research on sexual orientation, not to paraphilias (Section 9.10.6); do not mix the two.

Brain disease can also release paraphilic behaviour that was never there before, or that was held in check. DSM-5-TR's differential diagnosis for the courtship disorders lists major neurocognitive disorder, personality change due to another medical condition, schizophrenia, manic episodes and substance intoxication, all of which can disinhibit sexual behaviour, and it notes that fetishistic disorder is rarely associated with neurological conditions. The practical rule follows: when a man with no previous history begins in middle or old age to expose himself, grope or show some new sexual interest, the first thought should be of a brain disorder, such as a dementia or a frontal injury, and the person should have a cognitive and neurological assessment (the neurocognitive disorders are taught in Chapter 10).

The last biological strand concerns the brain's transmitters. In 1997 the psychiatrist Martin P. Kafka set out a monoamine hypothesis of paraphilic disorders in men. Noradrenaline, dopamine and serotonin, he argued, help regulate sexual motivation and the appetitive side of sexual behaviour in mammals, humans included; the side effects of antidepressants, stimulants and antipsychotics show that altering these transmitters changes human sexual functioning, including sexual appetite; the same transmitters modulate impulsivity, anxiety, depression and compulsivity, which are often disturbed in men with paraphilias; and drugs that enhance serotonin can reduce paraphilic arousal and behaviour. Barlow and Durand mention a related idea, that a weak behavioural inhibition system in the brain, perhaps involving serotonin, may underlie the poor control seen across the paraphilic disorders (Ward and Beech, 2008). The hypothesis is the main rationale for using selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors in milder cases (Section 9.7.6), but, like the other biological ideas, it describes a contributing factor, not a cause.

Exam trap

Castration (Bremer, 1959) and antiandrogens reduce the strength of sexual drive; nothing in the evidence shows that they change its direction. In healthy men, testosterone level and sex drive are uncorrelated. The neurodevelopmental findings in pedophilia (lower IQ, non-right-handedness, reduced white matter; Cantor and colleagues, 2004 to 2008) are group differences, not a diagnostic test. The fraternal birth order effect is a finding about sexual orientation, not paraphilia. New paraphilic behaviour in late life suggests a brain disorder.

Assessment

Assessing a person with a possible paraphilic disorder is harder than assessing almost any other condition in this book, because the person often has strong reasons not to tell the truth. Santosh is facing a court; Jatin wants to return to his course; even Yogesh, who came voluntarily, may hold things back for fear of what the clinician will think or do. The assessment therefore rests on several sources at once. The interview covers the sexual history in detail (Book 2 Section 2.3.6), the content and frequency of fantasies and urges, what the person has actually done, with whom and how often, the sequence of events before each episode, and alcohol and drug use. Collateral information is essential: police reports, court records, the victims' statements where they exist, and, with consent, the account of a spouse or partner. The ascertainment rules of Section 9.5.2 tell the clinician how to weigh repeated behaviour against denial. Barlow and Durand add that the assessment must look beyond the paraphilia itself, to the level of the person's arousal to consenting adults, his social skills and his capacity for relationships, because these are what treatment will need to build.

The best-known laboratory measure is penile plethysmography, also called phallometry, which records changes in the size of the penis while the man is shown pictures or listens to descriptions of different kinds of people and activities. Kurt Freund developed the method in Prague in the 1950s and described it in English in 1963, in a paper on diagnosing a man's predominant erotic interest in men or in women; his device measured changes in penile volume, while the later, simpler strain gauge measures changes in circumference (Section 9.2.7 describes both). DSM-5-TR calls penile plethysmography the most thoroughly researched and longest used of the psychophysiological measures, but warns that its sensitivity and specificity vary from centre to centre, because centres use different stimuli, procedures and scoring. The key to reading it is the comparison: the diagnostic marker is the man's relative response to one category of stimulus (for example, children) compared with another (adults), not the size of any single response. Some men can suppress their responses deliberately, which is one more reason the method supports a clinical judgement rather than replacing it.

Two other methods are used alongside it. Viewing time measures how long a person looks at pictures of nude or minimally clothed people of different ages and sexes, on the well-established finding that people look longer at what interests them; it is usually combined with self-report questionnaires. And because possessing sexual images of children may itself be a crime, even for diagnosis, as DSM-5-TR warns American clinicians, many centres use audio descriptions as stimuli instead. Little research supports any of these methods in women. Some treatment programmes also use the polygraph to check what offenders report about their behaviour, but its accuracy is disputed and its results are not accepted as scientific proof in many courts (Book 2 Section 10.9.1 tells the story of the first "lie detector" case in an American court, and Book 2 Section 3.10 the Indian Supreme Court's ruling on the polygraph in Selvi v. State of Karnataka, 2010). All of these are tests, and the Assessment book describes them as such.

The final question is risk: how likely is this person to commit a sexual offence again? Here the evidence favours structured methods over unaided clinical judgement (Book 2 Section 10.9.5). The foundation was a meta-analysis by R. Karl Hanson and Monique Bussière (1998) of 61 follow-up studies of nearly 29,000 sexual offenders. Of the 23,393 whose sexual reoffending was recorded, on average only 13.4 percent were known to have committed another sexual offence during follow-up periods that averaged four to five years, a lower figure than public opinion assumes, though some subgroups reoffended at high rates. Sexual reoffending was best predicted by measures of sexual deviance, such as deviant sexual preferences and previous sexual offences, and to a lesser extent by general criminal factors such as young age and the number of past offences; offenders who failed to complete treatment were at higher risk. Actuarial risk assessment scales such as the Static-99 combine fixed, historical factors of this kind by an explicit rule. Because historical factors cannot change, clinicians also assess dynamic risk factors, those that treatment can change, such as deviant sexual interest, attitudes that excuse offending and problems of self-regulation. The scales themselves belong to the Assessment book.

Exam trap

Penile plethysmography (phallometry) was developed by Kurt Freund; DSM-5-TR calls it the most thoroughly researched and longest used measure, but its accuracy varies between sites, and the marker is the relative response to different stimulus categories, not the absolute response. Viewing time is the other laboratory measure. Hanson and Bussière's meta-analysis (1998) found an average sexual recidivism rate of 13.4 percent, best predicted by sexual deviance and prior sexual offences.

Psychological treatment

The psychological treatments for paraphilic disorders are taught in Book 2, and here they are only named, with what they aim at and what the evidence shows. Barlow and Durand describe four aims, and most programmes pursue all of them: to reduce the unwanted arousal, to strengthen arousal to consenting adults, to build the social and relationship skills that adult sexuality needs, and to prevent relapse. Attention to the person's marriage and family is often needed as well, because, as they put it, most people with paraphilic arousal patterns need a great deal of attention to the family or other interpersonal systems in which they live.

The oldest methods aimed at the first goal. Aversion therapy paired the paraphilic stimulus with nausea-inducing drugs or electric shock (Book 2 Section 4.4.1, with the classic case of the man aroused by handbags and prams); its misuse in attempts to change homosexual orientation is told in Book 2 Section 4.4.2 and is one of the darkest episodes in the history of behaviour therapy. Covert sensitisation, first described by Joseph Cautela in 1966 and 1967, moved the aversive event into the imagination: the client imagines the approach to the unwanted act and then a vivid, unpleasant consequence, usually shame, discovery or arrest; Barry Maletzky's assisted covert sensitisation adds a real foul odour at the climax of the scene (Book 2 Section 4.4.3, which describes its use with a father who had abused his daughter). To build desired arousal, Gerald Davison introduced orgasmic reconditioning in a 1968 case report titled "Elimination of a sadistic fantasy by a client-controlled counterconditioning technique": the client deliberately switches from his usual paraphilic fantasy to a conventional one at the moment of orgasm, and with practice makes the switch earlier and earlier, so that conventional fantasies take over the reinforcement that the paraphilic fantasy used to receive. It is McGuire's masturbatory conditioning (Section 9.7.1) run in reverse. Finally, relapse prevention, adapted for sexual offending from Alan Marlatt's work on addictions (Laws and O'Donohue, 1997; Chapter 10 teaches the original model), teaches the person to recognise the early signs of temptation and the high-risk situations that precede an offence, such as Jatin's examination stress or Santosh's evening drinking, and to use self-control strategies before the urge becomes too strong.

Modern programmes combine these elements in cognitive-behavioural packages, and they are organised by two broader frameworks taught in Book 2 Section 10.9.8: the risk-need-responsivity model, which matches the intensity of treatment to the person's risk and targets the factors linked to offending, and Tony Ward's Good Lives Model (2002), which helps the person build a satisfying life that meets ordinary human needs without harming others. For couples like Pankaj and his wife, or Gitanjali and her husband, whose problem is a fetishistic or transvestic disorder within a marriage, the work is closer to couples therapy and sex therapy (Book 2 Sections 7.8 and 7.9): understanding the interest, reducing shame, and helping the couple decide together what place it will have in their sexual life.

Does any of this work? The answer depends heavily on who is treated and how the outcome is measured. A meta-analysis led by Hanson (2002) combined 43 studies of 9,454 sexual offenders. Averaged across all studies, the sexual reoffending rate was 12.3 percent in treated groups and 16.8 percent in comparison groups; current treatments, mostly cognitive-behavioural, were associated with a fall from 17.4 to 9.9 percent, while older treatments used before 1980 appeared to have little effect. A later meta-analysis by Friedrich Lösel and Martin Schmucker (2005) also found cognitive-behavioural programmes the most effective of the psychological treatments. The most rigorous single study, however, was discouraging. California's Sex Offender Treatment and Evaluation Project, reported by Janice K. Marques and colleagues in 2005, randomly assigned imprisoned offenders to an inpatient relapse prevention programme or to untreated control groups and followed them for 8 years after release: there was no significant difference in sexual or violent reoffending, for rapists or for men who had offended against children, although the men who actually met the programme's treatment goals did reoffend less. At the other extreme, the psychiatrist Barry M. Maletzky reported on more than 8,000 offenders treated in his outpatient clinic in Oregon over 20 years, in programmes of 3 to 4 months, with outcomes checked by penile measures, family reports and legal records. Counting as failures everyone who did not complete treatment, he found success rates of 75 to 95 percent depending on the type of offence; men who had raped did least well among those with a single diagnosis (75 percent), and men with multiple paraphilias did least well of all. The strongest predictors of failure were unstable relationships and employment, strong denial, many victims, and continuing to live with a victim. But this was not a controlled trial.

Two findings in younger people are more hopeful. Judith Becker's programme for adolescent offenders found that only about 10 percent of those who completed treatment committed further sexual offences, and a study of children aged 5 to 12 with aggressive or highly inappropriate sexual behaviour found that only 2 percent of those given cognitive-behavioural treatment committed a sexual offence over the next 10 years, a better result than for a comparison group given play therapy (Carpentier, Silovsky and Chaffin, 2006). Barlow and Durand's overall verdict is sober. The evidence for psychological treatment is mixed; results are better in outpatient settings with less severe, more stable clients; most results are uncontrolled observations from a few specialised centres; and because paraphilic disorders run a chronic course and recurrence is common, the outlook for most people with them is guarded. Programmes that reach people before they offend, like the one Section 9.5.6 describes for people in Yogesh's situation, are one answer to that problem.

Clinical link: matching treatment to the client

The clients of Section 9.6 need different things. Jatin, ashamed and motivated, might be offered a cognitive-behavioural programme combining relapse prevention (his examination stress is a clear high-risk trigger), work on the social and intimacy skills he lacks, and, if his urges stay strong, an SSRI. Santosh, under a court order, needs the same psychological work plus treatment for his drinking, with the court's monitoring as part of the plan. Yogesh needs a confidential, non-judgemental setting, help with his depression and suicidal thoughts, a safety plan built around avoiding unsupervised contact with children, and perhaps medication if the urges become hard to resist. Pankaj and his wife, and Gitanjali and her husband, need couples and sex therapy far more than any technique for removing arousal. The diagnosis is only the beginning of the formulation.

Drug and physical treatments

Drugs for paraphilic disorders do one of two things. Most of them lower testosterone or block its action, and so reduce the strength of sexual drive, fantasies and urges; one class, the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), dampens sexual drive and the impulsive or compulsive quality of the urges by another route. None of them changes what a person is attracted to. Their job is to turn down the volume until psychological treatment can do its work, or, in the most dangerous cases, to keep it down. They are always one part of a treatment plan, combined with psychotherapy, and they require informed consent and medical monitoring.

SSRIs. The SSRIs, such as fluoxetine and sertraline (Book 3 Section 5.7.2), are used in paraphilias for two reasons: they reduce sexual drive as a side effect, and Kafka's monoamine hypothesis (Section 9.7.3) gives a rationale for raising serotonin. They are used for milder cases, for people whose urges have a compulsive or impulsive quality or who also have depression or anxiety, and for hypersexuality. The doses used are at the higher end of the antidepressant range. Their side effects are the familiar ones, nausea and other stomach upsets, insomnia and sexual dysfunction, the last of which here overlaps with the purpose of treatment.

Antiandrogens. An antiandrogen is a drug that blocks the action of male hormones or reduces their production. Cyproterone acetate does both: it blocks androgen receptors, and as a progestogen it suppresses the pituitary's release of the gonadotropins that drive the testes, so testosterone falls. It is taken by mouth daily or given as a depot injection. Barlow and Durand call it the most popular drug for paraphilic disorders, a "chemical castration" drug that eliminates sexual desire and fantasy by reducing testosterone dramatically, but they add that the fantasies and arousal return as soon as the drug is stopped. It is widely used in Europe, Canada and elsewhere but is not approved in the United States. Its side effects include fatigue, low mood, weight gain, breast enlargement (gynaecomastia), hot flushes, reduced sperm production, erectile failure, raised liver enzymes and, rarely, liver damage and blood clots. Medroxyprogesterone acetate, whose injectable form is Depo-Provera, is a progestogen that lowers testosterone by suppressing gonadotropin release; it has been used mainly where cyproterone is unavailable, as in the United States. Its side effects include weight gain, gynaecomastia, hot flushes, headache, raised blood pressure, diabetes, gallstones and depressive symptoms. Barlow and Durand note that these drugs may help dangerous offenders who do not respond to other treatment, but are not always successful, and that in Maletzky's earlier report only 8 of about 5,000 patients needed them because they had not responded at all to psychological treatment.

Gonadotropin-releasing hormone agonists. The newest and most powerful drugs are synthetic versions of the hypothalamic hormone that normally tells the pituitary to release luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone. A GnRH agonist such as triptorelin or leuprorelin (leuprolide) is given as a long-acting depot injection, monthly or every three months, and its effect looks paradoxical. At first the constant stimulation makes the pituitary release more gonadotropin, so testosterone rises for a few days, the so-called flare. Then the pituitary becomes desensitised, its GnRH receptors are down-regulated, gonadotropin release falls, and within two to four weeks testosterone falls to the level seen after castration. Because the flare could briefly worsen urges, an antiandrogen is given to cover it: the 2020 international guidelines described below advise starting cyproterone acetate one week before the first injection and continuing it through the first month. The landmark evidence is a single study, and it is worth knowing in detail.

Key study: Rösler and Witztum (1998), triptorelin for severe paraphilia

Question. Could selectively shutting down the pituitary-testis system with a long-acting GnRH agonist abolish deviant sexual behaviour in men for whom psychotherapy, antidepressants, progestins and antiandrogens had often failed? Method. Ari Rösler, an endocrinologist at the Hadassah-Hebrew University Medical Center in Jerusalem, and the psychiatrist Eliezer Witztum treated 30 men with severe, long-standing paraphilia (mean age 32; 25 with pedophilia and 5 with other paraphilias) with monthly injections of 3.75 milligrams of triptorelin, together with supportive psychotherapy, for 8 to 42 months. They reported the results in the New England Journal of Medicine in 1998. Result. Deviant sexual fantasies and desires fell from an average of 48 a week to none during treatment, and incidents of abnormal sexual behaviour from about 5 a month to none. The effects were evident after 3 to 10 months and persisted in all 24 men who continued for at least a year. Mean testosterone fell from 545 to 23 nanograms per decilitre, the castrate range. The main side effects were erectile failure, hot flushes and a decrease in bone mineral density in some men. Criticism. It was an uncontrolled observational study, with no comparison group, in a selected sample, and most outcomes were self-reported. Barlow and Durand judge the drug "somewhat more effective" than the older ones, with fewer side effects, "based on this one study".

How do clinicians choose among these options? The World Federation of Societies of Biological Psychiatry (WFSBP) published guidelines on the biological treatment of paraphilias in 2010 (Florence Thibaut and colleagues), proposing an algorithm with six levels of treatment for different categories of paraphilia, and updated them in 2020, when they concluded that GnRH analogue treatment is the most relevant treatment for patients with severe paraphilic disorders. The 2020 algorithm has five levels, so do not be surprised to see both numbers. The principle is a ladder of steps matched to the risk of harm to others. As a 2024 review of paraphilias from an Indian perspective in the Indian Journal of Psychological Medicine summarises the 2020 algorithm (Thakur, Yadav and Bhanwar), psychotherapy alone, preferably cognitive-behavioural, is advised for cases such as voyeuristic, fetishistic or frotteuristic disorder with no risk of rape or child abuse; an SSRI is added for mild cases with a low risk of sexual violence, such as exhibitionistic disorder without such risk, when psychotherapy alone is not enough; an antiandrogen (cyproterone acetate, or medroxyprogesterone acetate where cyproterone is unavailable) is used for a moderate risk of sexual violence, including in people with intellectual disability or dementia; a long-acting GnRH agonist is used for a moderately high to high risk and for severe disorders such as pedophilic disorder or sexual sadism with violence; and in the most severe cases a GnRH agonist is combined with an antiandrogen, and an SSRI may be added. Psychotherapy continues at every step. The evidence behind each step is modest, rated in the review's table at grade C for most steps and B to C for the GnRH agonists: there are few randomised trials in this field, for ethical and practical reasons. Table 9.18 summarises the drugs.

Table 9.18 Drugs used in paraphilic disorders. None changes the direction of sexual interest; all reduce its strength, and drive returns when they are stopped.
Class and examplesHow it worksTypical useMain side effectsEvidence
SSRIs: fluoxetine, sertralineRaise serotonin; damp sexual drive and compulsivityMild cases, low risk; compulsive or impulsive urges; hypersexualityNausea, insomnia, sexual dysfunctionLow (grade C)
Antiandrogen: cyproterone acetateBlocks androgen receptors; as a progestogen lowers gonadotropins and testosteroneModerate risk; oral or depotFatigue, low mood, weight gain, gynaecomastia, liver effects, clots (rare)Grade C; "the most popular drug" (Barlow and Durand); not approved in the USA
Progestogen: medroxyprogesterone acetate (Depo-Provera)Suppresses gonadotropin release, lowering testosteroneWhere cyproterone is unavailableWeight gain, gynaecomastia, raised blood pressure, diabetes, gallstones, low moodGrade C
GnRH agonists: triptorelin, leuprorelinFlare, then pituitary desensitisation; testosterone to castrate levels in 2 to 4 weeksHigh risk; severe pedophilic disorder or sexual sadismBone loss, hot flushes, erectile failure, fatigue, mild gynaecomastiaGrade B to C; Rösler and Witztum (1998); first choice for severe cases (WFSBP 2020)

Three practical matters follow from the pharmacology. First, the hormonal drugs have serious long-term costs, the most important being loss of bone density, which can lead to osteoporosis, so bone density, weight, blood pressure, blood sugar and liver function are monitored. Second, "chemical castration", the popular name for antiandrogen and GnRH agonist treatment, is reversible: testosterone and sexual drive return when the drug is stopped, which is reassuring for the patient's long-term health and worrying for the people who rely on it to protect others. Treatment works only as long as it is taken, and Barlow and Durand note frankly that, facing prison as the alternative, most offenders are highly motivated to comply. Third, surgical castration, the treatment Bremer described, is permanent; it is now rare and ethically contested, and the reversible drugs have largely replaced it.

Clinical link: treatment or punishment?

Hormonal treatment raises the hardest ethical questions in this chapter. Can consent be free when the alternative is a longer prison sentence? Some jurisdictions have written "chemical castration" into law as a condition of release or as part of a sentence, which turns a medical treatment into a punishment imposed by a court. Book 2 Section 4.4.2 drew the lesson from the history of aversion therapy: consent given under the pressure of stigma, family or the law is not the same as free consent, and the clinician's duty is to the patient's informed choice. In practice this means explaining the drug's purpose, its effects on drive but not on the direction of interest, its side effects and its reversibility; offering it as part of a wider treatment plan, never alone; and keeping the clinician's role (treating a person) separate from the court's role (protecting the public). For a man like Yogesh, who has never offended and asks for help with urges he fears, a drug that turns down the volume can be an act of care; for a man ordered to take it, the same drug raises questions that no clinician should ignore.

Exam trap

Cyproterone acetate is an antiandrogen (and progestogen); medroxyprogesterone acetate (Depo-Provera) is a progestogen that lowers testosterone; both are called antiandrogen treatments. Triptorelin and leuprorelin are GnRH agonists: they first cause a testosterone flare, then suppress it to castrate levels. SSRIs are for milder cases. "Chemical castration" is reversible. No drug changes the direction of sexual interest, only its strength. Rösler and Witztum (1998) used triptorelin in 30 men, 25 with pedophilia, in an uncontrolled study.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  3. Pedophilic disorder concerns prepubescent children, generally aged or younger; the person must be at least 16 and at least years older than the child.
  4. The only specifier unique to sexual masochism disorder is "with ", for arousal related to restriction of breathing.
  5. Recurrent, intense arousal from a non-living object, or from a highly specific focus on a non-genital body part (called ), is fetishistic disorder.
  6. A man's paraphilic tendency to be aroused by the thought or image of himself as a woman is called , a term coined by Ray Blanchard in ; in DSM-5-TR it is a specifier of disorder.
