Answers: (1) courtship; algolagnic, (2) 18; no, (3) 13; 5, (4) asphyxiophilia, (5) partialism, (6) autogynephilia; 1989; transvestic, (7) acquisition, (8) scatologia; klismaphilia, (9) boots; courtship, (10) gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH); flare; antiandrogen.

9.8 Becoming Male or Female: Sexual Differentiation and Gender IdentityLower yield · not asked yet

Section 9.1 drew a line between sex, which is biological, and gender identity, which is a person's inner sense of being a man, a woman or another gender. The line raises an obvious question. If the two are different things, why do they usually agree? Most people with XY chromosomes have testes, a penis, a male body and a male identity; most people with XX chromosomes have ovaries, a uterus, a female body and a female identity. Something builds that agreement, link by link, before birth and again at puberty. This section follows the chain from the first cell to the sense of self: chromosomes, gonads, hormones, the internal reproductive ducts, the external genitals, the brain and, last of all, identity. Along the way you will meet people in whom one link of the chain gave way at a known point. Biologists sometimes call such people "experiments of nature", because they let researchers test ideas that could never ethically be tested on purpose. You will also return to the twin raised as a girl whose story opened this chapter, and ask what his life can and cannot tell us about where gender identity comes from. That question matters for everything in Section 9.9, because a diagnosis of gender dysphoria rests on the claim that a person's gender identity can differ from the sex the body was assigned, and that it cannot simply be reset.

Male and female are not opposites

Most of us carry a ready-made picture of the sexes: there are two of them, they are opposites, and everything about a person (chromosomes, body, hormones, brain, behaviour, identity, attraction) lines up on one side or the other. John P. J. Pinel, whose textbook of biopsychology is the source for most of the biology in this section, gives this picture a deliberately clumsy name, the men-are-men-and-women-are-women assumption, which he shortens to "mamawawa". The mamawawa is the habit of thinking of male and female as mutually exclusive, opposite categories. It is a useful first approximation, because most people do fall clearly into one group, but it is wrong as an account of how the sexes come to be, and it causes real errors in thinking.

What replaces it? Not two parallel programmes, one for building a boy and one for building a girl, but a single shared starting point followed by a series of switches. Each switch can be set more or less independently of the others: one gene decides what the gonads become, the gonads' hormones decide what the internal ducts and external genitals become, the body's receptors decide whether those hormones have any effect, and the brain's own development follows its own timetable. Usually every switch is set the same way. When one is not, the result is a person who is, in Pinel's words about the three exceptional cases he describes, "male in some respects and female in others". Such people are not mistakes in a two-box system; they show that the system was never two boxes.

Pinel adds a useful point about method. People often say that "the exception proves the rule", meaning that an exception somehow confirms it. That cannot be right, because exceptions challenge rules. The word "prove" in the proverb comes from the Latin probare, "to test", as in a proving ground. The exceptional cases in this section prove the rules of sexual development in that older sense: they put them to the test. Keep the idea in mind as you read about androgen insensitivity, congenital adrenal hyperplasia and the twin who lost his penis, because each one tests a different link in the chain.

Chromosomes, gonads and hormones

Start at fertilisation. Each human cell has 23 pairs of chromosomes. Twenty-two pairs are the same in both sexes; the twenty-third, the sex chromosomes, usually differs. Females usually have two X chromosomes (XX) and males one X and one Y (XY). Every ovum carries an X chromosome, while half of a man's sperm carry an X and half carry a Y, so it is the sperm that settles the chromosomal sex of the child. (That plain biological fact is worth knowing in a country where a mother is still sometimes blamed for "giving" the family a daughter.) Sexual differentiation, the process by which a fertilised egg becomes a male or a female body, therefore begins at fertilisation with an XX or an XY zygote. But the chromosomes do not build a body directly. For the first weeks of life they make no visible difference at all.

The gonads. Six weeks after conception, every fetus, XX or XY, has the same pair of primordial gonads ("primordial" means existing at the beginning). Each has an outer layer, the cortex, which can become an ovary, and an inner core, the medulla, which can become a testis. In the seventh week, in an XY fetus, a gene on the Y chromosome switches on. It makes a protein that causes the medulla of each primordial gonad to grow into a testis. In the absence of that protein the cortex develops instead, and the gonads become ovaries. The gene is the sex-determining region Y, discovered by Andrew Sinclair and his colleagues, who reported it in Nature in 1990. By convention the human gene is written in capitals, SRY, and the corresponding gene in other mammals in lower case, Sry; Pinel writes "Sry gene" and "Sry protein" throughout, and you may meet either form. Pinel describes two thought experiments that make the point vividly. If Sry protein were injected into a genetic female fetus six weeks after conception, the result would be a genetic female with testes; if drugs that block Sry protein were given to a genetic male fetus, the result would be a genetic male with ovaries. The chromosome decides the gonad only by way of this one gene, and once the gonad is formed, the chromosomes step back and the gonads take over.

The internal ducts. At six weeks every fetus also has two complete sets of internal reproductive ducts. The Wolffian system can develop into the male ducts: the vas deferens, through which sperm travel, and the seminal vesicles, which hold the fluid in which sperm are ejaculated. The Müllerian system can develop into the female ducts: the uterus, the upper part of the vagina and the fallopian tubes. In the third month of male development, the new testes begin to secrete two hormones. Testosterone stimulates the Wolffian system to develop. The second hormone, Müllerian-inhibiting substance, makes the Müllerian system degenerate (it also helps the testes descend into the scrotum). Notice that two separate signals are needed to make a male internal tract, one to build the male ducts and one to remove the female ones, and notice where the signals come from: the testes, not the chromosomes. Because testosterone rather than the Y chromosome triggers Wolffian development, a genetic female exposed to testosterone at the right point in fetal life develops male ducts as well as her female ones (she still has no Müllerian-inhibiting substance, so the Müllerian ducts survive).

What, then, builds the female ducts? The surprising answer came from the French physiologist Alfred Jost, who in a series of experiments reported from 1947 removed the gonads of rabbit fetuses inside the womb and watched what developed. Female fetuses developed female ducts, as expected. Female fetuses whose ovaries had been removed also developed female ducts. And male fetuses whose testes had been removed developed female ducts too. The conclusion was clear: female duct development does not depend on ovarian hormones at all (the fetal ovary is almost completely inactive), while male development depends on active signals from the testes. In Pinel's summary, Müllerian development occurs in any fetus that is not exposed to testicular hormones during the critical fetal period. Pinel also gives the vocabulary that goes with these experiments, which examiners sometimes test: ovariectomy is removal of the ovaries, orchidectomy removal of the testes (from the Greek orchis, testicle), and gonadectomy, or castration, removal of either.

The external genitals. Here the logic changes, and the change matters for everything that follows. The internal organs begin as two separate sets of precursors, of which only one develops. The external genitals begin as a single bipotential precursor that can become either male or female. At the end of the third month it has four parts, and each has a male and a female fate. The glans becomes the head of the penis or the clitoris. The urethral folds fuse in the male or become the labia minora in the female. The lateral bodies form the shaft of the penis or the hood of the clitoris. The labioscrotal swellings become the scrotum or the labia majora. Which way they go depends on androgen. With androgen at the right stage, the precursor develops along male lines; without it, development proceeds along female lines. In the tissues of the genital precursor (and of the prostate), testosterone works mainly after it has been converted into a more potent androgen, dihydrotestosterone (DHT), by the enzyme 5-alpha-reductase; testosterone itself is enough for the Wolffian ducts. Because one precursor gives rise to both outcomes, the external genitals can also come out somewhere in between, when androgen is present but in the wrong amount, at the wrong time or acting on unresponsive tissue. That is why the most visible differences of sex development, the ones noticed at birth, are differences of the external genitals.

Figure 9.11 draws the whole sequence and marks the three points where the conditions described in Section 9.8.4 branch off: androgen insensitivity at the step where hormones act on their receptors, 5-alpha-reductase deficiency at the conversion of testosterone to DHT, and congenital adrenal hyperplasia where androgen from an unexpected source reaches a female fetus.

All this has led to a famous generalisation: the female default. For the reproductive organs, the female programme is the one that runs unless it is overridden, and in genetic males it is overridden by testicular hormones around the time of birth. Pinel accepts this for the reproductive organs and for the hypothalamus, but he warns that the idea "falters badly" when it is extended to the brain in general. Mice genetically engineered to lack oestradiol receptors do not simply develop a female brain by default, which shows that oestradiol plays an active part in the development of the female brain. "Default" should not be read as "passive", and it certainly should not be read as "the female is an unfinished male". It means only that, for the genital organs, the male pattern needs extra signals and the female pattern does not.

Exam trap

The Sry (SRY) gene on the Y chromosome makes the testis; testosterone does not make the testis, the testis makes testosterone. Wolffian ducts become the male internal organs (vas deferens, seminal vesicles); Müllerian ducts become the female ones (uterus, tubes, upper vagina): think "M for mother's organs". Müllerian-inhibiting substance comes from the testes, not the ovaries. Jost showed that female ducts develop without any ovarian hormone. Testosterone builds the Wolffian ducts, but the external genitals are masculinised mainly by dihydrotestosterone, made from testosterone by 5-alpha-reductase. The chromosomal sex of a child is set by the sperm.

Hormones, the brain and behaviour

Bodies are only half the story. If hormones before birth can turn a bipotential genital precursor into a penis or a clitoris, can they also shape the brain, and through it behaviour? To answer, you need a distinction you met in Book 1 (Section 10.9.6). Hormones have organisational effects: permanent effects on the structure of the body and brain, produced during sensitive periods before or around birth. And they have activational effects: temporary effects, from puberty onwards, that switch on behaviour in structures that are already built. Think of organisational effects as laying the wiring of a house, and activational effects as flicking the switches; the same current produces different results in houses that were wired differently. The classic evidence for organisational effects comes from rodents.

Key study: testosterone before birth (Phoenix and colleagues, 1959; Grady, Phoenix and Young, 1965)

Charles Phoenix and his colleagues (Robert Goy, Arnold Gerall and William C. Young, writing in Endocrinology in 1959) injected pregnant guinea pigs with testosterone. When the litters were born, they removed the ovaries of the female offspring and waited for them to grow up. As adults, these females were given testosterone and observed with other animals. Females that had been exposed to testosterone before birth showed more male-like mounting than females that had not. When they were later given progesterone and oestradiol, the hormones that normally make a female rodent receptive, they showed less lordosis, the arched-back posture by which a female rodent signals receptivity. Prenatal testosterone had both masculinised them (added male-typical behaviour) and defeminised them (removed female-typical behaviour), two separate effects that have to be measured separately.

Six years later, Grady, Phoenix and William C. Young ran the mirror-image experiment. Male rats castrated shortly after birth, and therefore deprived of their own early testosterone, did not show the typical male copulatory pattern when they were given testosterone as adults; given oestrogen and progesterone, they showed more lordosis than intact males. Lack of early testosterone had feminised and demasculinised them. In rats the window is short: single injections of testosterone masculinise and defeminise behaviour only in about the first 5 days after birth. The criticism, which matters for everything that follows, is that rodent copulation is a simple, hormone-driven reflex pattern, and human sexual behaviour, let alone human gender identity, is not.

The rodent work produced one of the strangest findings in endocrinology. In rats and mice, testosterone does not masculinise the brain directly. Inside certain brain cells an enzyme called aromatase converts it into oestradiol, and it is this oestradiol, a so-called female hormone, that masculinises the rodent brain. The process is called aromatisation. (Female rodent fetuses are protected from their mothers' oestradiol by a blood protein, alpha fetoprotein, that binds it; whether anything similar matters in humans is, in Pinel's word, controversial.) Pinel's conclusion for our own species is different: current research suggests that any masculinising effects of testosterone on the human brain are direct, and that aromatisation, though important in rats and mice, does not play this role in humans. Women with complete androgen insensitivity, whom you will meet in a moment, fit that view: their testes make plenty of testosterone, which their bodies can turn into oestradiol, yet without working androgen receptors their bodies, behaviour and identity develop along female lines.

What about the human brain itself? Here the mamawawa misleads most. The great majority of documented sex differences in the human brain are, as Pinel puts it, "slight, variable, and statistical": average male and female brains differ in many small ways, but the distributions overlap so much that some researchers argue we should stop speaking of "male brains" and "female brains" at all. Brains are not masculinised or feminised as a whole. Different sex differences develop independently, in different parts of the brain, at different times and by different mechanisms, so one person's brain can be more male-typical in one region and more female-typical in another. None of this means the differences are unimportant. Taken together they matter for health: autism spectrum disorder and Parkinson's disease are far more common in males, Alzheimer's disease in females, and depression and anxiety are about twice as common in women (the disorder chapters of this book give the figures). But it does mean that a single brain measurement can never tell you whether a person is "really" male or female, a point to remember when you read the brain studies of transgender people in Section 9.9.4.

The second great hormonal event comes at puberty. The pituitary increases its release of growth hormone, which produces the growth spurt, and of the gonadotropic hormones and adrenocorticotropic hormone, which make the gonads and the adrenal cortex release more of their own hormones. The general principle is simple: in boys androgens come to exceed oestrogens and the body is masculinised; in girls oestrogens predominate and the body is feminised. These hormones bring the secondary sex characteristics: breasts, a female fat distribution and menstruation in girls; facial and body hair, a deeper voice and greater muscle mass in boys. A person castrated before puberty does not mature sexually unless replacement hormones are given. Even here the mamawawa stumbles. The growth of pubic and underarm hair in girls is driven mainly by an androgen from the adrenal cortex, androstenedione, which led Pinel to remark that it is hard to take seriously the practice of calling androgens "male hormones" when one of them produces the female pattern of pubic hair. Both sexes make both kinds of hormone; what differs is the balance. Puberty will return in Section 9.9, because for many young people with gender dysphoria it is the arrival of an unwanted puberty that turns discomfort into crisis, and because the most debated treatment in the field, puberty suppression, works by pausing exactly the hormonal changes described in this paragraph. (The hypothalamus, the pituitary and the endocrine system in detail belong to the Physiological book; puberty as a developmental stage, to the Developmental book.)

Exam trap

Organisational effects are permanent and early (before or around birth); activational effects are temporary and from puberty. Phoenix and colleagues (1959) used guinea pigs and prenatal testosterone in females; Grady, Phoenix and Young (1965) castrated newborn male rats. Lordosis is the receptive posture of a female rodent, not a spinal disorder in this context. Aromatisation (testosterone to oestradiol) masculinises the brain of rats and mice, but in humans testosterone appears to act directly. Most human brain sex differences are slight, variable and statistical.

Differences of sex development

Now test the rules against the exceptions. When one link of the chain in Figure 9.11 develops differently from the others, the result is a difference of sex development (DSD), also called a disorder of sex development: a congenital condition in which chromosomal, gonadal or anatomical sex develops in an atypical way. The term was proposed in a 2006 international consensus statement on the management of these conditions (Lee, Houk, Ahmed and Hughes), which replaced older labels; many people with these conditions and their organisations prefer the word "difference" to "disorder", and many use intersex as a community and identity term. The words hermaphrodite and pseudohermaphrodite are outdated (DSM-5-TR lists them as historical terms), although you will still meet them in older papers, including two of the classic studies below. DSM-5-TR sorts the DSDs into ambiguous genitalia (such as an enlarged clitoris or a very small penis), a mismatch between internal and external anatomy (complete androgen insensitivity), incomplete development of sex anatomy (such as gonads that never form), sex chromosome variations (Turner and Klinefelter syndromes) and disorders of gonadal development (such as an ovotestis, a gonad containing both ovarian and testicular tissue). A DSD can be specified alongside a diagnosis of gender dysphoria (Section 9.9.2), but most people with a DSD have no gender dysphoria at all. Table 9.19 gathers the conditions; the text tells their stories.

Complete androgen insensitivity syndrome. Pinel's first case is a woman he calls Anne S. At 26, married for four years and unable to conceive, she sought help because she had never menstruated and because intercourse was painful. She looked like a healthy young woman; the only unusual signs were sparse pubic and underarm hair, a vagina only 4 centimetres long and an underdeveloped uterus. Three lines of evidence then showed that she was genetically male: cells scraped from the inside of her cheek contained an X and a Y chromosome; a small abdominal incision revealed internal testes and no ovaries; and her hormone levels were typical of a man. Anne had complete androgen insensitivity syndrome, caused by a mutation of the androgen receptor gene that left her receptors totally unresponsive. It is rare, occurring in about 1 in 60,000 genetic male births.

Push the case through Figure 9.11 and every feature falls into place. Her Sry gene made testes, and her testes made male levels of androgens, but because no cell could respond to them, development proceeded as if no androgens had been released. Her Wolffian ducts did not develop, her external genitals and her brain developed along female lines, and her testes stayed inside her body because there was no scrotum for them to descend into. Yet her Müllerian-inhibiting substance worked normally, which is why her uterus was underdeveloped and her vagina short. At puberty her testes released enough oestrogens to feminise her body (oestrogen works through a different receptor), but adrenal androstenedione, an androgen, could not grow pubic and underarm hair. Her gender identity was female, as it is in almost everyone with this condition. Anne's case tests two rules at once. A Y chromosome and high testosterone are not enough to make a male; the body must be able to respond. And a female identity developed in a person with XY chromosomes, testes and male hormone levels, which tells you that whatever builds gender identity, it is not the chromosomes on their own.

The case also raises a question of ethics that Pinel puts directly to the reader. Should a woman be told that she has XY chromosomes and testes? Should her husband? Anne's doctor told neither. Her vagina was surgically enlarged, she was advised to consider adoption, and as far as Pinel knows she remained happily married and unaware of her genetic sex. On the other side, several women with partial androgen insensitivity told Pinel and his co-author that they would recommend full disclosure, because learning the cause had helped them make sense of ambiguities they had faced all their lives. Practice has moved firmly in their direction: the 2006 consensus statement calls for open communication with patients and families, care by a multidisciplinary team, and the person's own participation in decisions.

Clinical link: Rachna and the question of telling

Rachna, 17, is brought by her mother to the gynaecology department of a district hospital because she has never had a period. Her breasts developed at the usual age; she has little body hair. Investigations show 46,XY chromosomes, testes inside the abdomen and no uterus: complete androgen insensitivity. The gynaecologist asks the clinical psychologist to join the team before anyone speaks to Rachna. The psychologist's first contribution is to say what the diagnosis does not mean: Rachna is a girl, she has been a girl all her life, and nothing in the result changes that. The work then turns to the questions the family is already asking in the corridor. How much should Rachna be told, and when? (Most teams now favour honest disclosure in stages, in words she can understand, rather than secrecy.) What will this mean for marriage, which her relatives have begun to discuss, and for her inability to carry a pregnancy? Who else needs to know? (That is her decision, and the clinician's duty of confidentiality is to her.) The psychologist's task is not to settle her identity, which is not in question, but to help her and her mother live with a fact that the family fears will bring shame.

Congenital adrenal hyperplasia. Pinel's second case, whom he calls Elaine, sought treatment in 1972. She had been born with somewhat ambiguous genitals but was raised as a girl without incident until puberty, when she suddenly began to develop male secondary sex characteristics, which distressed her greatly. Surgery and hormone treatment followed, and as an adult her narrow hips and husky voice were the only signs of her brush with masculinity. The condition that explains cases like hers is congenital adrenal hyperplasia, also called the adrenogenital syndrome: a congenital deficiency in the release of cortisol from the adrenal cortex, which leads to compensatory overactivity of the adrenal glands and the excessive release of adrenal androgens. It affects about 1 in 10,000 people. In genetic males it has little sexual effect beyond an earlier puberty if untreated. In genetic females, the adrenal androgens reach the fetus at the stage when the external genitals are forming, so the girl is born with an enlarged clitoris and partly fused labia (marker C in Figure 9.11). Her internal ducts are typical female ducts, because the androgens arrive too late to build Wolffian ducts and there is no Müllerian-inhibiting substance to remove the Müllerian ones. Before cortisol therapy was developed in 1950, such children were left untreated; some were raised as boys and some as girls, and their puberty went unpredictably one way or the other, sometimes with devastating emotional consequences. Today the cortisol deficiency is treated from birth, and genital surgery has traditionally been done early, though (as you will see below) that tradition is now contested.

What does early androgen do to the behaviour of these girls? Treated girls with congenital adrenal hyperplasia tend, as a group, to be more tomboyish, stronger and more aggressive than other girls, and to prefer boys' clothes, toys and playmates. Pinel adds an important caution: many girls without the condition show the same preferences, and the behaviour of girls with congenital adrenal hyperplasia, although shifted towards the stereotypically masculine, is usually within the typical range for girls. As adults they tend to start dating later; most are heterosexual, although a few studies have found a somewhat greater interest in women, and even that may reflect different experiences (living with atypical genitals and body hair) rather than a direct effect of the hormones. The decisive question for this chapter is gender identity. Heino Meyer-Bahlburg and his colleagues (2004) compared 15 girls with congenital adrenal hyperplasia, aged 5 to 12, with 30 control girls and 16 control boys, using observed play and a gender identity interview. The girls with the condition scored more masculine than the control girls on every measure of gender-related behaviour, with large effects, but they did not differ at all on the gender identity scales. DSM-5-TR draws the same conclusion from the wider literature: "the prenatal androgen milieu is more closely related to gendered behavior than to gender identity". Gender-typed behaviour and gender identity are different things, and early hormones move the first far more than the second.

5-alpha-reductase deficiency. The third condition is not in Pinel, but no account of gender identity is complete without it. In 1974 Julianne Imperato-McGinley and her colleagues reported in Science on a group of related families in the Dominican Republic. Some genetic males in these families were born with ambiguous, largely female-looking external genitals, yet at puberty they became markedly masculine. The cause was 5-alpha-reductase deficiency, an inherited (autosomal recessive) lack of the enzyme that turns testosterone into dihydrotestosterone. Without enough DHT before birth, the external genitals were incompletely masculinised (marker B in Figure 9.11); but their testes worked and their testosterone levels were normal, so at puberty the surge of testosterone did the job that DHT had failed to do: the voice deepened, the muscles grew, the phallus enlarged. The community had its own name for these children, güevedoces, a nickname referring to male genitals that appear at about twelve (from the Dominican Spanish güevos a los doce, usually translated "testicles at twelve" and sometimes "penis at twelve").

The finding that made the condition famous came five years later. In the New England Journal of Medicine in 1979, Imperato-McGinley and her colleagues reported that of 38 affected people, 18 had been raised unambiguously as girls; yet during or after puberty, 17 of those 18 changed to a male gender identity and 16 of the 18 to a male gender role. The authors concluded that exposure of the brain to normal testosterone before birth, after birth and at puberty contributes substantially to male gender identity, and can override the effect of being reared as a girl. Not everyone accepted the conclusion. The anthropologist Gilbert Herdt and others argued that the condition was well known in these villages, that the community had a recognised category for such children, and that a child known to be a possible güevedoce was not, in any simple sense, "unambiguously" raised as a girl; they also pointed out that a man's role carried far more freedom and status than a woman's, which gave a practical reason to switch. Herdt and Davidson (1988) then studied 14 people with the same deficiency among the Sambia of Papua New Guinea. Those reared as female also changed to a male role, but "in circumstances of social trauma", and the authors concluded that social and cultural factors play a significant part in the change. The fair summary is that the Dominican cases show something powerful pulling towards a male identity when male hormones act on the brain before birth and at puberty, and that culture shapes how, and how easily, a person follows that pull.

Sex chromosome variations. Two conditions complete the list, and both are far more common than the three above. In Turner syndrome a girl has only one X chromosome (45,X). Her ovaries fail to develop properly, so she is usually infertile and often needs hormone treatment to start puberty; she tends to be short. In Klinefelter syndrome a boy has an extra X (47,XXY). His testes are small and his testosterone is low, so he is usually infertile; he is often tall, and some boys develop breast tissue at puberty. Many men with Klinefelter syndrome are diagnosed only when they seek help for infertility. In both conditions the external genitals at birth are unambiguous, and gender identity almost always matches the sex assigned at birth, which is one reason why many clinicians do not regard them as "intersex" at all.

Table 9.19 Differences of sex development that test the rules of sexual differentiation. MIS is Müllerian-inhibiting substance; DHT is dihydrotestosterone.
ConditionChromosomes and gonadsWhat develops differentlyBody at birth and pubertyUsual gender identity
Complete androgen insensitivity syndrome46,XY; internal testesAndrogen receptors do not respond; MIS worksFemale external genitals, short vagina, no uterus; breasts develop, little pubic hairFemale (Pinel's Anne S.)
Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (adrenogenital syndrome)46,XX; ovariesCortisol deficiency; excess adrenal androgensEnlarged clitoris, partly fused labia; typical female internal organsUsually female; play more male-typical (Meyer-Bahlburg, 2004)
5-alpha-reductase deficiency46,XY; testesToo little DHT; testosterone normalFemale-looking or ambiguous genitals; marked virilisation at puberty17 of 18 raised as girls became male (Imperato-McGinley, 1979); role of culture debated

Klinefelter syndrome47,XXY; small testesExtra X; low testosteroneMale; small testes, often tall, sometimes breast tissue at pubertyMale

How common are DSDs, and what should be done about them? The answer to the first question depends entirely on the definition. The biologist Anne Fausto-Sterling, who provocatively proposed that there are not two sexes but five, estimated that about 17 of every 1,000 births, or 1.7 percent, show some difference of sex development. The psychologist and physician Leonard Sax replied in 2002 that this figure included conditions that most clinicians do not regard as intersex at all, such as Klinefelter syndrome, Turner syndrome and a late-onset, mild form of adrenal hyperplasia. Restricting the term to conditions in which chromosomal sex is inconsistent with the body's sex, or in which the body cannot be classified as male or female, he arrived at about 0.018 percent, almost 100 times lower. Neither number is simply "right"; they answer different questions, and an exam that asks how common intersex is expects you to know that the 1.7 percent figure comes from a broad definition.

The second question, what to do, has divided medicine for half a century. For decades a baby born with ambiguous genitals was treated almost as an emergency: a sex was assigned quickly and genital surgery performed early, often in infancy, so that the child's body would match the assigned gender before the child could remember it. Many of those children grew up dissatisfied with surgery they had never chosen, and some grew up with a gender identity different from the one they had been assigned. Fausto-Sterling and many clinicians have since urged caution about early, irreversible genital surgery, and practice has moved towards delaying operations that are not medically necessary until the person can take part in the decision, while still assigning a gender of rearing and supporting the family. One of the cases that did most to bring about that change was not, in fact, a DSD at all. It was a boy born with typical male anatomy who lost his penis as a baby.

Exam trap

Match the condition to the chromosomes. Complete androgen insensitivity: XY, testes, female body, female identity (the "woman with testes"). Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (adrenogenital syndrome): XX, ovaries, masculinised external genitals, usually female identity. 5-alpha-reductase deficiency: XY, female-looking genitals at birth, virilisation at puberty (the Dominican güevedoces). Turner is 45,X (female); Klinefelter is 47,XXY (male). The 1.7 percent prevalence is Fausto-Sterling's broad estimate; Sax's narrow definition gives about 0.018 percent. "Hermaphrodite" is an outdated term.

Nature, nurture and the Reimer case

To see why the case of Bruce, Brenda and David Reimer, told at the start of this chapter, became the most famous in the literature on sexual development, you need to know the theory it was supposed to prove. Its author, John Money (1921 to 2006), was a New Zealand-born psychologist who worked at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore from 1952 until his death and who built his reputation on the study and treatment of children born with differences of sex development. Money gave the field much of its vocabulary. In a 1955 paper on what was then called human hermaphroditism he introduced the term gender role for everything a person says and does to show others that he or she is a boy or man, a girl or woman. The related term gender identity, for the private experience of being male or female, is usually credited to the psychiatrist Robert Stoller in the early 1960s (sources give 1963 or 1964), and it was Money who popularised it. With Anke Ehrhardt he set out his views at book length in Man and Woman, Boy and Girl (1972).

Money's central claim was that gender identity is not fixed at birth. A newborn, he argued, is psychosexually open, and gender identity is established by rearing and social learning during the first two or three years of life, after which it is firm. The clinical policy that followed, later called the optimal gender policy, was meant to give intersex children the best possible lives: decide early which sex would give the child the best outcome (judged largely by the appearance of the genitals and what surgery could achieve), assign that sex, make the body match it surgically, raise the child consistently in that gender, and avoid anything that might plant doubt, which in practice often meant not telling the child the full history. If the theory was right, the policy was humane. If it was wrong, it was a recipe for lifelong distress.

Money's theory rested mainly on children with DSDs, and that was its weakness, because in those children it is never clear how much of the outcome comes from rearing and how much from the very hormonal differences that made their genitals atypical. The Reimer twins seemed to remove that problem. Here was a genetically male child, with typical male prenatal hormones and typical male genitals at birth, who lost his penis in an accident, was castrated, and was raised as a girl. And he had an identical twin brother, raised as a boy, who could serve as what Pinel calls "a genetically identical control". It was, as some saw it, the ultimate test of nature and nurture. In his 1975 report, written when the child was 9, Money described "her" as having developed as a typical girl, and he took the outcome as strong support for his social-learning theory: removal of the gonads, genital surgery and rearing as a girl had, he believed, overridden the masculinising effects of male genes and early androgens. The case was widely cited as evidence that gender identity is learned.

The opening of this chapter told you what was really happening: a child who from early childhood preferred boys' activities, felt herself to be a boy by the age of seven, rebelled against the oestrogen given at 12, and at 14, when told the truth, decided to live as a boy, at which point "all of a sudden everything clicked". The scientific world learned this only in 1997, and the way it learned is worth studying as a lesson in how a scientific claim is tested.

Key study: Diamond and Sigmundson (1997): sex reassignment at birth, a long-term review

The question. Had the child reared as a girl after the loss of his penis really developed as a girl, as the literature had reported for two decades? Milton Diamond (1934 to 2024), a sexologist at the University of Hawaii, had challenged the idea of psychosexual neutrality at birth as early as 1965, in a paper critically evaluating the development of human sexual behaviour.

The method. With the cooperation of H. Keith Sigmundson, the psychiatrist who had supervised the child's care for a period, Diamond traced the patient as an adult and reconstructed his history from childhood onwards. They published it in the Archives of Pediatrics and Adolescent Medicine in March 1997; in the literature the case became known by the pseudonym "John/Joan".

The findings. The individual had rejected the sex of rearing, had switched at puberty to living as a male, and, at the time of the report, had lived successfully as a man from then on. Despite female genitals and female rearing, in Pinel's summary, "he/she developed along male lines".

The conclusion. The standard recommendation, that an infant boy with extensive penile damage should be reared as a girl, should be reconsidered, and future cases managed in the light of this evidence. Pinel draws the wider lesson: "Apparently, the organ that determines the course of psychosocial development is the brain, not the genitals."

The criticism. It is one case, reconstructed long after the events, and the person at its centre had been through castration, hormone treatment, surgery and years of distress in a family under great strain. A single case can refute a universal claim (that rearing always determines identity), but it cannot by itself establish the opposite universal claim.

One case, however striking, is still one case. Was the Reimer outcome a fluke? Evidence from a larger group came in 2004, from the same hospital where Money had worked. William Reiner and John Gearhart studied genetic males born with cloacal exstrophy, a rare defect of the whole pelvis in which the penis is severely underdeveloped or absent but the testes and the prenatal hormones are typically male. For about 25 years, assigning such babies to the female sex had been the advocated practice, in line with the optimal gender policy. Reiner and Gearhart assessed all 16 genetic males with the condition in their clinic, aged 5 to 16. Fourteen had been assigned female at birth, socially, legally and surgically; the parents of the other two had refused. During the study, 8 of the 14 assigned female declared themselves male, while both children raised as boys remained boys. All 16 showed moderate to marked male-typical interests and attitudes. The authors concluded, in the New England Journal of Medicine, that routine assignment of genetic males to the female sex at birth "can result in unpredictable sexual identification". Notice both halves of that result. More than half of the children declared a male identity despite female rearing, which is strong evidence against Money's theory; but five of the fourteen were living as girls, which shows that typical male prenatal hormones do not determine gender identity in every case either.

Put the three lines of evidence together (the Reimer case, the cloacal exstrophy series and the Dominican 5-alpha-reductase families of Section 9.8.4) and a consistent picture emerges. Gender identity is not a blank slate written on by rearing. Something established before birth, probably involving the action of androgens on the developing brain, pulls strongly towards a particular identity, and surgery, hormones and upbringing in the other direction often fail to override it. At the same time, the evidence is not consistent with a simple rule that "male hormones make a male identity": girls with congenital adrenal hyperplasia are exposed to high androgen levels and almost all have a female identity, and some children with cloacal exstrophy accept their female assignment.

What the Reimer case cannot show deserves equal care, because it is often over-read. It is a single life. The child was not simply "raised as a girl": he had been castrated at 22 months, was given oestrogen at puberty, knew that he was being treated differently from other children, and lived in a family under extraordinary strain; any of these could have contributed to his distress. The reassignment was also made at 22 months, within the period in which, on Money's own account, identity was still open but already beginning to firm up. And the case says nothing directly about why the gender identity of transgender people differs from the sex they were assigned, since David Reimer's identity in fact matched his chromosomes, his gonads before castration and his prenatal hormones. The parallel that can fairly be drawn is narrower: in both, a gender identity once formed resisted strong external pressure to change it, whether that pressure came from surgery and rearing or, as many transgender people report, from family and society.

Pinel ends the case with a clinical conclusion, and it is the one you should carry into an interview. The practice of surgically modifying a person's sex at birth should be curtailed: irrevocable treatments should await early puberty and the emergence of the person's own gender identity, at which point a compatible course of treatment can be chosen. That principle, that irreversible steps should follow rather than precede the person's own identity, now guides the care of children with DSDs, and a version of the same caution runs through the debate about medical treatment for adolescents in Section 9.9.6.

How gender identity develops

If gender identity is neither fixed by chromosomes nor written by rearing alone, when and how does it form? The developmental picture is clearer than the causal one. Most children begin to label themselves and others as boys or girls between about two and three years of age, and Barlow and Durand, summarising Money and Ehrhardt (1972) and later work, describe gender identity as firming up between 18 months and 3 years. DSM-5-TR notes that this is also the age at which most children begin to show gendered behaviour and interests, and the age at which gender-nonconforming behaviour usually first appears in children who are later referred to gender clinics (between 2 and 4). How a child's understanding of gender then matures, from labelling to the understanding that gender is stable over time and constant across changes in clothing or activity (Lawrence Kohlberg's gender constancy), and how children organise what they learn about gender (Sandra Bem's gender schema theory), are taught in the Developmental book.

Prenatal hormones appear to shape gender-typed behaviour even in children without any DSD. Bonnie Auyeung and her colleagues in Cambridge (2009) measured testosterone in the amniotic fluid of pregnant women who had undergone amniocentesis for routine clinical reasons, and later asked about their children's play using a standard questionnaire of sex-typical play. Higher fetal testosterone predicted more male-typical play in both boys and girls. It was the first evidence linking ordinary variation in prenatal testosterone to gender-typed play in the general population. Notice what was measured, though: play, not identity. The study fits the conclusion from congenital adrenal hyperplasia that early androgens shape gendered behaviour, and it leaves open how gender identity itself is built.

The last point of this section is the one most often missed. A person's body type, gender identity and sexual orientation are separate dimensions. Pinel puts it plainly: "sexual orientation, gender identity, and body type are sometimes unrelated". A transgender woman may be attracted to men, to women, to both or to neither, and her orientation is an independent matter that is not changed by transition. Pinel's explanation is the one you met in Section 9.8.3: the brain's sex differences develop at different times and by different mechanisms, so there is no reason for every dimension of sex and gender to line up in every person. The biology of sexual orientation is taught in Section 9.10.6; the vocabulary that keeps these dimensions apart is in Section 9.1.2 and Table 9.1.

Exam trap

Sex is biological (chromosomes, gonads, hormones, anatomy); gender identity is psychological (the inner sense of being male, female or another gender); they usually agree but can come apart. Congenital adrenal hyperplasia shifts gender-typed behaviour far more than gender identity (Meyer-Bahlburg 2004; DSM-5-TR). The Dominican güevedoces have 5-alpha-reductase deficiency, not androgen insensitivity (in androgen insensitivity there is no virilisation at puberty). John Money held that identity is learned in the first years and coined "gender role" (1955); Diamond and Sigmundson (1997) showed that the "John/Joan" child had rejected female rearing. Gender identity firms up between about 18 months and 3 years.

9.9 Gender Dysphoria and Gender IncongruenceMedium yield · 3 Qs

Section 9.8 ended with a conclusion: gender identity forms early, it is shaped by forces that are present before birth as well as by experience, and once formed it is very hard to change from outside. For most people it matches the sex they were assigned at birth, and they never have reason to notice that the two are separate things. For a minority they do not match. Some of these people are content, or become content once they can live in their own gender; many experience intense distress, especially when the body changes at puberty in a direction that feels wrong, or when family, school or society refuses to accept who they are. This section is about how psychiatry and medicine have described that experience, and how the description has changed, edition by edition, from a sexual deviation to an identity disorder, to a diagnosis centred on distress, and finally, in the World Health Organization's classification, to a condition outside the mental disorders altogether. You will learn the DSM-5-TR criteria and why each is there, what is known about how common gender dysphoria is, how it develops and what may cause it, how it is told apart from conditions that resemble it, what treatment involves and why some of it is fiercely debated, and how Indian law has dealt with the country's long-established transgender communities.

From transsexualism to gender dysphoria and gender incongruence

People who live in a gender other than the one they were assigned at birth have been recorded in many cultures and in many periods of history; DSM-5-TR itself notes that institutionalised gender categories beyond man and woman exist in India, Sri Lanka, Myanmar, Oman, Samoa, Thailand and among Indigenous Peoples of North America. Medicine's interest began in Berlin early in the twentieth century, with Magnus Hirschfeld, whose life is told in Section 9.1.3. In Die Transvestiten (1910) Hirschfeld coined the word "transvestite" for people who felt a strong need to dress and live as the other sex, a broad group that included many people who would today be called transgender, and in 1923 he used the word "transsexual". His Institute for Sexual Science arranged some of the first gender-affirming operations. Dora Richter had operations arranged through it in 1922 and 1931. The Danish painter Lili Elbe had a series of operations in 1930 and 1931 and died on 13 September 1931, after the last of them.

The subject reached the general public two decades later. On 1 December 1952 the front page of the New York Daily News announced "Ex-GI Becomes Blonde Beauty". It was the story of Christine Jorgensen (1926 to 1989), an American former soldier who had been treated with hormones and surgery in Copenhagen, and it made her internationally famous. In 1966 the German-born American endocrinologist Harry Benjamin (1885 to 1986) published The Transsexual Phenomenon, which argued that for these patients psychotherapy could not change the conviction of belonging to the other sex, so the humane course was to change the body to fit the mind with hormones and surgery. The same year, the Johns Hopkins Hospital in Baltimore, where John Money (Section 9.8.5) was among the founders of its gender identity clinic, announced that it was performing such surgery, the first American academic medical centre to do so (sources differ on whether the clinic dates from 1965 or 1966). It stopped in 1979, after a follow-up of 50 applicants by Jon Meyer and Donna Reter, published in the Archives of General Psychiatry that year, reported that those who had surgery showed no objective advantage in social rehabilitation over those who had not; the study's methods were later strongly criticised. Also in 1979, a professional society named after Benjamin, the Harry Benjamin International Gender Dysphoria Association, published the first Standards of Care, guidelines setting out the steps of assessment and treatment. In 2006 the association was renamed the World Professional Association for Transgender Health (WPATH), and its Standards of Care are still the most widely used international guidelines (Section 9.9.6).

The manuals followed. Table 9.20 traces the diagnosis edition by edition, Figure 9.12 sets the whole century on one timeline, and the names themselves tell the story. In the World Health Organization's ICD-9 (1975), trans-sexualism (code 302.5) sat among the sexual deviations and disorders. DSM-III (1980) introduced two diagnoses within its psychosexual disorders: transsexualism, for adults who wished to live as the other sex and to change their bodies, and gender identity disorder of childhood. DSM-III-R (1987) added a third, gender identity disorder of adolescence or adulthood, nontranssexual type, for people with persistent discomfort about their assigned sex who did not seek bodily change, and moved the whole group into the disorders usually first evident in infancy, childhood or adolescence. ICD-10 (approved in 1990) listed the gender identity disorders under F64 in its mental and behavioural chapter: F64.0 transsexualism, F64.1 dual-role transvestism (wearing the clothes of the other sex for part of the time to enjoy temporary membership of it, without sexual arousal and without wishing for a permanent change) and F64.2 gender identity disorder of childhood. DSM-IV (1994) merged the DSM-III diagnoses into a single gender identity disorder, with criteria for children and for adolescents or adults, placed beside the sexual dysfunctions and paraphilias in one class, "sexual and gender identity disorders". DSM-IV described it as a condition in which a person's experienced gender identity does not match the sex they were assigned. The name carried a message: the identity itself was the disorder.

DSM-5 (2013) changed the message. It renamed the diagnosis gender dysphoria ("dysphoria" means distress or unease, the opposite of euphoria), gave it a chapter of its own, separate from both the sexual dysfunctions and the paraphilias (Section 9.1.5), and moved the focus from identity to distress. In DSM-5-TR's words, the current term "is more descriptive than the previous DSM-IV term gender identity disorder and focuses on dysphoria as the clinical problem, not identity per se". Being transgender is not, in itself, a disorder in DSM-5-TR; what can be diagnosed is the clinically significant distress or impairment that may accompany the incongruence between experienced and assigned gender. Why keep a diagnosis at all? Largely because many people with gender dysphoria need medical treatment, and in most health systems a diagnosis is what makes assessment and treatment possible. DSM-5-TR (2022) keeps the diagnosis and the criteria, and its text speaks of "experienced gender" and "gender-affirming" treatment.

The World Health Organization went one step further. ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and in force from 1 January 2022, renamed the condition gender incongruence and moved it out of the chapter on mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders into a new Chapter 17, "Conditions related to sexual health", alongside the sexual dysfunctions and the sexual pain disorders (Section 9.1.6). The reasoning was the logical end of the DSM-5 argument. Being transgender is not a mental disorder, and classifying it as one adds to the stigma transgender people already face; but many of them need health care, such as hormones and surgery, and health systems need a code to provide and pay for it. Chapter 17 gives them the code without the label of mental illness. ICD-11 has two categories, with definitions worth knowing in detail:

  • HA60, gender incongruence of adolescence or adulthood: a marked and persistent incongruence between a person's experienced gender and the assigned sex, which often leads to a desire to "transition", that is, to live and be accepted as a person of the experienced gender, through hormonal treatment, surgery or other health care that brings the body into line, as far as the person wishes and as far as possible, with the experienced gender. It cannot be assigned before the onset of puberty.
  • HA61, gender incongruence of childhood: a marked incongruence between the experienced or expressed gender and the assigned sex in pre-pubertal children, including a strong desire to be a different gender, a strong dislike of one's sexual anatomy or anticipated secondary sex characteristics (or a strong desire for those of the experienced gender), and make-believe play, toys, games, activities and playmates typical of the experienced gender. The incongruence must have persisted for about 2 years.

Both definitions state that gender-variant behaviour and preferences alone are not a basis for the diagnosis, and both exclude the paraphilic disorders. Notice two differences from DSM-5-TR: neither ICD-11 definition requires distress or impairment, and the childhood category requires about 2 years rather than 6 months. For examination purposes, the most important fact is the correspondence: DSM-5's gender dysphoria corresponds to ICD-11's gender incongruence, just as DSM-IV's gender identity disorder corresponded to ICD-10's gender identity disorders (F64).

Table 9.20 The gender diagnosis, edition by edition. GID is gender identity disorder.
Manual (year)Name of the diagnosisWhere it satWhat the change meant
ICD-9 (1975)Trans-sexualism (302.5)Sexual deviations and disordersMedicalised as a sexual deviation
DSM-III (1980)Transsexualism; GID of childhoodPsychosexual disordersFirst DSM diagnoses
DSM-III-R (1987)Adds GID of adolescence or adulthood, nontranssexual typeDisorders usually first evident in infancy, childhood or adolescenceFramed as a developmental disorder
ICD-10 (1990)F64 gender identity disorders: F64.0 transsexualism, F64.1 dual-role transvestism, F64.2 GID of childhoodChapter V, mental and behavioural disordersA mental disorder
DSM-IV (1994), DSM-IV-TR (2000)Gender identity disorder (in children; in adolescents or adults)Sexual and gender identity disordersThe identity itself framed as the disorder
DSM-5 (2013), DSM-5-TR (2022)Gender dysphoria (in children; in adolescents and adults)A chapter of its ownThe distress, not the identity, is the clinical problem
ICD-11 (adopted 2019, in force 2022)Gender incongruence of adolescence or adulthood (HA60); of childhood (HA61)Chapter 17, conditions related to sexual healthNot a mental disorder; a code kept for access to care
Exam trap

In ICD-11, gender incongruence (HA60, HA61) is in Chapter 17, "Conditions related to sexual health": not Chapter 6 (mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders), not Chapter 5 (endocrine, nutritional or metabolic diseases), and not removed altogether. The same mismatch between identity and assigned sex is transsexualism (DSM-III, ICD-9, ICD-10 F64.0), gender identity disorder (DSM-IV, ICD-10 F64), gender dysphoria (DSM-5, DSM-5-TR) and gender incongruence (ICD-11). DSM-5's gender dysphoria corresponds to ICD-11's gender incongruence. A question that defines "a condition in which a person's sexual identity does not match his or her physical gender" is loosely worded (the correct terms are gender identity and assigned sex) and is keyed gender identity disorder, the DSM-IV and ICD-10 name; if the options include both gender identity disorder and gender dysphoria and the question asks about DSM-5, choose gender dysphoria. When gender incongruence moved to Chapter 17, the sexual dysfunctions and the sexual pain disorders moved with it; only the paraphilic disorders stayed in Chapter 6 (Section 9.1.6). The distractors are different things: a paraphilia concerns what arouses a person, a sexual dysfunction concerns the sexual response cycle, and androgyny is a blend of masculine and feminine personality traits (Section 9.1.2).

The DSM-5-TR criteria

DSM-5-TR has one overarching diagnosis, gender dysphoria, with two developmentally appropriate sets of criteria: one for children and one for adolescents and adults. Both share a core: a marked incongruence between one's experienced or expressed gender and one's assigned gender, lasting at least 6 months, together with clinically significant distress or impairment. What differs is how the incongruence shows itself, because a five-year-old and a twenty-five-year-old express the same inner experience in very different ways. The criteria for children are concrete and behavioural; those for adolescents and adults centre on the body and on identity.

Gender dysphoria in children (ICD-10-CM code F64.2) requires at least six of eight indicators, and one of the six must be the first:

  1. A strong desire to be of the other gender, or an insistence that one is the other gender (or some alternative gender different from the one assigned).
  2. In children assigned male, a strong preference for cross-dressing or simulating female clothing; in children assigned female, a strong preference for wearing only typically masculine clothing and a strong resistance to typically feminine clothing.
  3. A strong preference for cross-gender roles in make-believe or fantasy play.
  4. A strong preference for the toys, games or activities stereotypically used or engaged in by the other gender.
  5. A strong preference for playmates of the other gender.
  6. In children assigned male, a strong rejection of typically masculine toys, games and activities and a strong avoidance of rough-and-tumble play; in children assigned female, a strong rejection of typically feminine toys, games and activities.
  7. A strong dislike of one's sexual anatomy.
  8. A strong desire for the primary or secondary sex characteristics that match one's experienced gender.

Why must indicator 1 be present? Look at the other seven. Five of them (clothing, play roles, toys, playmates, rejection of typical toys) describe gender-nonconforming behaviour, and gender-nonconforming behaviour in childhood is common, normal and usually tells you nothing about a child's future. A girl who climbs trees, plays cricket and refuses frocks is a tomboy, not a patient. If those five indicators alone could add up to a diagnosis, DSM-5-TR would be turning ordinary variation in play into a mental disorder, the very error for which the older childhood diagnosis was criticised. Requiring the strong desire to be, or the insistence that one is, another gender anchors the diagnosis to identity rather than to play. DSM-5-TR makes the same point in its own words when it warns that gender-nonconforming behaviour "by itself should not be interpreted as an indicator of current or future gender dysphoria", and that the diagnosis "is not meant to merely describe nonconformity to stereotypical gender role behavior". The phrase "or some alternative gender" in indicator 1, and throughout the adult criteria, recognises that a person's experienced gender need not be the "other" binary gender; it may be in between or outside the categories of male and female.

Gender dysphoria in adolescents and adults (F64.0) requires at least two of six indicators, with no single indicator required:

  1. A marked incongruence between one's experienced or expressed gender and one's primary or secondary sex characteristics (or, in young adolescents, the anticipated secondary sex characteristics).
  2. A strong desire to be rid of one's primary or secondary sex characteristics because of that incongruence (or, in young adolescents, to prevent their development).
  3. A strong desire for the primary or secondary sex characteristics of the other gender.
  4. A strong desire to be of the other gender (or some alternative gender).
  5. A strong desire to be treated as the other gender (or some alternative gender).
  6. A strong conviction that one has the typical feelings and reactions of the other gender (or some alternative gender).

The clauses about "young adolescents" exist because puberty begins at different ages (somewhere between 9 and 13 in those assigned female and between 11 and 14 in those assigned male, according to DSM-5-TR), and a young adolescent whose body has not yet changed may not dislike characteristics she or he does not yet have, but may be markedly distressed by the changes that are about to come. For both age groups, Criterion B, clinically significant distress or impairment in social, school, occupational or other important areas of functioning, is what turns a gender identity into a diagnosable condition. And DSM-5-TR adds an important qualification to the very idea of dysphoria: it "does not refer to distress related to stigma", which is a distinct, though possibly co-occurring, source of distress. A transgender woman who is distressed only because her neighbours mock her has a real and serious problem, but the problem is discrimination, not gender dysphoria.

Specifiers and related categories. Two specifiers can be added. With a disorder/difference of sex development is used when the person has a specific, codable DSD documented in the medical record, such as congenital adrenal hyperplasia or androgen insensitivity syndrome (Section 9.8.4); the DSD is coded as well as the gender dysphoria. Posttransition, for adolescents and adults only, is used when the person has transitioned to full-time living in the experienced gender (with or without a legal change) and has undergone, or is preparing to have, at least one gender-affirming medical procedure or treatment regimen, namely regular gender-affirming hormone treatment or gender reassignment surgery. Why diagnose someone whose transition is complete? Because such a person may still need continuing treatment (hormones for life, for example), and the specifier records that. When the symptoms cause distress but do not meet the full criteria, the clinician may use other specified gender dysphoria (F64.8), recording the reason, for example "brief gender dysphoria" when the duration is under 6 months, or unspecified gender dysphoria (F64.9) when no reason is given or there is too little information. Table 9.21 sets the two criteria sets side by side with their ICD-11 equivalents.
