Exam trap

Children: 6 of 8 indicators, one of which must be A1 (the strong desire to be, or insistence that one is, another gender). Adolescents and adults: 2 of 6, none compulsory. Both: at least 6 months plus distress or impairment. ICD-11's childhood category needs about 2 years. The posttransition specifier applies only to adolescents and adults. Distress that comes only from stigma is not gender dysphoria. Codes: F64.2 for children and F64.0 for adolescents and adults (the old ICD-10 codes for gender identity disorder of childhood and for transsexualism).

Who, how many and what follows

Before the numbers, a person. Shirin is 27, a graphic designer in Kolkata. She was assigned male at birth and given a boy's name, but she says she has known she was a girl "since before I knew words for it": as a small child she draped her mother's dupatta over her head as long hair and cried when her hair was cut short for school. Through adolescence she hid it, and the hiding cost her: she dreaded the changes of puberty, avoided the boys' changing rooms, and stopped looking in mirrors. For the last three years she has lived as a woman among her friends and at work, where her colleagues know her as Shirin. At home in her parents' flat she still lives as their son. She comes to the clinic because for six months she has been sleeping badly, has lost interest in work, cries most evenings and is afraid of what will happen when her family finds out; an aunt has begun to talk about arranging a marriage.

Push Shirin through Table 9.21. She describes a marked incongruence between her experienced gender and her sex characteristics, a strong desire to be a woman, a strong desire to be treated as one and a conviction that her feelings and reactions are those of a woman: four of the six adult indicators, present for far longer than six months. The distress is clinically significant, and part of it, but not all, comes from fear of her family's reaction. She meets the criteria for gender dysphoria in adolescents and adults, and she also meets the criteria for a depressive episode. A clinical psychologist assessing her would explore all of this: the history of her gender identity, her mood and any thoughts of suicide, her safety at home, her support, and what she herself wants, which may or may not include hormones. Shirin is also a reminder of the classic phrase in which many transgender people have described their experience, and which both Pinel and Barlow and Durand quote: being a woman "trapped" in a man's body. It is a vivid description, but not everyone's; some transgender people describe their experience quite differently, and many nonbinary people do not identify with either side of it.

How common is gender dysphoria? DSM-5-TR is frank: there are no large-scale population studies. In populations seeking gender-affirming treatment, the prevalence of the diagnosis has been estimated at less than 1 in 1,000 (under 0.1 percent) for people assigned male and for people assigned female at birth, and because many adults never attend specialist clinics, this is likely an underestimate. Surveys of the general population in the United States and Europe give higher figures, which depend on what is asked. Between 0.5 and 0.6 percent identify as transgender; between 0.6 and 1.1 percent experience an incongruent gender identity; between 2.1 and 2.6 percent feel that they are a person of a different sex; and between 0.2 and 0.6 percent want medical treatment to change their bodies. The sex ratio among people referred to clinics depends on age. In children, those assigned male outnumber those assigned female, by ratios from 1.25:1 to 4.3:1. In adults, more people assigned male seek gender-affirming treatment, with ratios from 1:1 to 6.1:1 in most American and European studies. And the picture is changing: clinics report more children and adolescents referred, at younger ages, more often after an early social transition, and a shift towards more adolescents and young adults assigned female at birth.

What comes with it. DSM-5-TR reports an overrepresentation of autism spectrum traits in children, adolescents and adults with gender dysphoria, and notes that people with autism spectrum disorder are more likely to be gender diverse (Chapter 3 teaches autism). Clinically referred children show raised levels of anxiety, disruptive, impulse-control and depressive disorders, and referred adolescents and adults have high rates of anxiety and depressive disorders in particular; those who have suffered harassment and violence may develop posttraumatic stress disorder. The most serious association is with suicide. Reported rates of suicidal thoughts and attempts among transgender people range from 30 to 80 percent, with past maltreatment, victimisation because of gender, depression, substance misuse and younger age as risk factors. A study of 572 children referred to a Canadian gender clinic found them 8.6 times more likely to have self-harmed or attempted suicide than comparison children, even after allowing for general behaviour and peer problems. Suicide risk is raised before gender-affirming treatment and legal recognition; after treatment, adjustment varies, and some people continue to have prominent anxiety and mood symptoms and remain at increased risk. Suicide in sexual and gender minorities is discussed in Section 5.8.5 of this book, and the minority stress model that explains much of this burden (stigma, rejection, concealment and expected rejection) in Book 2, Section 10.6.9.

Indeed, much of the impairment in gender dysphoria comes from the way others respond. DSM-5-TR describes children teased, harassed and pressured to dress in the clothes of their assigned sex, especially in unaccepting homes and schools, and older children who become isolated from their peers. In adolescents and adults, gender dysphoria is associated with high levels of stigma, discrimination and victimisation, leading to a negative self-concept, depression, suicidality, school dropout, unemployment and economic marginalisation, especially in those without family or social support; and access to health care may be blocked by institutional discomfort, inexperience or hostility. The reverse is also true: children and adolescents who feel supported and accepted in their gender nonconformity may show few or no psychological problems.

Development and course. In clinic-referred children in Canada and the Netherlands, gender-nonconforming behaviour usually begins between the ages of 2 and 4, the age at which most children begin to express gendered behaviour and interests (Section 9.8.6). Expressions of anatomic dysphoria (discomfort with one's sexual anatomy, or the wish for a different anatomy) become more common as children approach and anticipate puberty. What happens next is the most debated question in the field. In studies from North America and the Netherlands, gender dysphoria persisted into adolescence or adulthood in between 2 and 39 percent of children assigned male and between 12 and 50 percent of those assigned female. In the others it remitted, a process often called desistance. Most of those whose gender dysphoria continued into adolescence identified as heterosexual (attracted to people of their birth-assigned sex); most of those whose dysphoria had ended by adolescence identified as gay, lesbian or bisexual. Persistence was modestly related to the severity of the dysphoria at the first assessment, and DSM-5-TR adds that early social transition may also be a factor in persistence. Some children whose dysphoria remits in adolescence experience a recurrence in adulthood.

These figures are cited by both sides of a fierce argument, so it is important to state their limits. The older follow-up studies diagnosed children by the criteria of their time, and critics have argued that some of the children counted as "desisting" may never have had gender dysphoria in the present sense, because the earlier childhood criteria could be met by gender-nonconforming behaviour without a stated desire to be another gender; DSM-5's requirement of indicator A1 closes that gap for future studies. Others reply that the studies show, at the least, that a child's gender dysphoria does not always continue, which is a reason for caution about irreversible steps in childhood. What is not in dispute is that persistence is more likely when the childhood dysphoria is more intense, and that puberty is the point at which the paths divide.

DSM-5-TR describes two broad trajectories. In early-onset gender dysphoria, beginning before puberty, symptoms meet the childhood criteria; the dysphoria may continue into adolescence and adulthood, or it may desist (or be denied) for a period, sometimes to recur. These people often present for care in childhood, adolescence or young adulthood, which may reflect a more intense dysphoria. In late-onset gender dysphoria, beginning around puberty or much later in life, some people recall a childhood wish to be another gender that they never expressed, while others recall no childhood signs at all, and their parents are often surprised. Whichever the path, the onset of puberty, or the knowledge that it is coming, can bring feelings of incongruence to a peak. A third path concerns people with a DSD: they come to attention early because of decisions about gender assignment, many have had gonadectomy or genital surgery in childhood, and many experience uncertainty about their gender rather than a firm conviction that they are of another gender; the proportion who develop gender dysphoria varies greatly with the particular condition and the gender assigned.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR prevalence: under 1 in 1,000 in treatment-seeking populations; in population surveys, 0.5 to 0.6 percent identify as transgender. DSM-5 (2013) printed far lower figures based on specialist clinics (about 0.005 to 0.014 percent for adults assigned male and 0.002 to 0.003 percent for adults assigned female); if a question quotes those, it is using DSM-5, not DSM-5-TR. Referred children: more assigned male (1.25:1 to 4.3:1), but referrals of adolescents have shifted towards those assigned female. Persistence from childhood: 2 to 39 percent (assigned male), 12 to 50 percent (assigned female). Suicidality and attempts: 30 to 80 percent. Onset of nonconforming behaviour in referred children: 2 to 4 years.

Causes

What makes a person's gender identity differ from the sex they were assigned? The honest answer is that no cause has been established. Barlow and Durand write that research has yet to uncover any specific biological contribution and that the development of an incongruent gender identity "is still something of a mystery". DSM-5-TR lists a number of findings, each suggestive and none decisive. They are worth knowing, both for examinations and because families often ask "why?", and the clinician's task is to answer truthfully without pretending to more certainty than the evidence allows.

Temperament and environment. Among children with gender dysphoria, a greater intensity of gender nonconformity and an older age at presentation make persistence into adolescence and adulthood more likely. People assigned male at birth with gender dysphoria (and without a DSD) more often have older brothers than cisgender males do, a finding that echoes the fraternal birth order effect in male sexual orientation (Section 9.10.6). For postpubertal onset in people assigned male, DSM-5-TR mentions as a predisposing factor "under consideration" a history of transvestism that may develop into autogynephilia (sexual arousal associated with the thought or image of oneself as a woman). That idea comes from a wider theory proposed by the Canadian sexologist Ray Blanchard, who in 1989 distinguished two types of transgender women: those attracted to men, with an early onset, and those not attracted to men (mostly attracted to women, but also bisexual or asexual), whose gender dysphoria he linked to autogynephilia. The typology remains strongly disputed, and many transgender women reject it as a description of their experience; it is not part of the DSM-5-TR criteria, where autogynephilia appears only as a specifier of transvestic disorder (Section 9.6.6).

Genes. DSM-5-TR reports weak familiality among non-twin siblings, greater concordance for gender dysphoria in identical than in fraternal same-sex twins, and some heritability, and suggests a polygenic basis: many genes, each with a small effect, perhaps acting on the sexual differentiation of the brain before birth. The best-known twin estimate comes from Frederick Coolidge, Thede and Young (2002), who asked the parents of 314 twins aged 4 to 17 (96 identical and 61 fraternal pairs) to complete a six-item scale based on the DSM-IV criteria for gender identity disorder. The best-fitting model attributed 62 percent of the variance to additive genetic factors and 38 percent to environmental factors not shared by the twins, with nothing left for the shared family environment. Read the method before you quote the number: these were parents' ratings on a short symptom scale in a community sample, not clinical diagnoses, and a heritability estimate describes variation in one population, not the cause in any one person.

Hormones. Since prenatal androgens shape so much of sexual differentiation (Section 9.8), an obvious hypothesis is that gender dysphoria reflects atypical hormone exposure. In people without a DSD the evidence is thin. No abnormalities of sex-hormone levels have been found in 46,XY people with gender dysphoria; in 46,XX people, androgen levels appear somewhat raised (in the range found in women with excess body hair, far below male levels). DSM-5-TR concludes that current evidence is "insufficient to label gender dysphoria without a DSD as a form of intersexuality limited to the central nervous system". In people with a DSD, the picture is clearer: the likelihood of later gender dysphoria rises when prenatal androgen production or response is grossly different from what is usual for the assigned gender. Examples are 46,XY people with typical male prenatal hormones but absent or severely underdeveloped genitals (cloacal exstrophy, Section 9.8.5) who were assigned female, and female-raised 46,XY people with 5-alpha-reductase deficiency who virilise at puberty. Even so, DSM-5-TR repeats the lesson of congenital adrenal hyperplasia: prenatal androgens are more closely related to gendered behaviour than to gender identity, and many people with DSDs and markedly gender-variant behaviour never develop gender dysphoria.

The brain. In 1995 a Dutch team, Jiang-Ning Zhou, Michel Hofman, Louis Gooren and Dick Swaab, reported in Nature a post-mortem study of a small region of the brain, the central subdivision of the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis (BSTc). The region was larger in men than in women, and in the transgender women they studied it was of female size. The authors reported that its size was not influenced by sex hormones in adulthood and was independent of sexual orientation, and they argued that gender identity develops through an interaction between the developing brain and sex hormones. The finding is famous and fragile. The samples were very small; the transgender women had received hormone treatment; and in 2002 Chung, De Vries and Swaab found that the sex difference in the BSTc becomes significant only in adulthood, which makes it hard to see how it could cause an identity that often appears in early childhood. As Barlow and Durand put it, it is not clear whether such differences are a cause or an effect. Remember also Pinel's warning from Section 9.8.3: brains are mosaics of slight, overlapping differences, so no single region can show that a brain is "male" or "female".

Identity and orientation. Finally, keep apart two questions that are often run together. The cause of a person's gender identity and the cause of his or her sexual orientation are different questions. Pinel stresses that gender identity and sexual orientation are independent, and that a transgender person's sexual orientation is not changed by transition. A transgender woman attracted to women is not "really" a heterosexual man, and a transgender man attracted to men is not "really" a heterosexual woman: identity and attraction are separate dimensions (Section 9.8.6). The most defensible summary of the causal evidence is that gender identity, like most human characteristics, probably arises from many genetic, hormonal and developmental influences acting together, that the evidence points towards influences present early in life, and that no test can predict or confirm it.

Exam trap

No cause of gender dysphoria has been established. The 62 percent heritability estimate (Coolidge, Thede and Young, 2002) comes from parents' ratings on a short scale in a community twin sample, not from diagnoses. Zhou and colleagues (1995) found a female-sized BSTc (central bed nucleus of the stria terminalis) in transgender women, but the sex difference in this region becomes significant only in adulthood, so cause and effect are unclear. Hormone levels are normal in 46,XY people with gender dysphoria; those assigned male more often have older brothers. Gender identity and sexual orientation are independent, and transition does not change orientation.

Differential diagnosis

Gender dysphoria has to be distinguished from several conditions that resemble it on the surface. In each case the question is the same: is there a marked incongruence between experienced gender and assigned gender, with distress, or is something else going on?

Nonconformity to gender roles. This is the most important distinction, and the one most often missed by worried parents. Ekta, 9, plays cricket with the boys in her colony, wants her hair cut short, will wear a frock only to weddings and argues with her grandmother about it. Her parents ask whether she "wants to be a boy". When the psychologist asks her, she laughs: she is a girl, she likes being a girl, and she wants to be a fast bowler for India. Ekta shows gender-nonconforming behaviour without any desire to be another gender, so indicator A1 is absent and the diagnosis cannot be made. DSM-5-TR says that gender dysphoria is distinguished from simple nonconformity "by the strong desire to be of another gender than the assigned one and by the extent and pervasiveness of gender-variant activities and interests", and gives as examples of nonconformity that are not gender dysphoria "tomboyism" in girls, "girly-boy" behaviour in boys and occasional cross-dressing in adult men.

Transvestic disorder. Transvestic disorder (Section 9.6.6) is diagnosed in heterosexual or bisexual adolescent and adult males (rarely females) for whom women's clothing produces sexual excitement and causes distress or impairment, without calling their assigned gender into question. The key difference is that cross-dressing in transvestic disorder is sexually motivated and the person's identity is male; in gender dysphoria, the person's identity is at stake and cross-dressing (if that is even the right word for wearing the clothes of one's own experienced gender) is not about arousal. The two can co-exist, and a person can receive both diagnoses; in some people assigned male at birth who are attracted to women and whose gender dysphoria begins after puberty, cross-dressing with sexual excitement is a precursor of gender dysphoria.

Body dysmorphic disorder. A person with body dysmorphic disorder wants a body part altered or removed because it is perceived as abnormally formed or ugly, not because it represents a repudiated assigned gender; a man who wants his nose remade is not rejecting his maleness. Both diagnoses can be given when both sets of criteria are met. People who wish to have a healthy limb amputated (body integrity dysphoria) usually do not want to change gender but to live as amputees. Both conditions are taught in Section 6.6.3.

Autism spectrum disorder. Because gender diversity is more common in autistic people, the two often co-occur, and diagnosing gender dysphoria in an autistic person can be difficult: DSM-5-TR notes that it can be hard to tell co-occurring gender dysphoria from an autistic preoccupation arising from concrete and rigid thinking about gender roles or a limited understanding of social relationships. The clinician must take more time, not dismiss the person's account.

Schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders. Delusions of belonging to another gender can occur in schizophrenia, and gender-themed delusions may occur in up to 20 percent of people with schizophrenia. They are usually recognised by their bizarre content (a belief, for example, that one's sex has been changed overnight by a machine) and by the way they wax and wane with the psychotic episodes. DSM-5-TR is explicit that, in the absence of psychotic symptoms, a person's insistence that he or she is another gender is not a delusion. Schizophrenia and gender dysphoria can also co-occur.

Other presentations. Some people with a wish to be rid of male genitals develop an identity that is neither male nor female and do meet the criteria for gender dysphoria. But some males seek genital surgery for aesthetic reasons or to remove the psychological effects of androgens without any change in male identity; they do not meet the criteria.

Exam trap

The deciding feature of gender dysphoria is a strong desire to be (or insistence that one is) another gender, with distress; tomboyish play, "girly-boy" behaviour and occasional cross-dressing are not enough. Transvestic disorder: arousal from cross-dressing, identity not in question (both diagnoses may be given). Body dysmorphic disorder: the part is "defective", not "the wrong gender's". In schizophrenia, gender-themed delusions (up to 20 percent) are bizarre and fluctuate with episodes; a steady insistence on one's gender without psychosis is not a delusion.

Care and treatment

Treatment for gender dysphoria differs from treatment for almost every other condition in this book in one fundamental way. The aim is not to remove the person's gender identity, which is not regarded as a disorder, but to relieve the distress that comes from the incongruence between that identity and the body, and from the way the world responds to it. For some people that means psychological support alone; for others it includes a social transition; for many it also includes medical steps that bring the body closer to the experienced gender. Barlow and Durand summarise the American Psychiatric Association's guideline (Byne and colleagues, 2012) as a sequence that begins with the least intrusive step, a full psychological evaluation and education, before moving to partly reversible steps such as hormones and, last, to irreversible surgery. Table 9.22 sets out the main interventions.

Psychological assessment and support. A clinical psychologist working with a person who has gender dysphoria explores the history and nature of the gender identity, assesses and treats co-occurring problems (depression, anxiety, self-harm, substance misuse, the effects of harassment or trauma), screens for conditions such as autism that may need to be taken into account, works with the family, and helps the person think through decisions and their consequences. The stance is affirmative: the person's account of their own gender is taken seriously and treated with respect, and the work addresses the minority stress (stigma, rejection, concealment, the expectation of rejection) that drives so much of the distress. Affirmative practice itself belongs to Book 2 (Section 10.6.9). What the clinician must not do is try to change the person's gender identity. Such conversion efforts are regarded as unethical by WPATH and by professional bodies of psychologists and psychiatrists, and in India the National Medical Commission declared conversion therapy professional misconduct for doctors in 2022 (Book 2, Sections 4.4.2 and 10.6.9).

Social transition. Many people live in their experienced gender before, or instead of, any medical treatment: they change their name, their pronouns, their clothing and hairstyle, and the way they present at work, at school and in public. Physically, social transition is fully reversible. It is not psychologically trivial, however; the Cass Review in England described it as an "active intervention" in young children, and DSM-5-TR notes that early social transition may be one of the factors associated with persistence of gender dysphoria into adolescence.

Puberty suppression. For adolescents, the most distinctive treatment is the use of gonadotropin-releasing hormone analogues (GnRH analogues, such as leuprorelin and triptorelin) to pause puberty. Normally the hypothalamus releases GnRH in pulses, and each pulse stimulates the pituitary to release the gonadotropins that drive the gonads. A long-acting analogue stimulates the pituitary continuously instead, and continuous stimulation switches the pituitary's response off: gonadotropin release falls, the gonads stop producing sex hormones, and the physical changes of puberty halt. (The same drugs are used to treat precocious puberty, prostate cancer and, as Section 9.7.6 explains, some paraphilic disorders.) Puberty suppression was developed in the Netherlands as part of what became known as the Dutch protocol, first reported by Peggy Cohen-Kettenis and van Goozen in 1998 and set out in full by Henriette Delemarre-van de Waal and Cohen-Kettenis in 2006; de Vries and Cohen-Kettenis described the Dutch approach to children and adolescents in 2012. In its original form, puberty was suppressed from about age 12, once it had begun, in adolescents whose gender dysphoria had started in childhood and intensified at puberty, who were psychologically stable and who had the support of their families; gender-affirming hormones could follow from about 16, and surgery from 18. The rationale was to spare the young person the distress of an unwanted puberty and its permanent changes (a deep voice, an Adam's apple, breasts) while giving time to explore their identity. Suppression was described as reversible in intent, because puberty resumes if the drug is stopped, but its long-term effects are debated. Bone density falls during treatment (the Cass Review found that bone health is compromised while blockers are taken), the effects on brain development and fertility are uncertain, and nearly all adolescents who start puberty suppression go on to gender-affirming hormones, which raises the question of whether blockers "buy time" or set a course.

Gender-affirming hormones. For people assigned male, treatment is usually with oestrogen, often combined with an antiandrogen such as spironolactone or cyproterone acetate; it produces breast growth, a more female distribution of fat, softer skin and slower growth of body hair, and it reduces erections and fertility. For people assigned female, treatment is with testosterone, which deepens the voice, brings facial and body hair and greater muscle mass, enlarges the clitoris and stops menstruation. Some effects reverse if treatment stops; others, such as breast growth and the deepening of the voice, are largely permanent, and fertility may be reduced, so clinicians discuss fertility preservation before treatment. The hormones carry medical risks (a raised risk of blood clots with oestrogen, for example, and a rise in red blood cells with testosterone) and are prescribed and monitored by an endocrinologist.

Gender-affirming surgery. DSM-5-TR names the main procedures: breast augmentation and vulvovaginoplasty in people assigned male at birth, and transmasculine chest surgery and phalloplasty or metoidioplasty (surgery that creates a phallus from the hormonally enlarged clitoris) in people assigned female. Other procedures include facial surgery and voice surgery. Surgery is irreversible, and it has traditionally required a period of living in the experienced gender (Barlow and Durand describe 1 to 2 years) and psychological stability.

Table 9.22 Interventions for gender dysphoria: stage, reversibility and evidence.
InterventionStage of lifeReversibilityEvidence or controversy
Psychological assessment and support (affirmative practice)Any ageNot a physical interventionBroadly agreed; treats co-occurring problems and minority stress
Social transition (name, pronouns, dress)Children, adolescents, adultsPhysically reversibleEffects on young children uncertain; Cass called it an "active intervention"
Puberty suppression (GnRH analogues)Early puberty (Dutch protocol from about 12)Reversible in intent; effects on bone, brain and fertility debatedEvidence judged "remarkably weak" by the Cass Review; restricted to research in England since 2024
Gender-affirming hormones (oestrogen with antiandrogen; testosterone)Adolescents (Dutch protocol from about 16) and adultsPartly irreversible (voice, breasts, fertility)Psychological benefit in observational studies; Cass urged caution under 18
Gender-affirming surgery (chest, genital, facial)Mainly adultsIrreversibleSatisfaction 75 to 100 percent, about 90 percent recently; regret 1 to 7 percent
Efforts to change gender identity (conversion)NoneNot applicableIneffective and harmful; unethical; professional misconduct for doctors in India (2022)

Outcomes. Among adults who have had gender-affirming surgery and could be reached for follow-up, Barlow and Durand report that between 75 and 100 percent are generally satisfied, with transgender men generally adjusting better than transgender women, and that satisfaction in recent years has averaged about 90 percent (a 2010 Swedish study by Johansson and colleagues). Approximately 1 to 7 percent of those reached later regret the surgery to some extent, and as many as 2 percent attempt suicide after surgery, a rate far higher than in the general population. Predictors of regret include misdiagnosis, co-occurring conditions such as psychosis or alcohol misuse, and poor family support. Two cautions apply. Many people were not available for follow-up, so the true figures are uncertain. And, as DSM-5-TR says, after gender-affirming treatment adjustment varies: co-occurring symptoms often improve, but some people continue to have prominent anxiety and mood symptoms and remain at raised risk of suicide, which is why psychological care should continue after medical treatment.

Standards of care. The most widely used international guidelines are WPATH's Standards of Care, Version 8, published by Eli Coleman and colleagues in the International Journal of Transgender Health in September 2022. Version 8 was the first to include nonbinary people explicitly, and its final text did not set specific minimum ages for medical treatment, recommending instead a comprehensive assessment of each adolescent.

The debate about young people. Gender-affirming medical treatment for adults is widely accepted. Medical treatment for adolescents is one of the most contested questions in medicine today, and you should be able to describe both sides fairly. In England, the rapid rise in referrals to the national children's gender service, with a shift towards adolescents assigned female at birth and high rates of co-occurring problems, led NHS England in 2020 to commission an independent review led by the paediatrician Hilary Cass. The final report of the Cass Review, published on 10 April 2024 and based on systematic reviews by the University of York, judged the evidence base for puberty blockers and hormones in under-18s to be "remarkably weak": only a small fraction of the studies were rated as high quality, and many lacked control groups or long-term follow-up. It recommended a holistic, multidisciplinary assessment of each young person, caution about medical treatment before 18, and better research. Even before the final report, on 12 March 2024, NHS England had decided that puberty blockers would no longer be routinely prescribed for gender dysphoria outside a research setting, and in December 2024 the UK government made its restriction on new prescriptions indefinite, pending further research, including a clinical trial. Finland and Sweden had already limited these treatments for young people, citing the weakness of the evidence.

Other bodies disagree. WPATH criticised the Cass Review, the Endocrine Society and the American Academy of Pediatrics reaffirmed their own guidance, and all three continue to support access to puberty suppression and hormones for adolescents after careful assessment. They point to observational studies, beginning with the Dutch cohorts, in which treated adolescents showed improved psychological functioning; to the distress, depression and suicidality of transgender adolescents who are denied treatment; and to the irreversible changes of an unwanted puberty, which are themselves a consequence of withholding treatment. Sceptics reply that observational studies without control groups cannot separate the effect of treatment from the effects of psychological support and time, that it is hard to predict which adolescents' dysphoria will persist, and that the young people now seeking care differ from those on whom the Dutch protocol was developed. In the United States the question has become a matter of law and politics: many states restricted such treatment for minors, and in June 2025 the Supreme Court, in United States v. Skrmetti, upheld a Tennessee law banning it. Both sides agree on more than the public argument suggests: on careful, individual assessment, on psychological support for the young person and the family, on attention to co-occurring problems, and on the need for better research.

For gender-variant children before puberty, no medical treatment is given, and the options are psychological. The American Psychiatric Association's guideline, as Barlow and Durand summarise it, outlined three approaches: working with the child and family to reduce cross-gender behaviour, on the assumption that it would probably not persist; "watchful waiting", allowing the child's gender expression to unfold while supporting the family; and actively affirming the child's identification. Barlow and Durand note that there was very little hard evidence on which approach was best for a given child. The first approach is now widely regarded as an attempt to change a child's gender identity, and is rejected as unethical by WPATH and many professional bodies.

Clinical link: Aakash, his mother and "is this a phase?"

Aakash, 16, was assigned female at birth. He has bound his chest since he was 13, cut his hair short, and for two months has refused to go to school because the uniform for girls is a salwar kameez; he asks to be called Aakash and to be treated as a boy. His mother, Nikita, a schoolteacher, brings him to the psychologist frightened and in tears. Her first question is: "Is this a phase?"

The psychologist answers honestly: no one can say for certain. The persistence figures she may have read about come mostly from children before puberty; for an adolescent whose feelings have lasted for years and grown stronger with puberty, the picture is different, although the research on adolescents is thinner and contested. Assessment takes time. The psychologist takes a developmental history with both of them (when Aakash first felt this way, how he played and dressed as a small child, how puberty felt), assesses his mood and any self-harm, asks about bullying and about friends, screens for autistic traits, and asks about the binding, because binding too tightly or for too long can cause pain and problems with breathing and the skin. Much of the work is with Nikita: her fear for his safety and future, her grief for the daughter she thought she had, and her worry about what relatives will say. The family is helped to talk with the school about a uniform he can wear so that he can return to class.

Aakash asks about testosterone. The psychologist explains what is known and not known about hormones and their effects (some of them permanent), that any medical step would involve an endocrinologist and his parents as well as him, and that the decision is not one to be made in a single visit. The psychologist neither tells Aakash he is "really" a girl nor promises him a medical outcome. The aims are a safe young man back at school, a mother who understands her son, and a decision about the body that is made carefully, with the best available information.

Exam trap

GnRH analogues (leuprorelin, triptorelin) suppress puberty by continuous stimulation that switches off the pituitary; they are "reversible in intent", but bone density falls during treatment. Hormones are partly irreversible; surgery is irreversible. The Dutch protocol (Cohen-Kettenis) originally used ages of about 12, 16 and 18. WPATH's Standards of Care began in 1979; Version 8 appeared in 2022. The Cass Review reported on 10 April 2024; NHS England's restriction of puberty blockers dates from March 2024. Satisfaction after surgery is high (about 90 percent) and regret low (1 to 7 percent). Conversion efforts are unethical.

India: hijra communities, the law and care

India did not need DSM to know that some people live outside the categories of man and woman. The country has some of the oldest continuous transgender communities in the world, and DSM-5-TR names India first among the cultures with institutionalised gender categories beyond men and women (it adds that it is unclear whether the diagnostic criteria for gender dysphoria would be met by individuals in such contexts). The best known are the hijras, found across north India and beyond. They are known by many other names in different regions and languages: aravanis in Tamil Nadu (where many now prefer the word thirunangai), jogtas and jogappas, devotees of the goddess Yellamma in Maharashtra and Karnataka, and kinnar, a respectful Hindi name. The Transgender Persons Act of 2019 itself lists "kinner, hijra, aravani and jogta" as socio-cultural identities.

Hijra communities are organised in households led by a guru, whose disciples, the chelas, are initiated into the community and learn its customs from her; gurus belong to wider lineages, or gharanas. This guru-chela system often takes the place of the birth family that many hijras have lost. Hijras have a traditional ritual role, called badhai, of blessing newborn children and newly married couples with song and dance, a role that rests on stories from the Ramayana and the Mahabharata. Every year in April and May, thousands of aravanis gather for an eighteen-day festival at the temple of Koothandavar, identified with Aravan of the Mahabharata, in the village of Koovagam in Tamil Nadu, where they re-enact Aravan's marriage to Krishna and then mourn his sacrifice. Respect in ritual has gone with exclusion in daily life. Under British rule the Criminal Tribes Act of 1871 brought hijras (whom it called "eunuchs") under registration and police surveillance, and the stigma long outlived the Act. Cut off from schooling and work, many hijras have depended on badhai, begging and sex work, with high rates of violence and of HIV infection. The 2011 Census, the first to offer a category other than male and female, recorded 4,87,803 people as "others", a figure that the government describes as including transgender persons but which was never a dedicated count.

The Supreme Court, 2014. The legal turning point came on 15 April 2014, when the Supreme Court decided National Legal Services Authority v. Union of India, usually called the NALSA judgment. The Court held that hijras and eunuchs must be treated as a third gender, so that their fundamental rights under the Constitution are protected, and it upheld the right of transgender persons to decide their self-identified gender, as male, female or third gender. It held that insisting on sex reassignment surgery as a condition for changing one's legal gender was illegal, and it directed the central and state governments to grant legal recognition of gender identity, to treat transgender people as backward classes for reservations in education and public appointments, and to provide health care, separate public toilets and welfare schemes. The judgment's language matters for this chapter: it rested legal gender on a person's own sense of identity rather than on the body, the same move that the manuals were making at about the same time.

The Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Act, 2019. Parliament's response was the Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Act, 2019 (Act 40 of 2019), which received presidential assent on 5 December 2019 and came into force on 10 January 2020; its Rules were notified on 25 September 2020. As enacted, the Act:

  • defined a transgender person (section 2(k)) as a person whose gender does not match the gender assigned at birth, including trans-men and trans-women (whether or not they had undergone surgery or hormone therapy), people with intersex variations, genderqueer people and people with socio-cultural identities such as kinner, hijra, aravani and jogta;
  • gave a transgender person the right to be recognised as such and, in section 4(2), "a right to self-perceived gender identity";
  • provided for a certificate of identity as a transgender person, issued by the District Magistrate on application, and for a revised certificate (as male or female) if the person had surgery to change gender;
  • prohibited discrimination against transgender persons in education, employment, health care, access to public goods and facilities, movement, residence and property, and required the government to provide health facilities, including gender-affirming surgery and hormone therapy;
  • set up a National Council for Transgender Persons to advise the government (section 16); and
  • made offences against transgender persons, such as forced labour, denial of access to public places, forcing a person out of the household and physical, sexual, emotional or economic abuse, punishable with imprisonment of not less than six months and up to two years, with a fine (section 18).

The Act was welcomed as the first national law for transgender people, and criticised by many in the community and by legal scholars on two main grounds: that requiring a certificate from the District Magistrate, and surgery for a certificate as male or female, fell short of the self-identification that NALSA had recognised; and that the penalties were far lower than for comparable offences against other people.

The Amendment Act, 2026. The law changed again in 2026. The Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Amendment Bill, 2026 was introduced in the Lok Sabha on 13 March 2026, passed by both Houses, and received presidential assent on 30 March 2026 as the Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Amendment Act, 2026 (No. 3 of 2026); the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment brought it into force from 25 May 2026. According to its text in the Gazette of India, it:

  • replaced the definition of a transgender person with a list of categories: people with socio-cultural identities such as kinner, hijra, aravani and jogta, or eunuch; people with intersex variations or congenital variations of sex characteristics; and people who have been compelled, by force, allurement, inducement, deceit or undue influence, with or without consent, to assume or outwardly present a transgender identity by mutilation, emasculation, castration, amputation or surgical, chemical or hormonal procedures. Trans-men, trans-women and genderqueer people are no longer named, and a proviso states that the definition does not include "persons with different sexual orientations and self-perceived sexual identities";
  • omitted section 4(2), the right to a self-perceived gender identity;
  • required the District Magistrate to issue a certificate of identity only after examining the recommendation of an "authority", a medical board headed by a Chief Medical Officer or Deputy Chief Medical Officer, while adding a right to change one's first name in the birth certificate and other official documents;
  • made it compulsory for a person who has had surgery to change gender to apply for a revised certificate, and required the medical institution to report the surgery to the District Magistrate; and
  • added severe new offences, including kidnapping a person and causing grievous hurt (by castration, for example) to compel them to assume a transgender identity, punishable with ten years to life imprisonment for an adult victim and life imprisonment for a child.

The government presented the amendment as directing the Act's protection to those who face the most severe exclusion (it argued that the 2019 definition was too vague to identify them, and that the Act had never been meant to cover self-perceived identities) and as protecting people from being forced into a transgender identity. Critics, including transgender activists and Amnesty International, argued that it abandoned the self-identification recognised in NALSA and placed legal recognition in the hands of medical boards. Petitions challenging its constitutional validity were filed, and on 4 May 2026 the Supreme Court issued notice to the central and state governments while declining to stay the law (it had not yet been notified); the Court later took up the challenges for final hearing, and at the time of writing no judgment had been given. For a clinical psychologist the practical points are that the law in this area is changing quickly, that legal recognition and clinical diagnosis are separate questions, and that a client's legal situation is part of the stress the clinician must understand.

Welfare and care. Alongside the law, governments have built welfare measures. Tamil Nadu formed the Transgender (Aravanigal) Welfare Board on 15 April 2008. Kerala adopted a State Policy for Transgenders on 12 November 2015. At the centre, the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment launched the SMILE scheme (Support for Marginalised Individuals for Livelihood and Enterprise) on 12 February 2022, which includes support for the rehabilitation of transgender persons; the ministry's Garima Greh shelter homes for transgender persons had begun as pilot homes in 2020 and 2021. In mental health care, the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 guarantees access to services without discrimination on the basis of gender, sex or sexual orientation, and forbids the determination of mental illness on the basis of non-conformity with moral, social or cultural values (Section 9.1.4). Even so, the structural barriers to care that DSM-5-TR describes (institutional discomfort, inexperience and hostility) are found in India as elsewhere, which is one reason this chapter belongs in the training of every clinical psychologist.

Indian context

The legal timeline. 1871: the Criminal Tribes Act brings hijras under police surveillance. 2008: Tamil Nadu's Transgender (Aravanigal) Welfare Board. 15 April 2014: the NALSA judgment recognises a third gender and the right to a self-identified gender. 2017: the Mental Healthcare Act forbids discrimination on the basis of gender, sex or sexual orientation. 2018: Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India reads down Section 377 (Section 9.10.8). 5 December 2019: the Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Act (in force 10 January 2020; Rules 25 September 2020). 2022: the National Medical Commission declares conversion therapy professional misconduct; the SMILE scheme begins. 2026: the Amendment Act (assent 30 March, in force 25 May) removes the right to a self-perceived gender identity and introduces medical-board certification; its validity is under challenge in the Supreme Court.

Exam trap

NALSA v. Union of India was decided on 15 April 2014 (third gender; right to self-identified gender); it is not the 2018 Section 377 case (Navtej Singh Johar). The Transgender Persons Act is Act 40 of 2019 (assent 5 December 2019, in force 10 January 2020); its 2019 penalties were six months to two years with fine. The 2026 Amendment omitted section 4(2), the right to a self-perceived gender identity, and requires a medical board's recommendation before a certificate is issued. Tamil Nadu's welfare board (2008) came before NALSA.
