Answers: (1) Sry (SRY); testis, (2) Müllerian; Müllerian-inhibiting substance, (3) dihydrotestosterone (DHT); 5-alpha-reductase, (4) androgen insensitivity, (5) behaviour (play); identity, (6) 5-alpha-reductase, (7) Milton Diamond; Keith Sigmundson, (8) six; two, (9) 17; 2, (10) National Legal Services Authority (NALSA); self-perceived.

9.10 Homosexuality: Pathologised and DepathologisedLower yield · 1 Q

On 2 May 1972, at the 125th annual meeting of the American Psychiatric Association in Dallas, a panel met under the title "Psychiatry: Friend or Foe to Homosexuals? A Dialogue". Two gay activists, Barbara Gittings (1932 to 2007) and Frank Kameny (1925 to 2011), sat with two psychiatrists. The fifth speaker was introduced only as "Dr. H. Anonymous". He wore a rubber mask from a joke shop, a wig and a baggy tuxedo, and he spoke through a microphone that distorted his voice. His first words were "I am a homosexual. I am a psychiatrist." He was John E. Fryer (1937 to 2003), a young psychiatrist from Philadelphia, and he had good reason to hide: in 1972 a psychiatrist known to be gay could expect to lose his post, because his own profession's manual listed what he was as a mental disorder. He went on to describe what it was like to be a gay psychiatrist who had to hide from his own colleagues.

Nineteen months later the Association's board voted to take homosexuality out of its manual. The World Health Organization (WHO) followed with the tenth revision of its classification in 1990, and in 2018 the Supreme Court of India, drawing on the same science, ended the crime that had been written into Indian law in 1860. Nothing about gay people changed in those years. What changed was the evidence, the definition of a mental disorder and the willingness of the professions to listen. That story is the subject of this last section of the chapter. It is the clearest case in the history of psychiatry of a condition being made into a disorder and then unmade, and it teaches lessons about diagnosis that reach far beyond sexuality: about who decides what counts as illness, about samples that see only patients, and about the difference between the distress a person feels and the cause of that distress. Sections 9.8 and 9.9 told the parallel story for gender identity; here the subject is sexual orientation, the pattern of a person's sexual and romantic attraction, whose vocabulary Section 9.1.2 set out.

What the two words mean

Two technical words carry this whole section. Pathologisation is the classification of a behaviour, an identity or a way of life as a mental disorder (from the Greek pathos, suffering or disease): once it is pathologised, the thing becomes something that can be diagnosed, coded, researched as a sickness and treated. Depathologisation is the reverse: the removal of that classification, so that the behaviour or identity is no longer regarded as a disorder at all. Book 2, Section 2.10.3 introduced the pair in its history of the DSM; the definitions are worth stating again in your own words, because the papers test them directly. Notice what both words are about. They concern the diagnostic status of something, its place inside or outside the manuals. They say nothing about any change in the people concerned.

That is why two tempting meanings are wrong. Pathologisation and depathologisation do not mean changing a person's sexual orientation, from homosexual to heterosexual or the other way round. Attempts to do that are a separate matter, called conversion therapy (also reparative therapy, or sexual orientation change efforts), and Book 2, Section 4.4.2 told the history of its aversive forms and the reasons the professions now condemn it; Section 9.10.7 below brings the story up to date. Nor do the words mean coming out, the process by which a gay, lesbian or bisexual person comes to accept his or her orientation and to disclose it to others. Coming out happens in a life; pathologisation and depathologisation happen in a classification.

A related word is medicalisation, the process by which something once seen as a moral, legal or social matter comes to be seen as a medical one. Medicalisation can be humane (an alcoholic is treated rather than jailed) or harmful (an inconvenient group is declared sick). Homosexuality went through both, which is what makes its history so instructive. For most of recorded history in Europe, sex between men was a sin, condemned by religious authorities. From the sixteenth century it was also a crime: the English Buggery Act of 1533 made it a capital offence, and that law is the ancestor of India's Section 377 (Section 9.10.8). In the nineteenth century physicians claimed it as a sickness, which some of them believed was a kinder view than sin or crime. And in the second half of the twentieth century the evidence persuaded the professions that it was none of these, but a normal variation of human sexuality. Sin, crime, sickness, variation: the last of these changes took barely a century, and each frame decided whether a gay person met a priest, a policeman, a psychiatrist or simply a neighbour.

The same pair of words now describes other histories. Book 2, Section 1.6.2 told how, in 1851, the American physician Samuel Cartwright described "drapetomania", a supposed disease that made enslaved people run away, and how Soviet psychiatry diagnosed dissidents with "sluggish schizophrenia": in each case social disapproval was dressed up as a diagnosis. Section 9.9.1 showed that gender identity is going through its own depathologisation, from transsexualism and gender identity disorder to gender dysphoria in DSM-5 and to gender incongruence outside the mental disorders chapter in ICD-11. The modern safeguard is written into the definition of mental disorder itself. DSM-5-TR states that socially deviant behaviour (it gives political, religious and sexual deviance as examples) and conflicts that are mainly between the individual and society are not mental disorders unless they result from a dysfunction in the individual (Section 1.1.3 teaches the definition in full). India's Mental Healthcare Act 2017 says the same in law: section 3(3) forbids a diagnosis of mental illness on the ground of non-conformity with the moral, social, cultural or religious values of one's community (Section 9.1.4).

Exam trap

"Pathologisation and depathologisation of homosexuality" means the classification of homosexuality as a mental disorder and the removal of that classification (Entrance Mock 2). It is about diagnostic status. It is not "changing sexual orientation from homosexual to heterosexual" (that is conversion therapy, which is unethical and ineffective), and it is not "coming out" (disclosing one's orientation). "None of the above" is wrong because option (b) is exactly right. The same words apply to gender identity: DSM-5's move from gender identity disorder to gender dysphoria and ICD-11's move of gender incongruence to Chapter 17 are steps of depathologisation.

Sin, crime and sickness: the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries

Until the middle of the nineteenth century, European law and religion spoke about acts, not about kinds of people. "Sodomy" or "buggery" was something a person did, a sin or a crime that anyone might commit, and the punishment fell on the act. The idea that some people are, by nature, attracted to their own sex, and that this attraction marks them out as a type, is surprisingly recent. It arrived in the 1860s, and it arrived first from campaigners who wanted the laws changed, before the doctors took it over.

The first was a German lawyer, Karl Heinrich Ulrichs (1825 to 1895). Writing at first under a pseudonym, he published a series of pamphlets from the mid-1860s in which he called a man who desires men an Urning (and a man who desires women a Dioning). His explanation was summed up in a Latin phrase, anima muliebris virili corpore inclusa, a female soul enclosed in a male body. The theory sounds odd today, because it confuses sexual orientation with gender identity (Section 9.1.2 keeps them apart), but its purpose was clear: if same-sex love is inborn and natural, it cannot be a crime. On 29 August 1867 Ulrichs stood up at the Congress of German Jurists in Munich to argue exactly that, and was shouted down. He is often described as the first person to defend homosexuality in public.

The word we use was coined by a friend of the cause, the Vienna-born Hungarian writer and translator Karl Maria Kertbeny (1824 to 1882), who was not a physician. He used "homosexual" (and "heterosexual") in a private letter of 6 May 1868, and the word first appeared in print in 1869 in anonymous pamphlets he wrote against the Prussian law that punished sex between men, Paragraph 143. Their campaign failed: when the German Empire adopted its criminal code in 1871, the Prussian rule became the notorious Paragraph 175, which stayed in German law in one form or another for more than a century. Britain, meanwhile, showed what the crime frame meant in practice. In 1895 the writer Oscar Wilde was convicted of "gross indecency" with men and sentenced to two years' hard labour.

Medicine entered the story in 1870, when the Berlin psychiatrist Carl Westphal (1833 to 1890) published a case report on what he called conträre Sexualempfindung, "contrary sexual feeling", and described it as an inborn condition that belonged to psychiatry rather than to the courts. A century later the French philosopher Michel Foucault (1926 to 1984), in the first volume of The History of Sexuality (1976), took Westphal's article as the birth certificate of a new idea: "The sodomite had been a temporary aberration; the homosexual was now a species." His point was that the medical frame did not simply describe a kind of person; it helped to create one, a person defined by a diagnosis.

The most influential medical voice was Richard von Krafft-Ebing (1840 to 1902), whose life and catalogue of the sexual "perversions" are told in Section 9.1.3 and Section 9.5.3. In Psychopathia Sexualis (1886) he treated homosexuality as an inborn disorder of the nervous system, a sign of hereditary degeneration. Degeneration theory, which came from French psychiatry, held that nervous and moral weaknesses accumulate down the generations of a family, so that a "tainted" family produces ever more defective offspring. Notice the double edge. Calling homosexuality inborn meant that the person was not to blame, and Krafft-Ebing, who corresponded with Ulrichs, came to support the repeal of the criminal laws; late in his life he softened his view further and stopped describing it as a degenerative illness. But calling it a hereditary disease made it something to be diagnosed, feared and, if possible, prevented.

Two other sexologists of the period pushed in the opposite direction. The English physician Havelock Ellis (1859 to 1939; Section 9.1.3) described sexual inversion, his term for same-sex attraction, in Sexual Inversion (1897), written with the critic John Addington Symonds. Ellis collected case histories of men and women, many of whom had never been patients, found most of them as healthy and productive as anyone else, and concluded that inversion was an inborn variation, an anomaly rather than a disease, which the law had no business punishing. In Berlin, the physician Magnus Hirschfeld (1868 to 1935) went further still. He saw human beings as arranged along a continuum of "sexual intermediaries" between the fully male and the fully female, and homosexual people as a natural "third sex". In 1897 he co-founded the Scientific-Humanitarian Committee, the first organisation anywhere to campaign for the rights of homosexual people, under the motto "Justice through science", and it petitioned the German parliament for the repeal of Paragraph 175. In 1919 he opened his Institute for Sexual Science in Berlin. In May 1933, months after the Nazis came to power, students ransacked the institute and its library was burned. Hirschfeld, abroad at the time, never returned to Germany, and he died in exile in France in 1935 (his work on gender identity is told in Section 9.9.1).

Then came Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), whose view fitted neither camp. In Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905) he argued that every human being starts life with a bisexual disposition and that adult heterosexuality is an achievement of development, not a given. Homosexuality, which he too called "inversion", arose when that development came to a halt short of its usual end point. It was therefore, in Freud's eyes, a form of immaturity: not a vice, not a crime and not exactly an illness. His most famous statement on the subject is a letter written in English on 9 April 1935 to an American woman who had written to him about her son without ever using the word. Freud replied:

"Homosexuality is assuredly no advantage, but it is nothing to be ashamed of, no vice, no degradation, it cannot be classified as an illness." He went on to call it "a variation of the sexual function", which he attributed to an arrest of sexual development; he pointed out that many highly respected people in history had been homosexual; and he wrote that "It is a great injustice to persecute homosexuality as a crime, and cruelty too." Could analysis change her son? "In a general way, we cannot promise to achieve it." What analysis could offer was help with unhappiness, conflict and inhibition, whether or not he remained homosexual. A copy of the letter was sent anonymously to the sexologist Alfred Kinsey in 1949, and it was published in the American Journal of Psychiatry in 1951.

The irony is that Freud's American followers took the opposite line. After the Second World War, psychoanalysis dominated American psychiatry, and many analysts came to treat homosexuality not as a variation but as a serious, curable illness caused by disturbed family relationships. The best-known study was published in 1962 by the psychiatrist Irving Bieber and his colleagues in the Society of Medical Psychoanalysts, Homosexuality: A Psychoanalytic Study of Male Homosexuals. It compared 106 homosexual men with 100 heterosexual men, and its data came not from the men themselves but from questionnaires completed by their psychoanalysts. Bieber's group concluded that homosexuality was a fearful inhibition of normal heterosexual development, produced by a "close-binding, intimate" mother and a detached or hostile father, and they claimed that a proportion of the men who stayed in analysis became heterosexual. The New York psychoanalyst Charles Socarides argued along similar lines that homosexuality was a grave disorder rooted in early childhood, and he became the leading opponent of the change of 1973; in 1992 he helped to found the National Association for Research and Therapy of Homosexuality, an organisation that promoted change therapies.

Look closely at Bieber's sample and you will see the flaw that undid the whole sickness model. Every one of the 206 men was a patient in psychoanalytic treatment. People in treatment are, almost by definition, people who are troubled, and their analysts, who were the source of the data, already believed the theory being tested. A study of heterosexual men drawn only from psychiatric clinics would make heterosexuality look like a disorder too. The obvious question, then, was what homosexual men who had never sought treatment were like. It took a psychologist working outside the clinic to ask it (Section 9.10.3).

The American psychiatrist and historian Jack Drescher (2015) has suggested a simple way to hold the whole of this history in mind. Every theory of homosexuality, he argues, belongs to one of three families, and each family implies its own answer to the question "What should be done?" Table 9.23 sets them out.

Table 9.23 Three ways of explaining homosexuality, after Drescher (2015), with what each implies.
ViewClaimMain proponentsWhat follows
Normal variationAn inborn or natural variant of human sexualityUlrichs, Hirschfeld, Ellis; later Kinsey and HookerNothing to treat; repeal the laws
ImmaturityA halt on the way to adult heterosexualityFreud (1905; letter of 1935)Not a vice or an illness; change rarely possible; help with unhappiness
PathologyA disease, from degeneration or from disturbed family relationsKrafft-Ebing (1886); Bieber (1962); SocaridesDiagnose and cure: psychoanalysis, later aversion therapy

The pathology view is the one that entered the manuals, and it dominated psychiatry in the middle of the twentieth century. The normal-variation view is the one the evidence eventually supported. The sections that follow show how the evidence was gathered and how the manuals changed.

Exam trap

Match each term to its author. "Homosexual" (and "heterosexual") was coined by Kertbeny (letter of 1868, in print 1869), not by Krafft-Ebing, Freud or Ellis. "Urning" is Ulrichs's word; "contrary sexual feeling" is Westphal's (1870); "sexual inversion" is Havelock Ellis's book (1897), and Freud also used "inversion"; the "third sex" and the Scientific-Humanitarian Committee (1897) are Hirschfeld's. Krafft-Ebing's Psychopathia Sexualis (1886) called homosexuality hereditary degeneration. Freud's 1935 letter says homosexuality "cannot be classified as an illness"; it was the later American analysts (Bieber 1962, Socarides) who called it a curable disease, using samples made up entirely of patients.

Counting and testing: Kinsey and Hooker

Two very different kinds of evidence broke the sickness model in the United States. The first was counting: how many ordinary people, outside any clinic, actually had same-sex experiences? The second was testing: were homosexual people who had never sought treatment any more disturbed than anyone else? Alfred Kinsey answered the first question and Evelyn Hooker the second.

Book 1, Section 10.9.6 introduced Alfred Charles Kinsey (1894 to 1956), the Indiana University zoologist who had spent years classifying gall wasps before he turned the same methods on human beings, and his two great surveys, Sexual Behavior in the Human Male (1948) and Sexual Behavior in the Human Female (1953), based on long face-to-face interviews with thousands of Americans. Here we need two things from Kinsey that Book 1 only mentioned: his scale and his numbers.

Kinsey distrusted categories. "The world is not to be divided into sheep and goats," he wrote in 1948; nature deals in continua, and only human beings insist on sorting it into boxes. To describe a person's sexual life without forcing it into "heterosexual" or "homosexual", Kinsey and his colleagues Wardell Pomeroy and Clyde Martin devised the heterosexual-homosexual rating scale, now called the Kinsey scale. It has seven points. A rating of 0 means exclusively heterosexual and 6 means exclusively homosexual; 3 means equally heterosexual and homosexual; and the points in between describe people who are predominantly one way but "only incidentally" or "more than incidentally" the other (Figure 9.13). A separate category, X, was added for people with no socio-sexual contacts or reactions at all. Two features of the scale matter more than its numbers. First, a rating was based on both overt experience and psychological reactions, that is, on what a person did and on whom he or she was attracted to or fantasised about. Second, a rating described a period of life, not a fixed identity: the same man might be rated 2 in his twenties and 0 in his forties.

Then came the numbers, and they caused a sensation. Of the American men Kinsey's team interviewed, 37 percent reported at least one homosexual experience to the point of orgasm between adolescence and old age; about 10 percent had been more or less exclusively homosexual (rated 5 or 6) for at least three years between the ages of 16 and 55; and 4 percent had been exclusively homosexual throughout their lives after the onset of adolescence. Among white men aged 20 to 35, 11.6 percent were rated 3. The figures for women in the 1953 volume were lower: between 2 and 6 percent of women aged 20 to 35 were more or less exclusively homosexual, and between 1 and 3 percent of unmarried women of that age were exclusively so.

Why did these figures matter so much? Because a disorder, in the medical imagination of the 1940s, was something rare and abnormal that set its victims apart. If more than a third of the ordinary men next door had had a homosexual experience, and if people spread out along a continuum instead of falling into two boxes, it was very hard to go on calling same-sex desire the rare sickness of a degenerate minority. Kinsey himself pointed out that the sex laws of the time, if they had been enforced, would have sent most American men to prison.

Kinsey's numbers have not survived unchanged. His samples were volunteers rather than random samples of the population, and they over-represented some groups, including men in prisons. The psychologist Abraham Maslow and his colleague James Sakoda (1952) found that the students who volunteered for Kinsey's interviews scored higher on Maslow's measure of self-esteem, a trait he had linked with less conventional sexual behaviour, so a volunteer sample may exaggerate unusual experiences. Later probability surveys, which sample people at random, give much smaller figures. A large American national survey reported by Mosher, Chandra and Jones (2005) found that 6.5 percent of adult men had ever had sex with a man and that 2.2 percent were attracted only to men; surveys of more than 20,000 people each in Britain and France in 1992 found that only 3.6 percent of British and 4.1 percent of French men had ever had a male partner (figures from Barlow and Durand). The popular "one person in ten is gay" comes from a misreading of Kinsey's 10 percent, which referred to three years of a man's life, not to a lifelong identity. But the central lesson held: same-sex attraction and behaviour are common, graded and found among people who have no other sign of disturbance. A second line of evidence pointed the same way. In Patterns of Sexual Behavior (1951), the anthropologist Clellan Ford and the psychologist Frank Beach surveyed reports from many societies and many animal species, and found same-sex behaviour widespread in both, and accepted in many of the societies.

Counting how many people had same-sex experiences could not, by itself, settle whether they were healthy. That needed a different study, and it was done by a psychologist who was not looking for a cause. Evelyn Hooker (1907 to 1996) taught psychology at the University of California, Los Angeles. In 1944 one of her students, a gay man named Sam From, befriended her and introduced her to his circle of friends: intelligent, productive, apparently contented men who bore no resemblance to the patients described in the psychiatric literature. He told her it was her "scientific duty to study people like us". It took her years to agree. In 1953 she applied to the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH) for a grant to study such men, and she was funded.

Key study: Hooker's blind judges (1956 to 1957)

Question. Are homosexual men who are not in treatment more maladjusted than comparable heterosexual men? Method. Hooker recruited 30 homosexual men, most of them with the help of the Mattachine Society, an early American homophile organisation, and 30 heterosexual men, and matched the two groups for age, IQ and education. No man was in therapy, and men who showed considerable disturbance were excluded from both groups. Each man took three projective tests (the tests themselves belong to the Assessment book): the Rorschach inkblots, then believed to be the best instrument for detecting homosexuality, the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) and the Make-a-Picture-Story test (MAPS). Three expert judges, among them the Rorschach authority Bruno Klopfer and the creator of the MAPS, Edwin Shneidman, received the 60 records without names or labels. They rated each man's overall adjustment on a five-point scale and tried to identify which men were homosexual.

Results. The judges could not tell the two groups apart at better than chance, and they rated the adjustment of the two groups alike, placing about two-thirds of the men in each group in the average-or-better range. Conclusion. Presented at the American Psychological Association's convention in Chicago in 1956 and published as "The adjustment of the male overt homosexual" in the Journal of Projective Techniques in 1957, the paper offered, "very tentatively", the conclusions that "homosexuality as a clinical entity does not exist" and that it may be a variation in sexual pattern within the normal psychological range. Criticism. The sample was small, all male and not random, and men recruited through a homophile organisation may have been better adjusted than average; projective tests have doubtful validity. But that last point cut both ways, because the claims of pathology had rested on the same tests, applied to patients.

The importance of Hooker's study lay less in its size than in its design. For the first time, the comparison was between homosexual and heterosexual men drawn from the same non-clinical world, judged by experts who did not know which was which. Under those conditions the "pathology" disappeared, which strongly suggested that it had come from the samples and from the expectations of the clinicians, not from homosexuality. In 1967 Hooker was asked to chair an NIMH task force on homosexuality, whose report recommended that private sexual acts between consenting adults be decriminalised. Later studies with larger samples and objective questionnaires reached the same conclusion, summarised in a review by the psychologist John Gonsiorek in 1991: when non-clinical samples are used, homosexual and heterosexual people do not differ in psychological adjustment. By the early 1970s, Kinsey's counting and Hooker's testing were the two pillars on which the case for removing homosexuality from the DSM was built.

Exam trap

Kinsey (1948) gave the 0 to 6 continuum (0 exclusively heterosexual, 6 exclusively homosexual, X for no socio-sexual contacts or reactions), rating behaviour and psychological reactions for a period of life; his 10 percent figure means 5 or 6 for at least three years, not a lifelong one in ten. Hooker (1957) compared non-patient gay and heterosexual men, matched for age, IQ and education, using the Rorschach, TAT and MAPS, and found that blind expert judges could not tell them apart. Neither study treated, cured or changed anyone, and Hooker was a psychologist, not a psychiatrist. Some books date her study to 1956, the year she presented it; it was published in 1957.

The 1973 decision and its aftermath in the DSM

To understand what was removed in 1973, start with what was there. The first edition of the American Psychiatric Association's manual, DSM-I (1952), placed homosexuality under sexual deviation, one of the subtypes of sociopathic personality disturbance, a personality disorder whose other subtypes included the antisocial and dyssocial reactions and addiction. The manual's list of examples put homosexuality alongside transvestism, pedophilia, fetishism and sexual sadism. The label "sociopathic" matters: it grouped gay people, in the official language of psychiatry, with people who exploit and harm others. DSM-II (1968) moved the sexual deviations into its large section on "personality disorders and certain other non-psychotic mental disorders" and split them into ten codes, of which the first, 302.0, was homosexuality, followed by fetishism, pedophilia, transvestitism, exhibitionism, voyeurism, sadism, masochism and two residual codes.

The diagnosis was not just a word in a book. In the 1950s the United States government barred "sexual perversion" from federal employment, and the psychiatric label lent that policy, under which thousands of people lost their jobs, the authority of medicine. Frank Kameny, the activist on the Dallas panel, had been an astronomer working for the Army Map Service until he was dismissed in 1957 for being gay. A diagnosis could also be used to deny a licence, to commit a person to a hospital or, as Book 2, Section 4.4.2 showed, to justify years of aversion therapy.

The change came from two directions at once: from the evidence of Section 9.10.3, and from a new political movement. In the early hours of 28 June 1969, police raided the Stonewall Inn, a gay bar in Greenwich Village, New York, and the customers, instead of dispersing, fought back. The nights of rioting that followed are usually taken as the start of the gay liberation movement; the annual pride marches held around the world commemorate them. Activists soon turned to psychiatry, which they saw as the source of the "sick" label. They disrupted the American Psychiatric Association's annual meeting in San Francisco in 1970 and its meeting in Washington in 1971, when Kameny took the microphone to call psychiatry "the enemy incarnate". In 1972 came the Dallas panel and Fryer's speech in disguise. In 1973 the Association's annual meeting held a symposium with the plain title "Should homosexuality be in the APA nomenclature?"

The psychiatrist who turned protest into a diagnostic argument was Robert Spitzer (1932 to 2015) of Columbia University, then a member of the Association's committee on nomenclature, who later led the writing of DSM-III (Book 2, Section 2.10.3). Spitzer met the activists, listened to their case and then did something characteristic of him: he looked at the other conditions in the manual to see what they had in common. He concluded that, to count as a psychiatric disorder, a condition must either regularly cause subjective distress or regularly be associated with some generalised impairment in social effectiveness or functioning. Homosexuality failed that test. Many homosexual people, like Hooker's 30 men, were content with their orientation and functioned well at work and in their relationships. A condition that left many of the people who had it neither distressed nor impaired could not be a disorder in itself.

On 15 December 1973 the Association's Board of Trustees voted 13 to 0, with two abstentions, to remove homosexuality from DSM-II. At the same meeting the board adopted statements declaring that homosexuality in itself implies no impairment in judgement, stability, reliability or general social or vocational capabilities, deplored discrimination against homosexual people in employment, housing and licensing, and urged the repeal of laws against private sexual acts between consenting adults. The opponents, led by Bieber and Socarides, collected enough signatures to force a referendum of the whole membership. Its result, announced in April 1974, upheld the board: 5,854 members (58 percent) voted for the decision and 3,810 (38 percent) against, with 367 abstaining, out of roughly 10,000 who voted. In 1975 the American Psychological Association adopted a resolution supporting the change and urging mental health professionals to take the lead in removing the stigma of mental illness that had long been associated with homosexuality.

Critics, then and since, have mocked the idea that a scientific question could be settled by a vote, and the historian Ronald Bayer titled his account of these years Homosexuality and American Psychiatry: The Politics of Diagnosis (1981). The point deserves a careful answer. Every diagnostic manual is a set of decisions by committees about where to draw lines, and every edition of the DSM has been approved by votes of the Association's governing bodies. What made 1973 different was not that a vote was taken but that a long-standing category was removed in the open, under public pressure. The vote did not create the evidence: Kinsey's surveys, Ford and Beach's cross-cultural data and Hooker's study had been published years earlier. What the vote did was to apply a clearer definition of disorder to that evidence.

The removal was a compromise, and the compromise left something behind. In the seventh printing of DSM-II (1974) the code 302.0 was renamed sexual orientation disturbance, a category for people whose sexual interests are directed primarily towards people of the same sex "who are either disturbed by, in conflict with, or wish to change their sexual orientation". The accompanying note stated that homosexuality "by itself does not constitute a psychiatric disorder". In other words, the orientation was no longer a disorder, but unhappiness about it could be.

When Spitzer's task force wrote DSM-III (1980), it kept the compromise under a new name, ego-dystonic homosexuality (code 302.00), listed among the psychosexual disorders. Spitzer said he wanted to take "a middle position" on the pathological status of homosexuality. Section 2.1.9 of this book explained the terms: an experience is ego-syntonic when it feels consistent with the person's sense of self and ego-dystonic when it feels alien, unwanted and distressing. The diagnosis required two things. First, the person complained that heterosexual arousal was persistently absent or weak and that this significantly interfered with starting or keeping heterosexual relationships that he or she wanted. Second, there was a sustained pattern of homosexual arousal that the person explicitly said was unwanted and a persistent source of distress.

Push a case through the editions to see what the category did. Imagine that Mridula, a 26-year-old woman, had seen a psychiatrist in 1981. She has never felt attracted to men; for years she has been attracted to women, and she has fallen in love with a friend. Her family is arranging her marriage. She cannot sleep, she cries most days, and she says, "I want to be like everyone else. Make these feelings go away." Under DSM-III she meets both criteria for ego-dystonic homosexuality, and the diagnosis frames her attraction to women as the problem to be treated, which in 1981 might well have meant attempts to change it. Under DSM-III-R (1987), DSM-IV (1994) and DSM-IV-TR (2000) the named category is gone, but a clinician could still record "sexual disorder not otherwise specified", whose list of examples included persistent and marked distress about one's sexual orientation. Under DSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR (2022) there is no diagnosis for her orientation or for her unhappiness about it. If she has a depressive episode, the depression is diagnosed (Chapter 5) and treated; the family pressure and the fear of rejection are recorded as the stresses behind it; and her attraction to women is not a target of treatment at all.

Why did ego-dystonic homosexuality not last? The arguments against it were strong. First, few clinicians used it, and there was almost no research on it. Second, it was lopsided: no one proposed a diagnosis of "ego-dystonic heterosexuality" for a heterosexual person distressed by his orientation, which showed that the category still treated homosexuality as the abnormal option. Third, and most important, the distress it described was usually a reaction to a hostile society. In a world that punishes, mocks and rejects gay people, almost every gay person passes through a period of unease about being gay. To call that reaction a disorder was to locate inside the individual what was really a conflict between the individual and his or her society, the very thing the DSM's definition of mental disorder now excludes, and it gave a respectable label to the conversion treatments. DSM-III-R dropped the category in 1987, and DSM-5 deleted the last trace, the example under sexual disorder not otherwise specified. Table 9.24 follows homosexuality through every edition of both manuals, including the ICD editions described in Section 9.10.5. Today the minority stress model explains the extra burden of depression, anxiety and suicidal behaviour among sexual minorities as the product of stigma, not of orientation (Book 2, Section 10.6.9; Section 9.10.6).

Key study: Spitzer's change study (2003) and his apology (2012)

Three decades after 1973, Spitzer himself asked whether some gay men and lesbians can change their orientation. He presented the study at the American Psychiatric Association's meeting in 2001 and published it in the Archives of Sexual Behavior in 2003. The participants were 200 self-selected people (143 men and 57 women) who reported at least some change from homosexual to heterosexual orientation lasting at least five years after some form of "reparative" therapy; many were referred by religious "ex-gay" groups and by therapists who offered such treatments. Each was interviewed once, by telephone, and asked to rate attraction, fantasy and behaviour in the year before therapy and in the year before the interview. Most described a shift from a predominantly homosexual to a predominantly heterosexual orientation, complete change was rare, and women reported more change than men. Spitzer judged the reports credible.

The critics' answer was simple: there was no way to know. The sample was recruited from people with every reason to report success, there was no comparison group, and a single retrospective telephone interview cannot distinguish change in orientation from change in behaviour, self-description or hope. In 2012 Spitzer published a letter in the same journal accepting that the major critiques were "largely correct", above all that there was no way to judge the credibility of the participants' reports, and wrote: "I believe I owe the gay community an apology for my study making unproven claims of the efficacy of reparative therapy." The episode is a lesson in research design: self-selected testimony, with no control group and no independent measure, cannot show that a treatment works.

Table 9.24 Homosexuality in each edition of the DSM and the ICD.
ManualYearHow homosexuality was classified
ICD-6 and ICD-71948; 1955Under sexual deviation (320.6), presumed to reflect a personality disorder

ICD-81965 (in force 1968)Homosexuality (302.0), under sexual deviation
DSM-II1968Homosexuality (302.0), first of the ten sexual deviations
DSM-II, seventh printing1974Replaced by sexual orientation disturbance: only people disturbed by, in conflict with or wishing to change their orientation

DSM-III1980Ego-dystonic homosexuality (302.00), a psychosexual disorder
DSM-III-R1987Category deleted; distress about orientation survives only as an example of sexual disorder not otherwise specified

Exam trap

Learn the DSM sequence as a chain. DSM-I (1952): a sexual deviation within sociopathic personality disturbance. DSM-II (1968): homosexuality, 302.0, a sexual deviation. 15 December 1973: the APA Board of Trustees votes to remove it (the membership referendum of 1974 upheld this by 58 percent). DSM-II seventh printing (1974): sexual orientation disturbance. DSM-III (1980): ego-dystonic homosexuality. DSM-III-R (1987): ego-dystonic homosexuality dropped. DSM-5 (2013) removed the last example. So "homosexuality was removed from the DSM in 1973" and "the last homosexuality category left the DSM in 1987" are both true; "DSM-III removed homosexuality" is false, because DSM-III added ego-dystonic homosexuality. The vote was taken by the American Psychiatric Association (which publishes the DSM), not the American Psychological Association.

The WHO and the ICD

The International Classification of Diseases told the same story more slowly, and with a twist at the end. Section 1.9 of this book traced the ICD's revisions; here we follow only homosexuality through them. ICD-6, published by the WHO in 1948, was the first edition to include a full classification of mental disorders, and it classified homosexuality under sexual deviation (code 320.6), on the assumption that it reflected an underlying disorder of personality. ICD-7 (1955) kept it there. ICD-8, agreed in 1965 and in force from 1968, gave it a code of its own, 302.0 homosexuality, within the group of sexual deviations, and ICD-9, agreed in 1975, kept 302.0. The ICD-9 entry carries a revealing instruction: homosexuality was to be coded there "whether or not it is considered as a mental disorder". Two years after the American vote, the world classification was no longer sure that what it was coding was an illness, but it went on coding it.

The decisive change came with ICD-10. On 17 May 1990 the Forty-third World Health Assembly adopted the tenth revision (resolution WHA43.24); it formally came into effect on 1 January 1993, and member states began to use it from 1994. Homosexuality was no longer listed. The date is still marked every year: 17 May is now the International Day Against Homophobia, Biphobia and Transphobia, chosen to commemorate the Assembly's decision.

Like DSM-III, however, ICD-10 left something behind. Its mental disorders chapter kept a block, F66, called "psychological and behavioural disorders associated with sexual development and orientation", headed by a sentence that sums up the compromise: "Sexual orientation by itself is not to be regarded as a disorder." The block had three main categories.

  • Sexual maturation disorder (F66.0): a person is uncertain about his or her gender identity or sexual orientation, and the uncertainty causes anxiety or depression. ICD-10 says this happens most often in adolescents who are not sure whether they are homosexual, heterosexual or bisexual, and in people who, after a period of apparently stable orientation, often within a long relationship, find that their orientation is changing.
  • Egodystonic sexual orientation (F66.1): the gender identity or sexual preference is not in doubt, but the person wishes it were different because of associated psychological and behavioural disorders, and may seek treatment to change it.
  • Sexual relationship disorder (F66.2): the gender identity or sexual preference is responsible for difficulties in forming or maintaining a relationship with a sexual partner.

Two residual codes (F66.8, other psychosexual development disorders, and F66.9, unspecified) completed the block, and a fifth character could record the orientation concerned: .x0 heterosexual, .x1 homosexual, .x2 bisexual and .x8 other, including prepubertal. So a man distressed about being gay and wishing to change would be coded F66.11, egodystonic sexual orientation, homosexual. Notice one improvement over DSM-III: the ICD-10 categories were symmetrical, so in principle a heterosexual person distressed by his or her orientation could receive the same diagnosis. Notice, too, the persistent difficulty. The categories were defined by distress or difficulty that arose because of orientation, and in a hostile world such distress usually comes from the hostility.

Consider how these categories would apply today. Sushant, 17, a Class 12 student in Lucknow, is brought by his elder sister to a clinical psychologist because he has stopped sleeping and his marks have fallen before the board examinations. In private he says that for two years he has wondered whether he is attracted to boys rather than girls, that he cannot tell anyone, and that the not-knowing frightens him more than either answer would. An Indian clinician using ICD-10 in 2015 could have recorded F66.0, sexual maturation disorder, which places the problem in Sushant's developing sexuality. A clinician using ICD-11 has no such code. If Sushant's anxiety meets the criteria for an anxiety disorder or an adjustment disorder, that is what is diagnosed; if it does not, no diagnosis is needed at all. Either way the work is the same: a safe space to explore his feelings at his own pace, accurate information that uncertainty about orientation is common in adolescence and not an illness, help with sleep and examination stress, and attention to his safety at home and at school (Book 2, Section 10.6.9 describes affirmative work with adolescents).

That is exactly the reasoning by which ICD-11 removed the block. A WHO working group on sexual disorders and sexual health, whose recommendations were published by Susan Cochran, Jack Drescher and their colleagues in the Bulletin of the World Health Organization in 2014, reviewed the evidence since 1990 and recommended deleting all of F66. It found three problems. There had been almost no research or clinical interest in the categories since ICD-10 appeared. The categories had no clinical use that other diagnoses did not already serve, because a person's anxiety or depression can be diagnosed and treated as anxiety or depression whatever its source. And the categories could do harm: they could delay accurate diagnosis and treatment of the real problem, and they could lend medical respectability to attempts to change orientation. ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in May 2019 and in force from 1 January 2022, accordingly has no category based on sexual orientation at all. Egodystonic sexual orientation, sexual maturation disorder and sexual relationship disorder are gone. (At the same time ICD-11 moved gender incongruence and the sexual dysfunctions out of the mental disorders chapter into Chapter 17, as Sections 9.1.6 and 9.9.1 explain; nothing about sexual orientation went to Chapter 17, because there was nothing left to move.)

Exam trap

The order of events: the APA voted in 1973 (DSM-II, seventh printing, 1974); ego-dystonic homosexuality lasted until DSM-III-R in 1987; the WHO acted with ICD-10, adopted by the World Health Assembly on 17 May 1990 (now the International Day Against Homophobia, Biphobia and Transphobia); and ICD-11 (adopted 2019, in force 2022) deleted F66, including egodystonic sexual orientation (F66.1). So ICD-10 did not "list homosexuality as a disorder", and it is wrong to say that ICD-10 removed every orientation-related category: F66 survived until ICD-11. Gender incongruence went to Chapter 17 in ICD-11; sexual orientation went nowhere, because it was no longer classified.

What science says about sexual orientation

Once homosexuality is no longer a disorder, the old question "What causes homosexuality?" changes shape. It becomes "What shapes a person's sexual orientation, whatever it is?", a question about heterosexual people as much as gay, lesbian and bisexual ones. Researchers usually separate three things that Section 9.1.2 distinguished: attraction (whom a person desires), behaviour (with whom a person has sex) and identity (the label a person adopts). They often line up, but not always: in the American survey of Section 9.10.3, men who had ever had a same-sex experience outnumbered men attracted only to men by about three to one. Most of the research below concerns attraction, and most of it, for historical reasons, concerns gay men. The Physiological book teaches the brain and hormones in detail; here we need only what the evidence says.

Genes. Sexual orientation runs in families, and the first question is whether that is because of shared genes or shared homes. Twin studies separate the two (Book 1, Section 9.7.1 explains the logic, using intelligence). J. Michael Bailey and Richard Pillard (1991) recruited gay men who had a twin brother and found that 52 percent of the identical (monozygotic) twin brothers were also gay, compared with 22 percent of the fraternal (dizygotic) twin brothers. In a matching study of women (Bailey and colleagues, 1993), 48 percent of the identical twin sisters of lesbian women were lesbian, against 16 percent of the fraternal twin sisters. Identical twins share all their genes and fraternal twins about half, so the higher concordance of identical twins points to a genetic influence. Two cautions follow at once. These twins were recruited through advertisements, and twins who are both gay may be more willing to take part, which would inflate the figures; a population-based study of adult twins drawn from the Swedish Twin Registry (Långström and colleagues, 2010), which avoided that bias, estimated that genes accounted for about 34 to 39 percent of the variation in same-sex behaviour in men and 18 to 19 percent in women, with most of the rest coming from environmental influences unique to each twin (in women, a further 16 to 17 percent came from the environment the twins shared). And in Bailey and Pillard's own data about half of the identical twins did not share their brother's orientation, although they had identical genes and grew up in the same house. Genes matter, but they are far from the whole story.

The search for specific genes followed a familiar path. In 1993 Dean Hamer and his colleagues reported in Science that gay brothers shared genetic markers in a region at the tip of the X chromosome, Xq28, more often than chance would predict, and newspapers announced the discovery of a "gay gene". Later studies by Bailey's group and by Rice and colleagues (both 1999) could not replicate the finding. The largest study so far, a genome-wide association study by Andrea Ganna and colleagues (Science, 2019), examined the DNA of 477,522 people from the UK Biobank and the company 23andMe. It found five locations in the genome significantly associated with ever having had a same-sex partner. Together, all the genetic variants tested accounted for between 8 and 25 percent of the variation in same-sex behaviour, they overlapped only partly between men and women, and they could not meaningfully predict any individual's sexuality. The authors also found no evidence for a single genetic continuum running from opposite-sex to same-sex behaviour. The modern picture, then, is the one Barlow and Durand describe for most complex behaviour: many genes, each of very small effect, interacting with the environment. There is no "gay gene", just as there is no "heterosexual gene".

Hormones. Many people assume that gay men have less testosterone or lesbian women more. They do not. Pinel and Barnes point out that adult gay and heterosexual men do not differ in their circulating sex hormones, that castration (orchidectomy) reduces the sexual behaviour of both but does not redirect it, and that replacement injections simply reactivate whatever preference existed before. If hormones matter, they matter before birth. In many laboratory species (rats, hamsters, ferrets, pigs, zebra finches and dogs among them) castrating males around the time of birth increases their adult preference for male partners, and exposing females to testosterone at that time increases their preference for females. Human evidence is weaker and controversial. Anke Ehrhardt and her colleagues (1985) interviewed women whose mothers had taken the synthetic oestrogen diethylstilbestrol during pregnancy and found that they reported more attraction to women than matched controls, but all except one of the 30 women were primarily heterosexual, and the finding has not been replicated. Section 9.8 explains the organising effects of prenatal hormones on the body and brain.

Older brothers. The most reliable environmental finding comes from a surprising source: birth order. Ray Blanchard and Anthony Bogaert (1996) compared 302 gay men with 302 heterosexual men matched for year of birth and found that gay men had more older brothers, but not more older sisters or younger siblings of either sex. Each additional older brother increased the odds of being gay by about 33 percent. This fraternal birth order effect has been replicated many times. In blended families it depends on the number of boys previously born to the same mother, not on the number of older boys a man is brought up with, which points to something that happens in the womb rather than in the nursery. The leading explanation is the maternal immune hypothesis: a mother's immune system may respond to a protein made only by male foetuses, and the response may grow stronger with each son she carries, altering the development of the brain structures that underlie attraction in her later sons. In 2018 Bogaert and his colleagues found that mothers of gay sons, especially those with older brothers, had higher levels of antibodies against one such protein, NLGN4Y, a Y-linked protein important in the development of the male brain. Keep the size of the effect in proportion: since most men are heterosexual, even a third more of a small probability remains a small probability, and most men with several older brothers are heterosexual.

Key study: LeVay's hypothalamus (1991)

The neuroscientist Simon LeVay (born 1943), working at the Salk Institute in California, measured four small cell groups in the anterior hypothalamus, the interstitial nuclei of the anterior hypothalamus (INAH) 1 to 4, in brains obtained after death from 41 people: 19 homosexual men (one of them bisexual), 16 men presumed to be heterosexual and 6 women. Earlier work had found INAH-3 larger in men than in women. LeVay found no group differences in INAH-1, 2 or 4, but INAH-3 was more than twice as large in the heterosexual men as in the homosexual men, whose nuclei were similar in size to the women's. Published in Science in August 1991, the paper concluded that sexual orientation "has a biological substrate".

The criticisms are as important as the finding. All 19 gay men had died of complications of AIDS, which might itself affect the brain. The heterosexuality of the comparison men was presumed, not known. The sample was small and the groups overlapped, so the size of a nucleus could not tell you an individual's orientation. A correlation in adult brains cannot show which came first, since experience can alter brain structure. Later attempts, notably by William Byne and colleagues in 2001, have not consistently replicated the difference. And Pinel and Barnes warn against the popular reading that gay men's brains lie halfway between "female-like" and "male-like" brains: that is a prime example of the men-are-men-and-women-are-women assumption, the "mamawawa" of Section 9.8, which treats maleness and femaleness as two opposite boxes.

When attraction begins, and whether it changes. Most people in Western studies, of every orientation, recall their first intense sexual attraction at about the age of 10, before the gonadal changes of puberty, which now begin at about 10.5 years in girls and 11.5 years in boys. Martha McClintock and Gilbert Herdt (1996) suggested that the trigger may be the maturation of the adrenal glands, which happens at about the age of 10. Orientation is usually stable from then on, but not rigidly fixed for everyone. The psychologist Lisa Diamond followed women over 10 years and found that more than two-thirds changed the label they used for their sexual identity, usually broadening rather than narrowing the range of people they were attracted to; men's orientation appears to be less fluid. This sexual fluidity is not evidence that orientation can be changed by treatment. It describes change that arises from within a person's life, mostly in women, mostly in the direction of wider attraction, and it is not under voluntary control.

What does not cause it. Two popular explanations have no support. The first is the parenting theory of Bieber's group: the "close-binding" mother and the distant father. When researchers at the Kinsey Institute (Alan Bell, Martin Weinberg and Sue Kiefer Hammersmith, 1981) interviewed about a thousand gay and lesbian people and several hundred heterosexual people outside any clinic, family relationships turned out to have little power to predict orientation; the best predictor was gender nonconformity in childhood, which itself probably reflects early biological influences. The patterns Bieber reported in his patients may well have been consequences rather than causes; one suggestion is that fathers tend to withdraw from sons who do not fit their idea of a boy. The second is choice. As Pinel and Barnes put it, "People discover their sexual orientation; they don't choose it."

Barlow and Durand draw the conclusion that best fits the evidence: neither the claim that homosexuality is caused by a gene nor the claim that heterosexuality is caused by healthy early experience is likely to be proved correct, and "biology sets certain limits within which social and psychological factors affect development". The same is true of heterosexual orientation, which no one thinks of as needing a cause.

What about mental health? Sexual minorities do have higher rates of depression, anxiety, substance misuse and suicidal thoughts and attempts than heterosexual people, and Section 5.8.5 gives the suicide figures. But the evidence does not point to orientation as the cause. Studies find the excess larger where stigma, rejection and violence are greater, and smaller where families are accepting and the law offers protection. The minority stress model of Ilan Meyer (2003) explains the excess as the result of the chronic stress of belonging to a stigmatised minority, distal (discrimination, rejection, violence) and proximal (concealment, expected rejection, internalised homophobia); Book 2, Section 10.6.9 teaches it with Caitlin Ryan's work on family rejection. Hooker's lesson holds: the extra distress belongs to the situation, not to the orientation.

These findings matter in the consulting room, because families ask about them. Deepali, 52, a schoolteacher in Pune, comes alone to see a psychologist a month after her 22-year-old son told her he is gay. She has not slept properly since. "Was it because his father was always travelling? Because I kept him too close to me?" she asks, and then, more quietly, "Can it still be reversed if we act now?" The psychologist can answer from the evidence. Nothing she did caused her son's orientation: the parenting theories came from studies of patients and have not held up, and orientation is shaped largely before birth and in early childhood by influences no parent controls. It cannot be reversed, attempts to do so harm young people, and in India a doctor who offers them now commits professional misconduct (Section 9.10.7). What she does next does matter, because family acceptance is one of the strongest protections against depression and suicidal behaviour in young gay people. The psychologist also attends to Deepali herself: her grief for the future she had imagined, her fear of what relatives will say, and her own need for support.

Exam trap

Bailey and Pillard (1991): 52 percent of identical and 22 percent of fraternal twin brothers of gay men were gay; for women (1993), 48 and 16 percent. About half of identical twins differ, so genes are not the whole story. Hamer (1993) linked male orientation to Xq28, but the finding was not replicated; Ganna and colleagues (2019) found five loci of small effect and no single "gay gene". LeVay (1991) measured INAH-3 in the human anterior hypothalamus, not the rat's sexually dimorphic nucleus. The fraternal birth order effect (Blanchard and Bogaert, 1996) concerns older brothers born to the same mother, not older sisters or younger siblings. Adult gay and heterosexual men do not differ in circulating hormones. Female fluidity (Diamond) does not mean orientation can be changed by therapy.

Conversion therapy and the professions

The removal of homosexuality from the manuals did not end attempts to change it. If anything, the attempts moved out of hospitals and into private practices, religious ministries and family homes. Book 2, Section 4.4.2 tells the history of the aversive methods: Kurt Freund's nausea-inducing injections in Prague in the 1950s, which his own careful follow-ups led him to abandon; M. P. Feldman and M. J. MacCulloch's electrical "anticipatory avoidance learning" in England; Gerald Davison's 1974 presidential address to the Association for Advancement of Behavior Therapy, published in 1976 as "Homosexuality: the ethical challenge", which asked behaviour therapists to stop offering change treatments at all; and the American Psychological Association's task force of 2009, which found no sound evidence that sexual orientation change efforts work and real evidence that they can harm. Book 2, Section 7.9.2 adds, in one clause, that Masters and Johnson ran a "conversion" programme of their own between 1968 and 1977. The five reasons the professions now condemn all such treatment (a false premise, no effect, harm, compromised consent, and the confusion of distress caused by stigma with the orientation itself) are set out there and need not be repeated. Spitzer's study and his retraction (Section 9.10.4) are the last word on the evidence for talk-based change therapy.

What has changed since 2009 is that the condemnation has become international and, in India, enforceable. On 17 May 2012 the Pan American Health Organization, the WHO's regional office for the Americas, issued a statement that services claiming to "cure" people with a non-heterosexual orientation lack medical justification and are a serious threat to health and well-being. In 2016 the World Psychiatric Association published a position statement on gender identity and same-sex orientation which says that there is no sound scientific evidence that innate sexual orientation can be changed, that so-called treatments of homosexuality can create a setting in which prejudice and discrimination flourish and can be harmful, and that same-sex orientation in itself implies no objective psychological dysfunction.

In India the professional bodies spoke first and the courts and regulators followed. The Indian Psychiatric Society's statements of 2014 and 2018 that homosexuality is not a psychiatric disorder are told in Section 9.10.8. In June 2020 the Society issued a further statement that it totally disapproved of conversion treatments and called for them to stop, and the Indian Association of Clinical Psychologists issued a position statement against conversion therapy the same year. In 2021 the Madras High Court, hearing a petition by a young lesbian couple seeking protection from harassment (S. Sushma v. Commissioner of Police), directed the authorities to act against anyone claiming to "cure" LGBTQIA+ people (Book 2, Section 10.6.9). In a further order of 8 July 2022 in the same case, the court directed the National Medical Commission to act, and on 25 August 2022 the Commission wrote to every state medical council declaring that conversion therapy is professional misconduct, which empowers the councils to discipline any doctor who practises it. The state councils were slower to act: the Tamil Nadu Medical Council, for example, sent its circular warning registered practitioners against conversion therapy only in September 2025, when it reported to the same court.

The harm is not abstract, and clinicians meet it. Hitesh, 31, a software engineer in Hyderabad, comes to a psychologist with low mood, poor sleep and thoughts that his life is not worth living. At 24, after his family found out he was gay, they took him to a private clinic that advertised a "cure". For months he took tablets whose names he was never told and attended sessions in which he was told that his feelings were a sickness caused by pornography and bad company; he was also taken to a faith healer. He married at 26, and he and his wife separated two years later. "They kept saying I was getting better," he says. "I was only getting better at hiding." He describes shame so intense that he cannot look at himself in a mirror, and vivid, intrusive memories of the sessions. The psychologist's assessment covers his suicide risk first, then the depressive episode, the trauma symptoms and the losses of his marriage and family relationships. Treatment targets the depression and the trauma, within an affirmative frame (Book 2, Section 10.6.9) that treats his orientation as part of who he is and the "treatment" as the injury. What the psychologist will not do is reopen the question of whether he could or should be changed.

Clinical link

When a client asks you to change his or her sexual orientation, the request is the beginning of the assessment, not its end. Ask what is driving it: family pressure, an approaching marriage, religious conflict, fear of discovery, internalised shame, depression. Give accurate information: orientation is not a disorder, change efforts do not work and can harm, and in India they are professional misconduct for doctors. Then offer what can help: treatment of any depression or anxiety, work on shame and on the conflict with family or faith, safety planning, and at the client's pace, support in deciding about disclosure. A psychologist whose personal or religious values make this impossible should refer the client, not impose those values.

Exam trap

Conversion therapy (reparative therapy, sexual orientation change efforts) is any attempt to change sexual orientation or gender identity; it is unethical, ineffective and harmful, and it is not what "depathologisation" means. Davison's address was given in 1974 and published in 1976; the American Psychological Association's task force reported in 2009; the World Psychiatric Association's statement dates from 2016. In India: the Indian Psychiatric Society opposed conversion therapy in June 2020; the Madras High Court ruled in S. Sushma (2021) and directed the National Medical Commission in July 2022; the NMC declared conversion therapy professional misconduct on 25 August 2022. Do not confuse these with the Supreme Court's decriminalisation in 2018.

India: from Section 377 to Navtej Singh Johar

India's part in this story began with a colonial law and ended, for now, in the Supreme Court. The Indian Penal Code (IPC) was drafted in the 1830s by the First Law Commission, chaired by Thomas Babington Macaulay (1800 to 1859), enacted in 1860 and brought into force in 1862. Its Section 377, headed "Unnatural offences", followed the English law against buggery that went back to 1533. It provided that "Whoever voluntarily has carnal intercourse against the order of nature with any man, woman or animal, shall be punished with imprisonment for life, or with imprisonment of either description for a term which may extend to ten years, and shall also be liable to fine", and an explanation added that penetration was sufficient to constitute the offence. Notice three features. The section did not mention homosexuality; it punished acts. It made no exception for consent or for privacy. And although it applied in principle to anyone, it fell most heavily on gay and bisexual men and on transgender people. Prosecutions of consenting adults were rare, but the section gave the police and others a lever for harassment, blackmail and extortion, and it pushed people away from health services out of fear of exposure.

That last effect is why the challenge came from public health. In 2001 the Naz Foundation (India) Trust, a Delhi organisation working on HIV and AIDS, petitioned the Delhi High Court, arguing that a law which turned the people most at risk of HIV into criminals made prevention impossible. The National AIDS Control Organisation later supported that argument in court. On 2 July 2009, in Naz Foundation v. Government of NCT of Delhi, a bench of Chief Justice Ajit Prakash Shah and Justice S. Muralidhar held Section 377 unconstitutional in so far as it criminalised consensual sexual acts of adults in private. It violated Article 21 (life and personal liberty, which the courts read to include dignity and privacy), Article 14 (equality before the law, because it singled out a class of people without a rational basis) and Article 15 (the ban on discrimination on grounds of sex, which the court held included sexual orientation). Lawyers call this kind of judgment reading down: the court does not strike the whole section from the statute book, but narrows its meaning so that it no longer covers the conduct that would make it unconstitutional. Section 377 still applied to non-consensual acts and to acts with minors.

The central government did not appeal, but a number of individuals and religious organisations did. On 11 December 2013, in Suresh Kumar Koushal v. Naz Foundation, a two-judge bench of the Supreme Court (Justices G. S. Singhvi and S. J. Mukhopadhaya) reversed the High Court. It held that Section 377 "does not suffer from the vice of unconstitutionality", that any change was for Parliament, and, in a phrase that became notorious, that "a miniscule (sic) fraction of the country's population constitutes lesbians, gays, bisexuals or transgenders". Consensual sex between adults of the same sex was a crime again. Review petitions were dismissed in January 2014.

Indian psychiatry was drawn into the controversy almost at once. In January 2014, at a panel discussion on the judgment, the immediate past president of the Indian Psychiatric Society described homosexuality as unnatural. Early in February 2014 the Society's president and general secretary issued a statement making clear that this was not the Society's position: there was no evidence, they said, that homosexuality is a psychiatric disorder. Two months later, on 15 April 2014, the Supreme Court's judgment in National Legal Services Authority v. Union of India recognised the rights of transgender people (Section 9.9.7), and its reasoning about identity, dignity and fundamental rights sat uneasily beside Koushal.

A crucial step came in a case that was not about sexuality at all. On 24 August 2017, in K. S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India, a bench of nine judges held unanimously that privacy is a fundamental right protected by Article 21. The plurality opinion, written by Justice D. Y. Chandrachud, stated that "sexual orientation is an essential attribute of privacy" and criticised Koushal's reasoning that the rights of a small minority could be set aside because the minority was small. The same year, Parliament passed the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, which forbids discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation in access to mental health care and in the treatment of people with mental illness (sections 18(2) and 21(1)(a)), and forbids diagnosis on the basis of non-conformity with prevailing moral or social values (section 3(3); Section 9.1.4).

Fresh petitions had been filed. One, filed on 27 April 2016, was led by the Bharatanatyam dancer Navtej Singh Johar, with the journalist Sunil Mehra, the chef Ritu Dalmia, the hotelier Aman Nath and the businesswoman Ayesha Kapur among the petitioners. A five-judge Constitution Bench began hearing the cases on 10 July 2018. In early July 2018, as the hearing was about to open (sources give the date as 2 or 10 July), the Indian Psychiatric Society issued a position statement: homosexuality is not a psychiatric disorder; same-sex sexuality is a normal variant of human sexuality; there is no scientific evidence that orientation can be altered by treatment; and the Society supported decriminalisation.

On 6 September 2018 the Supreme Court delivered judgment in Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India. The bench was Chief Justice Dipak Misra (who wrote the lead opinion for himself and Justice A. M. Khanwilkar), with Justices R. F. Nariman, D. Y. Chandrachud and Indu Malhotra writing concurring opinions. The decision was unanimous. The court read down Section 377: in so far as it criminalised consensual sexual conduct between adults, it violated Articles 14, 15, 19 and 21 and was unconstitutional. Koushal was overruled. The section survived only for non-consensual acts, acts with minors and bestiality. The judges held that the court must be guided by constitutional morality, the values of the Constitution, rather than by the prevailing views of society, and Justice Malhotra wrote that "History owes an apology to the members of this community and their families" for the delay in ending their ostracism.

Two later events complete the legal story so far. On 17 October 2023, in Supriyo Chakraborty v. Union of India, a five-judge bench (Chief Justice D. Y. Chandrachud and Justices S. K. Kaul, S. Ravindra Bhat, Hima Kohli and P. S. Narasimha) held unanimously that there is no fundamental right to marry and that the Special Marriage Act could not be read to include same-sex couples, so that recognising same-sex marriage is a matter for Parliament; by a majority of three to two (Chief Justice Chandrachud and Justice Kaul dissenting) it also declined to recognise civil unions and upheld the adoption rule that bars unmarried couples, queer couples among them, from adopting jointly. And on 1 July 2024 the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita replaced the Indian Penal Code. It contains no equivalent of Section 377. Because the new code's rape provision, like the old one, protects only women, commentators have pointed out that dropping Section 377 also removed the provision that had covered non-consensual sexual acts against adult men, a gap still under debate. Table 9.25 sets out the milestones.

Table 9.25 Indian legal and professional milestones on sexual orientation.
WhenEventWhat it decided or said
1860 (in force 1862)Indian Penal Code, Section 377"Carnal intercourse against the order of nature" punishable with up to life imprisonment; no exception for consent
2 July 2009Naz Foundation, Delhi High Court (Shah CJ, Muralidhar J)Section 377 read down: consensual sex between adults in private no longer an offence (Articles 14, 15, 21)
11 December 2013Suresh Kumar Koushal, Supreme Court (two judges)Naz reversed; Section 377 constitutional; change left to Parliament
February 2014Indian Psychiatric Society statementNo evidence that homosexuality is a psychiatric disorder
15 April 2014NALSA v. Union of IndiaRights of transgender people recognised (Section 9.9.7)
24 August 2017K. S. Puttaswamy, nine judges, unanimousPrivacy a fundamental right; sexual orientation an essential attribute of privacy
2017Mental Healthcare ActNo discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation in mental health care (Section 9.1.4)
July 2018Indian Psychiatric Society position statementNot a psychiatric disorder; a normal variant; supports decriminalisation
6 September 2018Navtej Singh Johar, five judges, unanimousSection 377 unconstitutional for consensual sex between adults; Koushal overruled
June 2020Indian Psychiatric Society statementConversion treatments disapproved and to be stopped
25 August 2022National Medical Commission, after the Madras High Court (S. Sushma)Conversion therapy declared professional misconduct
