Law and diagnosis meet in the consulting room. Alok, 28, is a chartered accountant in Jaipur. He has known since school that he is attracted only to men, and for three years he has been out to a small circle of friends. His parents do not know. For the past year they have been arranging meetings with prospective brides, and after the fourth such meeting he books an appointment with a clinical psychologist. "I want you to help me become normal," he begins. "My parents are good people. Everyone says that after marriage things settle down. If there is some medicine, or some therapy, at least I can do my duty." As he talks it emerges that he has been sleeping badly for months, has lost interest in the cricket and the friends he used to enjoy, drinks more on weekends, and sometimes thinks his family would be better off without him.

The psychologist's first task is assessment, and the request to "become normal" is part of what is assessed. She asks about suicidal thoughts directly and makes a safety plan, because the risk is real (Section 5.8.5). She assesses the depressive symptoms (with a standard rating scale, taught in the Assessment book) and his drinking. She asks what "normal" means to him and where the idea came from: his parents' expectations, his own internalised shame, his fear of being found out at work, his religious beliefs. She asks about his sources of support and whether there is any threat of coercion or violence at home. Her formulation is that Alok has a depressive episode, set off and maintained by the chronic stress of concealment and by mounting family pressure, the distal and proximal stressors of the minority stress model, and that his orientation is not the disorder.

She explains, then, why she will not try to change his orientation. Orientation is not a mental disorder in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; attempts to change it do not work and can deepen exactly the depression and shame he already feels; the Indian Psychiatric Society and the other professional bodies reject them, and for a doctor in India they are now professional misconduct. She also points out, gently, that a marriage entered into to hide his orientation would involve a second person, a wife who has not been told, whose life would be affected too. What she offers instead is treatment of the depression (cognitive therapy, Book 2, Sections 5.3 and 5.4, with a medical opinion on antidepressants if it does not improve), work on the shame and on his relationship with his parents within an affirmative frame (Book 2, Section 10.6.9), and, if and when he wants it, help in planning whether, when and how to tell his family, perhaps with a family session in which his parents can ask the questions Deepali asked in Section 9.10.6. Everything he says is confidential; he is an adult, and his parents will not be told anything without his consent (section 23 of the Mental Healthcare Act gives a person with mental illness a right to confidentiality). Since 2018 he is not, in the eyes of the law, a criminal, and his orientation had stopped being a disorder in the manuals years before he was born.

Indian context

The sequence matters in interviews. The Delhi High Court read down Section 377 in Naz Foundation (2 July 2009); the Supreme Court reversed it in Koushal (11 December 2013); the Indian Psychiatric Society stated in February 2014, and again in its position statement of July 2018, that homosexuality is not a psychiatric disorder; and the Supreme Court decriminalised consensual sex between adults in Navtej Singh Johar (6 September 2018), relying on the privacy judgment in Puttaswamy (2017). In India, unlike the United States, the professional statement came long after the manuals had changed: by 2018 neither DSM nor ICD had listed homosexuality as a disorder for decades, and the Society's statements told the court and the public what the profession had known since 1973 and 1990.

Exam trap

The Supreme Court decriminalised consensual same-sex relations between adults in 2018 (Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India, 6 September, five judges, unanimous, Chief Justice Dipak Misra). 2009 was the Delhi High Court (Naz Foundation, Chief Justice A. P. Shah and Justice S. Muralidhar), and 2013 was the Supreme Court's reversal (Suresh Kumar Koushal, two judges). Section 377 was read down, not struck down entirely: it still covered non-consensual acts, minors and bestiality until the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita replaced the IPC on 1 July 2024. The Indian Psychiatric Society's 2018 statement came shortly before the judgment, in July. NALSA (2014) is about transgender people, not about Section 377, and Supriyo (2023) did not legalise same-sex marriage.

Figure 9.14 gathers the whole story of this section in one place, from the coining of the word "homosexual" to the new criminal code. Read it from top to bottom and you will see the three strands of this section, the sexologists and the DSM, the WHO and the ICD, and the Indian courts and professions, braiding together across a century and a half.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Classifying homosexuality as a mental disorder is called , and removing that classification is called .
  2. The word "homosexual" was coined by , who used it in a letter of 1868 and in print in 1869.
  3. On the Kinsey scale a rating of 6 means exclusively homosexual, and a person with no socio-sexual contacts or reactions is rated .
  4. Evelyn Hooker's judges examined Rorschach, TAT and Make-a-Picture-Story records from 30 homosexual and 30 heterosexual men who were not in .
  5. The American Psychiatric Association's Board of Trustees voted to remove homosexuality from DSM-II on 15 December .
  6. DSM-III (1980) kept a category called homosexuality, which was dropped in DSM-III-R (1987).
  7. ICD-10's block F66 included egodystonic sexual orientation (F66.1); ICD-11 the whole block.
  8. The finding that each older brother raises a man's odds of being gay by about a third is the effect.
  9. Simon LeVay (1991) reported that the hypothalamic nucleus was more than twice as large in heterosexual as in homosexual men.

Answers: (1) pathologisation; depathologisation, (2) Karl Maria Kertbeny, (3) X, (4) therapy (treatment), (5) 1973, (6) ego-dystonic, (7) deleted, (8) fraternal birth order, (9) INAH-3, (10) Navtej Singh Johar.

The case of Bruce, Brenda and David Reimer, revisited

Read the story of the Winnipeg twin again with this chapter in your hands. In the vocabulary of Section 9.1.2, Bruce's sex was male on every count that existed when he was born: XY chromosomes, an Sry gene that had built testes (Section 9.8.2), and the typical male hormones before birth whose permanent, organisational effects Section 9.8.3 describes. The accident of April 1966 took away his penis and the operation at 22 months took away his testes, but neither could undo what had happened before birth. Money's advice rested on the theory of Section 9.8.5: that a newborn is psychosexually open and that gender identity is written by rearing in the first two or three years. So Brenda was given the gender role of a girl, Money's own term from 1955 for everything a person says and does to show others that he or she is a boy or a girl: the name, the upbringing and, at 12, the oestrogen. What no one could give her was a girl's gender identity, the private sense of being male or female. She preferred boys' games, had little interest in dolls or sewing, felt like a boy by seven and hated her developing breasts, and when her father told her the truth at 14, "all of a sudden everything clicked". Diamond and Sigmundson's follow-up of 1997 turned the most famous proof that gender identity is learned into evidence against it, and Reiner and Gearhart's cloacal exstrophy series (2004) and the Dominican 5-alpha-reductase families (Section 9.8.4) showed that the case was no fluke: something established before birth, probably the action of androgens on the developing brain, pulls strongly towards a particular identity, and surgery, hormones and upbringing in the other direction often fail to override it. Girls with congenital adrenal hyperplasia, almost all of whom have a female identity, are the reminder that the rule is not simply "male hormones make a male identity".

The opening also asked when a mismatch between body and identity counts as a disorder, and how these questions differ from the problems of sexual response and sexual interest. Brenda's childhood shows several of the indicators of Section 9.9.2 (a strong preference for the games of boys, a rejection of typically feminine toys and activities, a strong dislike of the sex characteristics that were developing), and her distress was unmistakable; but DSM-5-TR diagnoses the distress, not the identity, and ICD-11 has taken gender incongruence out of the mental disorders altogether, into Chapter 17 (Section 9.9.1). The chapter also warns against reading too much into one life. The child had been castrated, given hormones and raised in a family under extraordinary strain, and because David's identity matched his chromosomes, his gonads before castration and his prenatal hormones, his case says nothing directly about why a transgender person's identity differs from the sex assigned at birth. What can fairly be drawn is narrower: in both, a gender identity once formed resisted strong pressure from outside to change it. When David later had testosterone, a mastectomy and a phalloplasty, the same kinds of treatment that Section 9.9.6 describes as gender-affirming, his body was at last being changed to follow his identity instead of his identity being forced to follow his body, which is Pinel's conclusion: irrevocable treatments should await the emergence of the person's own gender identity. None of his suffering was a sexual dysfunction or a paraphilia. His problem was not with sexual response (Sections 9.2 to 9.4) or with what aroused him (Sections 9.5 to 9.7) but with being assigned a gender that was not his, which is why DSM-5 gave gender dysphoria a chapter of its own.

Chapter summary

9.1 Sex, Gender and Disorder: The Science of Sexuality and How the Manuals Classify It. Three families share this chapter: sexual dysfunctions (problems of response), paraphilic disorders (problems of what arouses a person) and gender dysphoria (distress from a mismatch between gender identity and assigned gender). Sex is biological; gender identity is the inner sense of being male, female or another gender; gender role and gender expression are how gender is lived in public; sexual orientation is whom one is attracted to; and androgyny, which Bem measured in 1974, is never a diagnosis. Sexology began with Krafft-Ebing's Psychopathia Sexualis (1886), Ellis, Hirschfeld and Freud's Three Essays (1905), then Kinsey's interviews (1948, 1953) and Masters and Johnson's laboratory (1966). Rarity and social disapproval cannot define disorder; harm and consent decide. DSM-IV's single class became three DSM-5 chapters (13, 14 and 19), and ICD-11 moved the sexual dysfunctions, the sexual pain disorders and gender incongruence to Chapter 17, leaving only the paraphilic disorders in Chapter 6.

9.2 The Sexual Response Cycle: Models, Biology and What Disrupts It. Masters and Johnson (1966) described four phases, excitement, plateau, orgasm and resolution, driven by vasocongestion and myotonia, with a refractory period in men; Kaplan (1979) put desire first in a triphasic model, and the DSM joined the two. No disorder belongs to plateau or resolution, and pain lies outside the cycle. Basson's circular model (2000) starts from sexual neutrality, with responsive desire that follows arousal. Erection is parasympathetic (nitric oxide, then cGMP, whose breakdown PDE-5 inhibitors block) and emission sympathetic; testosterone is necessary for male desire, prolactin suppresses desire and serotonin inhibits ejaculation. Barlow showed that anxiety can raise arousal in functional men (1983) and that dysfunction comes from cognitive interference (1986), and Bancroft and Janssen's dual control model (2000) balances excitation against inhibition. Relationships, sexual scripts, erotophobia and myths shape response, and nocturnal penile tumescence points to a psychological cause of waking erectile failure.

9.3 Sexual Dysfunctions I: The Shared Rules, and the Disorders of Desire, Arousal and Erection. DSM-5-TR's eight sexual dysfunctions share a template: a problem lasting about 6 months, on about 75 to 100 percent of occasions, with clinically significant distress, not better explained by another mental disorder, severe relationship distress, a substance or a medical condition, and specified as lifelong or acquired and generalised or situational; the exceptions are favourite questions. Laumann's 1992 survey found problems in 43 percent of women and 31 percent of men, mostly low desire in women and early ejaculation in men. Female sexual interest/arousal disorder (3 of 6 indicators) replaced DSM-IV's separate desire and arousal disorders in women, while male hypoactive sexual desire disorder keeps desire apart from arousal in men. Erectile disorder rises steeply with age, is linked to diabetes and vascular disease, predicts coronary artery disease and is treated with sensate focus and with sildenafil (1998) and the other PDE-5 inhibitors, never with nitrates.

9.4 Sexual Dysfunctions II: Orgasm, Ejaculation, Pain and Substance-Induced Dysfunction. A woman who reaches orgasm with clitoral stimulation but not in intercourse does not have female orgasmic disorder, which is treated by directed masturbation (LoPiccolo and Lobitz, 1972). Delayed ejaculation was inhibited male orgasm in DSM-III and male orgasmic disorder in DSM-IV. Premature (early) ejaculation, within about 1 minute of penetration for at least 6 months, is most commonly associated with anxiety, not with masturbation, diabetes or personality disorder, and is treated with Semans's stop-start (1956) and Masters and Johnson's squeeze techniques, dapoxetine, daily SSRIs and topical anaesthetics. DSM-5 merged vaginismus and dyspareunia into genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder (Binik, 2010), treated with graded dilators. Substance/medication-induced sexual dysfunction has no 6-month rule; SSRIs mainly delay orgasm and ejaculation, antipsychotics act partly through prolactin, and alcohol "provokes the desire, but it takes away the performance". Sexual aversion survives only as an example of other specified sexual dysfunction.

9.5 Paraphilias and Paraphilic Disorders: From Perversion to Disorder. A paraphilia is an intense and persistent sexual interest other than in genital stimulation or fondling with phenotypically normal, physically mature, consenting human partners. Since DSM-5, whose paraphilias group Ray Blanchard chaired, a paraphilia is necessary but not sufficient for a paraphilic disorder, which also needs current distress or impairment, or harm or risk of harm to others; Criterion A requires 6 months. Krafft-Ebing (1886) coined masochism, Binet (1887) gave fetishism its sexual meaning, Krauss (1903) coined "paraphilia" and Money popularised it, and the word entered the DSM in 1980. ICD-11 rebuilt the group around consent (6D30 to 6D3Z) and deleted fetishism, fetishistic transvestism and sadomasochism. The disorders are overwhelmingly male, interests are far commoner than disorders (Långström and Seto, 2006), paraphilias cluster (Abel, 1988), sexual offending is a legal rather than a clinical category, and compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72) is an impulse control disorder.

9.6 The Eight Paraphilic Disorders and the Other Paraphilias. DSM-5-TR groups its eight named disorders by focus. The courtship disorders are voyeuristic (watching an unsuspecting person; the person must be at least 18), exhibitionistic (exposing one's genitals; no minimum age) and frotteuristic (touching or rubbing against a nonconsenting person). The algolagnic disorders are sexual masochism (being humiliated, beaten, bound or made to suffer, with an asphyxiophilia specifier) and sexual sadism (the suffering of another person). The target disorders are pedophilic (a prepubescent child, generally 13 or younger, with the person at least 16 and 5 years older), fetishistic (non-living objects or non-genital body parts) and transvestic, specified with fetishism or with autogynephilia, Blanchard's term (1989) for arousal by the image of oneself as a woman. Masochism, fetishism and transvestism need distress or impairment; the other five are also met by acting on a nonconsenting person. Necrophilia, zoophilia and telephone scatologia are among the other specified paraphilias.

9.7 Why Paraphilic Disorders Develop, and How They Are Assessed and Treated. Rachman (1966) conditioned weak, extinguishable arousal to a picture of women's boots, and masturbatory conditioning explains how early experiences are rehearsed in fantasy and reinforced by orgasm, but learning cannot explain who is susceptible. Money's lovemap (1986) and Freund's courtship disorder hypothesis (1983) are the developmental accounts. Castration (Bremer, 1959) and antiandrogens reduce the strength of sexual drive, never its direction; Cantor's group found lower IQ and non-right-handedness in pedophilia, and Kafka proposed a monoamine hypothesis (1997). Assessment uses penile plethysmography (Freund's method), viewing time and actuarial risk assessment; sexual recidivism averaged 13.4 percent (Hanson and Bussière, 1998). Cognitive-behavioural programmes with relapse prevention have mixed evidence (Marques, 2005). SSRIs suit milder cases, while cyproterone acetate, medroxyprogesterone acetate and GnRH agonists such as triptorelin (Rösler and Witztum, 1998) lower testosterone, and their effect is reversible.

9.8 Becoming Male or Female: Sexual Differentiation and Gender Identity. Male and female are not opposites (Pinel's "mamawawa"): both develop from a shared start through a series of switches. The Sry gene on the Y chromosome turns the primordial gonads into testes; testosterone builds the Wolffian ducts, Müllerian-inhibiting substance from the testes removes the Müllerian ducts, and dihydrotestosterone, made by 5-alpha-reductase, masculinises the external genitals, while female ducts develop without any ovarian hormone (Jost, 1947). Hormones have permanent organisational effects around birth and temporary activational effects from puberty (Phoenix and colleagues, 1959). Complete androgen insensitivity, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, 5-alpha-reductase deficiency, Turner syndrome (45,X) and Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY) test the rules. Money held that gender identity is learned in the first years, but Diamond and Sigmundson's follow-up of the Reimer case (1997) and Reiner and Gearhart's cloacal exstrophy series (2004) showed that rearing often fails to override it. Gender identity firms up between about 18 months and 3 years.

9.9 Gender Dysphoria and Gender Incongruence. Hirschfeld coined "transvestite" (1910), and Benjamin's The Transsexual Phenomenon (1966) argued for changing the body. The diagnosis moved from transsexualism (DSM-III) through gender identity disorder (DSM-IV) to gender dysphoria (DSM-5), which diagnoses distress rather than identity, and to ICD-11's gender incongruence (HA60, HA61) in Chapter 17, which requires no distress. Children need 6 of 8 indicators, including a strong desire to be another gender; adolescents and adults need 2 of 6; both need 6 months and distress or impairment. No cause has been established. Gender nonconformity, transvestic disorder, body dysmorphic disorder and psychosis must be distinguished. Care includes psychological support, social transition, puberty suppression (the Dutch protocol), hormones and surgery, guided by WPATH's Standards of Care, Version 8 (2022), and debated since the Cass Review (2024). In India the NALSA judgment (2014) recognised a third gender, followed by the Transgender Persons Act (2019) and its 2026 amendment.

9.10 Homosexuality: Pathologised and Depathologised. Pathologisation is the classification of something as a mental disorder and depathologisation the removal of that classification; neither means changing orientation. Kertbeny coined "homosexual" (1868, in print 1869), Westphal made "contrary sexual feeling" a psychiatric matter (1870) and Krafft-Ebing called it degeneration, while Ellis and Freud, whose 1935 letter said it "cannot be classified as an illness", disagreed; Bieber's patient samples (1962) kept the sickness model alive. Kinsey's 0 to 6 scale and Hooker's blind judges (1957) broke it. The American Psychiatric Association's board removed homosexuality from DSM-II on 15 December 1973, and ego-dystonic homosexuality lasted until DSM-III-R (1987). ICD-10 (1990) dropped homosexuality but kept F66, which ICD-11 deleted. Twin studies, the fraternal birth order effect and LeVay's INAH-3 (1991) point to biological contributions, with no single gene. Conversion therapy is unethical, and in India Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India (6 September 2018) read down Section 377.

From the past papers

  1. The 'start-stop technique' and the 'squeeze technique' is useful in the treatment of: (RML 2024, real)

    (a) Male erectile disorder (b) Premature ejaculation (c) Inhibited male orgasm (d) Vaginismus

    Answer: (b) Premature ejaculation. Semans's stop-start technique (1956) and Masters and Johnson's squeeze technique teach the man to recognise and control the sensations before the point of no return (Section 9.4.3). Erectile disorder is treated with sensate focus and PDE-5 inhibitors, inhibited male orgasm is the DSM-III name for delayed ejaculation, the opposite problem (Section 9.4.2), and vaginismus is treated with graded dilators (Section 9.4.4).

  2. Premature ejaculation is most commonly associated with: (RML 2024, real)

    (a) Diabetes mellitus (b) Masturbation (c) Personality disorder (d) Anxiety state

    Answer: (d) Anxiety state. DSM-5-TR links premature ejaculation to anxiety disorders, especially social anxiety disorder, and anxiety raises the sympathetic arousal that partly drives ejaculation, so worry and early ejaculation feed each other; "anxiety state" is simply the older name (Section 9.4.3). Diabetes is the classic physical cause of erectile disorder, masturbation does not cause premature ejaculation (the belief belongs to dhat syndrome), and DSM-5-TR names no personality disorder among its risk factors.

  3. Autogynephilia is defined by: (RML 2022, real)

    (a) Recurrent, and intense sexual attraction to prepubescent children (b) Recurrent, and intense sexual attraction to thoughts, images, or thoughts about oneself as a woman (c) Recurrent, and intense sexual attraction to oneself (d) Recurrent, and intense sexual attraction to being humiliated

    Answer: (b) Recurrent, and intense sexual attraction to thoughts, images, or thoughts about oneself as a woman. Blanchard's term (1989), built from auto, gyne and philia, is in DSM-5-TR's words "a man's paraphilic tendency to be sexually aroused by the thought or image of himself as a woman", and it is a specifier of transvestic disorder (Section 9.6.6). Attraction to prepubescent children is pedophilia, to being humiliated is masochism, and attraction to oneself in general is not autogynephilia, which is specifically about the image of oneself as a woman.

  4. A condition characterised by recurrent intense sexually arousing fantasies and urges involving the use of an inanimate object to obtain sexual gratification: (Amity 2023, real)

    (a) Necrophilia (b) Fetishism (c) Frotteurism (d) Voyeurism

    Answer: (b) Fetishism. Fetishistic disorder is recurrent and intense arousal, for at least 6 months, from non-living objects or a highly specific focus on non-genital body parts, with distress or impairment (Section 9.6.5). Necrophilia needs a corpse and is an other specified paraphilia, frotteurism needs a nonconsenting person who is touched or rubbed against, and voyeurism needs an unsuspecting person who is watched.

  5. A condition in which a person's sexual identity does not match his or her physical gender: (Amity 2023, real)

    (a) Paraphilia (b) Gender identity disorder (c) Sexual dysfunction (d) Androgyny

    Answer: (b) Gender identity disorder. The loosely worded stem describes a mismatch between gender identity and assigned sex, which DSM-IV and ICD-10 called gender identity disorder, DSM-5 calls gender dysphoria and ICD-11 calls gender incongruence (Sections 9.1.2 and 9.9.1). A paraphilia concerns what arouses a person, a sexual dysfunction concerns the sexual response, and androgyny, which Bem measured in 1974, is a blend of masculine and feminine traits and never a diagnosis.

  6. In ICD-11, gender incongruence is placed in: (NIMHANS 2026 recall, real)

    (a) Chapter 6, Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders (b) Chapter 5, Endocrine, nutritional or metabolic diseases (c) Chapter 17, Conditions related to sexual health (d) Nowhere: it was removed from ICD-11 altogether

    Answer: (c) Chapter 17, Conditions related to sexual health. ICD-11, adopted in 2019 and in force from 1 January 2022, moved gender incongruence (HA60 and HA61) out of the mental disorders into the new Chapter 17, because being transgender is not a mental disorder but many transgender people need health care that health systems must code (Section 9.9.1). It is not in Chapter 6 or in Chapter 5, and it was not removed, since the code is what gives access to care.

  7. When gender incongruence moved out of the mental disorders chapter in ICD-11, which of these stayed in Chapter 6 (Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders)? (NIMHANS 2026 recall, real)

    (a) Sexual dysfunctions (b) Paraphilic disorders (c) Sexual pain disorders (d) Gender incongruence of childhood

    Answer: (b) Paraphilic disorders. Only the paraphilic disorders (6D30 to 6D3Z) stayed in Chapter 6, because they meet the general requirements for a mental disorder and their forensic use depends on that status (Sections 9.1.6 and 9.5.4). The sexual dysfunctions (HA00 to HA03), the sexual pain disorders (HA20) and gender incongruence of childhood (HA61), which is no more a mental disorder than the adult category, all went to Chapter 17.

  8. The DSM-5 (2013) diagnosis that corresponds to ICD-11 gender incongruence is: (NIMHANS 2026 recall, real)

    (a) Gender identity disorder (b) Transsexualism (c) Gender incongruence (d) Gender dysphoria

    Answer: (d) Gender dysphoria. DSM-5 renamed DSM-IV's gender identity disorder gender dysphoria, moving the focus from the identity to the distress, and it corresponds to ICD-11's gender incongruence (Section 9.9.1). Gender identity disorder is the DSM-IV name, transsexualism the DSM-III and ICD-10 (F64.0) name, and gender incongruence is ICD-11's own term, not a DSM diagnosis.

  9. Reports on the sexual behaviour of men were published by: (RINPAS 2019, real)

    (a) Sigmund Freud (b) Alfred Kinsey (c) Anna Freud (d) Karen Horney

    Answer: (b) Alfred Kinsey. Kinsey, a zoologist at Indiana University, interviewed thousands of ordinary volunteers face to face and published Sexual Behavior in the Human Male (1948) and Sexual Behavior in the Human Female (1953) (Sections 9.1.3 and 9.10.3). Freud's Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905) is a theory of sexuality, not a survey, and Anna Freud and Karen Horney are psychoanalytic distractors.

  10. Masters and Johnson's sexual response cycle is distinct from Kaplan's model. Which of the following correctly reflects Masters and Johnson's sequence? (IHBAS 2025, real)

    (a) Excitement → Plateau → Orgasm → Resolution (b) Plateau → Excitement → Orgasm → Resolution (c) Excitement → Desire → Plateau → Orgasm → Refractory (d) Desire → Arousal → Plateau → Orgasm → Resolution

    Answer: (a) Excitement → Plateau → Orgasm → Resolution. Human Sexual Response (1966) described four phases, the same in both sexes, with a refractory period in men after orgasm (Section 9.2.2). Desire comes from Kaplan's triphasic model (1979), so options (c) and (d) borrow from her, plateau follows excitement rather than preceding it, and the refractory period is normal physiology within resolution, not a phase of its own.

  11. According to Masters and Johnson, following is the correct sequence of human sexual response: (RINPAS 2024, real)

    (a) Excitement-Orgasm-Plateau-Resolution (b) Excitement-Plateau-Orgasm-Resolution (c) Excitement-Arousal-Orgasm-Resolution (d) Arousal-Excitement-Orgasm-Resolution

    Answer: (b) Excitement-Plateau-Orgasm-Resolution. The plateau, in which arousal levels off at a high level, comes between excitement and orgasm, and resolution returns the body to the unaroused state (Section 9.2.2). Option (a) puts plateau after orgasm, and "arousal" is not one of the four Masters and Johnson phase names: excitement is the phase of arousal, driven by vasocongestion.

  12. Kegel exercises strengthen which part of the body? (NIMHANS 2026 recall, real)

    (a) The abdominal (core) muscles (b) The pelvic floor muscles (c) The lower back (lumbar) muscles (d) The gluteal (buttock) muscles

    Answer: (b) The pelvic floor muscles. Kegel exercises (1948) teach a person to tighten and relax the pelvic floor deliberately, and sex therapy adds them to directed masturbation for female orgasmic disorder, to the stop-start programme for premature ejaculation and to graded dilators for vaginismus (Sections 9.4.1, 9.4.3, 9.4.4 and 9.4.6). The abdominal, lumbar and gluteal muscles are not their target.

  13. The twenty-third pair of chromosomes is missing an X, turning into a syndrome characterised by a short, infertile female: (IHBAS 2020, real)

    (a) Down Syndrome (b) Asperger's Syndrome (c) Turner's Syndrome (d) Klinefelter's Syndrome

    Answer: (c) Turner's Syndrome. In Turner syndrome a girl has only one X chromosome (45,X); her ovaries fail to develop properly, so she is usually infertile, and she tends to be short (Section 9.8.4). Klinefelter syndrome is the opposite case, an extra X in a boy (47,XXY), while Down syndrome (trisomy 21) and Asperger's syndrome (now part of autism spectrum disorder) are not sex chromosome conditions (Chapter 3, Sections 3.3 and 3.5).

  14. A condition characterised by the karyotype 'XXY' with an extra X chromosome: (Amity 2023, real)

    (a) Down's Syndrome (b) Turner Syndrome (c) Klinefelter's syndrome (d) Transsexualism

    Answer: (c) Klinefelter's syndrome. In Klinefelter syndrome a boy has an extra X (47,XXY), with small testes and low testosterone, so he is usually infertile, and he is often tall (Section 9.8.4). Turner syndrome is a missing X in a girl (45,X), Down's syndrome is trisomy 21 (Chapter 3), and transsexualism is the old diagnostic name for a gender identity that differs from the assigned sex (DSM-III, ICD-10 F64.0), not a chromosome condition (Section 9.9.1).

  15. A male child presents with tall stature, small testes, and learning difficulties. Karyotype analysis shows an extra X chromosome. This condition is: (IHBAS 2025, real)

    (a) Klinefelter syndrome (b) Turner syndrome (c) Prader-Willi syndrome (d) Down syndrome

    Answer: (a) Klinefelter syndrome. An extra X in a boy who is tall with small testes is Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY), which is often diagnosed only when a man seeks help for infertility (Section 9.8.4). Turner syndrome affects girls, who have one X, and Prader-Willi and Down syndromes are not sex chromosome conditions (Chapter 3, Section 3.3).

  16. Sex chromosome anomaly (XXY) results into the development of: (M.Phil Clinical Psychology Entrance Examination 2021, real)

    (a) Klinefelter's Syndrome (b) Turner Syndrome (c) Down Syndrome (d) Phenylketonuria

    Answer: (a) Klinefelter's Syndrome. The 47,XXY karyotype is Klinefelter syndrome: a male with an extra X, small testes and low testosterone (Section 9.8.4). Turner syndrome is 45,X, Down syndrome is trisomy 21, and phenylketonuria is an inborn error of metabolism (Chapter 3, Section 3.3); none of the three is an XXY anomaly.

  17. What do researchers mean by pathologization and depathologization of homosexuality? (Entrance Mock 2, practice)

    (a) None of the above (b) The classification of homosexuality as a mental disorder and the removal of this classification (c) The changing of sexual orientation from homosexual to heterosexual (d) The process of coming out as a gay or lesbian individual

    Answer: (b) The classification of homosexuality as a mental disorder and the removal of this classification. The DSM listed homosexuality until the American Psychiatric Association's Board of Trustees voted to remove it on 15 December 1973, and the WHO dropped it with ICD-10 in 1990 (Sections 9.10.1, 9.10.4 and 9.10.5). Changing orientation is conversion therapy, which is unethical and ineffective, coming out is disclosure, and "none of the above" is wrong because option (b) is exactly right.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Klinefelter syndrome is 47,XXY: a boy with an extra X, small testes and low testosterone, usually infertile and often tall, sometimes with breast tissue at puberty; many men are diagnosed only when they seek help for infertility. asked 4 times
  2. Turner syndrome is 45,X: a girl with only one X chromosome, whose ovaries fail to develop properly, so she is usually infertile, often needs hormones to start puberty and tends to be short. It is the mirror image of Klinefelter syndrome. asked 4 times
  3. The same mismatch between identity and assigned sex has had four names: transsexualism (DSM-III, 1980; ICD-10 F64.0), gender identity disorder (DSM-IV, 1994; ICD-10 F64), gender dysphoria (DSM-5, 2013, and DSM-5-TR, which diagnose the distress, not the identity) and gender incongruence (ICD-11); DSM-5's gender dysphoria corresponds to ICD-11's gender incongruence. asked 3 times
  4. In ICD-11 (adopted May 2019, in force 1 January 2022) gender incongruence (HA60 of adolescence or adulthood, HA61 of childhood) is in Chapter 17, "Conditions related to sexual health": not Chapter 6, not Chapter 5 and not removed, so that care can be coded without the label of mental illness. asked 2 times
  5. Only the paraphilic disorders (6D30 to 6D3Z) stayed in ICD-11's Chapter 6; the sexual dysfunctions (HA00 to HA03), the sexual pain disorders (HA20, sexual pain-penetration disorder) and both gender incongruence categories moved to Chapter 17. asked 2 times
  6. Masters and Johnson, Human Sexual Response (1966, 382 women and 312 men): four phases, excitement, plateau, orgasm, resolution, the same in both sexes, driven by vasocongestion and myotonia, with a refractory period in men after orgasm. asked 2 times
  7. Helen Singer Kaplan, Disorders of Sexual Desire (1979): the triphasic model put desire first (desire, excitement, orgasm); Masters and Johnson's model has no desire phase, and Basson's circular model (2000) adds responsive desire. asked 2 times
  8. Erectile disorder: difficulty obtaining or keeping an erection, or reduced rigidity, on 75 to 100 percent of occasions for about 6 months; its classic physical links are diabetes and vascular disease; it is treated with sensate focus and PDE-5 inhibitors such as sildenafil (1998), which need sexual stimulation and must never be combined with nitrates. asked 2 times
  9. The stop-start (Semans, 1956) and squeeze (Masters and Johnson) techniques treat premature ejaculation; they help 60 to 90 percent of men at first but about 25 percent after 3 years. asked once
  10. Premature (early) ejaculation (DSM-5-TR): ejaculation within about 1 minute of vaginal penetration and before the man wishes it, for at least 6 months, on 75 to 100 percent of occasions; it is most commonly associated with anxiety (anxiety disorders, especially social anxiety disorder), and dapoxetine was the first drug licensed for it. asked once
  11. Masturbation does not cause premature ejaculation: the belief belongs to the semen-loss worries of dhat syndrome; diabetes belongs to erectile disorder, and DSM-5-TR names no personality disorder among its risk factors. asked once
  12. Delayed ejaculation was called inhibited male orgasm in DSM-III and DSM-III-R and male orgasmic disorder in DSM-IV; it is the opposite of premature ejaculation, and the delay must occur in partnered activity without the man desiring it. asked once
  13. DSM-5 merged vaginismus and dyspareunia into genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder (Binik, 2010); one of four dimensions is enough (penetration difficulty, pain, fear of pain, pelvic floor tensing), and graded dilators let 80 to 100 percent of women overcome vaginismus. asked once
  14. Autogynephilia (Ray Blanchard, 1989) is a man's paraphilic tendency to be sexually aroused by the thought or image of himself as a woman; in DSM-5-TR it is a specifier of transvestic disorder (with fetishism, with autogynephilia), not a disorder of its own. asked once
  15. Fetishistic disorder: recurrent, intense arousal for at least 6 months from non-living objects or a highly specific focus on non-genital body parts (partialism), with distress or impairment; women's clothes worn in cross-dressing and genital stimulation devices do not count. asked once
  16. Match the target: necrophilia is arousal involving corpses (an other specified paraphilia), frotteuristic disorder is touching or rubbing against a nonconsenting person, and voyeuristic disorder is watching an unsuspecting person naked, undressing or having sex, with the person at least 18. asked once
  17. Pedophilic disorder involves a prepubescent child, generally 13 or younger, with the person at least 16 and at least 5 years older; sexual masochism disorder is arousal from being humiliated, beaten, bound or otherwise made to suffer, with the specifier "with asphyxiophilia". asked once
  18. Sex, gender identity, gender expression and sexual orientation are separate dimensions; a paraphilia concerns what arouses a person, a sexual dysfunction the sexual response, and androgyny (high masculine and high feminine scores on Bem's Sex Role Inventory, 1974) is never a diagnosis. asked once
  19. Alfred Kinsey interviewed thousands of volunteers for Sexual Behavior in the Human Male (1948) and Sexual Behavior in the Human Female (1953); Masters and Johnson observed the body in the laboratory (1966), and Freud's Three Essays (1905) was a theory, not a survey. asked once
  20. Kegel exercises (1948) train a person to tighten and relax the pelvic floor muscles; sex therapy adds them for female orgasmic disorder, premature ejaculation and vaginismus. asked once
  21. Pathologisation is the classification of a behaviour or identity as a mental disorder and depathologisation the removal of that classification; neither means changing sexual orientation or coming out. asked once
  22. The American Psychiatric Association's Board of Trustees removed homosexuality from DSM-II on 15 December 1973; DSM-III (1980) kept ego-dystonic homosexuality until DSM-III-R (1987); ICD-10 (adopted 17 May 1990) dropped homosexuality, and ICD-11 deleted the whole F66 block. asked once
  23. Conversion therapy (any attempt to change sexual orientation or gender identity) is unethical, ineffective and harmful; in India the National Medical Commission declared it professional misconduct on 25 August 2022. asked once
